| **CORUSCATIO QUINTA** | | **CORUSCATIO QUINTA** |
| *Sanctæ Inquisitionis Lusitanæ fundatam jurisdictionem ad prohibendam prædictam erroneam praxim, puniendosque ejus assertores clarissima luce perfundit.* | | *It bathes in the clearest light the well-founded jurisdiction of the Holy Portuguese Inquisition for prohibiting the aforesaid erroneous practice and for punishing its assertors.* |
| 1 Stabilita, firmataque doctrina, nempe non licere aliquo modo nomina complicum ad correctionem, seu ad alia colligenda bona inquirere, aut pœnitentes ad id adigere, per necessariam consequentiam sequitur sanctæ Fidei Tribunal potestatem inconcussam habere, habuisseque ad illam praxim improbandam, puniendosque ejus fautores, eosque denuntiari jubere. Muratorius aut deceptus, aut à suomet animo inductus prædictum Tribunal assumpsit lacerandum. Nullo jure id fecisse demonstrabimus. Ipse tamen, ut sui nominis mensuram adimpleret, minutissime corrosit. Fidei Areopagus murus est, quo sancta Ecclesiæ civitas ab hostium incursibus munitur, ac mille propugnaculis defenditur. Ast Muratorius, muris instar, dentibus suis hæc fundamenta comminuit, ut in ruinam trahat; in vanum tamen laborat. | | 1 The doctrine having been established and confirmed, namely, that it is not licit in any way to inquire after the names of accomplices for correction, or for gathering other goods, or to compel penitents thereto, it follows by necessary consequence that the Tribunal of the holy Faith has, and has had, unshaken power to condemn that practice, to punish its abettors, and to order that they be denounced. Muratori, either deceived, or led on by his own mind, took up the aforesaid Tribunal to be torn to pieces. We shall demonstrate that he did this by no right. He himself, however, that he might fill up the measure of his name, gnawed at it most minutely. The Areopagus of the Faith is a wall by which the holy city of the Church is fortified against the incursions of enemies, and defended by a thousand bulwarks. But Muratori, after the fashion of a mouse, crushes these foundations with his teeth, that he may drag them into ruin; yet he labors in vain. |
| 2 Nescio quæ causa ad tantum facinus impelleret, moveretque, ut à longe positus conspicuos viros in nihilo ei infensos sic quassaverit, sic lacessiverit. Nullus hucusque illud rectissimum Fidei sustentaculum irreverenter impetiit, nisi qui ei in aliquo debitor effectus sit. Videat Muratorius, ne hoc ære gravetur. Atque ut & nos judicium feramus, non ab scopo aberrare visus ero, si in trutinam revocemus doctrinas aliquas, quas ipse in suo *Lamindi Pritanii de ingeniorum moderatione* intermiscet in opere. Ut id assequar, antequam ad propositam materiam deveniamus, in animum venit paulisper ad alia divertere sermonem. Igitur sit | | 2 I know not what cause impelled and moved him to so great a deed, that, placed afar off, he should so shake, so provoke conspicuous men in nothing hostile to him. No one hitherto has irreverently assailed that most upright support of the Faith, except one who has been made its debtor in something. Let Muratori look to it, lest he be burdened with this debt. And that we too may pass judgment, I shall not seem to have strayed from the mark if we recall to the balance certain doctrines which he intermixes in his work *Lamindi Pritanii de ingeniorum moderatione*. That I may attain this, before we come to the proposed matter, it has come to mind to divert the discourse for a little while to other things. Therefore let there be |
| **RADIUS PRIMUS** | | **RADIUS PRIMUS** |
| *Aliquas doctrinas, quas Muratorius proponit in prædicto libro, omnino destruit.* | | *It utterly destroys certain doctrines which Muratori propounds in the aforesaid book.* |
| 1 Totus est Muratorius in præfato opere, ut moderationem ingeniorum in negotio fidei lectores edoceat. Sed doctrinam, quam aliis præbere studuit, sibi non assumpsit; suum etenim immoderatum numen in hac opella plane, pleneque ostendit, demonstratque hoc in sanctum Officium odium jam ab antiquo conceptum, animoque versatum. Siquidem jam in citato libro *fol. 230.* abunde in illud invehitur, atroque dente corrodit. Inquisitionem Lusitanam nimii rigoris accusat, veluti ejus procedendi rationem plene agnoscat, aut expertus sit. Levitati quidem suæ, atque animo in deteriora parato tribuendum est, quod fidem adhibeat querulis quibusdam aut profugis, aut punitis judæis; neque sibi persuasum habeat, omnia in prædicto Tribunali agi consuevisse secundum sacrorum Canonum, & Pontificiarum Constitutionum præscriptas regulas; atque sic semper, servato Themidis moderamine, justitiam cum misericordia, gladium cum oliva temperare, ut in pietatis, charitatisque partem præcipue declinare videatur. Non addubito, aliquem, vel aliquos errare potuisse, & in duriorem partem propendere; hoc tamen officium non Angelis, sed hominibus committendum est, quorum quidam ad justitiam, & rigorem, alii ad pietatem, & misericordiam insita quadam à natura vi proniores sunt. Horum tamen privatorum peculiare genium, aut ingenium Tribunalis vitio dandum non est, cujus semper æquitas, & benignitas singularissimum fuit stema. | | 1 Muratori is wholly intent, in the aforesaid work, on teaching his readers the moderation of minds in the business of faith. But the doctrine which he strove to furnish to others he did not take up for himself; for he plainly and fully displays his own immoderate spirit in this little work, and shows thereby a hatred against the Holy Office conceived long since and turned over in his mind. For already in the cited book, *fol. 230.*, he inveighs against it abundantly, and gnaws it with a black tooth. He accuses the Portuguese Inquisition of excessive rigor, as though he fully knew its manner of proceeding, or had experienced it. It must indeed be attributed to his levity, and to a mind prepared for the worse, that he lends faith to certain querulous Jews, whether fugitives or punished; and that he does not hold himself persuaded that all things in the aforesaid Tribunal have been accustomed to be done according to the prescribed rules of the sacred Canons and of the Pontifical Constitutions; and thus always, the governance of Themis being preserved, to temper justice with mercy, the sword with the olive, so that it seems to incline especially to the side of piety and charity. I do not doubt that someone, or several, could have erred, and leaned toward the harder side; yet this office is to be committed not to Angels but to men, of whom some, by a certain force implanted by nature, are more prone to justice and rigor, others to piety and mercy. Yet the peculiar genius or temperament of these private persons is not to be charged as a fault of the Tribunal, whose equity and benignity has always been its most singular emblem. |
| 2 Sub prætextu consilii, & admonitionis Quæsitoribus faciendæ (nescio quo jure, quave authoritate ei tanta provincia demandetur) nostros supponit *turbidi affectus*, *exercendæ potentiæ ambitus* crimine innodatos, *zeli ostentandi*, *famæ captandæ*, *ac adipiscendorum munerum* cupidos, in reorum perniciem verti, atque ex nimio rigore peccare. Quam hæc ab incorruptissimo Officio, mitissimoque Tribunali aliena sint, omnes, qui vident, qui experiuntur, unanimiter testantur. Nihil est, quod Muratorius observandum proponit, & Inquisitores nostri ad unguem non exequantur. Siquis forte aut per ignorantiam, aut per temeritatem in aliquem errorem lapsus est, aut aliquam minus sanam protulit propositionem, quæ fideli populo scandalo fuerit, secretò vocatur, admonetur, instruitur, edocetur, ut resipiscat, ut emendetur, & ut plurimum jubetur publice palinodiam canere, si publice errorem spargere ausus fuit: aliter in pace remittitur adeo caute, adeo benigne, ut famæ errantis consulentes, hæc foris innotescere non sinant. Si verò in errore contumax monitis non acquiescit, & falsam opinionem pertinaciter retinet, aut defendit, tunc quod canones decernunt, pro muneris, & justitiæ debito exequuntur. Omnem tamen diligentiam, omnem solicitudinem, omnem charitatem exhibentes nimium student, ut delinquens ille ad salubria pascua reducatur: quod si consequuntur, vel graviori, vel leviori adhibita pœna, prout criminis, aut pertinaciæ qualitas exposcit, benignitate præ oculis habita, remittunt. | | 2 Under the pretext of counsel and admonition to be given to the Inquisitors (I know not by what right, or by what authority, so great a province is entrusted to him) he supposes our men to be entangled in the crime of *turbid affection* and of *ambition to exercise power*, desirous *of displaying zeal*, *of courting fame*, *and of obtaining offices*, to be turned to the ruin of the accused, and to sin through excessive rigor. How foreign these things are from that most incorrupt Office and most mild Tribunal, all who see, who experience it, unanimously testify. There is nothing which Muratori proposes as to be observed which our Inquisitors do not execute to the letter. If perchance someone has fallen into some error, whether through ignorance or through rashness, or has uttered some less sound proposition which has been a scandal to the faithful people, he is called in secret, admonished, instructed, taught, that he may return to his senses, that he may be amended; and for the most part he is ordered publicly to sing a palinode, if he has dared to spread the error publicly: otherwise he is sent back in peace so cautiously, so kindly, that, taking counsel for the reputation of the one erring, they do not allow these things to become known abroad. But if, contumacious in error, he does not acquiesce in the warnings, and pertinaciously retains or defends the false opinion, then they execute what the canons decree, according to the debt of their office and of justice. Yet, exhibiting all diligence, all solicitude, all charity, they strive exceedingly that that delinquent may be led back to wholesome pastures: and if they attain this, applying either a graver or a lighter penalty, according as the quality of the crime or of the pertinacity demands, with benignity kept before their eyes, they release him. |
| 3 Carceribus nunquam addicunt, ut supponere vult Muratorius, sola aliqua levi suspicione maculatos. Præcedere semper solent denuntiationes, inquisitiones, & maturissimæ deliberationes, ne forte innocentes infamia aliqua afficiantur: cumque ad ulteriora progrediuntur, saltem sufficiens probatio, ut canones præcipiunt, præcedit, & tunc non aliter, quam promovente prius Fiscali, super negotio prudentissime proceditur. Se ipsos accusantes, & veniam humiliter deposcentes nunquam puniunt; secretò piissime admonent, secretò à censura fortasse contracta absolvunt, secretò remittunt, neque aliquando quod factum fuerit propalatur. In fide suspectos nunquam pronuntiant, nisi suspicio sit in jure fundata. Erga convictos, & contumaces omne studium impendunt, ut deposita contumacia, & legitima confessione facta ab errore recedant, & misericordiam consequantur, quam omnibus confessis, & veniam exposcentibus benignissime largiuntur; neque umquam ultimi supplicii pœna coercent, nisi convictos, & contumaces, aut relapsos, juxta sacros canones, quando nulla emendationis spes jam superest, nec jure sperari potest, aut debet. In quo igitur excedunt Inquisitores nostri, ut malam fidem apud exterum hominem nostri moris penitus ignarum promereantur; ipseque sub sanæ cautionis specie in nostram, Hispanamque Inquisitionem calamum acuat? Ingenii immoderantiæ, quam ipse curare exoptat, id tribuendum erit. | | 3 They never consign to prison, as Muratori would suppose, those stained with only some light suspicion. Denunciations, inquisitions, and most mature deliberations are always wont to precede, lest perchance the innocent be visited with any infamy: and when they proceed to further steps, at least sufficient proof, as the canons prescribe, precedes; and then not otherwise than with the Fiscal first promoting the cause is the business most prudently carried forward. Those who accuse themselves, and humbly beg pardon, they never punish; in secret they most piously admonish them, in secret they absolve them from the censure perchance contracted, in secret they dismiss them, nor is what has been done ever divulged. They never pronounce men suspect in faith, unless the suspicion is founded in law. Toward the convicted and contumacious they expend every effort, that, contumacy laid aside and legitimate confession made, they may withdraw from error and obtain mercy, which they most benignly bestow upon all who confess and beg pardon; nor do they ever coerce with the penalty of the last punishment any but the convicted and contumacious, or the relapsed, according to the sacred canons, when no hope of amendment now remains, nor can or ought by right to be hoped for. In what, then, do our Inquisitors go to excess, that they should earn ill credit with a foreign man utterly ignorant of our custom; and that he himself, under the appearance of sound caution, should sharpen his pen against our Inquisition and the Spanish? To that immoderation of mind which he himself desires to cure will this have to be attributed. |
| 4 Complurima in citato libro Parisiis edito, & Theologicis censuris destituto, scripta, atque à non multum fidedignis dissertationibus gallice scriptis, in Latinum versa sermonem inveniuntur, quæ omnia respuere præsentis muneris non est. Aliqua tamen, qua potero brevitate, perstringam, ut videamus cum quo homine contentio nobis sit, aut quem quærerent defensorem rigidissimi nostri temporis Confessarii, hominem scilicet in rigidas opiniones declinantem, capiteque minutum, eo quod sese venumdari passus sit, ut in innocuum, semperque venerandum Tribunal caput attollere, & rumen inflare non pertimesceret. | | 4 Very many things are found written in the cited book, published at Paris and destitute of Theological censures, and translated into the Latin tongue from not very trustworthy dissertations written in French, all of which to refute is not the business of the present office. Some, however, I shall touch upon with what brevity I can, that we may see with what man our contention is, or what defender the most rigid Confessors of our time would seek — a man, namely, inclining toward rigid opinions, and diminished in head, in that he suffered himself to be sold, so that he did not fear to lift up his head against the innocuous and ever venerable Tribunal, and to puff up his gullet. |
| 5 Et in primis *lib. 1. cap. 18. pag. 147.* id probandum suscipit, nempe, ad fidem non spectare hanc propositionem: *Hic, aut ille est verus Romanus Pontifex*, à recentioribus Theologis excitatam. Atque in hoc jam fallitur ipse; nam in ea videtur fuisse sententia *D. August. tom. 6. in epistol. contr. potest. Donat. & tom. 2. epist. 115. Irinæus lib. 3. cap. 3. Basilius, Hieronym. & Ambros. quos refert, P. Manc. 2. 2. q. 1. art. 10. de Auctorit. Papæ n. 5.* Eam comprobant complurima Concilia decernentia, Pontifices tunc temporis existentes veros esse Pontifices. Eam tenuerunt ex antiquis contra alios *Valent. 2. 2. disp. 1. q. 1. punct. 7. Aragon. 2. 2. q. 1. art. 10. tract. de Concil. dub. princip. ad 3. principal. ubi etiam Ludovicus de Leon. Martinez art. 10. 2. p. princip. dub. 1. ad 2. P. Bonaventur. tract. de Auctorit. Papæ dub. 3. Albertin. tom. 1. princip. 3. corolar. 3. dub. 2. Suares de Fid. disp. 1. sect. 5. & alii.* Ipse alio in loco asserit non condemnandos statim esse, qui aliquas doctrinas, & sententias statuunt, & quæ probabiles esse possunt, dum à supremo Ecclesiæ Capite non improbentur. Cur ergo ipse doctrinam neutiquam à sancta Sede prohibitam, cum tamen à multis, & optimis eam defendi sciat, infirmare, & reprobare contendit? Quo magisterii gradu fulcitur ille, ut tantam rem aggrediatur? Quod sciam, nullo alio, nisi Auctorum, fortasse sub religionis larva sanctæ Sedi infensissimorum, lectione. Profecto in re Theologica maioris auctoritatis habendus est doctissimus P. Ferdinandus Castro Palao, qui eamdem sententiam, quam tuemur, defendens *tract. 4. disp. 1. p. 2. num. 11.* licet eam certissimam asserat, non tamen in animo habuit *contrariam aliqua gravi nota inurere, siquidem tot, tamque graves Doctores illam defensarunt.* Ast Muratorius audet ipse, quod nullus insignium Theologorum ausus est. Et hoc immoderato ingenio adscribendum non erit? Quid damni Ecclesiæ, aut veræ Religioni ista Recentiorum sententia adducere potest, ut à Muratorio liberiori calamo proscindatur? Nihil profecto. Immo & ejus negativa opinio aliquibus periculis erit obnoxia. | | 5 And first of all, in *lib. 1. cap. 18. pag. 147.*, he undertakes to prove this, namely, that this proposition does not pertain to faith: *This man, or that, is the true Roman Pontiff*, raised by the more recent Theologians. And in this he is already deceived; for of that opinion seem to have been *D. August. tom. 6. in epistol. contr. potest. Donat. & tom. 2. epist. 115. Irinæus lib. 3. cap. 3. Basilius, Hieronym. & Ambros. quos refert, P. Manc. 2. 2. q. 1. art. 10. de Auctorit. Papæ n. 5.* Very many Councils confirm it, decreeing that the Pontiffs existing at that time are true Pontiffs. Among the ancients it was held against others by *Valent. 2. 2. disp. 1. q. 1. punct. 7. Aragon. 2. 2. q. 1. art. 10. tract. de Concil. dub. princip. ad 3. principal. ubi etiam Ludovicus de Leon. Martinez art. 10. 2. p. princip. dub. 1. ad 2. P. Bonaventur. tract. de Auctorit. Papæ dub. 3. Albertin. tom. 1. princip. 3. corolar. 3. dub. 2. Suares de Fid. disp. 1. sect. 5. & alii.* He himself in another place asserts that they are not to be condemned at once who lay down certain doctrines and opinions, even such as can be probable, so long as they are not disapproved by the supreme Head of the Church. Why then does he himself strive to weaken and reprove a doctrine in no way prohibited by the holy See, when nevertheless he knows it to be defended by many and excellent men? By what degree of magistery is he supported, that he should undertake so great a matter? So far as I know, by no other than the reading of Authors perhaps, under the mask of religion, most hostile to the holy See. Assuredly, in Theological matters the most learned Fr. Ferdinand Castro Palao is to be held of greater authority, who, defending the same opinion which we uphold, *tract. 4. disp. 1. p. 2. num. 11.*, although he asserts it to be most certain, nevertheless did not have it in mind *to brand the contrary with any grave mark, since so many and so grave Doctors have defended it.* But Muratori himself dares what none of the distinguished Theologians has dared. And shall this not have to be ascribed to an immoderate mind? What harm can that opinion of the more recent authors bring upon the Church, or upon true Religion, that it should be cut to pieces by Muratori with too free a pen? None, assuredly. Nay, even his negative opinion will be liable to certain dangers. |
| 6 Latissima eruditionis seges pullulabat, si meum in hoc opere institutum ad alia non constringeret properare. Sed data occasione, non omnino contineri possum, quin aliqua proferam, quæ primo in prospectu ad mentem veniunt. Ait: *De hac quæstione apud Patres, & antiquos Theologos altum silentium.* Eam indolem hæreticorum imitatur, qui nihil credendum arbitrantur, quod in Patribus expressum non habeatur. Sed hoc nullatenus admittendum. Quanta antiqui Patres silentio premerunt, quæ postea aut Ecclesia definivit, aut sequentes Theologi docuerunt? An solis primis Ecclesiæ Doctoribus Deus claves scientiæ tradidisse dicendus est? Hanc scientiæ clavem in sua Ecclesia, ejusque Doctoribus pro temporum opportunitate veras doctrinas reserantem semper conservasse, est proculdubio certum. *Qui hodie contemnunt suos Doctores, si olim cum antiquis vixissent, eosdem pariter contempsissent*, inquit Auctor operis imperfecti *Hom. 42. in Matth.* Contemnit recentiores suo immoderato ingenio Muratorius, contemneret etiam antiquiores quoties suo fallibili judicio non patrocinarentur. Pullulant frequentissime novæ hæreses, doctrinæ non sanæ, scandalosæ, temerariæ, piarum aurium offensivæ, schismaticæ, de hæresi suspectæ, nimiæ libertati faventes, quas, etiam ante ultimam sanctæ Sedis definitionem Theologis datum est theologica inurere censura; licet hæc infallibilis dici non possit, dum à supremo Capite ultimum non recipiat infallibilitatis apicem. | | 6 A most abundant harvest of erudition was springing up, did not my purpose in this work constrain me to hasten to other things. But, occasion being given, I cannot altogether contain myself from bringing forward some things which come to mind at first view. He says: *Concerning this question there is deep silence among the Fathers and the ancient Theologians.* He imitates that disposition of the heretics, who think nothing is to be believed which is not found expressed in the Fathers. But this is by no means to be admitted. How many things did the ancient Fathers pass over in silence, which afterwards either the Church defined, or the Theologians who followed taught? Is God to be said to have delivered the keys of knowledge to the first Doctors of the Church alone? That He has always preserved this key of knowledge in His Church and in her Doctors, unlocking true doctrines according to the opportunity of the times, is beyond doubt certain. *They who today despise their own Doctors, if of old they had lived with the ancients, would equally have despised them*, says the Author of the unfinished work, *Hom. 42. in Matth.* Muratori, with his immoderate mind, despises the more recent; he would despise the more ancient also, whenever they did not patronize his fallible judgment. New heresies spring up most frequently — doctrines not sound, scandalous, temerarious, offensive to pious ears, schismatic, suspect of heresy, favoring excessive liberty — which, even before the final definition of the holy See, it is given to Theologians to brand with theological censure; although this cannot be called infallible, so long as it does not receive from the supreme Head the final apex of infallibility. |
| 7 Opus non fuit antiquis Patribus hanc movere disputationem, quia tunc de ea nullum dubium vertebatur; sed de vera electione Pontificis Romani, ac rite electum verum esse Summum Pontificem, nullus dubitavit. Nullum legimus, qui rite electum Pontificem Romanum verum esse Pontificem, aliqua ambigendi vestigia nobis relinqueret, immo contraria, quæ sequeremur. Summus Pontifex Siricius *in Epist. 1.* ad Himmerium Tarraconensem Episcopum, Damasum agnoscit verum Pontificem, seque, & antecessores, ac successores suos vocat Petri hæredes, quos ipse primæ Sedis, & Fidei antesignanus tuetur. Concilium Nicænum non semel agnoscit Petri successorem, Romanumque verum Pontificem Silvestrum tunc Ecclesiæ Dei præsidentem. Episcopi Galliæ in Epistola Synodica ad Leonem I. eum verum Pontificem profitentur, ipseque Leo in Epistol. 3. ad eosdem Episcopos se Petri asserit auctoritate fungi. Summus Pontifex Gregorius II. sacræ Sedis administratorem se nominat, & auctoritate Beati Petri fulciri asseverat. Atque, ne in re trita oleum perdam, nusquam legitur Patres, aut Ecclesiam dubitasse quandoque hunc, aut illum Pontificem rite electum esse verum Pontificem. Si ergo Ecclesia semper retinuit veros esse Pontifices quotquot cognovit rite electos, cur errabit qui asserat, de fide esse id, quod Ecclesia per tot sæculorum curricula semper retinuit, & nunquam dubitavit, & è contra schismaticos, atque non rite electos veros Pontifices nunquam agnovit? Certe non errat qui sic teneat: & ratio est clara; quia scilicet ex eo, quod quemlibet verum esse Pontificem Ecclesia, quæ in re tam gravi errare non potest, recognoscit, verum esse Pontificem credere debemus. | | 7 There was no need for the ancient Fathers to raise this disputation, because at that time no doubt was agitated concerning it; but concerning the true election of the Roman Pontiff, and that the one duly elected is the true Supreme Pontiff, no one doubted. We read of no one who left us any traces of doubting that a duly elected Roman Pontiff is the true Pontiff — nay rather the contrary, which we might follow. The Supreme Pontiff Siricius, *in Epist. 1.* to Himmerius, Bishop of Tarragona, acknowledges Damasus as the true Pontiff, and calls himself, and his predecessors and successors, heirs of Peter, whom he himself, the standard-bearer of the first See and of the Faith, protects. The Council of Nicaea more than once acknowledges Silvester, then presiding over the Church of God, as the successor of Peter and true Roman Pontiff. The Bishops of Gaul, in their Synodical Epistle to Leo I, profess him the true Pontiff, and Leo himself, in Epistle 3 to the same Bishops, asserts that he exercises the authority of Peter. The Supreme Pontiff Gregory II names himself administrator of the sacred See, and affirms that he is supported by the authority of Blessed Peter. And, lest I waste oil on a well-worn matter, nowhere is it read that the Fathers, or the Church, ever doubted at any time whether this or that Pontiff, duly elected, was the true Pontiff. If, therefore, the Church has always held to be true Pontiffs as many as she knew to be duly elected, why shall he err who asserts that to be of faith which the Church, through the courses of so many ages, has always held and never doubted, and who, on the contrary, [asserts that] she has never acknowledged schismatics, and those not duly elected, as true Pontiffs? Certainly he does not err who so holds: and the reason is clear; namely, because, from the fact that the Church, which cannot err in a matter so grave, recognizes any given man to be the true Pontiff, we ought to believe that he is the true Pontiff. |
| 8 Non rem istam theologice pertractare constituo. Qui cupiat quæstionem prælibare, adeat Cardinalem Bellarminum, Eximium P. Suarium, Valentiam, Castro Palaum, Palancum optime materiam discutientem, Delbene, Ardeskin, Berninum, Mattheuci, (1) & alios. {marg. (1) Bellarm. tom. 1. lib. 2. de Cleric. Suar. de Fide disp. 10. sect. 4. Valent. supr. Castr. Palao ubi sup. Palanc. tr. de Fid. Theolog. disp. 3. q. 9. per tot. Delben. de locis Theolog. dub. 150. Ardeskin. & alii ubi sup.} Pro modulo tamen meo in Muratorium sic convertor. Si de fide, vel saltem fidei proximum non est, hunc, vel illum Pontificem Romanum esse verum Pontificem, neque etiam de fide erunt multa, quæ de fide tenemus. De fide est, Ecclesiam Catholicam esse omnium fidelium Congregationem sub uno capite visibili, vero scilicet Romano Pontifice. Hic fidei articulus est in Apostolorum Symbolo contentus: hanc veritatem à pueritia ediscimus: hanc nobis tradiderunt Patres nostri: hanc nos edocent Concilium Alexandrinum, Concilium Lateranense, & alia. In re igitur certa, & definita cur morabor? Oblatrent frustra hæretici, donec voces suæ faucibus hæreant. Eadem fidei firmitate credere debemus unam sanctam Catholicam, & Apostolicam Ecclesiam extitisse semper, atque in finem usque mundi, etsi contrariis semper ventis agitatam, infallibiliter extituram. Sic Patres intelligunt illud *Matth. 16. Tu es Petrus, & super hanc petram ædificabo Ecclesiam meam, & portæ inferi non prævalebunt adversus eam.* Hoc est, semper firma, inconcussaque permanebit. Et illud *Matth. cap. 28. Ecce ego vobiscum sum usque ad consummationem sæculi.* Sic definiit Concilium Alexandrinum his verbis: *Ecclesiam porro Apostolicam unam, & solam Catholicam agnoscimus, quæ sicut nunquam expugnari potest, etiamsi totus mundus eam oppugnare instituat.* Porro hanc Ecclesiam visibilem esse, & sub uno capite visibili definiit Concilium Sennonense, regente Ecclesiam Clemente VII. Parisiiis habitum anno 1528. contra Lutherum perverse asserentem, Ecclesiam invisibilem esse, ac proinde nobis incognitam. | | 8 I do not undertake to treat this matter theologically. Let him who desires to have a foretaste of the question go to Cardinal Bellarmine, the Eximious Fr. Suárez, Valentia, Castro Palao, Palanco, who discusses the matter excellently, Delbene, Ardeskin, Bernini, Matteucci, (1) and others. {marg. (1) Bellarm. tom. 1. lib. 2. de Cleric. Suar. de Fide disp. 10. sect. 4. Valent. supr. Castr. Palao ubi sup. Palanc. tr. de Fid. Theolog. disp. 3. q. 9. per tot. Delben. de locis Theolog. dub. 150. Ardeskin. & alii ubi sup.} Yet, according to my small measure, I turn upon Muratori thus. If it is not of faith, or at least proximate to faith, that this or that Roman Pontiff is the true Pontiff, then neither will many things be of faith which we hold as of faith. It is of faith that the Catholic Church is the Congregation of all the faithful under one visible head, namely, the true Roman Pontiff. This article of faith is contained in the Apostles' Creed: this truth we learn from childhood: this our Fathers handed down to us: this the Council of Alexandria, the Lateran Council, and others teach us. Why, then, shall I tarry over a matter certain and defined? Let the heretics bark in vain, until their voices stick in their throats. With the same firmness of faith we ought to believe that the one holy Catholic and Apostolic Church has always existed, and that, even unto the end of the world, though ever tossed by contrary winds, she shall infallibly exist. Thus the Fathers understand that saying, *Matth. 16. Thou art Peter, and upon this rock I will build my Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against her.* That is, she shall remain ever firm and unshaken. And that saying, *Matth. cap. 28. Behold, I am with you even unto the consummation of the world.* Thus the Council of Alexandria defined in these words: *Moreover, we acknowledge the one and only Catholic Apostolic Church, which, as it can never be stormed, even though the whole world should set about to assail her.* [?] Further, that this Church is visible, and under one visible head, was defined by the Council of Sens, Clement VII ruling the Church, held at Paris in the year 1528, against Luther, who perversely asserted that the Church is invisible, and therefore unknown to us. |
| 9 Licet autem hæc veritas apud orthodoxos alia probatione non egeat, ex abundanti liceat elegantem Sanderi (2) locum hic referre: {marg. (2) Lib. de visibil. Monarch. cap. 4.} „Primum, (*ait ille*) ne longe præclarissimum gubernationis genus (qualis est Monarchia) à Christi Ecclesia abesse censeatur, in qua secundum ordinem omnia fieri debere Apostolus docet. Deinde, ut status Ecclesiæ cohæreat cum eo gubernationis modo, qui observatur in cæteris omnibus creaturis, qui ad unum suæ naturæ, conditionisque principium referri solent, ut constat de cœlis, sideribus, animantibusque, corporibus, & gregibus. Tum, ut Ecclesia militans in suo genere sit similis triumphanti Ecclesiæ, quam nemo negat ab uno Christo ita gubernari, ut Christus ab eadem Ecclesia facie ad faciem videatur. Unde & huic militanti Ecclesiæ unus militans Pastor præesse debet, qui ab eadem Ecclesia videri queat. Præterea, ut regimen Ecclesiæ Christi respondeat statui, quem eadem Ecclesia sub Patriarchis habuit, quorum nonnisi unus eadem ætate, eodemque tempore primas tenuit, & cui cæteri omnes velut Primati cedebant, sicut constat de Melchisedech, qui benedixit Abrahæ inferiori Patriarchæ. Atque etiam, ut status Ecclesiæ Christi responderet statui legis Moysaicæ, sub qua unus tantum Pontifex omnibus, quæ in mundo erant, synagogis præfuit. Ut status Ecclesiæ Christi non dissentiat à Prophetarum doctrina, qui prædixerunt, eam fore similem aciei castrorum ordinatæ, in qua est unus primus Dux, seu Imperator. Ut respondeat Christi doctrinæ, qui Ecclesiam, Regnum, ovile, hospitium, seu diversorium, in quo unus Rex, Dominus, aut Pastor, isque visibilis, & ejusdem conditionis cum subditis, præest. Ut respondeat doctrinæ Apostolorum, qui Ecclesiam dixerunt similem esse corpori, cujus unum est præcipuum membrum, & similem domui, in qua unus paterfamilias dominatur. Ut Ecclesiæ gubernatio consentiat ipsa secum, sitque non minus in tota militante Ecclesia unus militans Pastor, qui in singulis militantibus Provinciis, & Parœciis singuli sunt militantes Pastores. Ut æterna Monarchia per unum ad tempus Pastorem demonstretur. Nam Ignatius tradit, Episcopum figuram Dei in Ecclesia gerere, in Epistola ad Smirnenses. Sit ergo unus in Ecclesia Summus Pontifex, qui figuret unitatem Dei. Ut visibilis Monarchia, quam Christus in terris agens per se ipsum inchoavit, & qua in hodiernum diem Ecclesiam gubernat, in terris per Vicarium ipsius continuetur, & perseveret. Nec alia nunc Ecclesia esse putetur, quam sub Christo fuit. Esset quippe alia, & non una, eademque Ecclesia, si forma regiminis, quæ sub Christo in terris agente nonnisi regia fuit, nunc in Aristocratiam, vel Democratiam mutaretur. Ut Christi unius in æternum Pastoris unitas hic modo figuretur. Nam cum Ecclesia sit imago rerum ipsarum, quæ in cœlis elucent, ut ibi constat unum Christum esse Ecclesiæ suæ æternum Pastorem, sic oportuit illam Christi præsidentiam per unius Pastoris primatum in terris figurari. Ut etiam visibilis unitas Ecclesiæ hoc visibili unius Pastoris primatu designetur, quæ Cypriani est ratio in lib. de Unitate Ecclesiæ. Ex illa visibili connexione, qua cohærent inter se Presbyteri, Episcopi, Primates, & Summus Pontifex, facile intelligitur quanta illa spiritualis unitas, quæ intercedit Ecclesiæ tam cum Christo capite, quam cum reliquis ipsius membris. Ut militantis Ecclesiæ externa unitas, & pax per unum Pastorem conservetur, schismataque tollantur, quæ est Optati ratio in lib. 2. de schismate Donatistarum, & Divi Hieronymi adversus Jovinianum. Ut non desit in Ecclesia, qui jure divino peccata inferiorum Pastorum puniat, & negligentiam Prælatorum suppleat, quæ est Gregorii Magni ratio lib. 7. epist. 8. Ut quando Synodus propter externam persecutionem congregari non potest, sit tamen qui pro auctoritate sua ea decidat, quæ est Basilii Magni ratio in epist. 78. Ut cum singulis Pastoribus portio gregis aliqua sit assignata, nec illi teneantur curam suscipere eorum, qui non sunt ipsis commendati: non desit tamen, qui & propagandi Euangelium curam gerat, & Episcopos novos illis constituat, qui de novo ad fidem convertuntur. Hæc enim cura nisi ad unum aliquem ante omnes alios spectaret, non tot nationes post Apostolorum ex hac vita migrantium tempora Christi fidem suscepissent. Ut sit, qui Episcopatus antea constitutos vel dividat, vel uniat, prout res ipsa videbitur postulare; quod nisi ab uno præcipue fieret, vel non fieret id omnino, vel cum gravi scandalo sæpe fieret. Ut certum sit, quæ Concilia sint legitima: & quis vel congreget, quemadmodum oportet, vel quis è contra dissipet: quæ est ratio Damasceni contra Concilium Ariminense. Ut quæ de uno inter Apostolos maiori, & prædecessore in Euangelio scribuntur, vera, in Ecclesiæ gubernatione, perpetua esse credantur. Ut quæ de primatu Petri tam perspicue in Euangelio narrantur, & à sanctis Patribus exponuntur, vana esse non existimentur. Ut Ecclesia Christi dæmonum societate inferior non judicetur, si ea visibili unius primatu careat, quem Princeps dæmoniorum sortitus est. Ut quæ de Romani Pontificis primatu sancti Patres, OEcumenica Concilia tanto consensu docent, vera esse non dubitetur. | | 9 Although, however, this truth needs no other proof among the orthodox, let it be permitted, out of abundance, to cite here an elegant passage of Sander: (2) {marg. (2) Lib. de visibil. Monarch. cap. 4.} „First, (*says he*) lest by far the most excellent kind of government (such as Monarchy is) be thought absent from the Church of Christ, in which the Apostle teaches that all things ought to be done according to order. Next, that the state of the Church may cohere with that mode of government which is observed in all other creatures, which are wont to be referred to one principle of their nature and condition, as is evident of the heavens, the stars, living creatures, bodies, and flocks. Then, that the Church militant may in its kind be like the Church triumphant, which no one denies to be so governed by the one Christ that Christ is seen by that same Church face to face. Whence also over this militant Church one militant Pastor ought to preside, who can be seen by the same Church. Moreover, that the rule of the Church of Christ may answer to the state which the same Church had under the Patriarchs, of whom only one, in the same age and at the same time, held the first place, and to whom all the rest yielded as to a Primate, as is evident of Melchisedech, who blessed Abraham, an inferior Patriarch. And also, that the state of the Church of Christ might answer to the state of the Mosaic law, under which one Pontiff alone presided over all the synagogues that were in the world. That the state of the Church of Christ may not dissent from the doctrine of the Prophets, who foretold that she would be like an ordered battle-line of a camp, in which there is one first Leader, or Commander. That it may answer to the doctrine of Christ, who [set forth] the Church as a Kingdom, a sheepfold, a lodging or inn, in which one King, Lord, or Shepherd — and he visible, and of the same condition as his subjects — presides. That it may answer to the doctrine of the Apostles, who said the Church is like a body, of which there is one principal member, and like a house, in which one father of the family bears rule. That the government of the Church may agree with itself, and that there may be no less one militant Pastor in the whole militant Church, as in the several militant Provinces and Parishes there are several militant Pastors. That the eternal Monarchy may be shown forth through one Pastor for a time. For Ignatius teaches, in the Epistle to the Smyrnaeans, that the Bishop bears the figure of God in the Church. Let there be, therefore, one Supreme Pontiff in the Church, to figure the unity of God. That the visible Monarchy, which Christ, while living on earth, began by His very self, and by which unto the present day He governs the Church, may be continued and persevere on earth through His Vicar. Nor let the Church now be thought other than she was under Christ. For she would be another, and not one and the same Church, if the form of rule, which under Christ living on earth was none other than regal, were now changed into an Aristocracy or a Democracy. That the unity of Christ, the one Pastor unto eternity, may here meanwhile be figured. For since the Church is an image of the very things that shine forth in the heavens, as there it is established that the one Christ is the eternal Pastor of His Church, so it was fitting that that presidency of Christ be figured on earth through the primacy of one Pastor. That also the visible unity of the Church may be designated by this visible primacy of one Pastor — which is Cyprian's reason in the book On the Unity of the Church. From that visible connection by which Priests, Bishops, Primates, and the Supreme Pontiff cohere among themselves, it is easily understood how great is that spiritual unity which obtains between the Church and Christ her head, as well as with the rest of His members. That the external unity and peace of the Church militant may be preserved through one Pastor, and schisms taken away — which is the reason of Optatus in book 2 On the Schism of the Donatists, and of St. Jerome against Jovinian. That there be not lacking in the Church one who by divine right may punish the sins of inferior Pastors, and supply the negligence of Prelates — which is the reason of Gregory the Great, book 7, epistle 8. That, when a Synod cannot be gathered on account of external persecution, there may nevertheless be one who by his own authority may decide those matters — which is the reason of Basil the Great in epistle 78. That, since to each Pastor some portion of the flock is assigned, nor are they bound to undertake the care of those who are not committed to them, there may nevertheless not be lacking one who both bears the care of propagating the Gospel, and appoints new Bishops for those who are newly converted to the faith. For unless this care pertained to some one man before all others, so many nations would not have received the faith of Christ after the times when the Apostles departed from this life. That there may be one who may either divide or unite Episcopates previously constituted, according as the matter itself shall seem to demand; which, unless it were done chiefly by one, either would not be done at all, or would often be done with grave scandal. That it may be certain which Councils are legitimate: and who may either convoke them, as is fitting, or who, on the contrary, may dissolve them — which is the reason of Damascene against the Council of Ariminum. That the things which are written in the Gospel concerning one greater among the Apostles, and a predecessor, may be believed to be true, and perpetual in the government of the Church. That the things which are so clearly narrated in the Gospel concerning the primacy of Peter, and expounded by the holy Fathers, may not be thought vain. That the Church of Christ be not judged inferior to the society of the demons, if she should lack that visible primacy of one which the Prince of the demons has obtained. That it be not doubted that those things are true which the holy Fathers and the Ecumenical Councils with so great consensus teach concerning the primacy of the Roman Pontiff. |
| 10 Nunc sic. Si hic, aut ille Romanus Pontifex, v. g. si S. D. N. Benedictus XIV. verus Pontifex non esset credendus, neque ejus prædecessor talis crederetur, & similiter cæteri antecessores; neque etiam de fide crederemus, his temporibus veram extitisse Ecclesiam legitimo suo visibili sponso copulatam: quod etiam de omnibus sigillatim esset asserendum; quia si de hoc numero Pontifice non debemus credere verum esse Pontificem, de nullo in individuo credere tenebimur. Ergo non est de fide Ecclesiam semper extitisse veram suo legitimo capiti, sive sponso copulatam. En destruimus certam, infallibilemque in Petri Cathedra successionem, qua hæreticorum versutias, suarumque Ecclesiarum falsitatem confundamus. En favemus Calvinistarum Confessioni *art. 31.* En falsam facimus Christi promissionem *Matth.* 18. 20. En quandoque portæ inferi adversus Ecclesiam vero Pastore destitutam prævalebunt. E' contra, si dicamus, ut debemus, & verum dogma tenet, Ecclesiam à principio, usque ad hæc nostra tempora derivari, veramque semper extitisse, etiam tenere debemus omnes Summos Pontifices, quos Ecclesia ipsa recognovit, & ad hæc usque tempora sponsos habuit, fuisse veros Christi Vicarios, veros Petri successores. Quod enim de toto asseritur, de parte omnino necessaria, & sine qua totum subsistere nequit, asserendum est. Ex hac de uno ad alium derivata Romanorum Pontificum successione Controversistæ omnes Ecclesiæ Romanæ infallibilitatem deducunt. Videatur post alios Ardeskin *p. 1. cap. 3. & 4.* ego enim sine allegationibus percurro, ut brevius me expediam. Hanc continuatam legitimam successionem si demas, firmitas Ecclesiæ Romanæ nutare visa erit. Si proinde omnimodam veritatem circa hunc, vel illum Pontificem è medio tollas, illa continuatæ successionis inconcussa veritas penitus periclitabitur. | | 10 Now thus. If this or that Roman Pontiff — for example, if our Most Holy Lord Benedict XIV — were not to be believed the true Pontiff, neither would his predecessor be believed such, and likewise the rest of his antecessors; neither also should we believe as of faith that in these times the true Church has existed, joined to her legitimate visible spouse: which would likewise have to be asserted of all of them singly; because, if of this Pontiff of the series [?] we ought not to believe that he is the true Pontiff, we shall be bound to believe it of none in the individual. Therefore it is not of faith that the Church has always existed true, joined to her legitimate head, or spouse. Behold, we destroy the certain and infallible succession in the Chair of Peter, whereby we might confound the wiles of the heretics and the falsity of their Churches. Behold, we favor the Confession of the Calvinists, *art. 31.* Behold, we make false the promise of Christ, *Matth.* 18. 20. Behold, at some time the gates of hell shall prevail against a Church destitute of her true Pastor. On the contrary, if we say, as we ought, and as the true dogma holds, that the Church is derived from the beginning even to these our times, and has always existed true, we ought also to hold that all the Supreme Pontiffs whom the Church herself has recognized, and has had as spouses even to these times, have been true Vicars of Christ, true successors of Peter. For what is asserted of the whole must be asserted of a part altogether necessary, and without which the whole cannot subsist. From this succession of the Roman Pontiffs, derived from one to another, all the Controversialists deduce the infallibility of the Roman Church. Let Ardeskin, *p. 1. cap. 3. & 4.*, be seen after others; for I, without allegations I run through, that I may dispatch the matter more briefly. If you take away this continued legitimate succession, the firmness of the Roman Church will seem to totter. If, accordingly, you remove from the midst all manner of truth concerning this or that Pontiff, that unshaken truth of the continued succession will be utterly imperiled. |
| 11 Quid dico, successionis veritas? Omnia œcumenica Concilia, eorumque in rebus fidei definitiones incertitudini, & fallibilitati erunt obnoxiæ. Veram Catholicam Ecclesiam esse veram, & infallibilem fidei regulam, ac in rebus definiendis errare nunquam posse, est inter Orthodoxos indubitabile. Id patet ex illo Christi Domini *Matth. 16. Tu es Petrus, & super hanc petram ædificabo Ecclesiam meam.* Laberetur autem hoc firmissimum fundamentum in Petro, ejusque successoribus (illud enim divinum effatum de omnibus verificatur) stabilitum, si Ecclesia in definiendis rebus fidei, aut morum universam Ecclesiam spectantium, aliquando erraret; tota enim Ecclesiæ firmitas in integra fidei veritate reposita est. Identidem promisit idem primus Ecclesiæ divinus Pontifex, quod *Portæ inferi non prævalebunt adversus eam.* Fallibilis verò hæc promissio esset, si quandoque hæreses admitteret, aut incertis doctrinis macularetur. Patet deinde ex illo Apostoli 1. *ad Timoth. 3. Ut scias quomodo tè oporteat in domo Dei, quæ est Ecclesia Dei vivi, & firmamentum veritatis.* Hoc autem intelligendum esse de vera Ecclesia, prout ex vero capite, & membris coalescit, meridiano sole clarius elucet. | | 11 What do I say — the truth of the succession? All the ecumenical Councils, and their definitions in matters of faith, will be subject to uncertainty and fallibility. That the true Catholic Church is the true and infallible rule of faith, and can never err in defining matters, is beyond doubt among the orthodox. This is evident from that saying of Christ the Lord, *Matth. 16. Thou art Peter, and upon this rock I will build my Church.* But this most firm foundation, established in Peter and in his successors (for that divine utterance is verified of them all), would collapse, if the Church should at some time err in defining matters of faith, or of morals regarding the universal Church; for the whole firmness of the Church is placed in the integral truth of the faith. Likewise the same first divine Pontiff of the Church promised that *the gates of hell shall not prevail against her.* But this promise would be fallible, if she should at any time admit heresies, or be stained with uncertain doctrines. It is evident, further, from that saying of the Apostle, 1 *ad Timoth. 3. That thou mayest know how thou oughtest to behave thyself in the house of God, which is the Church of the living God, and the ground of truth.* But that this is to be understood of the true Church, insofar as she coalesces from her true head and members, shines forth clearer than the noonday sun. |
| 12 Hanc infallibilitatem à suo vero capite Romano Pontifice Ecclesiam accipere, est proculdubio certum. Licet enim pauculi Doctores, ut ait Duvallius, (3) de hac veritate dubitent; nullus tamen est, qui hanc neget Ecclesiæ infallibilitatem, dummodo suo legitimo capiti adhæreat: ex quo sequitur, eam ab ipso capite extra controversiam accipere. {marg. (3) Tract. de Roman. Pontif. potest. p. 4. q. 7.} Id evidenter liquet ex Christi effatu ad Petrum, ejusque successores prolato. *Luc. 22. Ego rogavi pro te, ut non deficiat fides tua, & tu aliquando conversus confirma fratres tuos.* Ex illo *Joan. 21. Petre amas me? Pasce oves meas;* & illo *Matth. 16. Tu es Petrus &c.* Non minus concludit Synodus Chalcedonensis IV. in qua nihil aliud necessarium fuit, ut de fide haberetur quod decretum fuerat, nisi quod Leo antecedenter definiret. Clamaverunt Patres *act. 2. Anathema qui ita non credit: Petrus per Leonem locutus est.* Simile habetur in Concilio Constantinopolitano II. *act. 4.* erga Agapetum. Simile in Synodo VI. *act. 18.* erga Agathonem. Simile in Synodo VIII. *act. 5.* erga Adrianum II. & Nicolaum I. Idem decrevit Concilium Florentinum *p. 2. coll. 22. Definimus sanctam Apostolicam Sedem, & Romanum Pontificem successorem esse B. Petri verticis Apostolorum, & verum Christi Vicarium, & totius Ecclesiæ caput, & omnium Christianorum Patrem, & Doctorem existere, & in universum orbem tenere primatum, & in B. Petro pascendi, regendi, & gubernandi universalem Ecclesiam à Domino nostro JESU Christo plenam potestatem traditam esse.* Idem diserte docuit, ac definiit Concilium Tridentinum de Baptismo *sess. 7. can. 3. Ecclesia Romana autem nonnisi ratione Pontificis est magistra cæterarum.* | | 12 That the Church receives this infallibility from her true head, the Roman Pontiff, is beyond doubt certain. For although a very few Doctors, as Duvallius says, (3) doubt of this truth; yet there is no one who denies this infallibility to the Church, provided she adhere to her legitimate head: from which it follows that she receives it from the head itself beyond controversy. {marg. (3) Tract. de Roman. Pontif. potest. p. 4. q. 7.} This is evidently clear from the saying of Christ uttered to Peter and to his successors. *Luc. 22. I have prayed for thee, that thy faith fail not; and thou, being once converted, confirm thy brethren.* From that of *Joan. 21. Peter, lovest thou me? Feed my sheep;* and that of *Matth. 16. Thou art Peter, etc.* No less conclusive is the Fourth Synod, that of Chalcedon, in which nothing else was necessary, in order that what had been decreed should be held as of faith, than that Leo should define it antecedently. The Fathers cried out, *act. 2. Anathema to him who does not so believe: Peter has spoken through Leo.* The like is found in the Second Council of Constantinople, *act. 4.* with regard to Agapetus. The like in the Sixth Synod, *act. 18.* with regard to Agatho. The like in the Eighth Synod, *act. 5.* with regard to Adrian II and Nicholas I. The same did the Council of Florence decree, *p. 2. coll. 22. We define that the holy Apostolic See and the Roman Pontiff is the successor of Blessed Peter, the chief of the Apostles, and the true Vicar of Christ, and the head of the whole Church, and that he is the Father and Teacher of all Christians, and holds the primacy over the whole world, and that to him, in Blessed Peter, full power was delivered by our Lord JESUS Christ to feed, rule, and govern the universal Church.* The same the Council of Trent expressly taught and defined, concerning Baptism, *sess. 7. can. 3. But the Roman Church is not the mistress of the others except by reason of the Pontiff.* |
| 13 Latissime in hanc rem probandam sermonem protrahere possem, si Concilia, si Patres in memoriam revocarem, vel AA. de materia tractantes aperirem. Sed ad intentum his obiter dictis, jam, ni fallor, eruitur quod dixeram, nempe fidei articulos à Conciliis definitos infallibilitate carere posse, neque indubitanter de fide esse credendos, si certiori, quam humana morali credulitate, non teneremus ut certum, hunc, aut illum Romanum Pontificem esse verum Petri successorem, & Christi Vicarium. Non maior ratio est circa hunc, vel illum, quam circa Gelasium, Damasum, Lucium, Julium, Silvestrum, Alexandrum, Innocentium, Leonem, Agapetum, Gregorium, Martinum, Eugenium, Pium &c. Si de fide non est eos, aut quemlibet illorum verum fuisse Romanum Pontificem: ergo neque de fide erit Concilia ab iisdem congregata vera fuisse œcumenica Concilia: ergo quæcumque ibi decreta sunt, non erunt de fide credenda: hæc enim infallibilitas à vera Ecclesia legitime in Spiritu Sancto congregata, & à suo vero capite, veroque Petri successore omnino dependet; neque enim hanc fidei firmitatem Ecclesiæ acephalæ, aut non vero Deus promisit Pontifici; sed Ecclesiæ suæ, & Petro, ejusque veris, & legitimis successoribus. Nulla igitur fidei certitudo iis articulis inesse posset, si verum Pontificem eos decernentem non agnosceremus. Id exoptant hæretici, id blaterant Lutherani. Demamus hanc certitudinem, & in nos libentius audebunt. Nec sat esset illos observare ex vi obedientiæ, ut hethorodoxi intendunt; siquidem Ecclesia cum hos definiit articulos, non modo ut observandos, sed ut credendos proponit: aliàs quisquis de illis dubitaret, inobediens solum, non verò hæreticus esset habendus. En quam facile fides omnis in maximo versaretur discrimine: en quam lata hæreticis aperiretur porta, ut Conciliaribus definitionibus assensum non præberent; in promptu namque esset, vera non fuisse congregata Concilia, neque veros Pontifices illa congregantes. | | 13 I could draw out my discourse at very great length in proving this matter, if I were to recall to memory the Councils and the Fathers, or open the authors treating of the subject. But for our purpose, from these things said in passing, there is already drawn, unless I am mistaken, what I had said, namely, that the articles of faith defined by Councils could lack infallibility, nor be believed indubitably as of faith, if we did not hold as certain, with a credence more certain than human moral credence, that this or that Roman Pontiff is the true successor of Peter and Vicar of Christ. There is no greater reason concerning this or that one, than concerning Gelasius, Damasus, Lucius, Julius, Sylvester, Alexander, Innocent, Leo, Agapetus, Gregory, Martin, Eugene, Pius, etc. If it is not of faith that they, or any one of them, was a true Roman Pontiff: then neither will it be of faith that the Councils congregated by them were true ecumenical Councils: therefore whatever things were there decreed will not be to be believed of faith: for this infallibility depends entirely upon the true Church legitimately congregated in the Holy Spirit, and upon her true head, the true successor of Peter; for God did not promise this firmness of faith to a headless Church, or to a Pontiff who is not true; but to His Church, and to Peter, and to his true and legitimate successors. Therefore no certitude of faith could be present in those articles, if we did not acknowledge a true Pontiff decreeing them. This the heretics eagerly desire; this the Lutherans babble. Let us take away this certitude, and they will dare against us the more freely. Nor would it suffice to observe them by force of obedience, as the heterodox intend; since the Church, when she defines these articles, proposes them not only as to be observed, but as to be believed: otherwise whoever doubted of them would have to be held merely disobedient, but not a heretic. Behold how easily the whole faith would be placed in the greatest peril: behold how wide a gate would be opened to heretics, that they should not give assent to Conciliar definitions; for it would be ready at hand [to allege] that the Councils congregated were not true, nor those who congregated them true Pontiffs. |
| 14 Quam proxima est assertio illa, qua de agimus, errori illo, nempe, post Urbanum VI. nullum esse verum Papam. Hunc errorem Wiclephi ut hæreticum damnavit Concilium Londinense anno Domini 1382. in hunc modum: *Quod post Urbanum VI. non est aliquis recipiendus ut Papa, sed vivendum more Græcorum suis propriis legibus: concedimus, quod est hæresis.* Ergo hæresis est, nullum esse verum Papam, neque ut talem recipiendum. Cum ergo hæresis alicui fidei articulo opponatur, fit consequens, fidei articulum esse, aliquem Papam esse verum Papam. De quo igitur Pontifice hic articulus intelligendus est? Nulla est assignabilis ratio, cur potius de hoc, aut illo, quam de aliis intelligatur: ergo solum de rite electo, & ab Ecclesia recepto: ergo de omnibus rite electis, & ab Ecclesia receptis: ergo de hoc, aut illo. Sic definit Martinus V. in Concilio Constantiensi, ubi decrevit, hæreticos non aliter admittendos quando ad Ecclesiam convertuntur, quam si credant, & profiteantur existentem Papam esse verum Pontificem. | | 14 How near is that assertion of which we are treating to that error, namely, that after Urban VI there is no true Pope. This error of Wycliffe the Council of London condemned as heretical, in the year of our Lord 1382, in this manner: *That after Urban VI no one is to be received as Pope, but men are to live after the manner of the Greeks, under their own laws: we grant that this is heresy.* Therefore it is heresy, that no one is a true Pope, nor to be received as such. Since, then, a heresy is opposed to some article of faith, it follows that it is an article of faith, that some Pope is a true Pope. Of what Pontiff, then, is this article to be understood? There is no assignable reason why it should be understood rather of this or that one than of others: therefore only of the one duly elected and received by the Church: therefore of all duly elected and received by the Church: therefore of this or that one. So defines Martin V in the Council of Constance, where he decreed that heretics, when they are converted to the Church, are not otherwise to be admitted than if they believe and profess that the existing Pope is the true Pontiff. |
| 15 Insuper credere debemus, Christum suæ Ecclesiæ promisisse sua peculiari providentia non defecturum: alioquin non plene invisibilis capitis muneri satisfaceret. Certum est etiam misisse Spiritum Sanctum suum, qui omnem Apostolis veritatem edoceret. Cum autem ex principiis certis fidei certæ deducantur fidei conclusiones, fit consequens infallibile, ad fidem pertinere hunc, vel illum Pontificem esse verum Pontificem: aliàs non rectè Ecclesiæ suæ in re tam gravi provideret; cum permitteret eam per multos fortasse annos deceptam illegitimo sponso copulatam permanere, falsum Doctorem pro vero reputari, suum Vicarium credi, qui revera suus non esset, aliisque damnis inde provenientibus suam sponsam dilectam exposuisset; quod ab ejus amore, ejus vigilantia, ejus infallibili, & indefectibili providentia cogitare nefas, & piaculum erit. Cur ergo Muratorius hanc à nobis credulitatem expellere nititur? Cur verborum lenocinio, rhetorices apparatu, & sophismatum involucris, quos in eam videt abire sententiam, in suas partes satagit reducere? In sua opinione sequenda multa pericula latere poterunt, nullum in nostra. Mihi persuasum habeo, eum, qui adstruat S. D. N. Benedictum XIV. verum esse infallibiliter Romanum Pontificem, nullas pœnas daturum; qui verò eum asserat non esse verum Pontificem, arbitror alicui supplicio subjiciendum esse. Arbitror dixi, & infallibiliter arbitror: siquidem hæc propositio: *Hic numero Summus Pontifex non est verus Pontifex,* est propositio temeraria, scandalosa, schismatica, male sonans, & de hæresi suspecta, eamque proferens tamquam hæreticus est puniendus, ut docent Bañes, & Castro Palao. (4) {marg. (4) Bañes 2. 2. q. 1. art. 10. dub. 2. Palao loco supra cit. & alii.} Non me latet aliud esse asserere: *Hic Pontifex non est verus Pontifex;* aliud verò affirmare: *Non est de fide hunc Pontificem esse verum Pontificem.* Verum ideo illa prima propositio est de hæresi suspecta, quia eam proferens videtur negare in Ecclesia esse potestatem eligendi verum Pontificem (videat Muratorius, ne in hunc errorem impingat, dum ait nobis non constare promotionem Summi Pontificis per electionem esse faciendam.) Ergo similiter altera erit etiam de hæresi suspecta, quia eam proferens videtur sentire in re tam gravis momenti Ecclesiam falli posse, Deumque id permissurum fuisse. Dixerunt aliqui, ut supra memoravi, post Adrianum VI. nullum fuisse verum Pontificem; nec ut talem recipiendam: asseruerunt alii post Gregorium X. nullum verum Pontificem extitisse: alii de aliis Pontificibus Romanis blaterare ausi sunt: alii de omnibus post tertium sæculum affirmarunt. Sed qui? Non alii, quam hæretici, vel schismatici. Omnes tamen, quos Pontificum catalogus in diptychis habet veros Pontifices, dubitavit orthodoxus nemo, Ecclesia nunquam dubitavit. Cur ergo non credemus, quod Ecclesia, quæ errare non potuit, semper retinuit, quod Conciliorum infallibilitatem confirmat, quod Pontificiam successionem statuit solidissime? | | 15 Moreover, we ought to believe that Christ promised His Church that He would not fail her by His peculiar providence: otherwise He would not fully satisfy the office of invisible head. It is certain also that He sent His Holy Spirit, to teach the Apostles all truth. But since from certain principles of certain faith conclusions of faith are deduced, it follows infallibly that it pertains to the faith that this or that Pontiff is a true Pontiff: otherwise He would not rightly provide for His Church in so grave a matter; since He would permit her, deceived perhaps for many years, to remain joined to an illegitimate spouse, a false Teacher to be reputed a true one, one to be believed His Vicar who in truth was not His, and would have exposed His beloved spouse to the other harms thence arising; which it will be an impiety and a sin to think of His love, His vigilance, His infallible and indefectible providence. Why, then, does Muratori strive to expel this belief from us? Why, by the allurement of words, by the apparatus of rhetoric, and by the wrappings of sophisms, does he busy himself to draw over to his own side those whom he sees going over to that opinion? In following his opinion many dangers may lie hidden; none in ours. I am persuaded that he who maintains that our Most Holy Lord Benedict XIV is infallibly the true Roman Pontiff will pay no penalties; but he who asserts that he is not the true Pontiff, I judge, is to be subjected to some punishment. I said "I judge," and infallibly I judge: since this proposition, *This individual Supreme Pontiff is not a true Pontiff,* is a proposition temerarious, scandalous, schismatical, ill-sounding, and suspect of heresy, and he who utters it is to be punished as a heretic, as Bañes and Castro Palao teach. (4) {marg. (4) Bañes 2. 2. q. 1. art. 10. dub. 2. Palao loco supra cit. & alii.} It does not escape me that it is one thing to assert, *This Pontiff is not a true Pontiff;* and another to affirm, *It is not of faith that this Pontiff is a true Pontiff.* But that first proposition is suspect of heresy for this reason, that he who utters it seems to deny that there is in the Church the power of electing a true Pontiff (let Muratori see to it that he does not stumble into this error, when he says that it is not established for us that the promotion of the Supreme Pontiff must be made by election). Therefore the other will likewise also be suspect of heresy, because he who utters it seems to hold that in a matter of such grave moment the Church can be deceived, and that God would have permitted it. Some have said, as I recalled above, that after Adrian VI there was no true Pontiff, nor is one to be received as such: others asserted that after Gregory X no true Pontiff existed: others have dared to babble about other Roman Pontiffs: others have affirmed it of all after the third century. But who? None other than heretics, or schismatics. Yet all those whom the catalogue of Pontiffs holds in its diptychs as true Pontiffs, no orthodox man has doubted; the Church has never doubted. Why, then, shall we not believe what the Church, which could not err, has always retained, what confirms the infallibility of the Councils, what establishes most solidly the Pontifical succession? |
| 16 Ut hanc infallibilitatem Muratorius respuat, dogma certum esse negat regulam supra positam, nempe: *Omnem Pontificem rite electum esse verum Pontificem.* Nullum orthodoxum tam expresse audentem me legisse recordor; heterodoxos verò non admitto. Si recta ipse loquitur, erravit ergo Concilium Lateranense tertium, & cum eo Alexander III. cum statuit: *Ille absque ulla exceptione ab universali Ecclesia Pontifex habeatur, qui à duabus partibus electus fuerit, & receptus.* Erravit Concilium generale Lugdunense, & cum eo Gregorius X. cum idem decrevit. Erravit Concilium Florentinum, quando definiit: *Romanum Pontificem successorem esse B. Petri verticis Apostolorum, & verum Christi Vicarium, & totius Ecclesiæ caput, & omnium Christianorum Patrem, ac Doctorem existere, & in universum orbem tenere primatum, & in B. Petro pascendi, regendi, & gubernandi universam Ecclesiam à Domino nostro JESU Christo plenam potestatem traditam esse.* De quo Pontifice id definiit, nisi de legitime electo, & ab Ecclesia recepto? Erravit Concilium Viennense, & cum eo Clemens V. cum Alexandri III. & Gregorii X. Constitutiones confirmavit. Erraverunt Clemens VII. in sua Constitutione *Cum charissimus* anno 1529. Paulus IV. in sua Constitutione *In eligendis* anno 1562. Gregorius XV. in sua Constitutione *Æterni* anno 1621. Urbanus VIII. in sua Constitutione *Ad Romanum* anno 1625. Erravit Martinus V. in sua Constitutione *Inter cunctas,* qua habetur in Concilio Constantiensi, in qua statuit, *prætendendum esse ab hæreticis, qui cupiunt Ecclesiæ reconciliari, quod credant Papam, qui pro tempore fuerit, esse verum Pontificem,* ut scribit Delbene *de Loc. Theolog. dub. 250.* omnino videndus. | | 16 That he may spurn this infallibility, Muratori denies that the rule set down above is a certain dogma, namely: *Every Pontiff duly elected is a true Pontiff.* I do not recall having read any orthodox writer daring so expressly; the heterodox I do not admit. If he himself speaks rightly, then the Third Lateran Council erred, and with it Alexander III, when it established: *Let him, without any exception, be held Pontiff by the universal Church, who shall have been elected by the two parts, and received.* The general Council of Lyons erred, and with it Gregory X, when it decreed the same. The Council of Florence erred, when it defined: *That the Roman Pontiff is the successor of Blessed Peter, the chief of the Apostles, and the true Vicar of Christ, and the head of the whole Church, and that he is the Father and Teacher of all Christians, and holds the primacy over the whole world, and that to him, in Blessed Peter, full power was delivered by our Lord JESUS Christ to feed, rule, and govern the universal Church.* Of what Pontiff did it define this, if not of one legitimately elected and received by the Church? The Council of Vienne erred, and with it Clement V, when it confirmed the Constitutions of Alexander III and Gregory X. Clement VII erred in his Constitution *Cum charissimus* in the year 1529; Paul IV in his Constitution *In eligendis* in the year 1562; Gregory XV in his Constitution *Æterni* in the year 1621; Urban VIII in his Constitution *Ad Romanum* in the year 1625. Martin V erred in his Constitution *Inter cunctas,* which is contained in the Council of Constance, in which he established *that it is to be required of heretics who desire to be reconciled to the Church, that they believe the Pope who shall be for the time being to be the true Pontiff,* as writes Delbene, *de Loc. Theolog. dub. 250.,* who is altogether to be consulted. |
| 17 Quam absurdum hoc sit, nemo non videt. *Pontifices Romanos in suis Canonibus, seu Constitutionibus nunquam errasse; quia juxta Prophetam; nec in Galaat resina, nec Medicus deest,* edocuit contra impiissimum Lutherum S. P. Leo X. in sua Bulla, quæ incipit *Exurge Domine* anno 1520. *Contra Decreta Summorum Pontificum, contra statuta Conciliorum, contra sanctiones orthodoxorum Patrum, in quibus tribus summa auctoritas, summa veritas, summaque sanctitas consistunt:* atque, *Decretis Summorum Pontificum auctoritatem dedisse omnium Imperatorem dicentem Petro in Euangelio Matthæi: Quidquid ligaveris super terram, erit ligatum & in cœlo,* dogmatice scripsit Joannes Archiepiscopus Cantuarensis ad Eduardum Regem Angliæ anno 1281. *Decreta sanctissimorum Romanorum Pontificum si possemus, etiam studiosius, quam illa quatuor Concilia, venerari, & observare debemus, cum & ipsa Concilia omni firmitate carerent, si non Apostolicæ Sedis Pontifices eadem per Apostolicam auctoritatem, congregare, & corroborare decrevissent.... Si ergo quatuor Concilia omni auctoritate carerent, nisi principaliter ex decretis Romanorum Pontificum firmitatem obtinerent, quis inficiari poterit, quanto decreta per ipsos Apostolicos viros promulgata maiori veneratione digna merito censeantur, quam ipsa Concilia.... Ergo reverentiam, sive obedientiam, quam sacratissimis quatuor Conciliis juxta Sanctum Gregorium merito exhibemus, decretis Apostolicæ Sedis nullatenus denegare, immo, si possibile est, studiosius impendere debemus; cum sine eorum auctoritate nec ipsa Concilia fas esset recipere,* decreverunt orthodoxi Patres in Spiritu Sancto congregati in Concilio Romano I. sub Gregorio VIII. anno 1074. *Etiam ipsis dogmatibus fidei* (quod argumentum supra assumebam) *circa Sanctam, & individuam Trinitatem detrimenta generari posse, si omne firmitatis robur Pontificum decretis non concedamus,* scripsit Adrianus Papa II. ad Synodum Tricassinam anno 8[?]7. Si ergo Conciliis œcumenicis eadem fides danda est, ac quatuor Euangeliis: si Pontificum decretis eadem adhibenda est fides, ac ipsis Conciliis: si quod Euangelia, quod œcumenica Concilia decernunt, de fide credenda sunt; cur non credendum est Pontificibus decernentibus, Pontificem rite electum esse verum, & legitimum Romanum Pontificem? Quibus hæc confundit Muratorius? ineptis, & frivolis argumentationibus. | | 17 How absurd this is, everyone sees. *That the Roman Pontiffs in their Canons, or Constitutions, have never erred; because, according to the Prophet, neither is balm lacking in Galaat, nor a Physician,* taught the Holy Father Leo X against the most impious Luther, in his Bull which begins *Exurge Domine,* in the year 1520. *Against the Decrees of the Supreme Pontiffs, against the statutes of Councils, against the sanctions of the orthodox Fathers, in which three the highest authority, the highest truth, and the highest sanctity consist:* and, *That the Emperor of all gave authority to the Decrees of the Supreme Pontiffs, saying to Peter in the Gospel of Matthew: Whatsoever thou shalt bind upon earth shall be bound also in heaven,* dogmatically wrote John, Archbishop of Canterbury, to Edward, King of England, in the year 1281. *The Decrees of the most holy Roman Pontiffs we ought, if we could, to venerate and observe even more studiously than those four Councils, since even the Councils themselves would lack all firmness, if the Pontiffs of the Apostolic See had not decreed, by Apostolic authority, to congregate and corroborate them.... If, therefore, the four Councils would lack all authority, unless they obtained firmness principally from the decrees of the Roman Pontiffs, who will be able to deny how much more worthy of veneration the decrees promulgated by those Apostolic men themselves are deservedly judged, than the Councils themselves.... Therefore the reverence, or obedience, which according to Saint Gregory we deservedly show to the four most sacred Councils, we ought by no means to deny to the decrees of the Apostolic See, nay rather, if it is possible, to bestow it more studiously; since without their authority it would not be lawful to receive even the Councils themselves,* decreed the orthodox Fathers congregated in the Holy Spirit in the First Roman Council under Gregory VIII in the year 1074. *That even to the very dogmas of the faith* (which argument I was assuming above) *concerning the Holy and undivided Trinity, detriments can be generated, if we do not concede all strength of firmness to the decrees of the Pontiffs,* wrote Pope Adrian II to the Synod of Troyes in the year 8[?]7. If, therefore, the same faith is to be given to ecumenical Councils as to the four Gospels: if the same faith is to be given to the decrees of the Pontiffs as to the Councils themselves: if the things which the Gospels, which the ecumenical Councils decree, are to be believed of faith; why is credence not to be given to the Pontiffs decreeing that a Pontiff duly elected is the true and legitimate Roman Pontiff? With what does Muratori confound these things? With inept and frivolous argumentations. |
| 18 Quæ maioris ponderis esse videtur, & in qua plurimum confidit Muratorius, quasi ei insistere videatur, est Constitutio 19. Pauli IV. in qua Pontifex ait: *Si ullo unquam tempore apparuerit, aliquem ante eius in Romanum Pontificem assumptionem à fide Catholica deviasse, aut in aliquam hæresim incidisse.... promotio, seu assumptio de eo, etiam in concordia, & de unanimi etiam consensu Cardinalium facta, nulla, irrita, & inanis existat, nec per ipsius Romani Pontificis inthronizationem; aut adorationem, seu per ei præstitam ab omnibus obedientiam, & cujusque temporis in præmissis cursum, convaluisse dici, aut convalescere possit.* Ex qua Pontificis Constitutione infert Muratorius: *Igitur supernaturalis fidei assensus exigi non potest, quo aliquis credatur verus Summus Pontifex, etiam si ab universa Ecclesia receptus fuerit, quando Ecclesiam ab hoc errore immunem in hoc negotio nequaquam arbitratur ipsemet Paulus IV.* Nihil tamen concludit argumentum. Et in primis nullatenus offendit thesim illam: *Omnis Pontifex rite electus est verus Pontifex.* Siquidem, si forte eligatur hæreticus, hic non est rite electus; & per consequens Constitutio versatur circa Pontificem non rite electum, utpote cui obstat nullitas, quam de jure divino inesse docent Theologi. Prædictam thesim esse certissimam, & de fide tenendam, docent etiam ii, qui tenent non esse de fide, *hunc, vel illum Romanum Pontificem esse verum Pontificem;* adeo ut Palancus, & Palao, aliique supra citati dicant, nullum Doctorem orthodoxum de ea dubitare. Unde cum non modo dubitet Muratorius, sed absolute neget, videtur inferendum, eum, etsi talis videatur, revera orthodoxum non esse. | | 18 What seems to be of greater weight, and in which Muratori most trusts, as though he may be seen to rest upon it, is Constitution 19 of Paul IV, in which the Pontiff says: *If at any time it shall appear that anyone, before his assumption to the Roman Pontificate, had deviated from the Catholic faith, or fallen into some heresy.... the promotion, or assumption, made of him, even in concord, and even with the unanimous consent of the Cardinals, shall be null, invalid, and void; nor can it be said to have gained force, or be able to gain force, through the enthronization of the same Roman Pontiff, or his adoration, or through the obedience rendered to him by all, and the course of whatever length of time in the premises.* From which Constitution of the Pontiff Muratori infers: *Therefore an assent of supernatural faith cannot be exacted, whereby anyone should be believed to be the true Supreme Pontiff, even if he has been received by the universal Church, since Paul IV himself by no means judges the Church immune from this error in this affair.* Yet the argument concludes nothing. And in the first place, it in no way strikes against that thesis: *Every Pontiff duly elected is a true Pontiff.* Since, if perchance a heretic be elected, he is not duly elected; and consequently the Constitution deals with a Pontiff not duly elected, inasmuch as there stands against him a nullity, which the Theologians teach to be present by divine law. That the aforesaid thesis is most certain and to be held of faith, is taught even by those who hold that it is not of faith *that this or that Roman Pontiff is a true Pontiff;* so much so that Palanco, and Palao, and the others cited above, say that no orthodox Doctor doubts of it. Whence, since Muratori not only doubts, but absolutely denies, it seems to be inferred that he, although he may seem such, is in reality not orthodox. |
| 19 Deinde Pauli IV. Constitutioni opponimus Constitutionem Martini V. de qua supra, in qua hæretici, de quibus ibi, non aliter reconciliantur Ecclesiæ, ni professi sint, se credere præsentem Pontificem verum esse Pontificem. Ergo Martinus *Ecclesiam ab eo errore immunem in hoc negotio arbitratur.* Opponimus Lateranense Concilium, Synodum Florentinam, Alexandrum III. & cæteros supra citatos definientes pro veris Pontificibus habendos omnes rite electos juxta formam ibidem constitutam. Ergo ii omnes *Ecclesiam ab eo errore immunem arbitrantur.* Quid tamen? Num dicemus Paulum IV. his omnibus contrarium extitisse, aut contraria definiisse? Absit. Explicandus est igitur Paulus IV. Certe nullatenus definiit omnem Ecclesiam in acceptatione Papæ errare posse, ut vult deducere Muratorius; sed totus fuit in exponenda forma, qua servata, electio proculdubio certa evaderet; & ex suppositione sententiæ asserentis fieri posse, ut Cardinales eligentes quandoque errarent, proposuit nullitatem provenientem ex eo, quod electus esset, aut fuisset hæreticus occultus, quæ ita intrinsece, & ex constitutione divina reddit nullam Pontificiam electionem, ut nullo unquam tempore, neque per totius Ecclesiæ acceptationem sanari possit; ad differentiam aliarum juris positivi exceptionum, quæ Pontificiæ electioni opponi queunt, & quæ post electionem factam, & Ecclesiæ acceptationem non admittuntur. Non tamen intellexit Paulus de facto fieri posse, quod à principio ad hæc usque tempora nec contigisse constat, nec Deus quandoque permisit. Immo, si id arbitraretur, in aliud absurdum incidisse diceretur, nempe Deum in necessario negotio Ecclesiam suam sua infallibili providentia destituisse. Unde locutus fuit Paulus per quamdam exaggerationem: prout in simili alter Paulus, Apostolus scilicet, & Doctor Gentium *ad Galat. 1.* sic dicebat: *Licet nos, aut Angeli de cœlo euangelizent vobis, præterquam quod euangelizavimus, anathema sit.* Non docuit, aut intellexit Apostolus, eum, aut Angelos de cœlo posse euangelizare aliquid contrarium veritati, quam ipse euangelizaverat; sed tantum voluit dicere, adeo certam, & infallibilem esse suam doctrinam, ut si per impossibile Angelus diversa enuntiaret, nullatenus esset credendus. Sic Paulus IV. nihil aliud voluit docere, nisi adeo obstare, aut repugnare legitimæ Pontificis electioni exceptionem hæresis, ut etiamsi per impossibile Deus permitteret, quod Ecclesia hæreticum eligeret, & ut verum Pontificem recognosceret, id efficere non posse, ut talis hæreticus verus Pontifex esset, aut electio aliquando ex diuturnitate temporis, aut Ecclesiæ acceptatione convalesceret. | | 19 Then, to the Constitution of Paul IV we oppose the Constitution of Martin V, of which above, in which the heretics there in question are not otherwise reconciled to the Church, unless they have professed that they believe the present Pontiff to be the true Pontiff. Therefore Martin *judges the Church immune from that error in this affair.* We oppose the Lateran Council, the Synod of Florence, Alexander III, and the others cited above, defining that all duly elected according to the form there established are to be held as true Pontiffs. Therefore all these *judge the Church immune from that error.* What then? Shall we say that Paul IV stood contrary to all these, or defined contrary things? Far be it. Paul IV, therefore, must be explained.
