Certainly he in no way defined that the whole Church can err in the acceptation of a Pope, as Muratori wishes to deduce; but he was wholly occupied in setting forth the form, which being observed, the election would beyond doubt come out certain; and on the supposition of the opinion asserting that it can happen that the electing Cardinals might at some time err, he proposed the nullity arising from this, that the one elected was, or had been, an occult heretic; which nullity so intrinsically, and by divine constitution, renders the Pontifical election null, that at no time, not even by the acceptation of the whole Church, can it be healed; in contrast to the other exceptions of positive law, which can be opposed to a Pontifical election, and which, after the election has been made and the Church's acceptation, are not admitted. Yet Paul did not understand that this could in fact happen — a thing which, from the beginning down to these times, is neither known to have occurred, nor has God at any time permitted it. Nay, if he thought so, he would be said to have fallen into another absurdity, namely, that God had left His Church destitute of His infallible providence in a necessary affair. Whence Paul spoke by a certain exaggeration: just as, in a similar case, the other Paul, namely the Apostle and Doctor of the Gentiles, *ad Galat. 1.,* spoke thus: *Though we, or Angels from heaven, preach a gospel to you besides that which we have preached, let him be anathema.* The Apostle did not teach, or understand, that he, or Angels from heaven, could preach something contrary to the truth which he himself had preached; but he only wished to say that his doctrine was so certain and infallible, that if, *per impossibile,* an Angel should announce different things, he would in no wise be to be believed. So Paul IV wished to teach nothing else than that the exception of heresy so obstructs, or is repugnant to, the legitimate election of a Pontiff, that even if, *per impossibile,* God should permit the Church to elect a heretic, and to recognize him as true Pontiff, this could not bring it about that such a heretic should be a true Pontiff, or that the election should at any time gain force from length of time, or from the Church's acceptation. |
| 20 *Ubicumque fuerint duo, vel tres congregati in nomine meo, ibi sum ego,* promisit summa veritas in Euangelio. Id omnino credendum est. Indefectibilis, veracissimusque Dominus nec falli, nec fallere potest, nec aliquatenus Ecclesiam suam quandoque decipi patietur. Congregantur Cardinales Electores in nomine Domini ad rem faciendam arduissimam, à qua tota Ecclesiæ, & fidelium gubernatio, securitas, doctrinaque dependet. An in tam arduo, gravique negotio deficiet Spiritus Domini dirigens voluntates, incendens corda, & illuminans mentes? Id sentiant hæretici, id arbitretur Muratorius fieri posse. Nobis Catholicis id dubitare maximum profecto piaculum esset. *Spiritus Sancti præsentiam testatur congregatio Sacerdotum. Verum est enim quod legimus, quia nec potest veritas mentiri, cujus in Euangelio sententia est:* Ubi duo, vel tres congregati fuerint in nomine meo, ibi & ego sum in medio eorum. *Quod cum ita sit, si nec huic tam brevi numero Spiritus Sanctus deest, quanto magis eum nunc interesse credimus, quando in unum convenit tanta turba Sacerdotum?* Scripsit Cœlestinus Synodo Ephesi congregatæ anno 431. *Quis credat unicuilibet Deum examinis inspirare justitiam, & innumeris in Concilium congregatis Sacerdotibus denegare,* dixerunt Patres Concilii Africani anno 424. & nos ad rem tanti negotii referre possumus. Innumeras ad rem auctoritates adducere facile esset; hæ sufficiant. Si id verum esse non crederemus, deciperetur universa Ecclesia verum Pontificem agnoscens à Deo non inspiratum, neque ipsi acceptum. Hanc Pontificis electionem divinum opus esse multis, & concludentibus argumentis probat Scipio Amiratus *in Dissert. Polit. lib. 3. cap. 1.* & Franciscus Hallier *de Sacris Electionib.* Multoties Electorum suffragia discrepasse, tot in Ecclesia exorta schismata apertissime pronuntiant; sed nec inde contra supra dicta efficax resultat argumentum. Turbidi hominum affectus in dissentiones prorumpunt multoties; nec aliter fieri potest ubi non vera charitas, sed uniuscujusque privata voluntas operatur. Sic in sacris electionibus, id permittente Deo, ut fluctuet Petri navicula, ejusque agnoscatur fidelis, & admirabilis providentia, quæ inter medios schismatum vortices Ecclesiam suam non deserit. Interim dum humanæ penitus contentiones electionis partes assumere intendunt, Spiritus Sancti locum non habet infallibilis assistentia, neque in schismaticos quandoque influit, qui pacem, & concordiam amat, præcipiente ipso Christo Domino: *Ubicumque intraveritis, primum dicite: Pax huic domui;* atque: *Pacem relinquo vobis, pacem meam do vobis;* & docente Apostolo: *Pacem, & veritatem diligite.* Inter hos tamen contentionum turbines schismaticos Pontifices nunquam veros, & legitimos Pontifices Ecclesia habuit universa, neque hi dicendi erant legitime electi. Loquimur igitur de electione canonica, ad quam secundum ecclesiasticarum legum præscripta concordia suffragantium vota, aut duarum partium conflaunt, & quam universa Ecclesia sine dubio recipit, & approbat: qua posita, electum, & receptum Pontificem verum esse Christi Vicarium, & Petri successorem inconcusse cum ipsa Ecclesia confitemur. | | 20 *Wheresoever there shall be two or three gathered together in my name, there am I,* promised the Supreme Truth in the Gospel. This must altogether be believed. The indefectible and most truthful Lord can neither be deceived nor deceive, nor will He in any measure suffer His Church at any time to be deceived. The Cardinal Electors are congregated in the name of the Lord to accomplish a most arduous matter, upon which the whole government, security, and doctrine of the Church and of the faithful depends. In so arduous and grave an affair, shall the Spirit of the Lord be lacking, directing wills, kindling hearts, and illuminating minds? Let heretics hold this; let Muratori judge that this can happen. For us Catholics, to doubt it would assuredly be the greatest sin. *The congregation of Priests testifies the presence of the Holy Spirit. For that is true which we read, since Truth, whose sentence in the Gospel is:* Where two or three shall be gathered together in my name, there am I also in the midst of them, *cannot lie. Since this is so, if the Holy Spirit is not absent even from so small a number, how much more do we believe that He is present now, when so great a throng of Priests has come together in one?* So wrote Celestine to the Synod congregated at Ephesus in the year 431. *Who would believe that God inspires the justice of examination in any single man, and denies it to innumerable Priests congregated in Council,* said the Fathers of the African Council in the year 424; and we may refer this to a matter of so great moment. It would be easy to adduce innumerable authorities on the point; let these suffice. If we did not believe this to be true, the universal Church would be deceived, acknowledging a true Pontiff not inspired by God, nor accepted by Him. That this election of the Pontiff is a divine work, Scipio Amiratus proves with many and conclusive arguments, *in Dissert. Polit. lib. 3. cap. 1.,* and Franciscus Hallier, *de Sacris Electionib.* That the suffrages of the Electors have many times been at variance, the many schisms that have arisen in the Church most openly proclaim; but not even thence does an efficacious argument result against what has been said above. The turbid affections of men many times burst forth into dissensions; nor can it happen otherwise, where not true charity, but the private will of each one is at work. So it is in sacred elections, God permitting it, that the little bark of Peter may be tossed, and that His faithful and admirable providence may be acknowledged, which amid the very whirlpools of schisms does not desert His Church. Meanwhile, while contentions wholly human strive to take upon themselves the parts of the election, the infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit has no place, nor does He at any time flow into schismatics, He who loves peace and concord, Christ the Lord Himself commanding: *Into whatsoever house you enter, first say: Peace be to this house;* and: *Peace I leave with you, my peace I give unto you;* and the Apostle teaching: *Love peace and truth.* Yet amid these whirlwinds of contentions the universal Church has never held schismatic Pontiffs to be true and legitimate Pontiffs, nor were these to be called legitimately elected. We speak, therefore, of a canonical election, unto which, according to the prescripts of the ecclesiastical laws, the concordant votes of the electors, or of the two parts, combine [?], and which the universal Church without doubt receives and approves: which being posited, we confess unshakenly, with the Church herself, that the elected and received Pontiff is the true Vicar of Christ and successor of Peter. |
| 21 Concors omnium Theologorum, & Canonistarum est sententia, Pontificem rite electum non ab hominibus, sed immediate à Deo confirmationem, potestatem, & jurisdictionem in universam accipere Ecclesiam. Vel ergo Christus Dominus Pontifici rite, & canonice electo hanc tribuit supremam potestatem, vel non? Si non tribuit, decipit Ecclesiam, & permittit eam à non vero Pastore gubernari, quod de ejus infallibili providentia affirmare, vel intelligere temerarium, scandalosum, erroneum, immo & hæreticum esset. Si vero tribuit, ut certum est, quomodo non erit de fide credendum, eum esse verum Pontificem, cui Deus in universam Ecclesiam suas vices committit, omnimodaque ecclesiastica, & spirituali condecorat, & fulcit potestate? Est verus Pontifex *Apex sublimissimus Apostolatus,* ut cum vocat Lactant. *epist. ad Nicolaum I. Apex supremus Ecclesiarum Dei,* ut cum dicit Theodor. Studit. *epistol. ad Imper. Michaelem,* qui etiam *epist. 33.* ad Leonem III. ei tribuit titulum *Archipastoris Ecclesiæ, quæ sub cœlis est.* Eum esse *Caput totius corporis, cui, quidquid fidelium est, ubique submittitur,* scripsit Ennodius *in Apolog.* Sanctus Petrus Damianus *opusc. 4. ex edict.* Constantini cum nominat *Caput Christianæ religionis,* cujus sublimitas est *Cacumen, & vertex humani generis,* ut ipse Damianus asserit *opusc. 23. cap. 1. Dominum domus Dei, & Principem omnis possessionis suæ, ac Maiestate Apostolica refulgentem* vocat Bernardus *epist. 237. 261. & 268.* In ore D. Anselmi *lib. de Incarnat. cap. 7.* dicitur: *Dominus, & Pater totius Ecclesiæ in terra peregrinantis.* Eum *Dominum omnium gentium* esse, invenitur in Concilio Nicæno I. *can. 44. Vers. Arabic.* *Ducem universalis Ecclesiæ* vocavit Damascenus *orat. de Transfigur.* *Episcopus unice summus, & singulariter generalis* ipse est juxta *Petr. Blesens. epist. 27. in fin.* *Gubernatorem throni Apostolici* eum dicit *Concil. Chalced. act. 1.* Ejus *arbitrio claves æterni aditus tradi; cujus terrestre judicium præjudicata auctoritas sit in cœlo, ut quæ in terris, aut ligata fuerint aut soluta, statuti ejusdem conditionem obtineant etiam in cœlis*, docuit *Hilar. cap. 16. in Matth.* Ipse est *Præfectus toti orbi terrarum, Claviger cœlorum, cujus arbitrio, & potestati cuncta Deus permisit; & Præpositus universo orbi terrarum, non ut Jeremias uni genti*, ut auree scripsit *Chrysost. homil. de S. Ignat. & homil. 11. & 55. in Matth.* *Præsidens Ecclesiæ totius orbis terrarum*, ut ait *August. lib. 1. contra Julian. cap. 2.* *Præsul summus à nemine judicandus, quia non est discipulus supra magistrum*, ut ait *Concil. Roman. sub Silvestro.* Quo me rapuit spiritus, ut hæc enuntiarem! Si quæ de Summi Pontificis dignitate à SS. PP. & Conciliis scripta inveniuntur, recensere vellem, novum librum conscriberem. Quid amplius? Est Ecclesiæ Sponsus, Petri Successor, Christi Vicarius, cui traditæ sunt claves Regni cœlorum, Petra, Basis, Fundamentum Ecclesiæ, Columna Fidei, ejusque viva, & indefectibilis regula. Et hunc permitteret Deus eligere, qui tamen falsus Doctor, & illegitimus Pastor esset? Credat id Muratorius; defendat non esse tamquam de fide certum, eum esse verum Romanum Pontificem: mordeat Recentiores, qui id asserunt; ego enim in obsequium piissimæ, dilectissimæque Matris meæ his potius assentiendum esse constituo. | | 21 It is the concordant opinion of all Theologians and Canonists, that the Pontiff duly elected receives confirmation, power, and jurisdiction over the universal Church not from men, but immediately from God. Either, then, Christ the Lord bestows this supreme power upon the Pontiff duly and canonically elected, or He does not? If He does not bestow it, He deceives the Church, and permits her to be governed by one who is not a true Shepherd; which to affirm, or to think, of His infallible providence, would be temerarious, scandalous, erroneous, nay even heretical. But if He does bestow it, as is certain, how will it not be to be believed of faith, that he is the true Pontiff, to whom God commits His own place over the universal Church, and whom He adorns and supports with all manner of ecclesiastical and spiritual power? The true Pontiff is *the most sublime Apex of the Apostolate,* as Lactant. calls him, *epist. ad Nicolaum I.;* *the supreme Apex of the Churches of God,* as says Theodor. Studit., *epistol. ad Imper. Michaelem,* who also, *epist. 33.,* to Leo III, gives him the title of *Archpastor of the Church which is under heaven.* That he is *the Head of the whole body, to whom whatever is of the faithful is everywhere subject,* wrote Ennodius *in Apolog.* Saint Peter Damian, *opusc. 4. ex edict.* of Constantine, names him *the Head of the Christian religion,* whose sublimity is *the Summit and crown of the human race,* as Damian himself asserts, *opusc. 23. cap. 1.* Bernard, *epist. 237. 261. & 268.,* calls him *the Lord of the house of God, and the Prince of all His possession, and refulgent with Apostolic Majesty.* In the mouth of St. Anselm, *lib. de Incarnat. cap. 7.*, he is called: *Lord and Father of the whole Church journeying as a pilgrim on earth.* That he is *Lord of all nations* is found in the First Council of Nicaea, *can. 44. Vers. Arabic.* Damascene, *orat. de Transfigur.*, called him *Leader of the universal Church.* He is *the uniquely supreme and singularly general Bishop*, according to *Petr. Blesens. epist. 27. in fin.* The *Concil. Chalced. act. 1.* calls him *Governor of the Apostolic throne.* That *to his judgment the keys of the eternal entrance are handed over; whose earthly judgment is an authority pre-judged in heaven, so that whatever things on earth have been either bound or loosed obtain the condition of that same decree also in the heavens*, taught *Hilar. cap. 16. in Matth.* He is *Prefect over the whole world, Key-bearer of the heavens, to whose judgment and power God has entrusted all things; and Overseer of the whole world, not, like Jeremias, of one nation*, as *Chrysost.* wrote in golden words, *homil. de S. Ignat. & homil. 11. & 55. in Matth.* *Presiding over the Church of the whole world*, as says *August. lib. 1. contra Julian. cap. 2.* *The supreme Prelate, to be judged by no one, because the disciple is not above the master*, as says the *Concil. Roman. sub Silvestro.* Whither has the spirit carried me, that I should utter these things! If I wished to review whatever is found written by the holy Fathers and Councils concerning the dignity of the Supreme Pontiff, I should be writing a new book. What more? He is the Spouse of the Church, the Successor of Peter, the Vicar of Christ, to whom have been handed over the keys of the Kingdom of heaven, the Rock, the Base, the Foundation of the Church, the Column of the Faith, and its living and indefectible rule. And would God permit such a one to be elected who was nevertheless a false Doctor and an illegitimate Pastor? Let Muratori believe that; let him defend that it is not certain as *of faith* that he is the true Roman Pontiff; let him carp at the more recent authors who assert it: for I, in obedience to my most pious and most beloved Mother, determine that assent is rather to be given to these. |
| 22 Ab ipso, tamquam ex fonte, potestatem legislativam hauriunt Concilia, Cardinales, Episcopi, Prælati. (5) {marg. (5) Dist. 17. per tot. cap. 4. de Elect. cap. 11. de Maiorat. & Obed. cap. Fin. de Offic. Leg. cap. Inferior dist. 21. cap. 4. & 5. de His, quæ fiunt à Prælat. cap. 11. cap. 12. 2. q. 6. cap. 8. 3. q. 6. cap. 79. q. 3. cap. 2. dist. 2. & innumera alia.} Ab eo dependent promotiones Cardinalium, Archiepiscorum, & Episcoporum ordinationes, Missionariorum facultates, Ordinum collationes, matrimoniorum, & impedimentorum dispensationes, gratiæ, indulgentiæ, & omnia alia, quæ ab universo Capite promanant. Nullitate permulta laborarent, rueret Fides, periclitaretur Religio, totus Ecclesiæ cardo verteretur, si verus Pontifex non esset is, qui rite eligeretur, & ab universa Ecclesia pro tali haberetur. Petri navis fluctibus hinc inde quassantibus obruta periret, ni dexterrimus adesset Palinurus, qui clavum teneret, eumque fidelissime regeret. Exurgerent, ut horridæ procellæ, hæreses, & errores, nec esset, qui, afflante Spiritu Sancto, tot monstra dissiparet, si verus Pontifex non adesset. Deus fallax, improvidusque diceretur, si hæc permitteret, atque electioni canonicæ se subtraheret, & rite electo suam assistentiam, & potestatem non tribueret. Igitur rite electus est verus Pontifex certissime tenendus. | | 22 From him, as from a fountain, Councils, Cardinals, Bishops, and Prelates draw their legislative power. (5) {marg. (5) Dist. 17. per tot. cap. 4. de Elect. cap. 11. de Maiorat. & Obed. cap. Fin. de Offic. Leg. cap. Inferior dist. 21. cap. 4. & 5. de His, quæ fiunt à Prælat. cap. 11. cap. 12. 2. q. 6. cap. 8. 3. q. 6. cap. 79. q. 3. cap. 2. dist. 2. & innumera alia.} On him depend the promotions of Cardinals, the ordinations of Archbishops and Bishops, the faculties of Missionaries, the conferrals of Orders, the dispensations of matrimonies and of impediments, graces, indulgences, and all the other things which flow forth from the universal Head. Very many things would labor under nullity, the Faith would collapse, Religion would be imperiled, the whole hinge of the Church would be turned, if he were not the true Pontiff who was duly elected and held for such by the universal Church. The bark of Peter, overwhelmed by billows dashing it on this side and that, would perish, were there not present a most skillful Palinurus to hold the helm and steer it most faithfully. Heresies and errors would rise up like dreadful storms, nor would there be anyone who, by the breathing of the Holy Spirit, might scatter so many monsters, if a true Pontiff were not present. God would be called deceitful and improvident, if he permitted these things, and withdrew himself from the canonical election, and did not bestow his assistance and power upon the one duly elected. Therefore it is most certainly to be held that the one duly elected is the true Pontiff. |
| 23 Antequam ad alia progrediar, his valde notanda esse Muratorii verba advertere voluissem. Ait ille: *Qua in re nemo non videt, quanta cura sit omnibus, ut hinc nova aliqua Dei revelatio excludatur, bene siquidem perspecta, habet quilibet Catholicorum intoleranda incommoda;* (Catholicus ego sum, & hæc intoleranda incommoda non attingo) *quæ sequerentur, si quidquam ad vetus doctrinæ depositum adjungi posse credatur, quod non revelarit Deus, antequam utriusque fœderis libri concluderentur morte Apostolorum.* Insinuare mihi visus est in relata propositione, nullam in Ecclesia Dei esse revelationem, nullam veritatem revelatam, omnique revelationi divinæ aditum morte Apostolorum interclusum esse. Si ita est, nihil amplius Deus suæ Ecclesiæ revelare poterit, neque Pontifices abstrusos Scripturæ sensus declarare valebunt, si eis ipse abditus sensus non revelatur. Nescio an in hoc consentiat hæreticis asserentibus nihil de fide credendum esse, nisi quod in Scripturis expressum habetur. Ego Theologum non ago, nec censuram ferre mei est muneris. Propono dubium, ut doctissimi Qualificatores ad trutinam revocent. Interim, legendum offero P. Ardeskin *lib. 1. tract. 1. cap. 4. & 5.* ubi ostendit non solam Scripturam esse fidei regulam. Hac prærogativa donari Summum Pontificem, nemo est orthodoxus, qui ambigat. Quidquid ipse decernat de fide credendum, etiamsi antiquis Patribus non sit revelatum, de fide est. Id Patres, id Concilia, id Pontifices constituunt: non opus est novam controversiam instituere; postea fortasse, occasione data, aliquid dicemus. | | 23 Before I proceed to other matters, I should have wished to remark that these words of Muratori are greatly to be noted. He says: *In which matter no one fails to see how great a care it is to all that hence any new revelation of God be excluded; since indeed every Catholic holds well in view the intolerable inconveniences* (I am a Catholic, and I do not perceive these intolerable inconveniences) *which would follow, if it be believed that anything can be added to the ancient deposit of doctrine which God did not reveal before the books of both covenants were closed by the death of the Apostles.* He seemed to me to insinuate, in the proposition cited, that there is no revelation in the Church of God, no revealed truth, and that access to every divine revelation was shut off by the death of the Apostles. If this is so, God will be able to reveal nothing further to his Church, nor will the Pontiffs be able to declare the hidden senses of Scripture, if that hidden sense is not revealed to them. I know not whether in this he agrees with the heretics who assert that nothing is to be believed as *of faith* except what is contained expressly in the Scriptures. I do not play the Theologian, nor is it my office to pass censure. I propose the doubt, that most learned Qualificators may recall it to the balance. Meanwhile, I offer for reading Fr. Ardeskin, *lib. 1. tract. 1. cap. 4. & 5.*, where he shows that Scripture alone is not the rule of faith. That the Supreme Pontiff is endowed with this prerogative, there is no orthodox man who doubts. Whatever he decrees is to be believed as *of faith*, even if it was not revealed to the ancient Fathers, is *of faith*. This the Fathers, this the Councils, this the Pontiffs establish: there is no need to institute a new controversy; afterwards perhaps, occasion being given, we shall say something. |
| 24 Similiter etiam Theologis discutienda relinquo illa verba: *Si ad alteram, oportet ut Spiritus Sanctus prædixerit, & revelaverit, hunc virum, potius quam alterum à sacris Electoribus rite, & sine errore, sine vitio aliquo esse eligendum; & tunc ex revelatione constabit, hunc, & non alium virum esse verum Pontificem Romanum. Cum autem constet Spiritum Sanctum neque revelasse olim prophetice, nec nunc revelatione peculiari aperire hunc potius, quam alium in successorem Petri fuisse electum, aut eligendum.* Videtur sensisse ad quid credendum revelationis peculiaris, & expressæ actum necessarium nobis esse, nec sufficere generale de fide effatum, ut ad particuria, in quibus illud effatum verificatur, per assensum fidei descendamus. Ex generali effatu: *Omnes in Adam peccaverunt*, quod de fide credendum est, per necessariam consequentiam deducimus: *Petrus est homo: ergo Petrus in Adam peccavit.* An indigemus peculiari revelatione, ex qua de fide teneamus Petrum peccasse in Adam? Quis hoc somniavit? De fide credimus, omnem hominem à Deo creatum esse: indigebimus fortasse peculiari revelatione, ut de fide credamus hunc numero hominem à Deo creatum fuisse? Sic in aliis multis. Ex ne recurrat ad evidentiam naturalem, quæ in illis minoribus propositionibus datur, vel ad id, quod illæ minores necessario in maioribus propositionibus comprehenduntur; idem observandum esse quando intercedit evidentia moralis, ipse infra fatetur. Deinde ut alio exemplo utar: ipse asserit, quod Conciliorum de fide decreta de fide credenda sunt. Nunc sic inquiro: *Num Spiritus Sanctus prædixerit, & revelaverit* illud Concilium *rite, & sine errore, sine vitio aliquo*, & sub vero Pontifice Romano congregatum fuisse? *Cum autem constet Spiritum Sanctum neque revelasse olim prophetice, nec nunc revelatione peculiari aperire hoc Concilium fuisse rite, & sub vero Pontifice congregatum*, neque ex peculiari aliqua revelatione nobis constet in dicto Concilio decreta fuisse à Deo revelata; inde est, quod ea decreta non sint de fide credenda. Si hæc inepta sunt, etiam inepte scriptæ dicendæ erunt Muratorii ratiocinationes. Et quidem quid ineptius, quam quæ ex principiis certis deducuntur, peculiari revelatione indigeant, ut credenda sint. Simile argumentum conficitur ex quocumque articulo à Summo Pontifice ex Cathedra loquente nobis proposito. Sed Muratorius id parvi pendet, ut enim optimus orthodoxus sequetur eos, qui infallibilitatem in definiendis fidei articulis Pontifici non concedunt, quorum ratio eadem est ac ea, qua ipse utitur. Et profecto non capio, quomodo hæc peculiaris revelatio, quam exigit Muratorius, verificanda sit. An veniet Angelus de cœlo, qui nobis eas veritates euangelizet? An loquetur nobis, ut olim, per Prophetas? An descendet Spiritus Sanctus in columbæ specie, qui nobis ad aures has revelatas inspiret veritates? Principia nota non sat erunt, ut cognoscamus, quibus fidei assensus exhibendus sit? Quid moror! Hæc adeo clara sunt, ut aliis argumentis non indigeant. | | 24 Similarly I also leave to be discussed by the Theologians those words: *If to the second, it must be that the Holy Spirit foretold and revealed that this man, rather than another, is to be elected by the sacred Electors duly and without error, without any defect; and then it will be established from revelation that this man, and no other, is the true Roman Pontiff. Since, however, it is established that the Holy Spirit neither revealed of old prophetically, nor now discloses by a peculiar revelation, that this man rather than another has been, or is to be, elected as successor of Peter.* He seems to have held that, for anything to be believed, an act of peculiar and express revelation is necessary to us, and that a general pronouncement *of faith* does not suffice for us to descend, by the assent of faith, to the particulars in which that pronouncement is verified. From the general pronouncement, *All have sinned in Adam*, which is to be believed as *of faith*, we deduce by necessary consequence: *Peter is a man: therefore Peter sinned in Adam.* Do we need a peculiar revelation, from which we may hold as *of faith* that Peter sinned in Adam? Who has dreamed this? We believe as *of faith* that every man was created by God: shall we perhaps need a peculiar revelation, in order to believe as *of faith* that this individual man was created by God? So in many other cases. And let him not have recourse to the natural evidence which is given in those minor propositions, or to the fact that those minors are necessarily comprehended in the major propositions; he himself confesses below that the same is to be observed when moral evidence intervenes. [?] Then, to use another example: he himself asserts that the decrees of Councils concerning faith are to be believed as *of faith*. Now I inquire thus: *Has the Holy Spirit foretold and revealed* that that Council was assembled *duly and without error, without any defect*, and under a true Roman Pontiff? *Since, however, it is established that the Holy Spirit neither revealed of old prophetically, nor now discloses by a peculiar revelation, that this Council was assembled duly and under a true Pontiff*, nor is it established to us from any peculiar revelation that the decrees in the said Council were revealed by God; hence it is that those decrees are not to be believed as *of faith*. If these things are inept, then Muratori's ratiocinations will likewise have to be called ineptly written. And indeed what is more inept than that things which are deduced from certain principles should need a peculiar revelation in order to be believed. A like argument is fashioned from any article whatever proposed to us by the Supreme Pontiff speaking *ex Cathedra*. But Muratori weighs this little, for, like the best of orthodox men, he will follow those who do not concede to the Pontiff infallibility in defining articles of faith, whose reasoning is the same as that which he himself employs. And truly I do not grasp how this peculiar revelation which Muratori demands is to be verified. Will an Angel come from heaven, to preach these truths to us as good tidings? Will he speak to us, as of old, through the Prophets? Will the Holy Spirit descend in the form of a dove, to breathe these revealed truths into our ears? Will known principles not be enough for us to know to which things the assent of faith is to be given? Why do I delay! These things are so clear that they need no other arguments. |
| 25 Alia etiam verba in veritatis statera à Theologis ponderanda credo, ea scilicet, quæ sequuntur: *Cur enim* (ait ille) *non revelatum quoque dicendum erit, hunc, illumve sanctum virum Canoni Beatorum adscriptum revera in cœlis regnare?* Fateor me non attingere quid revera in tali enuntiatione sibi vellet Muratorius. Nos cohærenter etiam procedimus in hoc casu, nec dubitamus Sanctum canonizatum revera in cœlis regnare, & beatifica Dei visione perfrui. Unde quoad nos illud argumentum nihil concludit. Nescio, an ille in ea opinione erronea sit, neminem scilicet ante diem judicii in cœlos ascendere. Si autem fatetur aliquos ascendere, vel ascendisse, & id de fide tenendum est, de quibus id jure asseri poterit, & firmiter teneri, nisi de iis, quos Pontifices Summi Sanctorum albo adscriptos definierunt? Contrarium sentire videtur, atque id suadent sequentia ejus verba: *Hæc igitur cum necessario consequantur, & pendeant à facto, cujus veritatem à cœlo non edocemur, ne ipsa quidem tam certa reputanda videntur.* Ex quibus verbis clare liquet, eum sequi sententiam asserentium, Pontifices Romanos in canonizatione Sanctorum fallibiles esse, nullamque habere specialem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam, ut sic infallibiliter decernant. Certe in hoc suo judicio communissimæ (ne aliud dicam) meliorum Theologorum sententiæ opponitur, & quæ apud nos tamquam certa retinetur. Videantur AA. infra citati. (6) {marg. (6) D. Thom. quodlib. 9. art. 16. Corduba lib. 4. q. 12. Bañes 2. 2. q, 1. dub. 7. Bellarm. de Sanct. beatif. cap. 9. Suar. de Fid. disp. 5. sect. 4. Vasq. in 1. 2. disp. 165. n. 94. Vivar. ad Marcum Maximum in S Joan. Vindic. §. 3. 6. & 8. Cæsar de Eccles. Hyerarch. disp. 1. §. 4. n. 8. Carrier tom. 1. digest. fidei lib. de Pontif. Roman. fol. 1104. Theoph. de Corona Aurea sub not. 2. verit 8. ArdesKin ubi supra q. 5. Palao dict. tract. 4. §. 4. Delbene de Locis Theolog. dub. 253. & innumeri alii.} Quantum ad me sufficit forma in canonizatione Sanctorum observata, nempe: *Authoritate Domini nostri Jesu Christi, & BB. Petri, & Pauli Apostolorum ejus, ac nostra F. in cœlestem Jerusalem fuisse receptum, & æternæ gloriæ corona donatum, de Fratrum nostrorum Concilio declaramus, eumque tamquam Sanctum publice, privatimque esse colendum decernimus, & definimus.* Et licet hæc definitio à facto dependeat; attamen de veritate facti dubitare non possumus, quando est connexa cum veritate juris, in qua definienda Pontifex, ut Pontifex, declarando, & universali Ecclesiæ proponendo errare non potest. Videantur post alios citatus Ardeskin *dict. p. 1. cap. 9. q. 3.* & P. Viva *in quæst. Prodrom. n. 11.* qui breviter, & nervose hanc expediunt quæstionem. | | 25 Other words also, I believe, are to be weighed by the Theologians in the balance of truth, namely those which follow: *For why* (says he) *shall it not also have to be said to be revealed, that this or that holy man, enrolled in the Canon of the Blessed, truly reigns in the heavens?* I confess that I do not grasp what Muratori really intended for himself in such an utterance. We too proceed coherently in this case, nor do we doubt that a canonized Saint truly reigns in the heavens and enjoys the beatific vision of God. Whence, as far as we are concerned, that argument concludes nothing. I know not whether he is in that erroneous opinion, namely, that no one ascends into the heavens before the day of judgment. But if he confesses that some do ascend, or have ascended, and that this is to be held as *of faith*, of whom will this rightly be able to be asserted, and firmly held, if not of those whom the Supreme Pontiffs have defined as enrolled in the album of the Saints? He seems to think the contrary, and his following words urge this: *Since therefore these things follow necessarily, and depend upon a fact of whose truth we are not taught from heaven, not even these very things seem to be reckoned so certain.* From which words it is clearly evident that he follows the opinion of those asserting that the Roman Pontiffs are fallible in the canonization of Saints, and have no special assistance of the Holy Spirit so as to decree infallibly in this. Certainly in this his judgment he is opposed to the most common (not to say more) opinion of the better Theologians, and to one which among us is retained as certain. Let the authors cited below be seen. (6) {marg. (6) D. Thom. quodlib. 9. art. 16. Corduba lib. 4. q. 12. Bañes 2. 2. q, 1. dub. 7. Bellarm. de Sanct. beatif. cap. 9. Suar. de Fid. disp. 5. sect. 4. Vasq. in 1. 2. disp. 165. n. 94. Vivar. ad Marcum Maximum in S Joan. Vindic. §. 3. 6. & 8. Cæsar de Eccles. Hyerarch. disp. 1. §. 4. n. 8. Carrier tom. 1. digest. fidei lib. de Pontif. Roman. fol. 1104. Theoph. de Corona Aurea sub not. 2. verit 8. ArdesKin ubi supra q. 5. Palao dict. tract. 4. §. 4. Delbene de Locis Theolog. dub. 253. & innumeri alii.} As for me, the form observed in the canonization of Saints suffices, namely: *By the authority of our Lord Jesus Christ, and of Blessed Peter and Paul his Apostles, and our own, we declare, by the Counsel of our Brethren, that F. has been received into the heavenly Jerusalem and endowed with the crown of eternal glory, and we decree and define that he is to be venerated publicly and privately as a Saint.* And although this definition depends upon a fact; nevertheless we cannot doubt of the truth of the fact, when it is connected with the truth of the law, in defining which the Pontiff, as Pontiff, declaring it and proposing it to the universal Church, cannot err. Let there be seen, after others, the cited Ardeskin, *dict. p. 1. cap. 9. q. 3.*, and Fr. Viva, *in quæst. Prodrom. n. 11.*, who briefly and vigorously dispatch this question. |
| 26 Noto illa verba: *Cujus veritatem à cœlo non edocemur.* Quomodo vult, quod à cœlo edoceamur? An exigit, quod de cœlo insonet vox, ut in Thabor, aut in Jordane? Non sat erit, quod facti veritatem nos edoceat Ecclesia post accuratissimum examen? Non sat erit, quod Summus Pontifex illud ut certum approbet, & per illud moveatur ad definiendam illius Beati sanctitatem? Quamvis autem admitteremus, aliquem errorem circa factum intervenisse, quod non est credendum, ut optime ait Mortonius Archiepiscopus Cantuariensis omnino videndus *tom. 9.* Conciliorum ex editione Arduini *pag. 1547.* id non impediret actum fidei, quo hic, & nunc crederemus, tamquam de fide, hunc, vel illum Sanctum æterna beatitudine in cœlis gaudere, ut bene ait citatus Mortonius. Supposita enim omnimoda certitudine facti, nempe, illum Sanctum esse ab Ecclesia canonizatum, habemus efficacissimum fundamentum, quod intellectum instruit, & voluntatem movet ad id credendum, videlicet illud, quod resultat ex principiis certis, qualia sunt, quod Ecclesia in rebus fidei, & morum, (ad quam materiam spectat hæc de canonizatione Sanctorum, ut ait citatus Mortonius, & communiter DD. ideoque ad solam Ecclesiam spectat eam definire) insimulque veracitas divina, (hæc enim est principium, in quo fides versatur, ut docent Theologi) cui innitimur ad credendum quidquid ab Ecclesia credendum proponitur. Hinc progredimur ad eliciendum hic, & nunc actum fidei, quo credimus hunc numero Sanctum beatifica perfrui Dei visione; quin hic, & nunc indigeamus aliqua peculiari Dei revelatione; licet aliàs fateamur Ecclesiam, & Pontificem indigere speciali revelatione, seu certissima inspiratione ad illum numero Beatum canonizandum, & definiendum ut Sanctum, sicut etiam eam habet infallibilem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam ad definiendum aliquid de fide; aliàs quidquid ab Ecclesia definiretur, non esset de fide, quia non fundaretur in aliqua veritate revelata. Si autem Muratorius, ut ejus verba sonant, hanc negat revelationem, seu specialem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam, Theologi dicent, quæ censura ejus thesi inurenda sit. | | 26 I note those words: *Of whose truth we are not taught from heaven.* How does he wish that we be taught from heaven? Does he demand that a voice resound from heaven, as on Thabor, or at the Jordan? Will it not be enough that the Church teach us the truth of the fact after a most accurate examination? Will it not be enough that the Supreme Pontiff approve it as certain, and by it be moved to define the sanctity of that Blessed? But even if we admitted that some error had intervened concerning the fact — which is not to be believed, as excellently says Mortonius, Archbishop of Canterbury, altogether worth seeing in *tom. 9.* of the Councils from the edition of Harduin, *pag. 1547.* — this would not impede the act of faith by which, here and now, we would believe, as *of faith*, that this or that Saint enjoys eternal beatitude in the heavens, as the cited Mortonius well says. For, supposing the complete certitude of the fact, namely, that that Saint has been canonized by the Church, we have a most efficacious foundation which instructs the intellect and moves the will to believe it, namely, that which results from certain principles — such as are the Church in matters of faith and morals (to which matter this of the canonization of Saints pertains, as says the cited Mortonius, and the Doctors commonly; and therefore it pertains to the Church alone to define it), and together with it the divine veracity (for this is the principle upon which faith turns, as the Theologians teach) — upon which we rest in order to believe whatever is proposed by the Church to be believed. Hence we proceed to elicit, here and now, the act of faith by which we believe that this individual Saint enjoys the beatific vision of God; without our needing, here and now, any peculiar revelation of God; although otherwise we confess that the Church and the Pontiff do need a special revelation, or a most certain inspiration, for canonizing that individual Blessed and defining him as a Saint, just as she also has the infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit for defining anything as *of faith*; otherwise whatever was defined by the Church would not be *of faith*, because it would not be founded upon some revealed truth. But if Muratori, as his words sound, denies this revelation, or special assistance of the Holy Spirit, the Theologians will say what censure is to be branded upon his thesis. |
| 27 Equidem scio Doctores, qui sequuntur, canonizationes Sanctorum de fide non esse, solummodo loqui de fide immediata, quatenus id ab Ecclesia expresse definitum non invenitur. Ideo S. Thomas loco citato asserit, canonizationem Sanctorum medium esse inter judicium infallibile circa res fidei, & circa alias res, quæ ad fidem immediate non spectant. Quod quidem idem est, ac docere, canonizationem Sanctorum attingere ex una parte extremum fidei propter maximam certitudinem, quæ in ea consideratur; ex alia vero aliud attingere extremum, quatenus non invenitur expresse, & immediate definitum, Ecclesiam in canonizatione Sanctorum errare non posse. Sic tamen omnes unanimiter asserunt, ita certum esse, Ecclesiam in canonizatione Sanctorum errare non posse, ut temerarium, scandalosum, ac hæresim sapiens sit affirmare contrarium. His igitur notis notandus venit Muratorius, dum insinuare visus est, Ecclesiam in actu canonizationis errori esse obnoxiam. Neque ipse censuram effugere poterit, quin herbam porrigat. Vel enim asserit Ecclesiam errare posse in canonizatione Sanctorum, & in propositionem temerariam, scandalosam, & hæresi proximam impingit: vel arbitratur errare non posse; & tunc negare non potest specialem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam, à quo solum omnis erroris immunitas dependet; & hac posita, habemus in canonizatione infallibilem veritatem à Deo revelatam, dicente Christo Domino: *Ipse docebit vos omnem veritatem.* Nescio profecto, quo impellente suggestu non modo Muratorio placuit à probabiliori, & certiori sententia discedere, sed adeo extra se ipsum rapi, ut piam, probabilissimam, &, etiam in adversariorum judicio, plusquam humana certitudine fultam sententiam ingenii immoderantiæ attribueret. | | 27 Indeed I know that the Doctors who follow the view that the canonizations of Saints are not *of faith* speak only of immediate faith, insofar as this is not found expressly defined by the Church. Hence St. Thomas, in the place cited, asserts that the canonization of Saints is a mean between the infallible judgment concerning matters of faith and that concerning other matters which do not immediately pertain to faith. Which indeed is the same as to teach that the canonization of Saints touches, on the one side, the extreme of faith, on account of the very great certitude which is considered in it; but on the other side touches the other extreme, insofar as it is not found expressly and immediately defined that the Church cannot err in the canonization of Saints. Yet all unanimously assert that it is so certain that the Church cannot err in the canonization of Saints, that it is temerarious, scandalous, and savoring of heresy to affirm the contrary. With these marks, then, Muratori comes to be marked, since he seemed to insinuate that the Church, in the act of canonization, is liable to error. Nor will he himself be able to escape censure, without owning himself beaten. For either he asserts that the Church can err in the canonization of Saints, and he runs into a proposition temerarious, scandalous, and proximate to heresy; or he judges that she cannot err, and then he cannot deny the special assistance of the Holy Spirit, upon which alone all immunity from error depends; and, this being posited, we have in canonization an infallible truth revealed by God, Christ the Lord saying: *He will teach you all truth.* Truly I know not by what impelling suggestion it pleased Muratori not only to depart from the more probable and more certain opinion, but to be so carried outside himself as to attribute to immoderateness of wit a pious, most probable opinion, supported, even in the judgment of adversaries, by a more than human certitude. |
| 28 Eum ad tantam licentiam movit, quod alicujus sanctitas non inveniatur revelata. Nescit ipse aliam esse revelationem singularem explicitam, aliam vero virtualem implicitam. Si ipse ad fidei assensum requirit revelationem explicitam, multa erunt ab Ecclesia definita de fide non credenda; in multis enim nobis de hac revelatione explicita non constat. Si verò loquitur de revelatione virtuali implicita, procul dubio fallitur. Certum est enim, revelatum esse in cœlo assistere æterna beatitudine donatum omnem Catholicum in gratia decedentem, & ab omni inquinamento jam mundatum. Aliter Deus verax, justusque non esset, qui coronam justitiæ omnibus legitime certantibus repromisit, dicente Apostolo: *Reposita est mihi corona justitiæ, quam reddet mihi Dominus.* Identidem revelatum est, Ecclesiam, ejusque Pontificem ob infallibilem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam errare non posse in iis, quæ ad Religionem universalis Ecclesiæ spectare dignoscuntur. Unde fit evidens, esse de fide saltem implicita hunc Sanctum canonizatum in cœlis esse. Siquidem illud implicite in veritate revelata continetur, quod evidenter deducitur ex una præmissa de fide, & altera evidenti. Sic est in proposito. Nam imprimis de fide est Ecclesiam errare non posse in iis, quæ ad cultum, & religionem universalis Ecclesiæ spectant. Deinde est evidens cultus obligans universos fideles ad venerationem exhibendam cuicumque Sancto canonizato. Fit consequens, Ecclesiam, & Pontificem in ea canonizatione errare non potuisse; alioquin aliquid constitueret Ecclesia legi divinæ, & naturali contrarium, cogeretque fideles ad assentiendum errori, & falsitati, nempe ad cultum tribuendum ipsius cultus pene indigno; quod legi divinæ, & naturali omnino repugnat. Stabilitur fortius hoc argumentum ex Clementina, & Extravaganti *de Reliq. & Venerat. Sanct.* & ex Tridentino *sess. 25.* decreto de eadem re: in quibus decretis definitum habetur Sanctos invocandos esse, & eorum reliquias venerandas, atque ad Ecclesiam spectare auctoritatem declarandi quibus ea veneratio exhibenda sit; idque tamquam ab Ecclesia definitum credere debemus. Falleretur ergo Ecclesia in re tam gravi mores spectante, nosque falleret, si Sanctus ille revera in cœlis non esset, neque propterea venerandus fuisset, legemque statueret divino, & naturali juri contrariam. En à quibus ambagibus liberemur, qui canonizationi Sanctorum fidei assensum non denegamus: en in quot diffidentiæ syrtes incidat qui fidei non attribuat assensum. Si ad aliquid credendum revelationes explicitas nobis factas expectemus, quam facile omne Christianæ Religionis negotium periclitabitur. Jure ergo Theologis relinquo judicandum, quam censuram Muratorio isti inurere debeamus. | | 28 What moved him to so great a license is that someone's sanctity is not found revealed. He does not know that one revelation is singular and explicit, another virtual and implicit. If he requires explicit revelation for the assent of faith, many things defined by the Church will not be believable as *of faith*; for in many, this explicit revelation is not established to us. But if he speaks of virtual implicit revelation, without doubt he is deceived. For it is certain that it is revealed that every Catholic dying in grace, and already cleansed from every defilement, stands in heaven endowed with eternal beatitude. Otherwise God would not be truthful and just, who has promised the crown of justice to all who strive lawfully, the Apostle saying: *There is laid up for me a crown of justice, which the Lord will render to me.* Likewise it is revealed that the Church, and her Pontiff, on account of the infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit, cannot err in those things which are recognized as pertaining to the Religion of the universal Church. Whence it becomes evident that it is *of faith*, at least implicit, that this canonized Saint is in the heavens. Since indeed that is implicitly contained in revealed truth which is evidently deduced from one premise *of faith* and another evident. So it is in the case proposed. For, in the first place, it is *of faith* that the Church cannot err in those things which pertain to the worship and religion of the universal Church. Next, the worship binding all the faithful to exhibit veneration to any canonized Saint whatever is evident. It follows that the Church and the Pontiff could not have erred in that canonization; otherwise the Church would establish something contrary to the divine and natural law, and would compel the faithful to assent to error and falsity, namely, to bestow worship upon one well-nigh unworthy of that worship; which is altogether repugnant to the divine and natural law. This argument is more strongly established from the Clementine and the Extravagant *de Reliq. & Venerat. Sanct.*, and from the Tridentine, *sess. 25.*, decree on the same matter: in which decrees it is defined that the Saints are to be invoked, and their relics venerated, and that to the Church pertains the authority of declaring to whom that veneration is to be exhibited; and this we ought to believe as defined by the Church. The Church, therefore, would be deceived in a matter so grave, regarding morals, and would deceive us, if that Saint were not truly in the heavens, and therefore ought not to have been venerated; and she would establish a law contrary to divine and natural right. Behold from what mazes we are freed, who do not deny the assent of faith to the canonization of Saints: behold into how many quicksands of diffidence he falls who does not attribute to it the assent of faith. If, for believing anything, we should await explicit revelations made to us, how easily the whole business of the Christian Religion will be imperiled. Rightly, therefore, I leave it to the Theologians to judge what censure we ought to brand upon this Muratori. |
| 29 Idem judicium exposco circa alia verba: *Aut etiam post maximum munus adeptum in hæresim declinasse, quod fieri posse similiter plerique affirmant.* Hæc generalis propositio explicatione, ac mica salis profecto indiget; comprehendere enim videtur tam casum, quo Pontifex erraret, ut Doctor particularis, & casum, quo erraret, ut Doctor universalis erudiendo Ecclesiam circa res fidei, proponendoque aliquid de fide credendum. Quoad primam partem sequuntur multi errare posse; sed de facto errasse, nullus evidenter demonstrat. Hæc calumnia Summos Pontifices Auctores eis non multum affectos affecisse, optime demonstrat Giballinus *de Scientia Canonica tom. 2. lib. 5. §. 11.* omnes has calumnias luculentissimo calamo disjiciens. Videndi etiam sunt Bellarminus *lib. 4. de Roman. Pontifice cap. 12. & alibi*, Duvallius *ubi supra*, Cardinalis Baronius multis in locis, P. Viva *de Thesib. damnat. in quæst. Prodrom. ubi num. 15.* sic ait: *Vehementer errant qui cum hæreticis docent, Pontificem non semel errasse in decretis fidei.* Hinc liquet, quoad secundam partem, falsam, calumniosamque esse eam sententiam, nec orthodoxus est aliquis, qui eam amplectatur. Quisquis in animo habeat ei adhærere, necesse est, quod opponatur Pontificum, & Conciliorum decretis. Nihil firmum in fide esset, si admitteremus Pontificem ex Cathedra loquentem errare posse. In hoc immorari frustraneus inter Catholicos labor esset. | | 29 I ask the same judgment concerning other words: *Or even, after the greatest office was obtained, to have declined into heresy, which likewise very many affirm can happen.* This general proposition assuredly needs explanation, and a grain of salt; for it seems to comprehend both the case in which the Pontiff would err as a particular Doctor, and the case in which he would err as universal Doctor, instructing the Church concerning matters of faith, and proposing something to be believed as *of faith*. As to the first part, many follow the view that he can err; but that he has in fact erred, no one evidently demonstrates. That authors not much well-affected toward them have assailed the Supreme Pontiffs with this calumny, Giballinus excellently demonstrates, *de Scientia Canonica tom. 2. lib. 5. §. 11.*, scattering all these calumnies with a most luminous pen. To be seen also are Bellarmine, *lib. 4. de Roman. Pontifice cap. 12. & alibi*, Duvallius, *ubi supra*, Cardinal Baronius in many places, Fr. Viva, *de Thesib. damnat. in quæst. Prodrom.*, where at *num. 15.* he says thus: *They err vehemently who teach with the heretics that the Pontiff has more than once erred in decrees of faith.* Hence it is clear, as to the second part, that that opinion is false and calumnious, nor is anyone orthodox who embraces it. Whoever has it in mind to adhere to it must necessarily be opposed to the decrees of Pontiffs and Councils. Nothing would be firm in the faith, if we admitted that the Pontiff speaking *ex Cathedra* can err. To dwell upon this would be, among Catholics, a fruitless labor. |
| 30 His breviter discussis, &, quin in evolvendis libris defatiger, obiter indigitatis, fundamenta quibus Muratorius innititur, quantum ingenioli mei tenuitas capit, non multum premunt. In primis non video, *quæ intoleranda incommoda sequantur* ex eo, quod ex certis generalibus principiis descendam ad aliqua peculiariter credenda, quæ ex ipsis principiis necessario deducuntur, quin sperem aut explicitam revelationem, aut expressam Ecclesiæ definitionem. Sic Doctores ea, quæ ex primis principiis juris naturalis deducuntur, juris naturalis non ambigunt esse præcepta. Sic ea, quæ ex certis juris divini principiis, seu præceptis descendunt, ejusdem juris præcepta esse non reluctantur. Qui fieri potest, ut in rebus fidei eodem jure uti nobis denegetur, & ingenii immoderantiæ tribuatur. Quod adducit ex Pauli IV. Constitutione, jam supra destructum reliqui. | | 30 These things having been briefly discussed, and — lest I weary myself in turning over books — indicated in passing, the foundations upon which Muratori rests, so far as the slenderness of my little wit grasps, do not press much. In the first place, I do not see *what intolerable inconveniences would follow* from this, that from certain general principles I descend to certain things to be believed in particular, which are necessarily deduced from those very principles, without hoping for either an explicit revelation or an express definition of the Church. Thus the Doctors do not doubt that those things which are deduced from the first principles of the natural law are precepts of the natural law. Thus they do not resist holding that those things which descend from certain principles, or precepts, of the divine law are precepts of the same law. How can it be that in matters of faith the use of the same right is denied us, and attributed to immoderateness of wit? What he adduces from the Constitution of Paul IV, I have already left destroyed above. |
| 31 Deinde asserit, hoc universale effatum: *Omnis homo rite electus ab Ecclesia in successorem Petri est verus Pontifex Romanus*, integre debere ad fidem spectare; hoc est, à Deo revelatum fuisse, ac proinde necessarium esse, quod à Deo sit revelatum, Petri successores ab Ecclesia eligendos esse. Nullam vim argumentum habet; nam in sententia, quam respuit iste Mutinensis Theologus, non est necessarium, quod integrum universale effatum sit à Deo revelatum. Ly *rite electus ab Ecclesia* stat per modum conditionis, seu requisiti logici, non verò veritatis revelatæ. Sicut in simili in hac thesi: *Omnis homo moriens in gratia, & amicitia Dei salvatur*; vel in contraria: *Omnis homo in peccato moriens condemnatur.* Utraque hæc thesis est de fide credenda; & tamen nec ideo dici debet, quod integrum universale effatum sit à Deo revelatum, ut sit de fide credendum: nam ly *moriens in gratia, moriens in peccato* ponitur per modum conditionis logicæ, ut certa evadat, & de fide credenda propositio. Et, ut utar eodem Muratorii exemplo, hæc propositio: *In omni hostia rite consecrata est realiter corpus Christi*, est de fide credenda propositio; & tamen ly *rite consecrata* est in ea propositione per modum conditionis; non quia illa propositio ex hoc reddatur conditionalis, sed quia illa conditio est imbibita, &, ut inquiunt Juristæ, ex natura rei inest, & exprimitur eo modo, quo inest, ita taliter, ut illa propositio sit simplex, & absoluta, atque simpliciter, & absolute de fide certa. Id, quod de his, & similibus thesibus certum est, etiam certum est in alia, de qua controvertimus; quin sit necessaria nova, & peculiaris revelatio illum hominem fuisse rite electum, ut illa propositio de fide certa evadat: sicut non est necessarium in aliis propositionibus peculiaris revelatio, quod ille homo decessisset in gratia, aut in peccato; vel quod hæc, vel illa hostia sit rite consecrata, ut de fide certæ evadant prædictæ absolutæ, generales, simplicesque propositiones, quæ ex suppositione imbibita in infallibili veracitate fundantur. | | 31 Next he asserts that this universal pronouncement: *Every man duly elected by the Church as successor of Peter is the true Roman Pontiff*, must belong integrally to the faith; that is, that it was revealed by God, and hence that it is necessary that it be revealed by God that the successors of Peter are to be elected by the Church. The argument has no force; for in the opinion which this Modenese Theologian rejects, it is not necessary that the whole universal pronouncement be revealed by God. The *ly* *duly elected by the Church* stands by way of a condition, or logical requisite, not of a revealed truth. As, in a like case, in this thesis: *Every man dying in the grace and friendship of God is saved*; or in the contrary one: *Every man dying in sin is condemned.* Each of these theses is to be believed as *of faith*; and yet it need not therefore be said that the whole universal pronouncement was revealed by God, in order that it be believed as *of faith*: for the *ly* *dying in grace*, *dying in sin*, is placed by way of a logical condition, so that the proposition may come out certain, and to be believed as *of faith*. And, to use Muratori's own example, this proposition: *In every duly consecrated host there is really the body of Christ*, is a proposition to be believed as *of faith*; and yet the *ly* *duly consecrated* is in that proposition by way of a condition; not because that proposition is thereby rendered conditional, but because that condition is imbibed and, as the Jurists say, is present from the nature of the thing, and is expressed in the manner in which it is present, in such wise that that proposition is simple and absolute, and simply and absolutely certain as *of faith*. That which is certain of these and similar theses is also certain in the other, about which we dispute; without there being necessary a new and peculiar revelation that that man was duly elected, in order that that proposition come out certain as *of faith*: just as in the other propositions a peculiar revelation is not necessary, that that man died in grace, or in sin, or that this or that host is duly consecrated, in order that the aforesaid absolute, general, and simple propositions — which, upon the imbibed supposition, are founded on the infallible veracity — come out certain as *of faith*. |
| 32 Retorqueat fortasse Muratorius. Licet prædictæ propositiones ad exemplum adductæ sint de fide, non tamen ex ipsis descendere possumus ad particulares conclusiones, nempe: *Isthæc numero hostia realiter continet corpus Christi: Hic homo salvus factus est, aut decessit in gratia*, quia infallibilitas harum propositionum dependet à peculiari revelatione, quod illa numero hostia sit rite consecrata: quod ille homo decesserit in gratia. Ergo similiter, ut ex illa generali propositione descendamus ad particularem, nempe: *Hic, aut iste est verus Pontifex*, opus erit peculiari revelatione, quod hic numero Pontifex sit legitime electus. Non recte retorquet; siquidem jam non sumus in particulari propositione; sed in generali, quam ipse negat esse de fide. Sed ei gratulor argumentum, ut securius reconveniam. | | 32 Muratori may perhaps retort. Although the aforesaid propositions adduced as examples are *of faith*, nevertheless we cannot from them descend to particular conclusions, namely: *This individual host really contains the body of Christ: This man has been saved, or died in grace*, because the infallibility of these propositions depends upon a peculiar revelation that that individual host is duly consecrated, that that man died in grace. Therefore, similarly, in order that from that general proposition we may descend to the particular, namely: *This man, or that man, is the true Pontiff*, there will be need of a peculiar revelation that this individual Pontiff was legitimately elected. He does not retort rightly; since we are now not in the particular proposition, but in the general one, which he denies to be *of faith*. But I congratulate him on the argument, that I may the more securely bring a counter-charge. |
| 33 Ipse ut illidat argumentum, quod deducitur ex eo, quod, ut credamus divinitus certissima esse Pontificis circa fidem decreta, necesse est, quod etiam ut certissimum divinitus habeamus hunc, vel illum esse verum Romanum Pontificem, ut supra expendimus; (statim ad id, quod ibi reponit, respondebimus) sic illud solvit: *Ut enim dogmata per fidem amplectamur, certaque credamus, sufficit, ut facta illa, & legitima electio Romani Pontificis nobis per moralem evidentiam innotescat.* Optime, nec renuimus; licet quoad intentum in illa enuntiatione: *Hic Pontifex est rite electus*, aliquid amplius consideremus, quam moralem evidentiam. Sed ex suppositione: Cur ita? Quia ad eliciendam conclusionem de fide certam, sufficit quod una propositio sit certa de fide, altera evidentiæ aut naturali, aut morali innitatur. Sic Theologi. | | 33 He himself, in order to dash the argument which is deduced from this, that, in order that we may believe the decrees of the Pontiff concerning the faith to be divinely most certain, it is necessary that we also hold as most certain by divine warrant that this man or that is the true Roman Pontiff, as we weighed above; (we shall respond presently to what he replies there) he resolves it thus: *For in order that we may embrace dogmas by faith, and believe them as certain, it suffices that those facts, and the legitimate election of the Roman Pontiff, become known to us through moral evidence.* Very well, nor do we refuse it; although, as regards the point intended in that enunciation, *This Pontiff has been duly elected*, we consider something more than moral evidence. But upon a supposition: Why so? Because, for eliciting a conclusion certain of faith, it suffices that one proposition be certain of faith, and that the other rest upon evidence either natural or moral. So the Theologians. |
| 34 En certissima ratio, cur illud effatum: *Quod Ecclesia cum Romano Pontifice recepit, & statuit tamquam revelatum à Deo, id certum nobis esse debet per divinam fidem.* Rationem reddit, quia scilicet ex duplici propositione, maiori scilicet de fide, qualis est: *Quidquid à Deo revelatum est, verum, certumque est*, & minori moraliter evidenti, qualis est illa: *Sed per evidentiam nobis constat Ecclesiam, & Romanum Pontificem cum Ecclesia hoc, vel illud dogma recepisse, sive statuisse tamquam revelatum à Deo*, per necessariam, & infallibilem consequentiam deducitur de fide conclusio. | | 34 Behold the most certain reason for that dictum: *What the Church with the Roman Pontiff has received and established as revealed by God, that ought to be certain to us by divine faith.* He gives the reason, namely, because from a twofold proposition — the major, namely, of faith, such as: *Whatever has been revealed by God is true and certain*, and the minor morally evident, such as this: *But it is established for us through evidence that the Church, and the Roman Pontiff with the Church, has received or established this or that dogma as revealed by God* — a conclusion of faith is deduced by a necessary and infallible consequence. |
| 35 Jam ex ipso Muratorio elucet manifestissima discriminis ratio inter illas propositiones ab eo adductas ad exemplum, & nostram propositionem. Nostra sic habet: *Omnis ab Ecclesia rite electus, & receptus, est verus Pontifex.* En maior certa de fide, quia præsupposita legitima electione, innititur veritati divinæ, ut supra probavimus. *Sed hic numero Pontifex fuit rite electus*: en alia propositio moraliter evidens; quia ea electio fuit facta ab Ecclesia modo ab ea determinato, & habita ut legitima, ita ut ex ea Pontificem electum ut verum Pontificem recognosceret. Sequitur necessario credendum, hunc numero Pontificem esse verum Pontificem. At verò in illa enuntiatione: *In hac numero hostia consecrata est realiter corpus Christi*, licet habeamus propositionem de fide certam, nempe: *In omni hostia rite consecrata est realiter corpus Christi*, non habemus tamen aliam moraliter evidentem, quia nullam evidentiam moralem habet illa altera propositio: *Hæc numero hostia est rite consecrata.* Cur hæc evidentia in una, & non in altera? Quia prima innititur publicæ auctoritati Ecclesiæ, & facto ab ipsa Ecclesia approbato, ac formæ ab ea constitutæ: secunda vero innititur facto privato hominis, cujus nullam moralem evidentiam habere possumus. Similiter in aliis propositionibus à Muratorio adductis discurrendum est. Alia ratio esse potest; quia ex illa electione rite non facta multa magni præjudicii incommoda in populo fideli exoriri possent, quæ vitare ad providentiam invisibilis capitis proculdubio spectat. Secus esset in factis particularibus, ex quibus Christiana Respublica nullum attendibile pati potest detrimentum. | | 35 Now from Muratori himself there shines forth a most manifest reason for the difference between those propositions adduced by him as examples and our proposition. Ours runs thus: *Everyone duly elected and received by the Church is a true Pontiff.* Behold a major certain of faith, since, the legitimate election being presupposed, it rests upon divine truth, as we proved above. *But this individual Pontiff was duly elected*: behold another proposition morally evident; because that election was made by the Church in the manner determined by her, and was held as legitimate, so that from it she recognized the elected Pontiff as true Pontiff. It follows that it must necessarily be believed that this individual Pontiff is a true Pontiff. But indeed, in that enunciation, *In this individual consecrated host there is really the body of Christ*, although we have a proposition certain of faith, namely, *In every duly consecrated host there is really the body of Christ*, yet we do not have another proposition morally evident, because that other proposition, *This individual host has been duly consecrated*, has no moral evidence. Why is there this evidence in the one and not in the other? Because the first rests upon the public authority of the Church, and upon a fact approved by the Church herself, and upon a form constituted by her; but the second rests upon the private act of a man, of which we can have no moral evidence. One must reason similarly concerning the other propositions adduced by Muratori. There can be another reason; because from that election not duly made many inconveniences of great prejudice could arise among the faithful people, which to avert doubtless pertains to the providence of the invisible Head. It would be otherwise in particular facts, from which the Christian Commonwealth can suffer no considerable detriment. |
| 36 Nunc redeamus ad propositionem Muratorii. Sic habet: *Quidquid per evidentiam nobis constat ab Ecclesia, & à Romano Pontifice cum Ecclesia receptum, aut statutum fuisse tamquam revelatum à Deo, certum quoque nobis per fidem divinam futurum est.* Obiter hic noto illud *à Romano Pontifice cum Ecclesia*, quod aliquoties legitur repetitum; quasi in ea videatur esse sententia, Pontificem per se solum nihil posse definire, sed simul cum Ecclesia. An hoc judicium, si forte illud concipit, verum sit, aliis relinquo cogitandum. Quo posito, inquiro à Muratorio: An *integrum effatum illud ad fidem spectet, hoc est, à Deo revelatum sit?* Si affirmative respondeat, necesse erit, quod de fide sit per peculiarem revelationem, eum sic definientem esse Romanum Pontificem, & id, quod statuit, à Deo revelatum fuisse. Si vero negative resolvat, idem reponimus circa illam propositionem: *Omnis homo rite electus ab Ecclesia in successorem Petri est verus Romanus Pontifex.* Deinde noto illa verba *tamquam à Deo revelatum.* Nunquam me legisse memini, Ecclesiam, cùm aliquid proponit de fide credendum, explicare, se sic statuere tamquam à Deo revelatum, vel ad id peculiarem (quam exigit Muratorius) revelationem accepisse. Credimus profecto revelatum propter infallibilem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam, qui nec falli, nec fallere potest. Dicat nunc Muratorius, ex quibus principiis deducat illam infallibilem Spiritus Sancti assistentiam? Et quæcumque assignaverit, eadem reponemus ad eamdem probandam in Summi Pontificis electione, totiusque Ecclesiæ acceptatione. | | 36 Now let us return to Muratori's proposition. It runs thus: *Whatever is established for us through evidence to have been received or determined by the Church, and by the Roman Pontiff with the Church, as revealed by God, will also be certain to us by divine faith.* In passing I note here that phrase, *by the Roman Pontiff with the Church*, which is read repeated several times; as though there seems to lie in it the opinion that the Pontiff by himself alone can define nothing, but only together with the Church. Whether this judgment, if perchance he conceives it, be true, I leave for others to consider. This being laid down, I inquire of Muratori: Does *that entire dictum pertain to faith, that is, has it been revealed by God?* If he answers affirmatively, it will be necessary that it be of faith, by a peculiar revelation, that he who thus defines is the Roman Pontiff, and that what he determines has been revealed by God. But if he resolves negatively, we make the same reply concerning that proposition: *Every man duly elected by the Church as successor of Peter is the true Roman Pontiff.* Next I note those words, *as revealed by God.* I do not remember ever to have read that the Church, when she proposes something to be believed of faith, declares that she so determines it as revealed by God, or that she has received for it a peculiar revelation (such as Muratori demands). We believe indeed that it is revealed on account of the infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit, who can neither be deceived nor deceive. Let Muratori now say from what principles he deduces that infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit? And whatever he shall assign, we shall bring forward the same to prove the same assistance in the election of the Supreme Pontiff and the acceptance of the whole Church. |
| 37 Ut eam respuat, sic increpando quærit: *Ecquando enim aut in Scripturis sacris, aut per traditionem revelatum est ab Ecclesia eligi, aut eligendos esse Petri successores?* Quanta hic notare possemus! Electio est modus de jure naturali, & divino approbatus, & regulariter ab universa Ecclesia receptus. Innumera hujus veritatis exempla tam ex historia sacra, quam ex profana adducere facile esset. Sed ad rem. Ex Euangelio *Luc. 6. 13.* constat Christum Dominum duodecim ex discipulis suis elegisse in Apostolos: *Et cum dies factus esset, vocavit discipulos suos, & elegit duodecim ex ipsis, quos Apostolos nominavit*, ut sic nos edoceret ad eam dignitatem per electionem esse procedendum; Christus enim Dominus quidquid fecit, ad nostram fecit doctrinam. Similiter illud probat *Joan. 6. 71. Nonne ergo vos duodecim elegi?* Et 13. 18. *Ego scio quos elegerim.* Et 15. 16. & 19. *Sed ego elegi vos.* Et *Actor. 1. 2. Per Spiritum Sanctum quos elegit.* Hujus electionis argumentum est infallibile, quod refertur in *Actib. Apostol. cap. 1. à vers. 15. Exurgens Petrus in medio fratrum dixit: (erat autem turba hominum simul fere centum viginti) Viri fratres oportet impleri scripturam.... Oportet ergo ex his viris, qui nobiscum sunt congregati in omni tempore, quo intravit, & exivit inter nos Dominus JESUS..... testem resurrectionis ejus nobiscum fieri unum ex istis. Et statuerunt duos, Joseph, qui vocabatur Barsabas, qui cognominatus est Justus, & Mathiam. Et orantes dixerunt: Tu Domine, qui corda nosti hominum, ostende quem elegeris ex his duobus unum &c.* Quis hic dubitare poterit, cognitam, & approbatam, inspiratamque fuisse electionem ut medium aptius ad illius muneris, Apostolatus scilicet, assumptionem? Quis negare ausus erit, Petrum, ducente Spiritu Sancto, sic congregationi illi discipulorum eum proposuisse modum? Quis non temere asserere quibit, Mathiam à Deo acceptum non fuisse, nec illico Apostolicam dignitatem, nullo alio confirmante, suscepisse? | | 37 In order to reject it, he asks thus, chidingly: *For when, either in the sacred Scriptures or through tradition, has it been revealed that the successors of Peter are elected, or are to be elected, by the Church?* How much we could note here! Election is a mode approved by natural and divine law, and regularly received by the universal Church. It would be easy to adduce innumerable examples of this truth, from sacred as well as profane history. But to the point. From the Gospel, *Luc. 6. 13.*, it is established that Christ the Lord chose twelve from among His disciples as Apostles: *And when day was come, he called unto him his disciples, and he chose twelve of them, whom he named Apostles*, that thus He might teach us that one must proceed to that dignity through election; for whatever Christ the Lord did, He did for our instruction. Likewise this is proved by *Joan. 6. 71. Have not I chosen you twelve?* And 13. 18. *I know whom I have chosen.* And 15. 16. & 19. *But I have chosen you.* And *Actor. 1. 2. whom he had chosen through the Holy Ghost.* An infallible argument for this election is what is related in *Actib. Apostol. cap. 1. à vers. 15. Peter rising up in the midst of the brethren, said: (now the multitude of persons together was about an hundred and twenty) Men, brethren, the scripture must needs be fulfilled.... Wherefore, of these men who have companied with us all the time that the Lord JESUS came in and went out among us..... one of these must be made a witness with us of his resurrection. And they appointed two, Joseph, who was called Barsabas, who was surnamed Justus, and Matthias. And praying, they said: Thou, Lord, who knowest the hearts of men, shew whom thou hast chosen of these two, one &c.* Who could here doubt that election was known, approved, and inspired as the fitter means for the assumption of that office, namely, the Apostolate? Who will dare to deny that Peter, with the Holy Spirit leading, thus proposed that mode to that congregation of disciples? Who will be able, without rashness, to assert that Matthias was not accepted by God, and did not straightway receive the Apostolic dignity, with no other confirming it? |
| 38 Idem patet ex alio sacræ Scripturæ loco *Actor. 6. 5. Convocantes autem duodecim multitudinem discipulorum.... Considerate ergo fratres, viros ex vobis boni testimonii septem, plenos Spiritu Sancto, & sapientia, quos constituamus super hoc opus..... Et placuit sermo coram omni multitudine, & elegerunt Stephanum &c.* Et *Actor. 15. 22. Tunc placuit Apostolis, & senioribus cum omni Ecclesia eligere viros ex eis.* Et 25. *Placuit nobis collectis in unum eligere viros &c.* En habemus in Sctipturis clarum, & expressum electionis modum; ad eum habemus Apostolos edoctos, & id fecisse inspiratione divina, nefas erit dubitare. Ergo insipienter nimis inquirit Muratorius, ubi legitur in Scripturis hunc electionis modum fuisse præscriptum? Non fuit expresse præscriptus quoad electionem Pontificis, sed fuit præscriptus ad electionem Apostolatus, quod idem est. Quoad Pontificis autem electionem expressam habemus Ecclesiæ traditionem, in ipsa Scriptura clarissime fundatam. | | 38 The same is evident from another place of sacred Scripture, *Actor. 6. 5. Then the twelve, calling together the multitude of the disciples.... Wherefore, brethren, look ye out among you seven men of good report, full of the Holy Ghost and wisdom, whom we may appoint over this business..... And the saying was pleasing before all the multitude, and they chose Stephen &c.* And *Actor. 15. 22. Then it pleased the Apostles and ancients, with the whole Church, to choose men of their own company.* And 25. *It hath pleased us, being assembled together, to choose out men &c.* Behold, we have in the Scriptures a clear and express mode of election; we have the Apostles instructed in it, and that they did this by divine inspiration it will be impious to doubt. Therefore Muratori inquires too foolishly, where is it read in the Scriptures that this mode of election was prescribed? It was not expressly prescribed as regards the election of the Pontiff, but it was prescribed for election to the Apostolate, which is the same thing. As regards the election of the Pontiff, however, we have an express tradition of the Church, most clearly founded in Scripture itself. |
| 39 Quid est traditio? Vel est Divina, vel Apostolica, vel Ecclesiastica. Divina ea est, quam Apostoli vel ex ore Christi verbo tenus, vel à Spiritu Sancto edocti acceperunt. Apostolica (quæ cum divina multoties confunditur) vocatur ea, quæ ab Apostolis instituta est cum assistentia Spiritus Sancti. En habemus electionis modum ab Apostolis institutum, nec in dubium revocare possumus, an id Spiritus Sancti assistentia institutum fuerit. Ecclesiastica traditio dicitur consuetudo quædam antiqua in Ecclesia introducta, semperque retenta. Hanc eligendi Pontificis consuetudinem ad mores pertinere, quis sanus ambigat? Quam habeat auctoritatem hæc diuturna Ecclesiæ consuetudo, nos edocebit *Concil. Trid. sess. 4. de Canonicis Scripturis.* Sic aiunt PP. à Spiritu Sancto illuminati: *Sacrosancta Synodus traditiones tum ad fidem, tum ad mores pertinentes, tamquam vel ore tenus à Christo, vel à Spiritu Sancto dictatas, & continua successione in Ecclesia conservatas, pari pietatis affectu, ac reverentia suscipit, ac veneratur.* Quis autem, ni deliret, in dubium revocat hunc electionis morem ab Ecclesia ab initio susceptum, institutum, & continuatum? | | 39 What is tradition? It is either Divine, or Apostolic, or Ecclesiastical. Divine is that which the Apostles received either from the mouth of Christ by word, or taught by the Holy Spirit. Apostolic (which is many times confounded with the divine) is the name given to that which was instituted by the Apostles with the assistance of the Holy Spirit. Behold, we have the mode of election instituted by the Apostles, nor can we call into doubt whether it was instituted with the assistance of the Holy Spirit. Ecclesiastical tradition is the name for a certain ancient custom introduced in the Church and always retained. That this custom of electing the Pontiff pertains to morals, what sane man will doubt? What authority this long-standing custom of the Church possesses, the *Concil. Trid. sess. 4. de Canonicis Scripturis* will teach us. Thus say the Fathers, illumined by the Holy Spirit: *The sacrosanct Synod receives and venerates, with an equal affection of piety and reverence, the traditions pertaining both to faith and to morals, as having been dictated either by the mouth of Christ or by the Holy Spirit, and preserved by continuous succession in the Church.* Who, however, unless he be raving, calls into doubt that this manner of election was received, instituted, and continued by the Church from the beginning? |
| 40 Hunc electionis modum juri naturali, & quasi divino indispensabili omnes Theologi, & Canonistæ attribuunt, qui asserunt Papam non posse sibi eligere successorem, sic testante Cardinali Petra (7) {marg. (7) Tom. 5. ad Constit. 5. Clement. VI. n. 7.} per hæc verba: *Non solum secundum juris canonici regulas, sed prohibente jure divino, & naturali, vel, ut alii dicunt, jure naturali, & quasi divino indispensabili.* Huncque tamquam juri naturali, & divino conformiorem, & ad vitanda incommoda, quæ aliter contingere possent, opportuniorem suscepit, constituitque Ecclesia. Ideo secundus post Petrum electus Pontifex fuit Linus, post Linum Cletus, & post Cletum Clemens, idque divino consilio actum fuisse edocet *Ciaccon. in vita S. Lini. Et licet Cletus Clementem successorem sibi designasset, Clemens tamen, modestia usus, juri suo cessit: & Linus Pontifex Romanus electus fuit.
