Clemens igitur secundus fuit Petri ordinatione, non Ecclesiæ nominatione, Petri jure, non functione, & administratione, quæ Lino fuit tributa divino consilio, ne in posterorum exemplum transiret successores sibi eligendi & libertas, & providentia Ecclesiæ in constituendo sibi Pastore isto modo fuisset adempta.* | | 40 This mode of election all the Theologians and Canonists attribute to the natural and, as it were, indispensable divine law, who assert that the Pope cannot elect a successor for himself, as Cardinal Petra testifies (7) {marg. (7) Tom. 5. ad Constit. 5. Clement. VI. n. 7.} in these words: *Not only according to the rules of canon law, but by the prohibition of the divine and natural law, or, as others say, of the natural and, as it were, indispensable divine law.* And this mode, as more conformed to the natural and divine law, and more opportune for avoiding the inconveniences which could otherwise occur, the Church took up and established. Therefore the second Pontiff elected after Peter was Linus, after Linus Cletus, and after Cletus Clement; and that this was done by divine counsel *Ciaccon. in vita S. Lini* teaches. *And although Cletus had designated Clement as his successor, yet Clement, exercising modesty, yielded his right: and Linus was elected Roman Pontiff. Clement therefore was second by Peter's ordination, not by the Church's nomination; by Peter's right, not by function and administration, which was attributed to Linus by divine counsel, lest both the liberty of choosing successors for oneself should pass into an example for those to come, and the providence of the Church in constituting a Pastor for herself should in that manner have been taken away.* |
| 41 Quamquam autem *in Ecclesiasticis monumentis non satis exprimatur, quomodo S. Clementi suæ designationi à S. Petro factæ cedenti Linus successerit, num ex Sancti Petri destinatione, vel miraculoso aliquo signo, ut divina providentia primævis illis temporibus assolebat ad firmandam nascentem fidem*, ut notat *Acarona de* Cardinalib. tract. 1. cap. 10. n. 3. *verumtamen probabilissimum etiam est, quod à Clero Romano fuerit Linus in Cathedra Petri locatus. Pro comperto enim est, quod à primordiis Ecclesiæ Summi Pontificis electio apud Presbyteros, & Diaconos urbis Romæ erat.... Merito quidem Summi-Pontificis electio usque à principio Romano Clero affixa fuit; etenim cum Papa, universalis Ecclesiæ Monarcha, simul etiam sit particularis Episcopus Romæ, summo jure convenit, quod à Clericis ipsius Ecclesiæ eligatur.... Juxta nunc dictum modum per quatuor circiter secula summa in Ecclesia Romana viguit libertas in eligendo à Presbyteris, & Diaconibus Summo Pontifice, suffragante populo.* Verba sunt Petræ supra citati. | | 41 But although *in the Ecclesiastical monuments it is not sufficiently expressed how Linus succeeded in place of S. Clement, who yielded the designation made of him by S. Peter — whether by Saint Peter's appointment, or by some miraculous sign, as divine providence was wont in those earliest times for the confirming of the nascent faith*, as *Acarona de* Cardinalib. tract. 1. cap. 10. n. 3. notes, *nevertheless it is also most probable that Linus was placed in the Chair of Peter by the Roman Clergy. For it is established as certain that from the beginnings of the Church the election of the Supreme Pontiff lay with the Priests and Deacons of the city of Rome.... And deservedly indeed was the election of the Supreme Pontiff from the very beginning attached to the Roman Clergy; for since the Pope, Monarch of the universal Church, is at the same time also the particular Bishop of Rome, it is fitting by the highest right that he be elected by the Clerics of his own Church.... According to the mode just described, for about four centuries the highest liberty flourished in the Roman Church in the electing of the Supreme Pontiff by the Priests and Deacons, with the people giving their suffrage.* These are the words of Petra, cited above. |
| 42 Sed maioris ponderis sunt, & auctoritatis tum D. Augustinus, tum S. P. Anacletus, Clementis successor. Primus *ad Hebræos 5.* sic inquiens: *Cooperatores Dei sumus, & ideo semper non est expectanda vocatio divinæ sententiæ, sed illum eligit Deus, quem eligit charitas Ecclesiæ, & communis consensus eligentium.* Quid clarius? Secundus verò sic ait: *Licet Summorum Pontificum electionem sibi Dominus reservaverit, eorumdem tamen electionem bonis Sacerdotibus, & spiritualibus populis concessit.* Vel ut melius habetur in Ivone *p. 5. cap. 138.* aut in Gratiano *in cap. fin. dist. 70. Ejectionem Summorum Sacerdotum sibi Dominus reservavit, licet electionem eorum Sacerdotibus, & spiritualibus populis concessisset.* Quid expressius? | | 42 But of greater weight and authority are both St. Augustine and the holy Father Anacletus, successor of Clement. The first, *ad Hebræos 5.*, speaks thus: *We are God's coadjutors, and therefore the calling of the divine sentence is not always to be waited for; but God elects him whom the charity of the Church and the common consent of the electors elects.* What clearer? The second indeed says thus: *Although the Lord has reserved to Himself the election of the Supreme Pontiffs, yet He has granted the election of the same to good Priests and to spiritual peoples.* Or, as it is better had in Ivo, *p. 5. cap. 138.*, or in Gratian, *in cap. fin. dist. 70. The Lord has reserved to Himself the ejection of the Supreme Priests, although He had granted their election to Priests and to spiritual peoples.* What more express? |
| 43 Stat igitur à Deo datam fuisse hanc eligendi normam, atque in Ecclesia semper observatam; nusquam enim legimus aliter quam per electionem factam fuisse Vicarii Christi, & Successoris Petri nominationem. Licet enim Simmachus, Bonifacius III. Stephanus III. Adrianus I. Leo IV. Nicolaus II. Alexander III. Gregorius X. Clemens VI. Clemens VII. Paulus IV. Pius IV. Gregorius XV. aliqua circa modum electionis, prout tempora exposcebant, & experientia erudierat, constituerint; nullus tamen electionem abstulit, sed ei modum præscripsit: quod quidem facere jure poterant; cum enim Christus Dominus jus collativum Papalis dignitatis reliquerit in Ecclesia, nec certæ formæ, aut cetris personis affixum, consequens necessarium erat, quod ejusdem vicariæ potestatis administratores hunc modum pro temporum opportunitate præscriberent, ut ex *Domin. à SS. Trinit. Roccabert. Dominic. à S. Thom. Azorio, Paris. & Lavorio* ait citatus Petra. Præcipue cum hæc electio humano modo facienda esset, ac proinde ejus modus, ut canonice fieret, ad supremum in terris legislatorem, regulamque vivam in rebus morum, ac tanti ponderis spectaret. Neque id novum est; nam licet de fide sit Deum esse colendum, modus tamen hujus cultus ab Ecclesia determinatur, idemque in multis aliis exemplis demonstrari poterat. | | 43 It stands, therefore, that this norm of electing was given by God, and always observed in the Church; for nowhere do we read that the nomination of the Vicar of Christ and Successor of Peter was made otherwise than through election. For although Symmachus, Boniface III, Stephen III, Adrian I, Leo IV, Nicholas II, Alexander III, Gregory X, Clement VI, Clement VII, Paul IV, Pius IV, and Gregory XV established certain things concerning the mode of election, as the times demanded and experience had taught; yet none took election away, but prescribed a mode for it: which indeed they could do by right; for since Christ the Lord left the collative right of the Papal dignity in the Church, affixed neither to a certain form nor to certain persons, it was a necessary consequence that the administrators of that same vicarious power should prescribe this mode according to the opportunity of the times, as the cited Petra says, from *Domin. à SS. Trinit. Roccabert. Dominic. à S. Thom. Azorio, Paris. & Lavorio.* Especially since this election was to be made in a human manner, and hence its mode, that it might be done canonically, pertained to the supreme legislator on earth, the living rule in matters of morals and of such great weight. Nor is this new; for although it is of faith that God is to be worshipped, yet the mode of this worship is determined by the Church, and the same could be demonstrated in many other examples. |
| 44 Adeo hæc consuetudo in Ecclesia viguit per traditionem accepta, ut etiam tempore, quo Imperatores, & Reges aliquam in Pontificum assumptione sibi sine jure potestatem arrogarunt, semper Pontifices per electionem ad Petri Cathedram evecti sunt. Sic Theodorus Augustus constituit, *illum solum in Sede Apostolica permansurum, quem ex numero Clericorum nova ordinatione divinum judicium, & universitatis consensus elegerit.* (8) {marg. (8) Cap. Si duo dist. 78.} Sic in diro schismate inter Damasum, & Ursicinum Imperator Valentinianus pro Damaso stetit rite electo. (9) {marg. (9) Ex Socrat. Sosom. & Theodoret. Baron. ad an. Christ. 367. Ciaccon. in vita S. Bonifacii.} Sic Odoacer Rex, licet per summam injuriam sibi assumeret, quod sua non præcedente consultatione ad Pontificis electionem non procederetur, attamen nullatenus dubitavit, eam esse faciendam, & solum prius consuli voluit eo prætextu, scilicet, *ne per occasionem seditionis status civitatis vocetur in dubium*, & ut in electione Ecclesiæ concordia servetur. (10) {marg. (10) Cap. Bene dist. 96.} Sic in schismate inter Simmachum, & Laurentium, cum ex pacto convento adiretur arbitrer Theodoricus Rex, ipse, quantumvis Arianus, ut refert Anastasius Bibliothecarius, hoc æquissimum protulit judicium: *Ut qui primo ordinatus fuisset, vel ubi pars maxima cognosceretur, ipse sederit in Sede Apostolica.* Unde obtinuit Simmachus, qui à maiori parte fuerat electus. Sic Theodoricus ipse per summum nefas sibi arrogare præsumpsit Summi Pontificis electionem, & de facto in Apostolicam Sedem intrusit Felicem, qui postea fuit dictus IV. sed Felix à Clero Romano fuit rejectus, nec antea pro vero Pontifice habitus, nisi postquam Clerus Romanus in eum consensit, & sua electione confirmavit. (11) {marg. (11) Bzovius de Roman. Pontif. cap. 2.} Idem contigit in Silverio. (12) {marg. (12) Anastasius in vita Silverii.} Cur defatigabor? Nullum ab origine ad hæc usque tempora assignabitur Pontifex, qui aut per inspirationem, aut per electionem Petri Cathedram non conscenderet. | | 44 So greatly did this custom, received through tradition, flourish in the Church, that even in the time when Emperors and Kings arrogated to themselves without right some power in the elevation of the Pontiffs, the Pontiffs were always raised to the Chair of Peter through election. Thus Theodorus Augustus decreed *that he alone should remain in the Apostolic See whom, from the number of the Clerics, by a new ordination, the divine judgment and the consent of the whole body shall have elected.* (8) {marg. (8) Cap. Si duo dist. 78.} Thus in the dire schism between Damasus and Ursicinus the Emperor Valentinian stood for Damasus, duly elected. (9) {marg. (9) Ex Socrat. Sosom. & Theodoret. Baron. ad an. Christ. 367. Ciaccon. in vita S. Bonifacii.} Thus King Odoacer, although by supreme injustice he took upon himself that no one should proceed to the election of the Pontiff without his prior consultation, nevertheless in no way doubted that it was to be made [by election], and only wished to be consulted first, on this pretext, namely, *lest by occasion of sedition the state of the city be called into doubt*, and that concord be preserved in the election of the Church. (10) {marg. (10) Cap. Bene dist. 96.} Thus in the schism between Symmachus and Laurentius, when by agreed compact King Theodoric was approached as arbiter, he himself, however much an Arian, as Anastasius the Librarian relates, delivered this most equitable judgment: *That he who was first ordained, or in whom the greatest part was recognized, should himself sit in the Apostolic See.* Whence Symmachus prevailed, who had been elected by the greater part. Thus Theodoric himself, by supreme wickedness, presumed to arrogate to himself the election of the Supreme Pontiff, and in fact intruded into the Apostolic See Felix, who afterwards was called the Fourth; but Felix was rejected by the Roman Clergy, nor was he held for a true Pontiff until after the Roman Clergy consented to him and confirmed him by its election. (11) {marg. (11) Bzovius de Roman. Pontif. cap. 2.} The same happened in the case of Silverius. (12) {marg. (12) Anastasius in vita Silverii.} Why should I weary myself? From the beginning down to these very times, no Pontiff will be pointed out who did not ascend the Chair of Peter either through inspiration or through election. |
| 45 Habemus igitur indisputabilem per tot sæcula consuetudinem, & ecclesiasticam traditionem, ac per consequens in præceps agitur istud Muratorii ineptissimum argumentum. Ausi sunt sæculares Principes per temporum vicissitudines in hac Pontificum creatione inserere manum: multæ contentiones, multi ad Pontificiam dignitatem ambitus, multa schismata Ecclesiam perturbarunt. Sed hæ sunt tempestates, hi tumultuosi fluctus, hæ commotæ procellæ, quæ naviculam in medio mari, & discipulos obruere videbatur; Dominus autem JESUS, qui dormire videbatur, ut deficientium animorum fidem experiretur, tandem tandem omnem sedavit tempestatem. Sic in omnibus tribulationum angustiis Ecclesia experta semper fuit. Oportere hæreses esse, ait Apostolus, ut veritas splendidior elucescat. Oportuit & schismata intercessisse, ut illustrior fieret in certamine triumphus. Oportuit, quod Ecclesia tribulationes tantas passa fuerit, ut fidei firmitas probaretur. Oportuit, ut Lucifero, & Angelis ejus isti in Ecclesiam seditionum incursus permitterentur, ut firmum, invariabileque agnoscatur divinum effatum, quod *portæ inferi non prævalebunt adversus eam.* Quamobrem necessarium fuit, juxta indefectibilis providentiæ, & Spiritus Sancti assistentiæ œconomiam, ut quibus Ecclesiasticæ navis puppis regenda traditur, prout surgentes Euri poposcerint, divino afflante Numine, constitutiones ederent opportunas, ut illa Pontificum electio securior, atque totius controversiæ immunior evaderet, & *ut non caro, & sanguis, aut humana sapientia, vel privatæ utilitatis calculus aliquod in ea habeat momentum, sed Electorum corda, ab omnibus mundanis affectibus vacua, Spiritus Sancti gratia dirigantur.* (13) {marg. (13) Cardin. Petra ubi sup.} | | 45 We have, therefore, an indisputable custom through so many centuries, and an ecclesiastical tradition, and consequently that most inept argument of Muratori is driven headlong. Secular Princes have dared, through the vicissitudes of the times, to insert their hand into this creation of the Pontiffs: many contentions, many ambitious strivings for the Pontifical dignity, many schisms have disturbed the Church. But these are the tempests, these the tumultuous waves, these the stirred-up storms, which seemed to overwhelm the little ship in the midst of the sea, and the disciples; but the Lord JESUS, who seemed to sleep, that He might try the faith of failing souls, at last, at last stilled every tempest. So in all the straits of tribulations has the Church ever found by experience. There must be heresies, says the Apostle, that the truth may shine forth more splendidly. It was needful also that schisms should intervene, that the triumph in the contest might become more illustrious. It was needful that the Church should have suffered such great tribulations, that the firmness of her faith might be proved. It was needful that these incursions of seditions against the Church should be permitted to Lucifer and his Angels, that the divine dictum might be acknowledged firm and invariable, that *the gates of hell shall not prevail against her.* Wherefore it was necessary, according to the economy of indefectible providence and of the assistance of the Holy Spirit, that those to whom the stern of the Ecclesiastical ship is entrusted to be steered should, as the rising East-winds demanded, with the divine Godhead breathing upon them, publish opportune constitutions, that that election of the Pontiffs might come forth more secure and more immune from all controversy, and *that not flesh and blood, or human wisdom, or the calculation of private advantage should have any weight in it, but that the hearts of the Electors, void of all worldly affections, may be directed by the grace of the Holy Spirit.* (13) {marg. (13) Cardin. Petra ubi sup.} |
| 46 Hucusque bono animo passus fui illam moralem evidentiam, quam Muratorius fatetur, ut etiam, ea data, eum revincerem. Nunc eum percontarer, si possem, in quibus ea moralis evidentia principiis fundata judicetur? In nullis aliis, nisi in ea publica Ecclesiæ auctoritate sic rite eligentis, sic electum pro vero Pontifice habentis, sic omnia profunde rimantis, ut electio sine exceptione aliqua, secundum Canonum sacrorum præscripta, canonica consistat, & incontroversa permaneat. Credo nulla alia esse, nec ipse alia assignat moralis certitudinis principia. Ex ipsis ego infero argumenta ad fidei assensum sufficientia. Ecclesia, quæ in rebus morum errare non potest, omnia prudentissime rimatur, ut electio nulli exceptioni sit obnoxia. Observat Canonum infallibilis auctoritatis decreta, utpote quæ ab ipsa Ecclesia in universalibus Synodis statuta sunt. Ita eligit: *Quasi Electores puri sint ministri, & divinæ voluntatis instrumenta, ut qui personam tantummodo designant, cui immediate Christus suam potestatem conferat: Deus enim profecto est, qui vere Pontifices eligit, quique ipsis clavium potestatem tradit*, (14) {marg. (14) Ex Suar. Caietan. & Faguan. Petr. ubi sup.} ut supra diximus ex Augustino. Rite electum Deus etiam indubitabiliter eligit: *Quidquid per evidentiam* (asserit idem Muratorius) *nobis constat ab Ecclesia, & à Romano Pontifice cum Ecclesia receptum, aut statutum fuisse tamquam revelatum à Deo, certum quoque nobis per divinam fidem est futurum.* Ergò hic numero Pontifex fuit electus juxta formam à Deo revelatam, & statutam: ergo de fide est fuisse rite electum: ergo de fide est hunc Pontificem rite electum esse verum Pontificem. | | 46 Thus far I have with good grace allowed that moral evidence which Muratori acknowledges, so that, even granting it, I might refute him. Now I would question him, if I could: upon what principles is that moral evidence judged to be founded? Upon no others than upon that public authority of the Church so duly electing, so holding the one elected for a true Pontiff, so profoundly searching into all things, that the election, without any exception, according to the prescriptions of the sacred Canons, may stand canonical and remain uncontroverted. I believe there are no others, nor does he himself assign other principles of moral certitude. From these very principles I infer arguments sufficient for the assent of faith. The Church, which cannot err in matters of morals, most prudently searches into all things, that the election may be liable to no exception. She observes the decrees of the Canons of infallible authority, inasmuch as they were established by the Church herself in universal Synods. She elects thus: *As though the Electors were pure ministers and instruments of the divine will, as those who merely designate the person upon whom Christ immediately confers His power: for it is God indeed who truly elects the Pontiffs, and who delivers to them the power of the keys*, (14) {marg. (14) Ex Suar. Caietan. & Faguan. Petr. ubi sup.} as we said above from Augustine. Him who is duly elected God also indubitably elects: *Whatever* (asserts the same Muratori) *is established for us through evidence to have been received or determined by the Church, and by the Roman Pontiff with the Church, as revealed by God, will also be certain to us by divine faith.* Therefore this individual Pontiff was elected according to the form revealed and established by God: therefore it is of faith that he was duly elected: therefore it is of faith that this duly elected Pontiff is a true Pontiff. |
| 47 Si dicat, ut argumentum effugiat, eam electionis formam non fuisse ab Ecclesia statutam, necesse est, ut incidat in Scyllam cupiens vitare Carybdim: opus est, ut asserat Summos Pontifices cum Ecclesia in legitimis Synodis congregata in ea electionis forma constituenda, in re tanti momenti, tam sublimi, tam ardua, à qua Christianæ Reipublicæ amplitudo, decor, salus, & stabilitas dependet, & in qua vertitur totius Religionis utilitas, Spiritus Sancti assistentiam non habuisse. Si hæc proferre audeat, quid de eo sentiendum sit, dicent Orthodoxi: sin verò, ut sua decet, non renuat, nec etiam veritatem à Deo revelatam negare poterit. | | 47 If he should say, to escape the argument, that that form of election was not established by the Church, he must fall upon Scylla in his desire to avoid Charybdis: he would have to assert that the Supreme Pontiffs, together with the Church gathered in legitimate Synods, in constituting that form of election — in a matter of such moment, so sublime, so arduous, upon which the amplitude, beauty, safety, and stability of the Christian Commonwealth depend, and in which the benefit of the whole of Religion is at stake — did not have the assistance of the Holy Spirit. If he dares to utter these things, what is to be thought of him the Orthodox will say; but if, as becomes him, he does not refuse it, neither will he be able to deny a truth revealed by God. |
| 48 Tandem vel hunc, aut illum Pontificem ab Ecclesia juxta canonum formam electum, & recognitum fatetur à Deo accipere, accepisseve immediate suam potestatem, vel negat. Si negat: de nullo Summo Pontifice infallibiter asseri poterit fuisse ei immediate à Christo Domino traditam Ecclesiæ potestatem supremam, ac proinde nihil de fide stabilitum credere tenemur. Si verò, ut debet, fatetur, de fide tenendum est, omnem rite electum à Deo accepisse Pontificiam dignitatem, ac proinde hunc rite electum esse verum Romanum Pontificem. Cæteras omitto Muratorii ratiunculas; ex dictis enim facillime refelluntur. Id concludo, eum, ut ingeniorium in moderantiam argueret, in impietatem maximam declinasse. Hanc sententiam à tot insignibus Theologis propugnatam, Ecclesiæ, ac Summorum Pontificum infallibilitati, & auctoritati plus consulere nemo ambiget, plus ad pietatem christianam, atque ad amorem ad Apostolicam Sedem accedere nullus reluctabitur. Ego plus errare volo cum Suario, Bellarmino, Caietano, de Lugo, & cæteris Theologis, quam uno adscribi Muratorio. Ad pios orthodoxos scribo, ut cum Nanzianzeno dicam: *Hunc sermonem piis auribus commendabo; nam apud profanas animas nihil pulchri fidem invenire potest.* | | 48 Finally, he either confesses that this or that Pontiff, elected and recognized by the Church according to the form of the canons, receives, or has received, his power immediately from God, or he denies it. If he denies it: of no Supreme Pontiff will it be possible infallibly to assert that the supreme power of the Church was delivered to him immediately by Christ the Lord, and hence we are bound to believe nothing established of faith. But if, as he ought, he confesses it, it must be held of faith that everyone duly elected has received the Pontifical dignity from God, and hence that this man, duly elected, is the true Roman Pontiff. I omit Muratori's other petty reasons; for from what has been said they are most easily refuted. This I conclude: that he, in seeking to censure the immoderation of wits [?], has declined into the greatest impiety. That this opinion, defended by so many distinguished Theologians, is more considerate of the infallibility and authority of the Church and of the Supreme Pontiffs, no one will doubt; that it comes nearer to Christian piety and to love for the Apostolic See, no one will gainsay. I would rather err with Suárez, Bellarmine, Cajetan, de Lugo, and the other Theologians, than be enrolled with Muratori alone. I write for the pious orthodox, that I may say with Nazianzen: *This discourse I shall commend to pious ears; for among profane souls faith can find nothing beautiful.* |
| 49 *Pag. 156. & sequentibus* in eo eleganter allaborat, ut fallibilitatem Ecclesiæ demonstret, cum judicium ferat de mente, animoque illius, qui doctrinam quampiam noxiam tradiderit. In eo verificari quod ipse scripserit, suspicor vehementer: *Fieri* (inquit) *igitur potest, ut qui prava scripsit, aut pronuntiavit, recte animo senserit; nam & qui recte sentiunt, non recte semper loquuntur, aut disputant, & cor interdum aliud credit, cum os ex simulatione aliud pronuntiat; sicuti & qui à veritate aberrant, sæpe errorem suum verborum ambiguitate obtegere solent.* Siquidem ipse eloquii pulchritudine, cui maxime studuit, & in qua dicendi methodo fere semper illustriores sunt ejus generis scriptores, ut quæ rationum momentis persuadere non valent, Rhetorices ornamento, & apparatu consequantur, totus est, ut erronum animos, mentesque defendat, neque inter certissimos hæreticos computandos censeat eos, quorum doctrinas ut hæreticas Ecclesia damnaverit; quippe quæ nullo firmo judicio decernere potest auctorem alicujus erroris reum esse, de quo accusatur; fieri enim potuit, quod secus animo sentiret. Aptissimè quidem ad excusandos hæreticos, noxiorumque dogmatum scriptores. | | 49 *Pag. 156. & sequentibus* he elegantly labors to demonstrate the fallibility of the Church when she passes judgment on the mind and intention of one who has handed down some noxious doctrine. In him, I vehemently suspect, is verified what he himself has written: *It can happen* (he says) *therefore, that he who has written or pronounced depraved things has thought rightly in his mind; for both they who think rightly do not always speak or dispute rightly, and the heart sometimes believes one thing while the mouth, out of dissimulation, pronounces another; just as also they who stray from the truth are often wont to cover their error with ambiguity of words.* Since indeed he, by the beauty of eloquence, to which he has most greatly applied himself — and in which method of speaking the more illustrious writers of that kind almost always excel, so that what they cannot persuade by the weight of reasons they may attain by the ornament and apparatus of Rhetoric — is wholly bent on defending the souls and minds of those in error, nor does he think that those are to be reckoned among the most certain heretics whose doctrines the Church has condemned as heretical; since indeed she can decide by no firm judgment that the author of some error is guilty of that of which he is accused; for it could have happened that he thought otherwise in his mind. Most apt indeed for excusing heretics and the writers of noxious dogmas. |
| 50 Eos protegere studet, quia multoties utuntur (melius diceret abutuntur) verbis, phrasibus, vocibusque ad suam explicandam doctrinam, aliisque tradendam: quo in munere explendo quandoque accidit, ut sub orthodoxis locutionibus perversas celare sententias, & rursus orthodoxas hæreticis vocibus convestire soleant. Ad quid tamen hæ significationum translationes, hæ verborum metamorphoseos? Ad nihil, nisi ut legentes decipiant: his fraudibus introducuntur hæreses, his dolis errores sparguntur, eò periculosiores, quò deceptoriis ornamentis vestiuntur. Hoc novatorum commune est studium, falsam scilicet doctrinam aut veritatis aut virtutis, aut charitatis fuco adornare, atque errores suos variis artibus, ac pigmentis decoratos, dicendi facundia, styli lepore indutos exhibere, ut veniant ad nos in vestimentis ovium, cum tamen intrinsecus sint lupi rapaces. Quanto satius esset, ut his tropis, figuratisque locutionibus abstineret, ne his involucris fidei veritas obvoluta periclitetur. Non hisce subdolis ornamentis veritas eget, ut eam libentissime amplectamur: suamet pulchritudine perstat ut alliciat, ut devinciat, ut attrahat: quò nudior, puriorque ostenditur, eò formosior, chariorque adamatur, eò gratior, splendidiorque refulget. | | 50 He strives to protect them, because they many times use (he would better say abuse) words, phrases, and terms for explaining their own doctrine and handing it on to others: in the discharge of which office it sometimes happens that they are wont to hide perverse opinions under orthodox locutions, and again to clothe orthodox ones in heretical terms. But to what end these transfers of significations, these metamorphoses of words? To nothing, except to deceive readers: by these frauds heresies are introduced, by these wiles errors are scattered, the more dangerous the more they are clothed with deceptive ornaments. This is the common endeavor of innovators, namely, to adorn false doctrine with the cosmetic of truth, or of virtue, or of charity, and to exhibit their errors decorated with various arts and pigments, clad in fluency of speech and charm of style, that they may come to us in the clothing of sheep, while inwardly they are ravening wolves. How much better would it be that he abstain from these tropes and figurative locutions, lest the truth of faith, wrapped up in these coverings, be endangered. Truth has no need of these crafty ornaments, in order that we may most gladly embrace it: by its very own beauty it stands firm, that it may allure, that it may bind fast, that it may draw: the more naked and the more pure it is shown, the more beautiful and the more dear it is loved, the more gracious and the more resplendent it shines forth. |
| 51 Non opus esse, ait, Spiritus Sancti assistentia, ut hi errores detegantur, hique damnentur Novatores, nec confingenda esse miracula absque necessitate. Inepta ex utraque parte sententia! Has detegere Novatorum fraudes, hos rimari obstrusorum verborum sensus, has dæmonis astutias deludere, has fallendi artes attingere, maioris, difficiliorisque aleæ negotium est, quam ut capiat divinis inspirationibus destituta, humana, fallibilisque sapientia. Ut callidores sunt lethiferi draconis astus, ita necessariora sunt nunquam defectibilis divinæ providentiæ adjumenta. Quò plus densioribus nebulis condensatur aer, eò plus intensioribus radiis opus est, ut dissipentur. Neque, ut eam Spiritus Sancti assistentiam intelligamus, ad miracula recurrere necesse erit. Hic specialissimus divini Numinis in his rebus definiendis concursus non ad miraculosum ordinem, sed ad peculiarem Dei providentiam, promissionisque suæ implementum referendus est. Miserat ipse Spiritum Sanctum suum, sine quo nihil est innocuum, nihil est innoxium, ut Apostolorum, & Pontificum suorum mentes dirigeret, & illuminaret. Promiserat Petro, & in eo cæteris successoribus in rebus ad fidem pertinentibus semper adfuturum, ipsiusque Christi Domini munus est, tamquam invisibile caput, capiti visibili aditare in similibus arduis negotiis, atque dirigendæ Ecclesiæ suæ semper præesse. Pie credimus pie viventes, nec merito dubitare possumus, Deum in nostris actionibus, cum ad eum devote recurrimus, ut peccata vitemus, ut rectas semitas inoffenso pede curramus, nos sua occulta adjuvare providentia, Regio Vate sic dicente: *Angelis suis Deus mandavit de te, ut custodiant te in omnibus viis tuis.* Ideoque, nec inaniter, ab eo cum Ecclesia quotidie poscimus, ut dirigat corda, & corpora nostra, sensus, sermones, & actus nostros; neque tamen firma fides, aut spes justa nos deludit; fidelis enim est Deus invocantibus eum in veritate. Quis dixerit, nisi insanus, hæc nobis cœlitus præstita auxilia miraculosa esse, nosque temere, & absque necessitate miracula postulare? Non sunt hæc extra, aut contra ordinem naturæ, aut divinæ providentiæ, quibus omnia invisibiliter gubernat, ut ea miracula dicamus. Mirabilis est Deus, mirabilia testimonia sua facta sunt nimis; sed nec ideo omnia, quæ sua invisibili manu, sua invisibili directione Deus in nobis quotidie operatur, miracula dicenda sunt. Sic in peculiaribus operibus nostris piè credimus: sic in edocenda Ecclesia sua Deum semper assistere infallibiliter tenemus. | | 51 There is no need, he says, of the assistance of the Holy Spirit, that these errors be detected and these Innovators condemned, nor are miracles to be feigned without necessity. An inept opinion on either part! To detect these frauds of the Innovators, to search out these senses of obscure words, to elude these cunning devices of the demon, to lay hold of these arts of deceiving, is a business of greater and more difficult hazard than that human and fallible wisdom, destitute of divine inspirations, should compass it. As the wiles of the death-dealing dragon are the more crafty, so the aids of the never-failing divine providence are the more necessary. The more the air is thickened with denser mists, the more need there is of more intense rays, that they may be scattered. Nor, that we may understand that assistance of the Holy Spirit, will it be necessary to have recourse to miracles. This most special concurrence of the divine Godhead in defining these matters is to be referred not to the miraculous order, but to the peculiar providence of God and to the fulfillment of His promise. He Himself had sent His Holy Spirit, without whom nothing is harmless, nothing is guiltless, to direct and enlighten the minds of His Apostles and of His Pontiffs. He had promised Peter, and in him the rest of his successors, that He would always be present with them in matters pertaining to the faith; and it is the office of Christ the Lord Himself, as invisible head, to stand by the visible head in like arduous affairs, and always to preside over the direction of His Church. We piously believe, living piously, nor can we with reason doubt, that God, in our actions, when we devoutly have recourse to Him that we may avoid sins, that we may run the right paths with foot unstumbling, aids us by His hidden providence, the Royal Prophet speaking thus: *God hath given His angels charge over thee, that they keep thee in all thy ways.* And therefore, and not in vain, we daily ask of Him with the Church that He direct our hearts and our bodies, our senses, our words, and our acts; nor yet does firm faith or just hope delude us; for God is faithful to them that call upon Him in truth. Who, unless a madman, would say that these helps afforded us from heaven are miraculous, and that we rashly and without necessity demand miracles? These things are not outside or against the order of nature, or of divine providence, whereby He invisibly governs all things, that we should call them miracles. God is wonderful, His testimonies are made wonderful exceedingly; but not therefore are all the things which God daily works in us by His invisible hand, by His invisible direction, to be called miracles. So in our particular works we piously believe: so we hold that God always infallibly assists in the teaching of His Church. |
| 52 Fundamentum Muratorii illud est, quod in rebus facti Ecclesia errare potest, neque in his Deum suam promisisse assistentiam: ad idque adducit illud Innocentii III. Abbati S. Andreæ, (15) {marg. In cap. A' nobis 28. de Sentent. Excommunic.} nempe: *Quod judicium Dei veritati, quæ non fallit, nec fallitur, semper innititur.* (En qua regula dirigimur, ut Ecclesiæ Spiritus Sancti assistentiam in similibus negotiis, licet à facto dependentibus, non ambigamus) *Judicium autem Ecclesiæ, nonnumquam opinionem sequitur, quam & fallere sæpe contingit, & falli.* Quam tamen Innocentii authoritatem non adduceret, si distingueret inter facta, & facta, inter facta, quæ nullam habent cum jure connexionem, & facta cum juris quæstione connexa. In illis procedit adducta authoritas, proceduntque aliæ multæ. Loquitur Innocentius de factis particularibus, ut ait Roa, & cum eo Gonzales ad dictum textum, & communiter tam Theologi, quam Canonistæ. Sic explicatur Bellarminus, (16) {marg. Lib. 4. de Pontif. cap. 2.} sic alii in quæstionibus facti particularis. Secus est in factis connexis cum quæstionibus juris in materiis fidem concernentibus. Videantur Neusser, Viva, Ardeskin, (17) {marg. Neusser. in Prodrom. velitari p. 1. cap. 3. Viva in quæst. Prodroma n. 8. & late circa Jansenii propositiones. Ardeskin in sua Theolog. tripart. cap. 10. q. 3. 4. 5.} aliique plures, ne ab eis dicta repetere videar. Neque de hoc jam hodie dubitant veri orthodoxi; ambigit verò Muratorius toties reverentiam in Apostolicam Sedem protestatus. Sciat tamen cum Jansenistis convenire, quorum ea sententia semper fuit, ut suum Jansenium defenderent, & erroris immunem demonstrarent. Sciat, se Eusebii Episcopi Nicomediensis, & Theognini, & Theonæ, & Secundi vestigia premere, qui condemnationi Arii, & formulæ fidei in Nicæno propositæ subscribere restiterunt, eo quod Arii doctrina, cujus ipsi sequaces erant, pertineret ad quæstionem facti non veri, aut saltem veritatis incertæ, quæ à Concilio definiri non poterat, ut refert Maimbourgh in historia Arianismi, & habetur ex P. Viva *ubi supra*; quod Concilium illud non admisit, neque probavit: immo ipsi ea de causa suis sedibus tandem pulsi sunt, & ab Imperatore Justiniano graviter reprehensi, ut constat ex epistolis ipsius Justiniani in actis Concilii insertis, & quas refert Baronius ad annum Christi 329. Sciat, se quodammodo damnatione Innocentii XII. implicari, quippe qui damnavit Jansenii propositiones non solum in sensu obvio, quem ipsamet verba exhibent, sed etiam in sensu à Jansenio intento, ut Jansenistæ asserebant, quorum partibus Muratorius patrocinari videtur, etiam cum Jansenium non probare demonstrat, eamdem enim doctrinam, quam illi asserebant, ipse nunc probare contendit. | | 52 The foundation of Muratori is this: that in matters of fact the Church can err, and that in these God has not promised His assistance; and for this he adduces that saying of Innocent III to the Abbot of St. Andrew, (15) {marg. In cap. A' nobis 28. de Sentent. Excommunic.} namely: *That the judgment of God ever rests upon truth, which neither deceives nor is deceived.* (Behold by what rule we are directed, that we should not doubt the assistance of the Holy Spirit to the Church in like affairs, although dependent upon fact) *But the judgment of the Church sometimes follows opinion, which it often happens both to deceive and to be deceived.* Which authority of Innocent, however, he would not adduce, if he distinguished between facts and facts — between facts which have no connection with right, and facts connected with a question of right. In the former the authority adduced holds good, and many others hold good. Innocent speaks of particular facts, as Roa says, and with him Gonzalez on the said text, and commonly both Theologians and Canonists. Thus is Bellarmine explained, (16) {marg. Lib. 4. de Pontif. cap. 2.} thus others, in questions of particular fact. It is otherwise in facts connected with questions of right in matters concerning the faith. Let Neusser, Viva, Ardeskin be seen, (17) {marg. Neusser. in Prodrom. velitari p. 1. cap. 3. Viva in quæst. Prodroma n. 8. & late circa Jansenii propositiones. Ardeskin in sua Theolog. tripart. cap. 10. q. 3. 4. 5.} and many others besides, lest I seem to repeat what has been said by them. Nor do true orthodox men today any longer doubt of this; but Muratori doubts, he who has so often protested his reverence toward the Apostolic See. Let him know, however, that he agrees with the Jansenists, whose opinion this always was, that they might defend their Jansenius and demonstrate him free from error. Let him know that he presses the footsteps of Eusebius Bishop of Nicomedia, and of Theognis, and Theonas, and Secundus, who refused to subscribe to the condemnation of Arius and to the formula of faith proposed at Nicaea, on the ground that the doctrine of Arius, of which they themselves were followers, pertained to a question of fact not true, or at least of uncertain truth, which could not be defined by the Council, as Maimbourg relates in his history of Arianism, and as is had from Fr. Viva *ubi supra*; which that Council did not admit, nor approve: nay rather, for that cause they themselves were at last driven from their sees, and gravely rebuked by the Emperor Justinian, as appears from the letters of Justinian himself inserted in the acts of the Council, and which Baronius relates at the year of Christ 329. Let him know that he is in a certain manner implicated in the condemnation of Innocent XII, seeing that he condemned the propositions of Jansenius not only in the obvious sense which the very words present, but also in the sense intended by Jansenius, as the Jansenists asserted — whose party Muratori seems to patronize, even while he demonstrates that he does not approve Jansenius; for the same doctrine which they asserted, he himself now contends to approve. |
| 53 Et quidem, si hæc sententia omnino explodenda non veniret, garrientibus Novatoribus Ecclesiæ semper infensis, latissima sterneretur via ad damnatos hæreticos, ac proscriptas hæreses excusandas, atque in bono semper sensu accipiendas. Nulla enim est, aut fere nulla, quæ sub verborum larva, & ambiguitate sermonum non occludatur, ut in Arianismo, Monothelismo, Nestorianismo, Jansenismo, & pluribus aliis videre est. Nulla propemodum, quæ secum facti quæstiones non involvat. Arius ipse, Nestorius, Euthyches, Waldensis, Wiclef, Joannes Hus, Hieronymus Pragensis, Arnaldus, Baius, Jansenius, & alii similes suarum propositionum verum sensum protendebant. Hæc erat, esseque solet simulata veritatis species; hæc sunt locutionum pigmenta, quibus error convestitus sese frequenter exhibet, ut incautos decipiat, interimque grassetur: hi flores sunt, inter quos reconduntur viperæ, ut securius mordeant: hæc pocula aurea, in quibus noxia venena propinantur: si Muratorio placent, nobis omnino displicent. | | 53 And indeed, if this opinion were not to be altogether exploded, then, while the Innovators, ever hostile to the Church, chatter on, a most broad way would be paved for excusing condemned heretics and proscribed heresies, and for taking them always in a good sense. For there is none, or almost none, which is not shut up beneath the mask of words and the ambiguity of speech, as may be seen in Arianism, Monothelism, Nestorianism, Jansenism, and many others. There is almost none which does not involve questions of fact along with it. Arius himself, Nestorius, Eutyches, Waldensis, Wyclif, John Hus, Jerome of Prague, Arnold, Baius, Jansenius, and others like them held forth the true sense of their propositions. This was, and is wont to be, the counterfeit appearance of truth; these are the paints of speech, wherewith error, clothed about, frequently exhibits itself, that it may deceive the unwary and meanwhile stalk abroad: these are the flowers among which vipers lie hidden, that they may bite the more securely: these the golden cups in which noxious poisons are proffered: if they please Muratori, they altogether displease us. |
| 54 Nec prætermittere sinit animus exempla, quibus utitur Muratorius. Hæc profert: *Sic Origenem, Theodoretum, Abailardum, Gotescalchum, Abbatem Joachimum, aliosque in ipsis etiam generalibus Conciliis damnatos sacrilegium non fuit pro viribus tueri.* Origenes proculdubio de hæresi damnatus invenitur. Sed qui ejus defensores? Adeamus Theophilum Alexandriæ Episcopum, ipse pro nobis Muratorio respondebit: *Dicere nonnulli ausi sunt, Originem Ecclesiæ esse Doctorem. An hi obsecro ferendi sunt? Si Ecclesiæ Doctor est Origenes, magnos sibi spiritus sumunt Ariani, & Eunominiani, & pagani illi in Filium blasphemantes; hi similiter, atque illi impii necnon mortuorum resurrectionem deridentes.* Concludit Justinianus Imperator in epistola, quæ habetur *tom. 3. Conciliorum pag. 262.* *Ex his perspicuum est, eum, qui sanctos Patres sequitur, non dubitare, quin ii, qui cum Origene sentiunt, paganorum errorem, Arianorumque insaniam sequantur.* Videatur prædicta epistola. En quam censuram promereantur Origenis tutores: en quomodo à sacrilegio liberi reddantur. | | 54 Nor does my mind suffer me to pass over the examples which Muratori uses. He brings forward these: *Thus it was no sacrilege to defend to the utmost of one's power Origen, Theodoret, Abelard, Gottschalk, the Abbot Joachim, and others condemned even in general Councils themselves.* Origen without doubt is found condemned for heresy. But who are his defenders? Let us go to Theophilus, Bishop of Alexandria; he himself will answer Muratori for us: *Some have dared to say that Origen is a Doctor of the Church. Are these men, I pray, to be endured? If Origen is a Doctor of the Church, the Arians take great spirits to themselves, and the Eunomians, and those pagans who blaspheme against the Son; these likewise, and those impious men, and also they who deride the resurrection of the dead.* [?] The Emperor Justinian concludes, in the epistle which is found *tom. 3. Conciliorum pag. 262.* *From these things it is clear that he who follows the holy Fathers does not doubt that they who think with Origen follow the error of the pagans and the madness of the Arians.* Let the aforesaid epistle be seen. Behold what censure the guardians of Origen deserve: behold how they are rendered free from sacrilege. |
| 55 Non me latet Eusebium Cæsariensem Origenem excusasse. Sed quod de Eusebio Ecclesiæ judicium? Id explicabit Concilium Romanum I. Sic habet: *Item chronica Eusebii Cæsariensis, & ejusdem ecclesiasticæ historiæ libros: quamvis in primo narrationis suæ libro tepuerit, & postea in laudibus, atque excusatione Origenis schismatici unum conscripserit librum: propter rerum tamen singularem notitiam, quæ ad instructionem pertinent, usquequaque non dicimus renuendos.* Ecce Ecclesia nullatenus probat Origenis excusationes, immo reprobat, sed propter Eusebii singularem eruditionem ejus erga Origenem laudationes dissimulat, sed nullatenus approbat. | | 55 It does not escape me that Eusebius of Caesarea excused Origen. But what is the Church's judgment concerning Eusebius? The First Roman Council will explain it. Thus it has: *Likewise the chronicles of Eusebius of Caesarea, and the books of the same man's ecclesiastical history: although in the first book of his narration he grew lukewarm, and afterwards wrote one book in the praises and excuse of Origen the schismatic: yet, on account of his singular knowledge of things which pertain to instruction, we do not say that they are utterly to be refused.* Behold, the Church in no wise approves the excuses of Origen — nay, it reproves them — but on account of the singular erudition of Eusebius it dissembles his laudations of Origen, yet in no wise approves them. |
| 56 Etiam non ignoro Gregorium Nissenum, & Hieronymum multum Originem extollere. Aliud tamen est ejus ingenium, ac maximam eruditionem laudare, aliud eum ab erroribus damnatis excusare. Eum nec injuste damnatum aut Gregorius, aut Hieronymus asseruerunt, nec damnatarum sententiarum defensionem susceperunt. Laudarunt quoad alias doctrinas, quæ proscriptæ non inveniuntur; cujus veritatis testis est ipsum Concilium Romanum in his verbis: *Item Origenis nonnulla opuscula, quæ vir beatissimus Hieronymus non repudiat, legenda suscipimus. Reliqua autem omnia cum auctore suo dicimus esse renuenda.* | | 56 I am likewise not ignorant that Gregory of Nyssa and Jerome greatly extol Origen. Yet it is one thing to praise his genius and his very great erudition, another to excuse him from condemned errors. Neither Gregory nor Jerome asserted that he was unjustly condemned, nor did they undertake the defense of the condemned opinions. They praised him as regards his other doctrines, which are not found proscribed; of which truth the Roman Council itself is witness in these words: *Likewise certain small works of Origen, which the most blessed man Jerome does not repudiate, we receive to be read. But all the rest we say are to be refused together with their author.* |
| 57 Theodoretum qui tuentur, non ejus erroribus patrocinantur, sed eum non fuisse hæreticum contumacem recte asserunt. Eum plura erronea conscripsisse nullus Orthodoxus dubitat, sed formaliter hæreticum fuisse, nec ipsa sancta Sedes pronuntiavit. Fidem huic veritati certam præstat Pelagius II. *in epist. 5.* ad Eliam, & Episcopos Istriæ per hæc verba. Retulerat ipse varios Theodoreti errores, & postea sic scribit: *Quis hæc, fratres charissimi, plena omni impietate non videat? A' quibus tamen constat, quod se postmodum ipse correxit, qui in sancta Chalcedonensi Synodo Nestorium anathematizare consensit. Quis non videat quanta temeritate plenum sit, Theodoreti scripta superbiendo defendere, quæ eumdem ipsum constat, recta post profitendo damnasse? Dum vero ejus & personam recipimus, & ea, quæ dudum latuerant, prava scripta reprobamus, in nullo à sancta Synodi actione deviamus; quia sola ejus hæretica scripta respuentes, & cum Synodo Theodoretum profitentem recta veneramur. Alia verò scripta illius non solum recipimus, sed eis etiam contra adversarios utimur.* | | 57 They who defend Theodoret do not patronize his errors, but rightly assert that he was not a contumacious heretic. That he wrote many erroneous things no Orthodox man doubts; but that he was formally a heretic not even the holy See itself pronounced. Pelagius II affords sure credit to this truth *in epist. 5.* to Elias and the Bishops of Istria, in these words. He had recounted various errors of Theodoret, and afterwards writes thus: *Who, most dear brethren, does not see that these things are full of every impiety? From which, nevertheless, it is established that he afterwards corrected himself, who in the holy Synod of Chalcedon consented to anathematize Nestorius. Who does not see how full of rashness it is, in pride to defend the writings of Theodoret, which it is established that the same man himself, by afterwards professing right things, condemned? But while we both receive his person, and reprove those depraved writings which had long lain hidden, we deviate in nothing from the action of the holy Synod; because, rejecting only his heretical writings, we venerate, with the Synod, Theodoret professing right things. But his other writings we not only receive, but even use them against adversaries.* |
| 58 Circa Petrum Abailardum brevissime me expediam. Contra eum nominatim nullam invenio damnationem ab Ecclesia factam; unde disputationi quid vetat locum esse? Eum ut hæreticum exagitat Theobaldus Stampensis, alii ab hac calumnia liberant. Adversus Roscelinum Compendiensem: *Qui in Deo tres personas ab invicem separatas, ita ut aut Patrem, & Spiritum Sanctum esse incarnatum, & tres Deos dici posse, si usus admitteret,* asserebat, habitum fuit Concilium Suessionense, in quo tam prava doctrina damnata fuit. Quod attinet ad factum: Sicut ipse Lanfranco, & Anselmo eam affigebat calumniam, ut eos diceret suæ sectæ patronos, ita alii Petro Abailardo ejus discipulo eumdem imputabant errorem, quem ipse tamen expresse negavit, & veram fidem clarissime professus est, ut constat ex ejus *epistol. 21.* ad Gaufredum Parisiensem Episcopum. | | 58 Concerning Peter Abelard I shall despatch myself most briefly. Against him by name I find no condemnation made by the Church; whence, what forbids there to be place for disputation? Theobald of Étampes harries him as a heretic; others free him from this calumny. Against Roscelin of Compiègne, who asserted *three persons in God separated from one another, in such wise that both the Father and the Holy Spirit were incarnate, and that three Gods could be said, if usage admitted it,* [?] the Council of Soissons was held, in which so depraved a doctrine was condemned. As regards the fact: Just as he fastened that calumny upon Lanfranc and Anselm, so as to call them patrons of his sect, so others imputed the same error to Peter Abelard, his disciple; which he nevertheless expressly denied, and most clearly professed the true faith, as appears from his *epistol. 21.* to Geoffrey, Bishop of Paris. |
| 59 Gothescalcus à nullo orthodoxo defenditur, quippe qui damnatus fuerat in Concilio Moguntino II. & Carisiaco I. eo quod hæresim Prædestinatianorum profiteretur, quæ antea jam multis in Conciliis damnata fuerat, idque verbis adeo claris, ut nulli tergiversationi locus esset; ideoque à Remensi Archiepiscopo condemnationem, & pœnam atrocem passus est. Qui eum tueri, aut in bonum sensum interpretari ausus fuerit, necesse est, quod ejusdem erroris se cupiat participem constituere. | | 59 Gottschalk is defended by no orthodox man, seeing that he had been condemned in the Second Council of Mainz and the First of Quierzy, because he professed the heresy of the Predestinarians, which had already before been condemned in many Councils, and that in words so clear that there was no room for any tergiversation; and therefore he suffered condemnation and a cruel punishment from the Archbishop of Rheims. Whoever shall have dared to defend him, or to interpret him in a good sense, must needs desire to make himself a partaker of the same error. |
| 60 Abbati Joachimo, quem maximis præconiis efferunt S. Antoninus, Xystus Senensis, Alphonsus à S. Victore, Viegas, & alii, injuriam infert Muratorius, dum dicit eum in generalibus Conciliis hæreseos damnatum. Eum hæreticum fuisse scribit Castro de Hæresi; recte tamen eum à calumnia defendit Viegas. (18) {marg. In Apocalyps. cap. 3. comment. 2. sect. 3.} Res facti est. Casus sic se habet: Cum Petrus Lombardus, Magister Sententiarum jure dictus, hæresis accusaretur apud S. P. Alexandrum III. eo quod docuisse videbatur, quod *Christus secundum quod est homo, non sit aliquid*; ipse Pontifex ad Archiepiscopum Senonensem rescripsit, ut convocatis prudentibus, doctisque viris examinaret, reprobaretque prædictam doctrinam. Harum literarum tenorem exhibet Gonzales. (19) {marg. In comment. ad cap. 2. de Summ. Trinit.} Hæc erroris Joachimi causa fuit, quippe invitatus, ut Lombardum refelleret, adeo hoc exarsit studio, ut non solum in prædictum articulum inveheretur, sed etiam in alios orthodoxos circa Sanctissimæ Trinitatis mysterium articulos liberius scriberet, eo deceptus contradictionis spiritu, ut Lombardum non solum Trinitatem, sed quaternitatem docuisse arbitraretur. (20) {marg. Cabassut. in notit. Concil. ad Concil. Lateran. IV. sub Innocent. III. Rifenst. in Decretal. lib. 1. tit. 1. §. 10. n. 212.} Nec tamen ideo hæreticus fuit, aut de hæresi damnatus; neque enim tenaciter doctrinæ suæ adhæsit, neque Pontificio restitit oraculo, sed vere Orthodoxus libentissime acquievit. Cujus veritatis testimonium perhibuit S. P. Honorius III. in his verbis: *Quod eum verum Catholicum reputamus, & regularem observantiam, quam instituit, salutarem.* Habentur in literis Apostolicis datis 13. Kalendas Januarii anno 1221. quæ incipiunt *Ex parte*, & referuntur à Gonzales, & Fagnano. (21) {marg. In cap. Damnamus de Sum. Trinit.} | | 60 Upon the Abbot Joachim, whom St. Antoninus, Sixtus of Siena, Alphonsus a S. Victore, Viegas, and others extol with the greatest praises, Muratori inflicts an injury, when he says that he was condemned of heresy in general Councils. Castro, De Hæresi, writes that he was a heretic; yet Viegas rightly defends him from the calumny. (18) {marg. In Apocalyps. cap. 3. comment. 2. sect. 3.} It is a matter of fact. The case stands thus: When Peter Lombard, rightly called the Master of the Sentences, was accused of heresy before the Holy Father Alexander III, because he seemed to have taught that *Christ, according as He is man, is not anything*; the Pontiff himself wrote back to the Archbishop of Sens that, having convoked prudent and learned men, he should examine and reprove the aforesaid doctrine. The tenor of these letters Gonzalez exhibits. (19) {marg. In comment. ad cap. 2. de Summ. Trinit.} This was the cause of Joachim's error: for, being invited to refute Lombard, he so blazed with this zeal that he not only inveighed against the aforesaid article, but also wrote too freely against other orthodox articles concerning the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity, deceived by such a spirit of contradiction that he supposed Lombard to have taught not only a Trinity but a quaternity. (20) {marg. Cabassut. in notit. Concil. ad Concil. Lateran. IV. sub Innocent. III. Rifenst. in Decretal. lib. 1. tit. 1. §. 10. n. 212.} Nor yet was he therefore a heretic, or condemned of heresy; for neither did he tenaciously adhere to his doctrine, nor resist the Pontifical oracle, but, truly Orthodox, most willingly acquiesced. To which truth the Holy Father Honorius III bore testimony in these words: *That we repute him a true Catholic, and the regular observance which he instituted, salutary.* They are found in the Apostolic letters given on the 13th of the Kalends of January in the year 1221, which begin *Ex parte*, and are related by Gonzalez and Fagnanus. (21) {marg. In cap. Damnamus de Sum. Trinit.} |
| 61 Accedamus ad Honorium I. quem Muratorius, quam modeste potest, hæreticum fuisse, seu hæresum assertorem insinuare non veretur, dum dicit ejus literas *in œcumenica Synodo VI. proscriptas fuisse quasi hæreticis sententiis scatentes.* Laudat Baronium, & Bellarminum, Honorii patronos, & laudare potuisset innumeros alios: sed ei potius placuit Historiographos sequi ecclesiasticæ potestati satis infensos, eos, inquam, qui ut fallibilitatem Romani Pontificis in rebus fidei decernendis astruant, non paucos Pontifices in errorem lapsos ostendere adnituntur. Hanc suam mentem esse, etsi frequentissime innumeris obsequiis dissimulatam, vel ex eo liquet, quod ineptissime hoc Honorii factum ad exemplum adducat; diversissimæ siquidem quæstiones sunt, an Summus Pontifex possit errare in quæstionibus facti fidem concernentis; an verò revera intercesserit factum damnationis Honorii. De illa, de qua præsens disputatio est, non dubitarunt unquam aut Baronius, aut Bellarminus, aut cæteri, qui Honorium ab omni impostura defendunt. Quod inficiantur, illud est, nempe, Honorium fuisse hæreticum, aut ut talem in VI. Synodo damnatum. Immo neque Cabassutius ipse, licet pusillanimitatis reum faciat, eum hæreticum fuisse asserere ausus est; quin potius ab hac calumnia vindicat: condemnatum tamen in VI. Synodo fuisse pluribus ostendit facili negotio dissolvendis. Ego solum, si mihi datum esset, eum percontarer, quo jure de hæresi damnari potuit, si hæreticus non fuit? | | 61 Let us come to Honorius I, whom Muratori, as modestly as he can, does not fear to insinuate to have been a heretic, or an assertor of heresies, when he says that his letters *were proscribed in the Sixth ecumenical Synod as swarming with heretical opinions.* He praises Baronius and Bellarmine, patrons of Honorius, and he could have praised innumerable others: but it pleased him rather to follow Historiographers hostile enough to ecclesiastical power — those, I say, who, in order to establish the fallibility of the Roman Pontiff in deciding matters of faith, strive to show that not a few Pontiffs lapsed into error. That this is his mind, although most frequently dissembled by innumerable acts of homage, is clear even from this, that he most ineptly adduces this deed of Honorius as an example; for the questions are most diverse: whether the Supreme Pontiff can err in questions of fact concerning the faith; and whether in very truth the fact of the condemnation of Honorius intervened. Of the former, of which the present disputation is, neither Baronius nor Bellarmine nor the rest who defend Honorius from all imposture ever doubted. What they deny is this, namely, that Honorius was a heretic, or condemned as such in the Sixth Synod. Nay, not even Cabassut himself, although he makes him guilty of pusillanimity, dared to assert that he was a heretic; rather he vindicates him from this calumny: yet he shows by several arguments, easy of dissolution, that he was condemned in the Sixth Synod. I only, were it given me, would ask him this: by what right could he be condemned for heresy, if he was not a heretic? |
| 62 Optarem sane plus æquo in hac materia immorari, sed multum ab scopo principali videbor aberrare. Legendos offero non jam Baronium, aut Bellarminum, qui, utpote sacræ Romanæ Ecclesiæ Cardinales, suspecti aliquibus fortasse videbuntur, sed doctissimos, eosque Gallicos scriptores, scilicet Duvallium, & Gibalinum, (22) {marg. Duval. p. 1. q. 4. Gibalin. de Scient. Canon. tom. 2. lib. 5. cap. 3.} & eruditissimos viros Scotum, & Coccium. (23) {marg. Scot. in notis ad Photium ad Cod. 19. Coccius in thesaur. Cathol. tom. 1. lib. 7.} Ego quidem cum multis abhinc retro annis tum VI. Synodi, cum aliquorum scriptorum super hac re sententiam accuratius volutarem, me in Sanctam Sedem, Romanosque Pontifices innatus amor libentissimum traxit, ut indubie assentirer asserentibus, acta illius Concilii à Theodoro Sicucello Patriarcha Constantinopolitano vitiata fuisse, loco ipsius reposito Honorii nomine. Quod quidem plane suadet, quod cum Theodorus ipse illius hæresis patronus acerrimus semper fuerit, & ob hoc, & quia nomen Vitaliani Pontificis è sacris diptycis abraderet, è sede sua pulsus, & postea in eam iterum, se Catholicum fingens, restitutus fuisset, & cum cæteris Patriarchis hæreticis damnatus, nusquam tamen ejus nomen inter aliorum Patriarcharum, qui damnati fuerant, nomina recensitum reperitur. | | 62 I could wish indeed to linger more than is fair upon this matter, but I shall seem to wander far from my principal scope. I offer for reading no longer Baronius or Bellarmine — who, as Cardinals of the holy Roman Church, will perhaps seem suspect to some — but most learned writers, and those French, namely Duval and Gibalin, (22) {marg. Duval. p. 1. q. 4. Gibalin. de Scient. Canon. tom. 2. lib. 5. cap. 3.} and those most erudite men Schott and Coccius. (23) {marg. Scot. in notis ad Photium ad Cod. 19. Coccius in thesaur. Cathol. tom. 1. lib. 7.} I indeed, when many years ago I was turning over more carefully the opinion both of the Sixth Synod and of certain writers upon this matter, an inborn love toward the Holy See and the Roman Pontiffs drew me, most willing, to assent without doubt to those who assert that the acts of that Council were vitiated by Theodore Sicucellus, Patriarch of Constantinople, the name of Honorius being set in the place of his own. And this indeed is plainly persuaded by the fact that, whereas Theodore himself was always a most fierce patron of that heresy, and on this account, and because he had erased the name of Pope Vitalian from the sacred diptychs, was driven from his see, and afterwards, feigning himself a Catholic, was restored to it again, and was condemned with the other heretical Patriarchs, yet nowhere is his name found reckoned among the names of the other Patriarchs who had been condemned. |
| 63 Et quidem ut id potius credam, me fert animus, quam ut mihi persuadere valeam illius Synodi Patres velo levato, contra omnes juris regulas, sine maturiori examine, non audita parte, non discusso Honorianæ epistolæ vero, & orthodoxo sensu, (quem habere potuisse, neque ipsi hethorodoxi diffitentur) nulla instituta in eum legitima accusatione, nullo assignato defensore in hominem mortuum, Romanum Pontificem, de Ecclesia benemeritum, de quo Joannes de Polemar Archidiaconus Barchinonensis in oratione habita in Concilio Barchinonensi hæc ait: *Honorius Papa miræ pietatis fuit, cujus tunica dæmoniacus quidam indutus fuit, & liberatus est*; non reclamantibus, nec dubitantibus sanctæ Sedis Legatis, (qui tamen pro Mænna, & Vigilio surgentes steterunt) damnationis sententiam in Honorium protulisse, eumque hæreticum fuisse proclamasse. Quis adeo iniuste acta de sancta Synodo in Sancto Spiritu congregata credere poterit? Quis Patres illos certos fecit, Honorium in illa sententia perstitisse, & contumacem obiisse, ut necessarium erat, (juxta Vigilii constitutum) ut Honorius, etiam supposito ejus errore, hæreticus pronuntiaretur? In qua Synodo ita in hæreticos ex abrupto sententiam latam fuisse unquam legitur? Factum fuisse cum Honorio Romano Pontifice, quod neque cum Nestorio, Eutychete, & aliis hæresiarchis in aliis Conciliis, immo nec cum Petro, Sergio, Machario, Stephano, Policronio illius hæresis assertoribus actum fuit, quomodo credendum? Quod ejus epistolæ, utpote vel de hæresi suspectæ, vel propter non rectam dicendi methodum hæreticis faventes damnarentur, id fieri potuisset: id actum lego, non in decimaquarta actione, ut Cabassutius ait, sed in fine decimæ tertiæ actionis. Neque ideo tamen rite Honorius, de cujus vero sensu non recte constabat, post mortem suam, ut hæreticus damnari jure poterat. Sicut in simili. Ibæ epistola damnata fuerat, non tamen Iba de hæresi damnatus fuit, ut supra diximus. Quod autem Honorii epistolæ verum, & orthodoxum sensum habere potuissent, id ostendit Maximus in eadem Synodo, id Joannes IV. id probat Giballinus supra citatus, id legentibus notum fieri potest. | | 63 And indeed my mind carries me rather to believe this, than that I should be able to persuade myself that the Fathers of that Synod — with the veil lifted, against all the rules of law, without more mature examination, the party unheard, the true and orthodox sense of the Honorian epistle undiscussed (which even the heterodox themselves do not deny it could have had), no legitimate accusation instituted against him, no defender assigned, against a dead man, a Roman Pontiff, well-deserving of the Church, of whom John de Polemar, Archdeacon of Barcelona, in an oration delivered in the Council of Barcelona, says these things: *Pope Honorius was of wondrous piety, with whose tunic a certain demoniac was clothed, and he was delivered*; the Legates of the holy See neither protesting nor doubting (who yet rose and stood for Menas and Vigilius) — brought forth a sentence of condemnation against Honorius, and proclaimed that he had been a heretic. Who could believe things so unjustly done by a holy Synod congregated in the Holy Spirit? Who made those Fathers certain that Honorius had persisted in that opinion, and had died contumacious, as was necessary (according to the constitutum of Vigilius) in order that Honorius, even supposing his error, might be pronounced a heretic? In what Synod is it ever read that sentence was thus passed against heretics out of hand? That there was done with Honorius, a Roman Pontiff, what was done neither with Nestorius, Eutyches, and other heresiarchs in other Councils — nay, not even with Peter, Sergius, Macarius, Stephen, Polychronius, the assertors of that heresy — how is it to be believed? That his epistles, as being either suspect of heresy, or, on account of an incorrect method of speaking, favorable to heretics, should be condemned — that could have been done: I read that this was done, not in the fourteenth action, as Cabassut says, but at the end of the thirteenth action. Nor yet for that reason could Honorius, of whose true sense there was no right certainty, rightly be condemned after his death as a heretic. As in a like case: the epistle of Ibas was condemned, yet Ibas was not condemned for heresy, as we have said above. But that the epistles of Honorius could have had a true and orthodox sense, this Maximus showed in the same Synod, this John IV showed, this Gibalin above cited proves, this can become known to those who read. |
| 64 Juvat maxime, quod S. P. Agatho, cujus authoritate ea Synodus congregata fuerat, quique ignorare non poterat quæ Honorius Sergio, & Sergius Honorio scripserant, cum in epistolis suis ad Synodum, & Imperatorem scriptis, hujusmodi hæresis, ejusque patronorum specialem, & individuam mentionem fecerit, Honorii, ex cujus authoritate maius error ille acciperet patrocinium, nullatenus meminerit; quod non prætermitteret magnus ille Pontifex, si Honorium ea perniciosa labe conspurcatum arbitraretur. Præcipue cum Imperator ipse Flavius Constantinus cum ad Agathonem scriberet, etiam de Honorio sermonem institueret, cum agit de suggestione sibi facta, ut Pontificem Vitalianum è diptychis ejiceret. Intendebant Theodorus Constantipolitanus, & Macarius Patriarcha Theopoleos, *ut ejiceretur Vitalianus beatissimus de diptychis, asserentes Honorium memorari in diptychis propter honorem Apostolicæ Sedis antiquæ nostræ Romæ.* Quomodo autem Agatho ad hæc scripta nihil referret? Quomodo eamdem nollent Vitaliano reverentiam, aut Honorio eamdem injuriam Patriarchæ illi, si Honorius rectam sententiam non tueretur? Ad hæc tamen siluit Agatho, & uno verbo omnes hos astus delusit asserens, nunquam Romanæ Ecclesiæ supremos Antistites aliquo in fide errore inquinatos extitiisse. Reverentiam tamen, quam habuerant hæretici ipsi, non habuit Concilium, cum Pontificem tot antea annis è vivis ereptum sic contaminarunt, ejusque cineres tanto dedecore affecerunt? Incredibile dictu! Istud non contemptibile fundamentum diluere intendit Cabassutius in *Notitia Conciliorum cap. 72. §. His tamen*, desumens ad id exemplum ex verbis Christi Domini ad Petrum: *Ego pro te rogavi, Petre, ut non deficiat fides tua.* Et sic discurrit: *Verumtamen sicut illa Christi ad Petrum asseveratio:* Rogavi pro te, Petre, ut non deficiat fides tua, *non obstat, quin ipse Petrus Christum paulo post voce negaverit; ita nec Agathonis assertio circa prædecessorum fidem ullatenus obsistit, quin aliqui eorum, ut ipsi testantur Baronius, & Bellarminus, contra debitam fidei professionem graviter peccaverint.* Meo tamen judicio inconcludenter nimis se gessit Cabassutius. Promissio facta Petro erat promissio de futuro, quando sibi datæ fuissent claves Ecclesiæ super ipsum ædificatæ: prolata fuerunt ea verba ante Domini passionem, quo tempore promissus fuerat, non vero collatus Petro Pontificatus, ut ait *D. Thom. in Matthæum: Claves istæ nondum essent fabrefactæ, & Christi sanguine intinctæ, ac temperatæ, cum quo pariter virtus, ac efficacia sacramentorum, & clavium potestas effluxit*; id enim muneris ei concessum fuit postea, cum eidem dictum fuit: *Pasce oves meas.... Pasce agnos meos.* Unde Petrus negando Dominum, & Magistrum peccavit ut homo, non ut Pontifex. At verò Agatho locutus fuit de promissione jam facta Pontificibus, quod scilicet eorum fides nunquam deficeret: qua stabilita, tamquam certa, Constantino asserenti, Honorium in ea sententia unius voluntatis stetisse, respondit, Pontifices Maximos nunquam in fide aliquando errasse, aut errare potuisse. | | 64 It helps very greatly that the Holy Father Agatho, by whose authority that Synod had been congregated, and who could not be ignorant of what Honorius had written to Sergius and Sergius to Honorius, when in his epistles written to the Synod and to the Emperor he made special and individual mention of this heresy and of its patrons, in no wise remembered Honorius, from whose authority that error would receive greater patronage; which that great Pontiff would not have passed over, if he judged Honorius to be befouled with that pernicious stain. Especially since the Emperor Flavius Constantine himself, when he wrote to Agatho, also entered upon discourse concerning Honorius, when he treats of the suggestion made to him, that he should cast Pope Vitalian out of the diptychs. Theodore of Constantinople and Macarius, Patriarch of Theopolis, were aiming *that the most blessed Vitalian should be cast out of the diptychs, asserting that Honorius was commemorated in the diptychs on account of the honor of the Apostolic See of our ancient Rome.* But how should Agatho make no reply to these writings? How would those Patriarchs not have wished the same reverence for Vitalian, or the same injury for Honorius, if Honorius did not uphold the right opinion? Yet to these things Agatho was silent, and with one word eluded all these wiles, asserting that the supreme Bishops of the Roman Church had never been defiled with any error in the faith. Yet did the Council not have that reverence which the heretics themselves had had, when they so contaminated a Pontiff snatched from the living so many years before, and visited his ashes with so great a disgrace? Incredible to say! This not contemptible foundation Cabassut endeavors to dissolve in the *Notitia Conciliorum cap. 72. §. His tamen*, taking for that purpose an example from the words of Christ the Lord to Peter: *I have prayed for thee, Peter, that thy faith fail not.* And he discourses thus: *Nevertheless, just as that asseveration of Christ to Peter:* I have prayed for thee, Peter, that thy faith fail not, *does not prevent Peter himself from having a little afterwards denied Christ with his voice; so neither does the assertion of Agatho concerning the faith of his predecessors in any wise prevent some of them, as Baronius and Bellarmine themselves testify, from having gravely sinned against the due profession of the faith.* Yet in my judgment Cabassut has borne himself far too inconclusively. The promise made to Peter was a promise concerning the future, when the keys of the Church built upon him should have been given to him: those words were uttered before the Lord's passion, at which time the Pontificate had been promised to Peter, but not conferred, as *St. Thomas on Matthew* says: *Those keys were not yet forged, and dipped in the blood of Christ, and tempered, with which likewise the virtue and efficacy of the sacraments and the power of the keys flowed forth*; for that office was granted him afterwards, when it was said to him: *Feed my sheep.... Feed my lambs.* Whence Peter, by denying the Lord and Master, sinned as a man, not as Pontiff. But Agatho spoke of the promise already made to the Pontiffs, namely, that their faith should never fail: which being established as certain, to Constantine asserting that Honorius had stood in that opinion of one will, he replied that the Supreme Pontiffs had never at any time erred in the faith, nor could have erred. |
| 65 Vim magnam dictis adaugeat, quod neque Theodorus I. qui Honorio, mediis solum Severino, & Joanne IV. qui non per multum tempus Ecclesiam rexerunt, in epistolis suis ad Paulum Patriarcham, & ad Episcopos qui Paulum elegerant, & ad Heraclium Imperatorem, cum de eadem hæresi profliganda curaret, Honorii meminerit; neque Paulus ipse apud Theodorum Honorii authoritate muniretur: neque similiter Martinus I. qui Theodoro successerat, & qui Monotholetarum hæresim in suo Lateranensi Concilio, & in epistola ad omnes fideles, quæ incipit *Gratia vobis*, cum Theodori, Sergii, Pauli, & aliorum recordaretur, Honorii mentionem nullam habuerit, quod minime contingeret, si Honorius ejus hæresis aut patronus, aut sectarius dicendus esset. | | 65 Let this add great force to what has been said: that neither Theodore I — who came after Honorius, with only Severinus and John IV between, who ruled the Church for no long time — when in his epistles to Paul the Patriarch, and to the Bishops who had elected Paul, and to the Emperor Heraclius, he was taking care for the overthrow of the same heresy, remembered Honorius; nor was Paul himself, in dealing with Theodore, fortified by the authority of Honorius: nor likewise did Martin I, who had succeeded Theodore, and who condemned the heresy of the Monothelites in his Lateran Council and in the epistle to all the faithful which begins *Gratia vobis*, when he recalled Theodore, Sergius, Paul, and others, make any mention of Honorius; which would by no means have happened, if Honorius were to be called either a patron or a sectary of that heresy. |
| 66 Maximum tandem robur huic adjicit negotio quod Joannes IV. Honorii immediatus successor in epistola ad Imperatorem Constantinum eum ab omni nævo, atque à calumnia ei imposita vindicaret per hæc verba: *Omnes occidentales partes scandalizatæ turbantur, fratre nostro Pirrho Patriarcha per literas suas huc, atque illuc transmissas nova quædam, & præter fidei regulam prædicante, & ad proprium sensum quasi sanctæ memoriæ Honorium Papam decessorem nostrum attrahere festinantem: quod à mente Catholici patris erat penitus alienum.* Postquam autem explicavit veram doctrinam, verumque Honorii sensum, sic concludit: *Prædictus ergo decessor meus docens de mysterio incarnationis Christi dicebat, non fuisse in eo, sicut in nobis peccatoribus, mentis, & carnis contrarias voluntates: quod quidam ad proprium sensum convertentes, divinitatis ejus, & humanitatis unam eum voluntatem docuisse suspicati sunt, quod veritati omnino est contrarium.* En Joannes, qui melius aliis Honorii sanum sensum optime noverat, eum explicans, & adversariorum calumnias retundens. Hanc Joannis assertionem non plurimi fecit Cabassutius, asserens id vitio verti posse, quod ipse tam suspectam epistolam scriberet, ac proinde ipsius testimonium suspectum esse. Temere dictum! Nam Joannes ipse in epistola sua duas in Christo voluntates profitetur, & solum explicat, has voluntates non esse inter se contrarias, & hoc solum sensisse Honorium; quod quidem scripsit cum jam Pontifex esset, ac proinde jam ejus testimonium omnem fidem debuerat promereri, saltem ut Honorius hæreticus non diceretur, de cujus recto sensu Joannes testabatur. Quo jure ergo post hanc Joannis Summi Pontificis declarationem expresse asserentis, Honorium Catholicum fuisse, potuit Synodus hæc contemnere, & Honorium ut hæreticum damnare? Cur saltem hæc Joannis epistola perlecta non fuit, cum alia documenta legerentur? Cur Sedem Apostolicam in re dubia non consuluerunt? Cur aliquem Honorio jam defuncto procuratorem, qui eum defenderet, non constituerunt? Hæc omnia Concilium posthabuisse, nunquam credere fas esse docebo. | | 66 Finally, the greatest strength is added to this business by the fact that John IV, the immediate successor of Honorius, in his epistle to the Emperor Constantine, vindicated him from every blemish and from the calumny laid upon him, in these words: *All the western parts are scandalized and troubled, since our brother Pyrrhus the Patriarch, by his letters sent hither and thither, preaches certain new things, and beside the rule of faith, and hastens to draw, as it were, Pope Honorius of holy memory, our predecessor, to his own sense: which was utterly alien from the mind of that Catholic father.* But after he has explained the true doctrine and the true sense of Honorius, he thus concludes: *The aforesaid predecessor, therefore my [predecessor], teaching concerning the mystery of the incarnation of Christ, said that there were not in him, as in us sinners, contrary wills of the mind and of the flesh: which certain men, twisting it to their own sense, suspected that he taught one will of his divinity and humanity, which is altogether contrary to the truth.* Behold John, who better than others knew most excellently the sound sense of Honorius, explaining him, and blunting the calumnies of his adversaries. Cabassutius did not make much of this assertion of John, asserting that it could be turned to his fault that he himself wrote so suspect a letter, and hence that his testimony is suspect. Rashly said! For John himself in his letter professes two wills in Christ, and only explains that these wills are not contrary to each other, and that Honorius held this alone; which indeed he wrote when he was already Pontiff, and hence his testimony ought already to have merited all credence, at least so that Honorius should not be called a heretic, to whose right sense John bore witness. By what right, therefore, after this declaration of John the Supreme Pontiff, expressly asserting that Honorius was Catholic, could the Synod despise these things and condemn Honorius as a heretic? Why at least was this letter of John not read through, when other documents were read? Why did they not consult the Apostolic See in a doubtful matter? Why did they not appoint some procurator for Honorius, now deceased, who might defend him? That the Council neglected all these things, I shall teach that it is never right to believe. |
| 67 Ipse Muratorius, licet dubius, in hanc partem propendere visus est *cap. seq. pag.* 172. Factum tamen VI.
