Synodi profitetur, eo quod negare non audeat, *quin anathema dictum Honorio fuerit, quippe in id conspirat totius antiquitatis consensus.* Iste consensus totus profluit ex illo vitiato principio, ut videre est in citato Giballino, ubi ad omnia respondet, ex quibus consensus ille traxit fundamentum, epistola scilicet Leonis I. quæ conficta à pluribus jure dicitur, & VII. ac VIII. Synodo, quæ ex deturpatis VI. Synodi actis totam suam probandi vim accepisse dicenda sunt. Pace tamen sua dicam, minus culpabile esse, quod cum Baronio, Bellarmino, cæterisque negemus omnino factum, quam quod, eo retento, tamquam certo, (ut Muratorius intendit) *Honorium male revera non sensisse asseramus de Christi voluntatibus, neque hæreticum plane fuisse*; cogeremur etenim dicere Concilium œcumenicum in re tanti ponderis, tam maximique momenti penitus errasse. Hoc effatum ejus authoritati in re tam ardua definienda plurimum detrahit: illud hæreticos solum calumniatores offendit: *Si*, ut ait, *nihil ibi viris doctissimis occurrit, quod considerata temporum, & rerum conditione, verum referat Monothelismum, hominemque faciat impia tradentem*, quomodo, *considerata temporum, & rerum conditione*, potuit Concilium definire, (quin dicamus errasse, quod absit) epistolam illam *verum referre Monothelismum, Honoriumque fuisse hominem impia tradentem*? Nos equidem hæresim, non in Honorio, ut ait, sed in aliis hæreticis à Concilio damnatam execramur, sed Concilii sententiam de persona Honorii, ut certam non suscipimus, quia certam non censemus, quia falso suppositam à Theodoro arbitramur. Mirari autem possemus, si id in more non habuisset Muratorius, quod in eadem periodo sibi contrarius ostendatur; si enim sententiam de persona Honorii *certam non censet*, quomodo asserit *certe suscipiendam*? An certe suscipiendum quod extitisse incertum est? Sic suomet ingenio fallitur scriptor iste. | | 67 Muratori himself, though doubtful, seemed to incline to this side, *cap. seq. pag.* 172. Yet he professes the deed of the Sixth Synod, because he dares not deny *that anathema was pronounced against Honorius, since the consensus of all antiquity conspires in this.* That whole consensus flows from that vitiated principle, as may be seen in the cited Giballinus, where he answers all the things from which that consensus drew its foundation, namely the letter of Leo I, which is by many rightly said to be forged, and the Seventh and Eighth Synods, which must be said to have received their whole probative force from the corrupted acts of the Sixth Synod. Yet, by his leave, I shall say that it is less culpable that, with Baronius, Bellarmine, and the rest, we deny the deed altogether, than that, retaining it as certain (as Muratori intends), *we assert that Honorius did not in truth think ill concerning the wills of Christ, nor was plainly a heretic*; for we should be forced to say that an ecumenical Council utterly erred in a matter of such weight and of the very greatest moment. This pronouncement detracts very greatly from its authority in defining so arduous a matter: the former offends only heretical calumniators. *If*, as he says, *nothing occurs there to most learned men which, the condition of the times and of things being considered, presents true Monothelism and makes the man one handing down impious things*, how, *the condition of the times and of things being considered*, could the Council define (lest we say it erred, which God forbid) that that letter *presents true Monothelism, and that Honorius was a man handing down impious things*? We indeed execrate the heresy condemned by the Council, not in Honorius, as he says, but in the other heretics; but the Council's sentence concerning the person of Honorius we do not receive as certain, because we do not judge it certain, because we deem it falsely foisted in by Theodore. But we could wonder — had Muratori not made it his custom — that in the same period he shows himself contrary to himself; for if he *does not judge certain* the sentence concerning the person of Honorius, how does he assert that it is *certainly to be received*? Is that to be certainly received whose existence is uncertain? Thus that writer is deceived by his own ingenuity. |
| 68 Aliud exemplum adducit *pag.* 170. ad probandum Ecclesiam non habere infallibilem assistentiam circa certum judicium de verborum sensu aut vero, aut hæretico proferendum. Circa hoc dicit, quod *Ecclesia impedire non potest quominus aliena dicta æquivocationibus scateant, & æquivocos sensus pariant. Neque hoc impedire miraculorum ope Deus voluit, aut vult, cum satis ipsi sit facere, ne in interpretandis, explicandisque divinis libris, & colligenda traditione, & proponenda vera doctrina errare possint legitimi Ecclesiæ Catholicæ judices.* Quî fieri potest, ut non errent, si eis datum non est veros sensus infallibiliter callere, aut falsos aperire? Non equidem potest impedire æquivoca verba, sed certo decernere potest, illa verba apta ex se esse ad recondendum hæresis venenum, atque de facto in ipsis verbis recondi, prohibereque, ne similibus vocibus, aut phrasibus utamur. Hoc autem ut infallibiliter faciat, peculiari eget lumine, cum tot sint verborum ambages, tot hæreticorum cavilli, tot phrasium larvæ, ut frequentissime deceptioni, & erroribus locus esset, si Ecclesia fulgentissima Spiritus Sancti luce non illustraretur, immo contingeret, quod aliquando in his rebus aut falli, aut fallere quiret fideles. | | 68 He adduces another example, *pag.* 170, to prove that the Church does not have infallible assistance concerning the certain judgment to be pronounced on the sense of words, whether true or heretical. On this he says that *the Church cannot prevent the sayings of others from teeming with equivocations and begetting equivocal senses. Nor did God will, or does he will, to prevent this by the help of miracles, since it is enough for him to bring it about that the legitimate judges of the Catholic Church cannot err in interpreting and explaining the divine books, and in gathering tradition, and in proposing true doctrine.* How can it come about that they do not err, if it is not given to them infallibly to discern true senses, or to lay open false ones? She cannot indeed prevent equivocal words, but she can with certainty decree that those words are of themselves apt for hiding the poison of heresy, and that in fact it lies hidden in those very words, and forbid that we use similar words or phrases. But that she may do this infallibly, she needs a peculiar light, since so many are the windings of words, so many the cavils of heretics, so many the masks of phrases, that most frequently there would be room for deception and errors, if the Church were not illuminated by the most refulgent light of the Holy Spirit; nay, it would happen that at times in these matters she could either be deceived, or deceive the faithful. |
| 69 Asserit, quod *Ecclesia enuntiationes easdem secundum varios sensus acceptas nunc approbavit, nunc improbavit, & eadem omnino verba modo aversata est, modo amplexa, prout ipsi visa sunt signare aut pravam, aut rectam doctrinam.* Certe fallitur; nam Ecclesia neque unquam in rebus fidei definiendis verbis ambiguis, & in duplici contrario sensu accipiendis usa fuit; neque sermones æquivocos, atque ad dissiminandas hæreses aptos quandoque applausit; etenim qui lucem diligit, tenebras omnes odisse pro munere semper habuit. Quandoquidem si verba bonum, aut malum sensum habere possunt, semper explicavit, ut erroribus minutissimas etiam rimulas omnino intercluderet. | | 69 He asserts that *the Church has now approved, now disapproved the same enunciations taken according to various senses, and has at one time turned away from, at another embraced, altogether the same words, according as they seemed to her to signify either depraved or right doctrine.* Certainly he is deceived; for the Church neither ever, in defining matters of faith, used ambiguous words, to be taken in a double contrary sense; nor did she at any time applaud equivocal speeches apt for disseminating heresies; for she who loves the light has ever held it as her office to hate all darkness. Since indeed, if words can have a good or a bad sense, she has always explained them, so as to shut off from errors even the minutest chinks entirely. |
| 70 Id patet in iisdem thesibus adductis ad exemplum. Ea enuntiatio: *Unus de Trinitate passus est*, à Summis Pontificibus Felice III. & Hormisda reprobata fuerat; sed non eadem omnino accepta fuit à Joanne II. Vigilio, & V. Synodo, sed cum aliquo addito, nempe: *Unus de Trinitate in carne passus est*; aliàs diceremus in fidei dogmate Romanos Pontifices discrepasse. Jam videris, doctus Lector, quantum inter utramque thesim intersit discrimen. In prima horribilis error latebat, ideo rejecta: in secunda infallibilis veritas continetur, ideo admissa. Una divinitatem passibilem dissimulabat, altera solam humanitatem assumptam passam fuisse aperiebat. Prima erat Petri Cnaphei impii hæretici, qui non modo Valentini, Manichæi, Arii, Sabellii, Apollinaris, Eutychetis, verum etiam gentilium errores in unum colligens, plures Deos asserebat, atque unum increatæ, & individuæ Trinitatis passionem subiisse prædicare ausus est. Secunda verò erat Procli in epistola ad Occidentales dicentis: *Non dicimus eum deitate passum fuisse, sed carne, quam pro nobis assumpsit ex nobis. Unus igitur ex Trinitate crucifixus est carne, qua factus est: non tamen deitate, qua unitus est Patri, & Spiritui Sancto, perpessus est; ne & illos crucifixos pariter asseramus.* Erat Gregorii Nanzianzeni in his verbis: *Et indiguimus Deo incarnato, & mortificato, ut nos vivamus.* Erat Augustini in libro de Charitate, qui sic ait: *Res enim vetus erat, ut homo moreretur: quod ne semper valeret in homine, res nova facta est, ut Deus moreretur: sed quia in carne mortuus est, non in divinitate.* Et alibi: (24) *Prædestinatus est ergo JESUS, ut futurus secundum carnem filius David, esset in virtute tamen filius Dei.* {marg. (24) Lib. 1. de Prædestin. cap. 15.} Erat Cyrilli *cap.* 12. per hæc verba: *Siquis non confitetur Verbum passum carne, & mortem carnem gustasse, factumque primogenitum ex mortuis, secundum quod vita, & vivificans est Deus, anathema sit.* Erat Leonis ad Juvenalem sic: *Veram igitur generationem crux vera confirmat, quoniam in carne nostra nascitur, qui in nostra carne crucifigitur.* Et ad Flavianum sic: *Impassibilis Deus non dedignatus est homo esse passibilis, & immortalis mortis legibus subjacere.* Sic profitetur, & explicat Ecclesia in Fidei Symbolo, & in omnibus œcumenicis Synodis, sic & nos infallibiliter profitemur non confundentes personas, neque substantiam separantes: non ineffabili Trisagio addentes, quod crucifixus sit unus ex sanctissimo Trisagio, ut confundebat Cnapheus, sed sigillatim, clare, distincteque credentes unum Deum Patrem, Filium, & Spiritum Sanctum, & ex his tribus personis secundam, Verbum scilicet, in omnibus, & per omnia æqualem, & consubstantialem Patri, ex MARIA Virgine hominem factum, natumque fuisse, de hocque firmiter credimus secundum humanam naturam natum, crucifixum, mortuum, & sepultum fuisse. | | 70 This is evident in the very theses adduced as an example. That enunciation, *One of the Trinity suffered*, had been reprobated by the Supreme Pontiffs Felix III and Hormisdas; but it was not altogether the same one that was accepted by John II, Vigilius, and the Fifth Synod, but with something added, namely: *One of the Trinity suffered in the flesh*; otherwise we should say that the Roman Pontiffs disagreed in a dogma of faith. Now you may see, learned Reader, how great a difference lies between the two theses. In the first a horrible error lay hidden, therefore it was rejected: in the second an infallible truth is contained, therefore it was admitted. The one dissembled a passible divinity; the other laid open that only the assumed humanity had suffered. The first was that of Peter Cnapheus, an impious heretic, who, gathering into one the errors not only of Valentinus, Manichaeus, Arius, Sabellius, Apollinaris, and Eutyches, but even of the gentiles, asserted several Gods, and dared to preach that one of the uncreated and undivided Trinity underwent the passion. But the second was that of Proclus, saying in his letter to the Westerners: *We do not say that he suffered in the deity, but in the flesh, which for us he assumed from us. One of the Trinity, therefore, was crucified in the flesh, in which he was made: yet he did not suffer in the deity, in which he is united to the Father and to the Holy Spirit; lest we assert that they too were likewise crucified.* It was that of Gregory Nazianzen, in these words: *And we needed a God incarnate and put to death, that we might live.* It was that of Augustine, in the book On Charity, who speaks thus: *For it was an old thing that man should die: and that this might not always prevail in man, a new thing was done, that God should die: but because he died in the flesh, not in the divinity.* And elsewhere: (24) *JESUS therefore was predestined, so that he who was to be, according to the flesh, the son of David, should nevertheless be, in power, the Son of God.* {marg. (24) Lib. 1. de Prædestin. cap. 15.} It was that of Cyril, *cap.* 12, in these words: *If anyone does not confess that the Word suffered in the flesh, and tasted death in the flesh, and became the firstborn from the dead, according as he is life, and God who gives life, let him be anathema.* It was that of Leo to Juvenal, thus: *The true cross therefore confirms the true generation, since he is born in our flesh who is crucified in our flesh.* And to Flavian thus: *The impassible God did not disdain to be a passible man, and the immortal to lie subject to the laws of death.* Thus the Church professes and explains in the Symbol of Faith, and in all the ecumenical Synods; thus we too infallibly profess, not confounding the persons, nor separating the substance: not adding to the ineffable Trisagion that one of the most holy Trisagion was crucified, as Cnapheus confounded it, but severally, clearly, and distinctly believing one God, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, and that of these three persons the second, namely the Word, in all things and through all things equal and consubstantial with the Father, was made man of the Virgin MARY, and was born; and of him we firmly believe that according to his human nature he was born, was crucified, died, and was buried. |
| 71 Stat ergo non eamdem omnino esse utramque enuntiationem, ut ait Muratorius, sed diversissimam, quam facit illa adjectio *in carne*. Una sordidum ulcus, vipereumque virus sub æquivoco sermone recondebat; altera certum dogma, indefectibilemque exprimit veritatem. Illam & Felix, & Hormisda, aliique sancti Patres respuerunt, ut noxiam: istam & Joannes, & Vigilius, & V. Synodus, & cæteri Ecclesiæ Doctores, ut innocentem, amplexati sunt. Nunc ad intentum inquirens, concludo: An Felix, & Hormisda Spiritus Sancti assistentia destituti dicendi sint, quando Cnapheum, ut hæreticum, damnarunt, ejusque perversum sensum, quod in illa enuntiatione latebat, aperuerunt? An erraverint Joannes, & Vigilius, ac sine Sancti Spiritus afflante Numine explicaverint quo in sensu illa enuntiatio accipienda fuerit? Quocumque dato, sic asserenti temeritatis nota attribuenda erit. Ergo non deest Spiritus Sancti assistentia, neque vana est Christi Domini promissio, quoties de vero alicujus propositionis sensu explicando, & declarando agitur. | | 71 It stands, therefore, that the two enunciations are not altogether the same, as Muratori says, but most diverse, which that addition *in carne* makes them. The one hid a filthy ulcer and a viperous venom beneath equivocal speech; the other expresses a certain dogma and an indefectible truth. The former both Felix and Hormisdas and other holy Fathers rejected as noxious: the latter both John and Vigilius and the Fifth Synod and the rest of the Doctors of the Church embraced as innocent. Now, inquiring toward my point, I conclude: Are Felix and Hormisdas to be said to have been destitute of the assistance of the Holy Spirit, when they condemned Cnapheus as a heretic, and laid open his perverse sense, which lay hidden in that enunciation? Or did John and Vigilius err, and explain without the inbreathing Godhead of the Holy Spirit in what sense that enunciation was to be taken? Whichever be granted, the mark of temerity will have to be attributed to one so asserting. Therefore the assistance of the Holy Spirit is not lacking, nor is the promise of Christ the Lord vain, whenever it is a question of explaining and declaring the true sense of some proposition. |
| 72 Quod addit, nempe: *In Synodo Antiochena vocem* Houmousion *Episcopi complures proscripsere, in sensu Pauli Samozateni acceptam*, fide sua dictum sit, nam quid tale in prædicta Synodo me legisse non memini; ni forte id habeatur in aliquo ex novem canonibus quos P. Pagi ad annum 56. spurios censet, & P. Petavius (25) supposititios vocat. {marg. (25) Lib. 15. de Incarnat. cap. 14.} Id scio, in prædicto Concilio Antiocheno, quod habetur *tom. I. Concil. pag.* 195. errores, vitiaque hæretici illius referri, de voce vero *Houmousion* nec verbum quidem haberi, aut quid ejus significationi correspondeat. Scio in Synodo Alexandrina anno 321. de hæretico eodem sermonem esse, ibique haberi eum *judicio omnium ubique Episcoporum, atque adeo judicio Ecclesiæ ejectum fuisse*, atque etiam ejus discipulos ex eo *Filium Dei ex nihilo ortum hausisse*; quod tamen vocem *Houmousion* non recte accepisset, id non lego. Scio in Nicæna sacrosancta Synodo in præfatione Arabica edoceri, sectam Paulianistarum, à Paulo Samozateno sic dictam, in eo versari, quod dicerent: *Deum esse unum tam secundum substantiam, quam secundum personas, eum trinum nomine tenus appellitare, &c.* Sed voce Houmousion usum fuisse, id ibi non exprimitur. Scio in altera Orientalium Episcoporum Synodo, quæ est altera Antiochena anno 344. erroris prædicti Pauli mentionem haberi per hæc verba: *Quoniam perfectum, ingenitum, carentem principio, & invisibilem Deum unum solum agnoscimus, Deum, & Patrem Unigeniti, qui solus ex se habet esse, solusque omnibus aliis benigne largitur, ut sint. Non tamen quia unum Deum esse Patrem Domini JESU Christi, solum ingenitum profitemur, ideo Christum Deum ante sæcula esse abnegamus: qualia faciunt Pauli Samozatensis discipuli, qui eum post incarnationem suam ex profectu Deum factum esse asseverant, eo quod merus homo esset.* Ubinam hic de voce Houmousion mentio, ubi disceptatio? Scio in Concilio Ephesino anno 431. *cap.* 1. ejusdem Samozateni doctrinam, Nestoriique ei consoni, clarissime exprimi, sed de prædicta voce nec verbum quidem invenitur. Sicut neque invenitur in *cap.* 17. ubi hæc habentur verba in supplicatione Basilii Diaconi, & aliorum Monachorum: *In sancta Synodo Antiochiæ contra Paulum Samozatenum congregata, quæ centum octoginta Patres complexa, illum propter suam impietatem (negabat enim Christum natura esse Deum, & Dei Patris Filium) exauctoravit.* Scio in libello Synodico, quod habetur *tom. 5. Concil. pag.* 1498. ejusdem Pauli mentionem fieri in hunc modum: *Synodus divina, & sacra Provincialis Anthiochiæ collecta.... quæ deposuit, & abdicavit Paulum Samozatenum, qui nihil aliud, quam Annas, & Caiphas de Christo docuit.* Tandem neque in Socrate, neque in Eusebio, aliisve invenire potui de prædicta voce disputationem. Quinimmo in Cabassutio in notitia Conciliorum exhibenda accuratissimo nihil aliud lego in *cap. 5.* nisi hoc: *Tria memorantur Antiochena Concilia, quorum priora duo fuerunt contra Paulum Samozatenum Antiochiæ Patriarcham impie asserentem de Christo, nihil fuisse quam verum hominem.* Si autem ibi circa illam vocem contentio aliqua esset, explicaret Cabassutius, explicaret Dionysius Alexandrinus, explicaret Eusebius, ad quos ipse se refert. Suspicor Muratorium non salientes limpidissimorum fontium scatebras, sed fugaces, arenisque jam turbidos rivulos adiisse. | | 72 As for what he adds, namely: *In the Synod of Antioch very many Bishops proscribed the word* Houmousion, *taken in the sense of Paul of Samosata*, let it be said on his own credit, for I do not remember to have read any such thing in the aforesaid Synod; unless perhaps it be contained in some one of the nine canons which Father Pagi, at the year 56, judges spurious, and Father Petavius (25) calls supposititious. {marg. (25) Lib. 15. de Incarnat. cap. 14.} This I know, that in the aforesaid Council of Antioch, which is contained *tom. I. Concil. pag.* 195, the errors and vices of that heretic are related, but concerning the word *Houmousion* not even a word is found, nor anything corresponding to its signification. I know that in the Synod of Alexandria in the year 321 there is speech of the same heretic, and that it is there contained that he *was cast out by the judgment of all the Bishops everywhere, and indeed by the judgment of the Church*, and also that his disciples drew from him *that the Son of God arose out of nothing*; but that he had not rightly taken the word *Houmousion*, this I do not read. I know that in the sacrosanct Synod of Nicaea, in the Arabic preface, it is taught that the sect of the Paulianists, so called from Paul of Samosata, consists in this, that they said: *that God is one as much according to substance as according to persons, and that they call him threefold in name only, etc.* But that he used the word Houmousion, that is not expressed there. I know that in the other Synod of the Eastern Bishops, which is the second of Antioch, in the year 344, mention is made of the error of the aforesaid Paul in these words: *Since we acknowledge one only God, perfect, unbegotten, lacking beginning, and invisible, the God and Father of the Only-begotten, who alone has being from himself, and alone benignly bestows upon all others that they may be. Yet not because we profess that the one God, the Father of the Lord JESUS Christ, is alone unbegotten, do we therefore deny that Christ is God before the ages: as do the disciples of Paul of Samosata, who assert that after his incarnation he was made God by advancement, because he was a mere man.* Where here is there mention of the word Houmousion, where a disputation? I know that in the Council of Ephesus, in the year 431, *cap.* 1, the doctrine of the same Samosatene, and of Nestorius consonant with him, is most clearly expressed, but concerning the aforesaid word not even a word is found. Just as neither is it found in *cap.* 17, where these words occur in the supplication of Basil the Deacon and of other Monks: *In the holy Synod gathered at Antioch against Paul of Samosata, which, comprising one hundred and eighty Fathers, deposed him on account of his impiety (for he denied that Christ is by nature God, and the Son of God the Father).* I know that in the Synodical booklet, which is contained *tom. 5. Concil. pag.* 1498, mention is made of the same Paul in this manner: *The divine and sacred Provincial Synod gathered at Antioch.... which deposed and removed Paul of Samosata, who taught nothing else concerning Christ than did Annas and Caiphas.* Finally, neither in Socrates, nor in Eusebius, nor in others could I find a disputation concerning the aforesaid word. Nay rather, in Cabassutius, most accurate in setting forth the notice of the Councils, I read nothing else in *cap. 5.* but this: *Three Councils of Antioch are recorded, of which the first two were against Paul of Samosata, Patriarch of Antioch, impiously asserting of Christ that he was nothing but a true man.* But if there had been any contention there about that word, Cabassutius would explain it, Dionysius of Alexandria would explain it, Eusebius would explain it, to whom he himself refers. I suspect that Muratori went not to the leaping springs of the most limpid fountains, but to fleeting rivulets already turbid with sands. |
| 73 Sed revertamur ad fontes, ut puriorem notitiam hauriamus. Hæreticos Arianos hanc vocem *Houmousion* respuisse, ejusque usum ab Ecclesia eliminandum voluisse, constat ex Concilio Sirmiensi, ubi Osius, & Potamius sic blasphemarunt: *Quod verò quosdam, aut multos movebat de substantia, quæ græce usia appellatur, id est, ut expressius intelligatur, Homousion, aut quod dicitur Homousion nullam omnino fieri oportere mentionem: nec quemquam prædicare; ea de causa, & ratione, quod nec in divinis scripturis continentur, & quod super hominis sit, nec quisquam possit nativitatem Filii enarrare &c.* Videat Muratorius, ne in hunc reprobatum lapidem impingat, dum asserit Ecclesiam nihil aliud definire, quam quod in Scriptura, aut Sanctis Patribus jam definitum esse constat. | | 73 But let us return to the fountains, that we may draw a purer knowledge. That the Arian heretics rejected this word *Houmousion*, and wished its use to be eliminated from the Church, is evident from the Council of Sirmium, where Hosius and Potamius blasphemed thus: *But as to that which moved some, or many, concerning substance, which in Greek is called usia, that is, to be understood more expressly, Homousion, or what is called Homousion, that no mention at all ought to be made, nor anyone preach it; for this cause and reason, that they are not contained in the divine scriptures, and that it is above man [?], nor can anyone declare the nativity of the Son, etc.* Let Muratori see that he not stumble upon this reprobated stone, while he asserts that the Church defines nothing else than what is established to be already defined in Scripture or in the holy Fathers. |
| 74 Postea in Conventu, seu Conciliabulo Sirmiensi anno 357. similis hæreticalis blasphemia ab aliquibus prolata est: *Vocabulum* (aiebant illi) *porro substantiæ*, (quod idem est, atque Homousion) *quia simplicius à Patribus positum est, & à populis ignoratur, & scandalum affert, eo quod in scripturis non contineatur, placuit, ut de medio tolleretur, & posthac de Dei substantia mentionem esse faciendam, eo quod sacræ Scripturæ nunquam meminere substantiæ Patris, & Filii: Filium autem per omnia similem dicimus, quemadmodum sacræ literæ dicunt, & docent.* Hanc Arii hæresim, Osii, & Potamii blasphemiam respuit Concilium in Gallia anno 359. cujus non extat epistola Synodica, cujus tamen Concilii notitiam nobis exhibet Hilarius de Synodis scribens ad Episcopos Galliæ, & Epiphanius *hæres.* 73. & Phæbadius Aquitaniæ Episcopus in libro adversus Arianos. Postea in Synodo, seu Conciliabulo Ariminensi eodem anno Patres Ariminenses antequam à Constantino territi à via veritatis declinarent, cum Nicæna Synodo eamdem vocem firmiter tenendam esse decreverunt, ut constat ex eorum professione: *Substantiæ quoque nomen, & rem à multis sanctis scripturis insinuatam mentibus nostris obtinere debere sui firmitatem. Quam rem cum suo nomine Ecclesia Catholica cum doctrina deifica semper confiteri consuevit.* Prævaricata autem fuit Synodus illa à Constantio conterrita, & eamdem vocem respuendam dixit, ut constat ex epistola ad Constantium, & fidei formula ad Orientales Episcopos Arianos missa, ubi sic aiunt Ariminensis Conciliabuli Patres: *Nomen autem substantiæ, quod simplicius insertum est à Patribus, & à populo non intellectum, scandalum pariebat, quoniam tale in scripturis non extat, tolli placuit, & de cætero nullam penitus fieri mentionem substantiæ, maxime cum divinæ literæ Patris, & Filii substantiæ nusquam meminerint.* Sic errarunt Patres illi vel ab Arianis callide deceptis, vel à Constantio itidem Ariani coacti. Hunc tamen Ariminensem Conventum rejecit Liberius primum ob id ab eodem Constantio exilio actus, postea Felix II. ex ea causa è Sede sua dejectus, & occulte gladio necatus, & cui pro viribus restitit. S. Gaudentius tunc Ariminensis Episcopus eumdem postea rescindens; ob quod à Proconsulis militibus trucidatus est, ut refert Baronius ad *Martyrolog.* 14. *Octob.* sic sævissime agentibus Arianis. | | 74 Afterwards, in the Assembly, or Conciliabulum, of Sirmium in the year 357, a similar heretical blasphemy was uttered by some: *Now the word* (said they) *substance*, (which is the same as Homousion) *because it was set down rather simply by the Fathers, and is not understood by the peoples, and brings scandal, because it is not contained in the scriptures, it has pleased that it be taken out of the way, and that hereafter no mention be made of the substance of God [?], because the sacred Scriptures never make mention of the substance of the Father and of the Son: but we say the Son is like in all things, as the sacred letters say and teach.* This heresy of Arius, this blasphemy of Hosius and Potamius, a Council in Gaul rejected in the year 359, whose Synodical letter is not extant, yet notice of which Council Hilary exhibits to us, writing On the Synods to the Bishops of Gaul, and Epiphanius, *hæres.* 73, and Phoebadius, Bishop of Aquitaine, in his book against the Arians. Afterwards, in the Synod, or Conciliabulum, of Ariminum in the same year, the Fathers of Ariminum, before, terrified by Constantine, they turned aside from the way of truth, decreed with the Nicene Synod that the same word was firmly to be held, as is evident from their profession: *Also that the name of substance, and the thing, insinuated into our minds by many holy scriptures, ought to obtain its firmness. Which thing, together with its name, the Catholic Church, with deific doctrine, has ever been accustomed to confess.* But that Synod prevaricated, terrified by Constantius, and said that the same word was to be rejected, as is evident from the letter to Constantius, and from the formula of faith sent to the Eastern Arian Bishops, where the Fathers of the Conciliabulum of Ariminum speak thus: *But the name of substance, which was rather simply inserted by the Fathers, and, not being understood by the people, begat scandal, since no such thing stands in the scriptures, it has pleased that it be removed, and that for the rest no mention at all be made of substance, especially since the divine letters nowhere make mention of the substance of the Father and of the Son.* Thus those Fathers erred, either craftily deceived by the Arians, or coerced by Constantius, himself likewise an Arian. Yet this Assembly of Ariminum was rejected by Liberius, who first on that account was driven into exile by the same Constantius; afterwards by Felix II, who for that cause was cast down from his See and secretly slain by the sword, and who resisted it with all his strength. St. Gaudentius, then Bishop of Ariminum, afterwards rescinding the same; for which he was butchered by the soldiers of the Proconsul, as Baronius relates at the *Martyrolog.* 14. *Octob.*, the Arians acting thus most savagely. |
| 75 Hi sunt Patres, quos Muratorius Nicænis Patribus contraponit: hi sunt, qui vocem *Homousion* reprobaverunt: hi sunt, qui Filium consubstantialem Patri esse negabant: hi sunt, quos adducit ad confirmandum non habere Ecclesiam peculiarem divini Numinis assistentiam ad explicandas proprias, & necessarias voces, quibus fidei mysteria proponantur. Non crederem, ni legissem. Enuntiationem *Homousion* dicit in uno sensu aliquibus Patribus displicuisse, in alio verò ab aliis fuisse approbatam. Superest, quod explicet, quam aliam significationem nomen illud propriam habere possit, expromatque, an sit, vel non sit de fide substantia, seu consubstantialitas, ut eam jure aliquo exploderent Antiocheni Patres. Id tamen nullatenus fecisse, immo vocem *Homousion* recepisse, aperte constat; nam in tertio Concilio Antiocheno admissa fuit Concilii Nicæni fides, receptumque *Homousion*, sive consubstantialitatis infallibile dogma, ut ex ejus contextu patet. Sic in eodem Concilio habetur: *Sed ne cum his, qui adulterant veritatis dogma, adnumeremur, tuæ pietati suggerimus, quoniam sancti Concilii in Nicæa olim, & ab initio celebrati fidem & suscepimus, & tenemus. Quando etiam consubstantialitatis nomen, quod aliter quibusdam se habere videtur, dicimus cautam interpretationem à Patribus excepisse; quippe significantibus, quod ex substantia Patris natus sit Filius, & quia similis per substantiam Patri est. Non enim velut aliqua passio circa ineffabilem nativitatem creditur, neque secundum aliquem paganorum usum percipitur à Patribus substantiæ nomen dictum; sed ad destructionem Arianorum, qui præsumpserunt ex non extantibus de Christo aliquid prædicare. Quod etiam isti, qui nunc inæqualitatem asserunt, asperius, & audacius ad vastandam Ecclesiæ concordiam imprudentes ausi sunt perpetrare. Quamobrem huic nostræ relationi subjunximus exemplar fidei Nicæni Concilii, quam etiam decenter amplectimur. Est enim hujusmodi: credimus in unum Deum Patrem omnipotentem, &c.* Et in Synodo Ephesina supra relata constat, confirmata, & approbata à Nicænis Patribus fuisse quæ in Antiochena Synodo contra Paulum Samosatenum acta fuerant: *A magna, & sancta trecentorum decem, & octo Episcoporum Synodo apud Nicæam habita, quæ sententiam eorum, qui Anthiochiæ contra Paulum Samozateno convenerant, confirmavit, & approbavit.* | | 75 These are the Fathers whom Muratori sets against the Nicene Fathers: these are they who reprobated the word *Homousion*: these are they who denied that the Son is consubstantial with the Father: these are they whom he adduces to confirm that the Church does not have a peculiar assistance of the divine Godhead for explaining the proper and necessary words by which the mysteries of faith are to be proposed. I should not believe it, had I not read it. He says that the enunciation *Homousion* displeased some Fathers in one sense, but in another was approved by others. It remains for him to explain what other proper signification that name can have, and to bring forth whether substance, or consubstantiality, is or is not *de fide*, so that the Antiochene Fathers might by some right have exploded it. Yet that they in no wise did this, nay, that they received the word *Homousion*, is openly evident; for in the third Council of Antioch the faith of the Council of Nicaea was admitted, and the *Homousion*, or infallible dogma of consubstantiality, was received, as is plain from its context. Thus it is contained in the same Council: *But lest we be numbered with those who adulterate the dogma of truth, we suggest to your piety that we have both received, and hold, the faith of the holy Council formerly, and from the beginning, celebrated at Nicaea. Where also we say that the name of consubstantiality, which seems to some to stand otherwise, received a cautious interpretation from the Fathers; who signified, namely, that the Son was born of the substance of the Father, and that he is like the Father through substance. For it is not believed as though there were some passion about the ineffable nativity, nor is the name of substance perceived to have been said by the Fathers according to any usage of the pagans; but for the destruction of the Arians, who presumed to predicate something of Christ out of things non-existent. Which also those who now assert inequality have, more harshly and more boldly, imprudently dared to perpetrate for the laying waste of the concord of the Church. Wherefore to this our relation we have subjoined a copy of the faith of the Nicene Council, which also we becomingly embrace. For it is of this sort: we believe in one God the Father almighty, etc.* And in the Synod of Ephesus related above it is evident that the things which had been done in the Antiochene Synod against Paul of Samosata were confirmed and approved by the Nicene Fathers: *By the great and holy Synod of three hundred and eighteen Bishops held at Nicaea, which confirmed and approved the sentence of those who had assembled at Antioch against Paul of Samosata.* |
| 76 Si ergo Samozatenus æqualitatem, seu consubstantialitatem, sive *Houmousion* negabat, & Antiocheni Patres eum ob id damnaverant, quomodo dici poterit, eamdem Synodum prædictam vocem, ejusque significationem reprobasse? Non quæstio erat de voce, sed de dogmate, adnitentibus hæreticis Filium non esse consubstantialem, sed similem Patri: Orthodoxis verò firmiter asserentibus eum Patri consubstantialem esse, à qua veritate nequaquam ipsi quandoque recesserunt. Si autem contentio erat de voce, non ea versabatur circa significationem vocis *Houmousion*, quæ proculdubio etiam apud ipsos hæreticos substantiam significabat: sed asserebant Ariani dicendum esse *Homoiusion*, quæ solum similitudinem exprimebat, ac proinde semper contentio ad dogma pertinebat. Concludamus ergo erravisse Muratorium, quatenus putavit eam vocem non attinere ad fidei dogma. Siquidem ut de fide recipiendam definiit sacrosancta Synodus, & etiam ante ejus definitionem de fide semper fuit Filium esse consubstantialem, & per omnia æqualem Patri. Unde licet nomen illud apud paganos, seu hæreticos aliquam significationem minus propriam habere potuisset, attamen postquam Ecclesia ejus sensum explicavit, & accepit; de fide tenendum esse nullus ambigit. Si ita non teneremus infallibiliter, quam facile foret omnem definitionis vim evertere opponendo, ibi non de jure, sed de significatione nominis actum fuisse, quod sine hæresis periculo alium sensum habere potest secundum scriptoris mentem, sicque omnes hæresum damnationes eluderentur. | | 76 If therefore the Samosatene denied the equality, or consubstantiality, or *Houmousion*, and the Antiochene Fathers had condemned him on that account, how could it be said that the same Synod reprobated the aforesaid word and its signification? The question was not about the word, but about the dogma, the heretics striving to maintain that the Son is not consubstantial with, but like, the Father: while the Orthodox firmly asserted that he is consubstantial with the Father, from which truth they themselves never at any time receded. But if there was contention about a word, it did not turn upon the signification of the word *Houmousion*, which without doubt even among the heretics themselves signified substance: but the Arians asserted that one must say *Homoiusion*, which expressed only likeness, and hence the contention always pertained to the dogma. Let us conclude, therefore, that Muratori erred insofar as he thought that word did not pertain to the dogma of faith. Since indeed the sacrosanct Synod defined that it was to be received as *de fide*, and even before its definition it was always *de fide* that the Son is consubstantial with, and in all things equal to, the Father. Whence, although that name among pagans or heretics might have had some less proper signification, nevertheless, after the Church explained and accepted its sense, no one doubts that it is to be held *de fide*. If we did not hold thus infallibly, how easy it would be to overturn all the force of a definition by objecting that there it was a question not of the right, but of the signification of a name, which without danger of heresy can have another sense according to the mind of the writer; and thus all condemnations of heresies would be eluded. |
| 77 Quod attinet ad aliud exemplum Zozimi circa Pelagii, & Cœlestii errores, & disceptationem cum Africano Concilio, mihi videtur Muratorium à vero quæstionis statu aberrasse. Zozimus nunquam definitive probavit eorum hæreticorum libros, aut errores in eis contentos. Quæstio omnis in eo sita erat, an damnandi essent, ut hæretici, aut Cœlestius, aut Pelagius. Renuebat Zozimus, & merito; nam Pelagius libellum suum cum vera fidei professione obtulit Pontifici, protestatus, quod si *aliquid minus caute, minusque perite forte positum esset*, id judicio Ecclesiæ subjiciebat cupiens emendari: idemque Cœlestii libellus continebat, in quo tamen expresse circa peccatum originale erravisse visus est. Maxima tamen Zozimo calumnia infertur, dum asseritur, eum illorum errorem: ante admonitionem Africanorum Patrum approbasse. Cognovit Pontifex errorem; sed quia Cœlestius se sanctæ Sedis judicio subjicere paratum esse affirmabat, voluit cum eo benignius agere, & ad resipiscentiam inducere, antequam eum ut hæreticum damnaret. Verba autem Zozimi *epist* 4. *Estne ullus locus, in quo Dei gratia, vel adjutorium prætermissum sit?* Respiciunt ipsius Pelagii libellum, in quo sic promtebatur[?]: *Liberum sic esse profitemur arbitrium, ut dicamus nos semper Dei indigere auxilio, & tam illos errare, qui cum Manichæis dicunt, hominem peccatum vitare non posse, quam illos, qui cum Joviniano asserunt, hominem non posse peccare; uterque enim tollit libertatem arbitrii. Nos verò dicimus hominem semper & peccare, & non peccare posse, ut semper nos liberi confiteamur esse arbitrii.* In quo ita ovinam pellem induit, ut nihil fidei non consonum asserere videretur. Quod autem asserebat circa gratiam Dei, & sinistrum sensum, quo eam accipiebat, Zozimo judicialiter non constabat, ideo de eo sensu, antequam causa omnis Sedis Apostolicæ judicio discuteretur, definire non constituerat. Hinc est, quod dum quæstionis examen pendebat, ultimum infallibile judicium proferri æquum non erat. Ubi verò constitit de perverso hæretici sensu, & de pervicacia, qua errorem damnare recusaverat, & error, & ejus perversus sensus, ipsique hæretici infallibili judicio condemnati sunt. Videantur Augustinus, & Prosper in Baronio, qui ad annum Christi 418. omnia ad rem concernentia clarissime exponit. | | 77 As regards the other example, of Zosimus concerning the errors of Pelagius and Coelestius, and the dispute with the African Council, it seems to me that Muratori has strayed from the true state of the question. Zosimus never definitively approved the books of those heretics, or the errors contained in them. The whole question lay in this, whether either Coelestius or Pelagius were to be condemned as heretics. Zosimus refused, and with reason; for Pelagius offered his booklet to the Pontiff with a true profession of faith, protesting that if *anything perchance had been set down less cautiously, and less skilfully*, he submitted it to the judgment of the Church, desiring to be corrected: and the booklet of Coelestius contained the same, in which nevertheless he seemed to have erred expressly concerning original sin. Yet a very great calumny is brought against Zosimus, when it is asserted that he approved their error before the admonition of the African Fathers. The Pontiff knew the error; but because Coelestius affirmed that he was ready to submit himself to the judgment of the holy See, he wished to deal more benignly with him, and to lead him to repentance, before he should condemn him as a heretic. But the words of Zosimus, *epist* 4, *Is there any place in which the grace, or help, of God is passed over?* regard the booklet of Pelagius himself, in which it was thus set forth[?]: *We so profess free will as to say that we always need the help of God, and that they err as much who with the Manichaeans say that man cannot avoid sin, as they who with Jovinian assert that man cannot sin; for each takes away the liberty of the will. But we say that man is always able both to sin and not to sin, so that we may always confess ourselves to be of free will.* In which he so put on the sheep's skin, that he seemed to assert nothing not consonant with the faith. But what he asserted concerning the grace of God, and the sinister sense in which he took it, was not judicially established for Zosimus; therefore he had not resolved to define concerning that sense before the whole cause should be examined by the judgment of the Apostolic See. Hence it is that, while the examination of the question was pending, it was not fitting that the final infallible judgment be pronounced. But when the perverse sense of the heretic was established, and the obstinacy with which he had refused to condemn the error, both the error, and its perverse sense, and the heretics themselves, were condemned by infallible judgment. Let Augustine and Prosper be seen in Baronius, who at the year of Christ 418 most clearly sets forth all things concerning the matter. |
| 78 Nunc aliqua breviori methodo lubet adnotare Theologorum judicio relinquenda. *Pag.* 160. sic habet: *Nostris autem temporibus quamquam tot animorum seditiones excitarit quinque propositionum damnatio in sensu à Jansenio intento, & postremo hanc in rem habeamus Clementis XI. Pontificis Maximi oracula, nondum tamen sancitum aperte fuit, spectare ad fidem (quod est ad Jansenii sensum) Romanæ Sedis, seu Ecclesiæ totius alioqui venerandam sententiam.* Propono Theologis Decretum Alexandri VII. die 31. Maii 1653. & doctrinam optimi controversistæ Ardeskin *dict. cap. 9. q. 5.* & P. Vivæ ad prædictas Jansenii theses, ut videant quanta fraude laborent citata verba. | | 78 Now it pleases me to note, by a briefer method, some things to be left to the judgment of the Theologians. *Pag.* 160 has thus: *But in our times although the condemnation of the five propositions in the sense intended by Jansenius has stirred up so many seditions of minds, and although at last we have upon this matter the oracles of Clement XI, Supreme Pontiff, nevertheless it has not yet been openly sanctioned that the otherwise venerable sentence of the Roman See, or of the whole Church, pertains to the faith (that is, as to the sense of Jansenius).* I set before the Theologians the Decree of Alexander VII of the 31st of May 1653, and the doctrine of that excellent controversialist Ardeskin, *dict. cap. 9. q. 5.*, and of Fr. Viva on the aforesaid theses of Jansenius, that they may see under how great a fraud the cited words labor. |
| 79 Eadem *pag.*... sic scribit: *Tum verò explorata traditio dicitur, cum ex Patrum, Theologorum, & Conciliorum documentis Episcopi evidenter intelligunt, an doctrina quæpiam tradita, an potius improbata à maioribus nostris fuerit.* Et pag. 163. *Et insuper si dogmatum, & scripturarum vera interpretatio, atque definitio haurienda est è sanctorum Patrum, Conciliorumque præcedentium documentis, ita hauriendam esse constat, necesse est ut Ecclesiastici judices hujusmodi monumenta, eorumque sensus intelligere &c.* Ex his verbis, cæteroque contextu hæc videtur sensisse. 1. Infallibilem Pontificis definitionem à Patribus, & Conciliis dependere. 2. Non posse Pontifices Maximos definire, nisi quod antea à Conciliis definitum fuerit. 3. Pontifices errare posse in novis dogmatibus impugnandis, quæ frequenter à Novatoribus sparguntur. 4. Solum à maioribus nostris tradita esse evidenter vera, vel ab ipsis improbata evidenter falsa. Si fallor, aut fallitur ipse, Doctorum judicio libentissime submitto discernendum. Sed quæri potest, antiquis Conciliis quis firmissimum præstiterit fidei robur? Quis sanctos Doctores, Conciliorumque Patres eam infallibilem edocuit veritatem? Quis nos certos fecit, eos scripturas, persæpe obscurissimas, ejusque arcanos sensus indubie calluisse? Inferrem igitur, omnem infallibilem interpretandi veros Patrum, & Conciliorum sensus regulam in Petri successore, ac Christi Vicario residere. Quod privilegium, si quoad omnes collectim sumptos libenter conceditur, quare quoad quemlibet eorum non dabitur? Si ea suprema authoritas Pontifici Maximo non denegatur in Scripturæ sacræ vero sensu aperiendo, cur negabitur in recipienda alicujus orthodoxi Patris catholica doctrina, ejusque vero sensu depromendo, cum istæ Patrum sententiæ ita fere semper conceptæ sint, ut dissimili verborum compagine designentur? | | 79 On the same *pag.*... he writes thus: *But then is a tradition said to be ascertained, when from the documents of the Fathers, Theologians, and Councils the Bishops evidently understand whether some doctrine was handed down, or rather condemned, by our forefathers.* And on pag. 163: *And moreover, if the true interpretation and definition of dogmas and of the scriptures is to be drawn from the documents of the holy Fathers and of preceding Councils — and it is agreed that it must be so drawn — it is necessary that the Ecclesiastical judges understand monuments of this kind, and their senses, etc.* From these words, and from the rest of the context, he seems to have held the following. 1. That the infallible definition of the Pontiff depends upon the Fathers and Councils. 2. That the Supreme Pontiffs cannot define anything except what has previously been defined by Councils. 3. That the Pontiffs can err in assailing new dogmas, which are frequently spread abroad by the Innovators. 4. That only the things handed down by our forefathers are evidently true, or the things condemned by them evidently false. Whether I am deceived, or he himself is deceived, I most willingly submit to the judgment of the Doctors to discern. But it may be asked: who furnished to the ancient Councils their most firm strength of faith? Who taught the holy Doctors, and the Fathers of the Councils, that infallible truth? Who has made us certain that they had undoubted mastery of the scriptures, very often most obscure, and of their hidden senses? I should infer, therefore, that every infallible rule of interpreting the true senses of the Fathers and Councils resides in the successor of Peter and Vicar of Christ. And if this privilege is freely conceded as regards all of them taken collectively, why shall it not be granted as regards any one of them? If that supreme authority is not denied to the Supreme Pontiff in laying open the true sense of sacred Scripture, why shall it be denied in receiving the catholic doctrine of some orthodox Father, and in drawing forth its true sense, since these sentences of the Fathers are almost always so framed that they are expressed in a dissimilar structure of words? |
| 80 *Pag. 164.* sic profert: *Quapropter, quamvis in quibusdam œcumenicis Conciliis, & plerumque à Dogmaticis, & Polemicis Theologis, loca sanctorum Patrum afferuntur in confirmationem sacrorum dogmatum, & ecclesiasticarum sanctionum; eorum tamen singillatim sumptorum sensus ad fidem non pertinet, neque divino assensu credendum est, singula ea verba, & phrases eum continere sensum, quem Episcopi, & Theologi intelligunt.* Hic misere Theologorum doctrinalem interpretationem, immo & Conciliorum, cum non ad definitionem, sed ad eruditionem iis authoritatibus utantur, & interpretationem authenticam Ecclesiæ confundere, Theologi, ut credo, judicabunt. | | 80 On *pag. 164.* he thus sets forth: *Wherefore, although in certain ecumenical Councils, and very often by Dogmatic and Polemical Theologians, passages of the holy Fathers are adduced in confirmation of sacred dogmas and ecclesiastical sanctions, yet the sense of these taken singly does not pertain to the faith, nor is it to be believed with divine assent that those individual words and phrases contain that sense which the Bishops and Theologians understand.* Here, as I believe, the Theologians will judge that he wretchedly confounds the doctrinal interpretation of the Theologians — nay, even of the Councils, when they use those authorities not for definition but for instruction — with the authentic interpretation of the Church. |
| 81 Eodem loco sic scribit: *Qua in re patet nihil novi tunc à Deo revelari, nullumque novum factum divinitus statui, sed divinam opem tantummodo adesse, ut quod jam antea revelatum fuerat, tunc à judicibus sine errore deprehendatur &c.* Hæc *divina ops* à revelatione, seu infallibili Spiritus Sancti assistentia in quo distinguitur? Et, quando sine errore deprehenditur factum illud *divinitus statutum*, non cadit ipsa Ecclesiæ definitio super illo facto? Quomodo declarari certo potest reconditissimus multoties Scripturæ sensus, nisi iste verus sensus Ecclesiæ, aut Pontifici aut reveletur, aut inspiretur? Quomomo sciri potest factum illud *divinitus statutum*, nisi Ecclesia doceat! Quomodo verò Ecclesia id infallibiliter edocere valet, nisi superiori lumine illustretur? Quomodo tandem dogmata certa esse queunt, ni certa sint etiam facta ad fidem spectantia, & cum eodem dogmate connexa? Spero in hac re non à Muratorio, neque ab hethorodoxis, sed ab orthodoxis Theologis erudiri. | | 81 In the same place he writes thus: *In which matter it is plain that nothing new is then revealed by God, and that no new fact is divinely established, but only that divine aid is present, so that what had already been revealed before may then be detected by the judges without error, etc.* In what is this *divine aid* distinguished from revelation, or from the infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit? And, when that fact *divinely established* is detected without error, does not the Church's very definition fall upon that fact? How can the oftentimes most hidden sense of Scripture be declared with certainty, unless that true sense be either revealed or inspired to the Church, or to the Pontiff? How can that fact *divinely established* be known, unless the Church teach it! But how is the Church able to teach this infallibly, unless she be illumined by a higher light? How, finally, can dogmas be certain, unless the facts pertaining to the faith, and connected with the same dogma, be certain also? I hope in this matter to be instructed not by Muratori, nor by the heterodox, but by orthodox Theologians. |
| 82 Sequenti pagina sic statuit: *Certum est unumquemque fidelium, simul ac evidenter cognovit dogma aliquod ab omnibus, aut plerisque Patribus tradi, consentire statim, illudque amplecti debere, etiamsi nulla Ecclesiæ definitio præcesserit.* Qui ergo requirit, quod *aperte sancitum sit ad fidem spectare* Ecclesiæ sententiam circa damnatas propositiones Jansenii quoad ejus sensum, si omnes Theologi verè tales id ad fidei quæstionem referant, idque apud doctos evidenter constet? An autem fideles de fide credere teneantur, quod ab Ecclesia nondum est definitum de fide, ex eo solum, quod ea sententia in sanctis Patribus habeatur, dicent Theologi. Mirum mihi videtur non teneri de fide credere quod Summus Pontifex circa sensus, factaque fidem concernentia definiit; teneri autem de fide credere, quod evidenter constat in sanctis Patribus doctrinaliter tantum scriptum inveniri: si hoc non est Pontificis authoritatem indetectibilem Patrum sententijs postponere, hasque pluris facere, equidem decipior. Sed hoc Novatorum systema esse, pluries apud Authores me legisse memini. | | 82 On the following page he thus lays down: *It is certain that each one of the faithful, as soon as he has evidently known that some dogma is handed down by all, or by most, of the Fathers, ought at once to consent, and to embrace it, even if no definition of the Church has preceded.* How then does he require that the sentence of the Church concerning the condemned propositions of Jansenius, as to his sense, be *openly sanctioned to pertain to the faith*, if all Theologians truly such refer this to a question of faith, and this is evidently established among the learned? But whether the faithful are bound to believe *de fide* what has not yet been defined by the Church as *de fide*, solely because that sentence is found in the holy Fathers, the Theologians will say. It seems strange to me that one should not be bound to believe *de fide* what the Supreme Pontiff has defined concerning senses and facts concerning the faith; but should be bound to believe *de fide* what is evidently established to be found written in the holy Fathers only doctrinally: if this is not to set the indefectible authority of the Pontiff after the sentences of the Fathers, and to value these more highly, then indeed I am deceived. But that this is the system of the Innovators, I remember to have read many times in the Authors. |
| 83 Eadem pagina sic habet: *Pelagianam pestem Julianus tuebatur, quibusdam pessime intellectis Patrum locis.* En cui periculo se exponerent fideles, si cuique datum esset eorum sententias perscrutari, & quod sibi persuasum est, de fide amplecti. Errarent frequentissime, ut errarunt hæretici omnes, ex maiori parte in Patrum sententiis versatissimi, & semel concepta sententia sua difficillimæ ab errore discederent, ut in Novatoribus experientia compertum est. Prosequitur: *At Augustinus à se stare.... & Irinæum, Cypriamum &c.* Augustino, etiamsi Ecclesiæ Doctori, opus erat suam sententiam aliorum sanctorum Patrum sententiis stabilire; quæ tamen ei infallibilis veritatis revelatæ authoritas esset, si Ecclesiæ authoritas non adstipularetur? Ad id, quod addit de calumnia à Socino, ejusque gregalibus Nicænæ Synodo objecta, ego inquirendo reponerem: Demus Nicænam Synodum nullis antiquiorum Patrum sententiis muniri, an ex eo deficeret ejus in rebus fidei inconcussa veritas? An non sufficeret plusquam trecentorum Patrum authoritas? Et, quod magis est, non sufficeret Summi Pontificis Silvestri infallibilis definitio? Qua ergo de causa Patrum sententias nobis tam frequenter obtrudunt, quæ tunc infallibiles fiunt, cum Ecclesiæ consensu roborantur? | | 83 On the same page he has thus: *Julian defended the Pelagian pest, certain passages of the Fathers being most wrongly understood.* Behold to what danger the faithful would expose themselves, if it were granted to each one to search through their sentences, and to embrace *de fide* what he has persuaded himself of. They would err most frequently, as all the heretics have erred, for the greater part most versed in the sentences of the Fathers; and, their own opinion once conceived, they would most hardly depart from error, as experience has shown in the Innovators. He continues: *But Augustine, that they stood on his side.... and Irenaeus, Cyprian, etc.* Augustine, even though a Doctor of the Church, had need to establish his own opinion by the sentences of other holy Fathers; yet what authority of infallible revealed truth would it have for him, if the authority of the Church did not lend its confirmation? To that which he adds concerning the calumny cast upon the Nicene Synod by Socinus and his fellows, I should reply by inquiring: Let us grant that the Nicene Synod is fortified by no sentences of the more ancient Fathers — would its unshaken truth in matters of faith thereby fail? Would not the authority of more than three hundred Fathers suffice? And, what is more, would not the infallible definition of the Supreme Pontiff Silvester suffice? For what cause, then, do they so frequently thrust upon us the sentences of the Fathers, which become infallible only then, when they are strengthened by the consent of the Church? |
| 84 *Cap. 20. pag. 168. & seq.* nititur persuadere Ecclesiam infallibili judicio non fulciri, cum alicujus particularis Doctoris verum, & catholicum sensum declarat. Sed oppono 1. (ut Theologi sententiam ferant) duodecim canones, quos Ephesinos dicunt, revera unius Cyrilli erant contra Nestorium, quos ipsa Ephesina Synodus ut Catholicos definiit, & approbavit, ut ex Concilio Chalcedonensi constat *act. 4.* An dicere possumus non esse de fide credendum, Cyrillum in catholico sensu locutum fuisse? Quid roboris haberet Synodus, si non posset citra erroris periculum de vero, & catholico Cyrilli sensu judicare? Oppono 2. Infallibilis fuit, ut etiam hæretici fatentur, Pontificia definitio in approbandis S. Augustini libris, præcipue contra Pelagianos, & Semipelagianos. Hæc approbatio, & definitio respiciebat verum, & orthodoxum Augustini sensum. Errare potuit Augustinus, etenim homo erat, ut de facto erraverat, cum in Manichæorum hæresim inciderat. Unde ergo ei tanta authoritas succrevit, nisi à Conciliis, & Pontificibus ejus orthodoxum sensum approbantibus, & definientibus. Si id infallibiliter decernere potuit Ecclesia circa Cyrillum, circa Augustinum, & circa alios, cur id non poterit circa cujuslibet privati Doctoris, aut vero, aut falso sensu infallibiliter definire? | | 84 In *cap. 20. pag. 168. & seq.* he strives to persuade that the Church is not supported by an infallible judgment when she declares the true and catholic sense of some particular Doctor. But I object, first (that the Theologians may pass sentence): the twelve canons which they call Ephesine were in truth those of Cyril alone against Nestorius, which the Ephesine Synod itself defined and approved as Catholic, as is established from the Council of Chalcedon, *act. 4.* Can we say that it is not to be believed *de fide* that Cyril spoke in a catholic sense? What strength would the Synod have, if it could not judge of the true and catholic sense of Cyril without danger of error? I object, secondly: the Pontifical definition in approving the books of St. Augustine, especially against the Pelagians and Semipelagians, was infallible, as even the heretics confess. This approbation and definition regarded the true and orthodox sense of Augustine. Augustine could err, for he was a man, as in fact he had erred when he had fallen into the heresy of the Manichaeans. Whence, then, did so great an authority accrue to him, except from the Councils and Pontiffs approving and defining his orthodox sense? If the Church could decree this infallibly concerning Cyril, concerning Augustine, and concerning others, why shall she not be able to define it infallibly concerning the sense, whether true or false, of any private Doctor whatsoever? |
| 85 Præteriit adnotare verba, quæ habentur *pag. 155. & seq.* Sic ait: *Tandem inter fidei dogmata nunquam referre licebit, hunc, illumve hominem hæreticum esse, aut fuisse, puta Arium, Lutherum, Calvinum.* Non alios invenit Muratorius, nisi hos manifestissimos hæresiarcas? Si hæc propositio non est ad minus piarum aurium offensiva, nullæ hujus generis theses assignabuntur. Id postea convestire intendit iis ornatissimis pigmentis, quod licet id de fide credere non teneatur, eo quod sibi per divinam revelationem non constet, attamen *Maximorum Pontificum sic judicantium sententiæ maximam deberi reverentiam, assensumque humilem, non autem divinam fidem.* Supponit, quod, ut quis teneatur assensu divino credere, necesse sit, quod *sibi per divinam revelationem* constet, quasi non sufficiat, sic ab Ecclesia definitum esse. Faxit Deus, ut ipse non sequatur eos, qui ad aliquid credendum prærequirunt privatum Spiritus Sancti testimonium. Inquiro tamen: Si quispiam diceret Lutherum non fuisse hæreticum, solum irreverentiæ, superbique assensus reus esset? Id deducendum videtur; qui enim ad nihil aliud tenetur, culpæ solummodo, de qua argui potest, commensuratam pœnam pati debet Sed Concilia aliud probant, si credimus Socrati. (25) {marg. (25) Histor. Eccles. lib. 1. cap. 10.} In Nicæna quippe Synodo pro hæreticis habiti fuere Eusebius Nicod. & Theognis Nicænus, eo quod Arii condemnationi subscribere detrectarent, eo *quod minime crederent, eum, qui erat accusatus, hominem ejus generis fuisse, qui eos errores doceret.* Ipsi damnationi errorum libenter assentiebant; sed tamen hæreticis fuere adnumerati. Hæresim nihil aliud esse, quam in fide errorem, etiam indocti norunt. Ergo ad fidem spectabat, quod Arius hæreticus esset, quia sic à Concilio definitum. Exemplum etiam præstat Concilium Constantiense, ubi ab eo, qui volebat Catholicam fidem profiteri, exigebatur, ut crederet Wicleff, Joan. Hus, & Hieronymum de Praga fuisse hæreticos, teste P. Viva *ubi supra.* Aliud exemplum affert Ardeskin *ubi sup.* deductum ex Concilio Chalcedonensi *act. 8.* Abjuraverat Theodoretus Nestorianam hæresim, sed Nestorio anathema dicere renuebat, quem Patres Concilii hæreticum declaraverant. Sed quid Patres? Unanimi sententia clamarunt: *Hæreticus est, Nestorianus est, hæreticum foras mitte.* Qui de Luthero ab Ecclesia damnato contrarium senserit, cur hæreticus dici non poterit? Id meliori judicio relinquo cogitandum. | | 85 It escaped me to note the words which are found *pag. 155. & seq.* He speaks thus: *Finally, it will never be lawful to reckon among the dogmas of faith that this or that man is, or was, a heretic — say, Arius, Luther, Calvin.* Did Muratori find no others, but these most manifest heresiarchs? If this proposition is not at least offensive to pious ears, no theses of this kind will ever be assigned. This he afterwards endeavors to clothe with these most ornate colors: that although one is not bound to believe it *de fide*, because it is not established for him by divine revelation, nevertheless *to the sentences of the Supreme Pontiffs so judging there is owed the greatest reverence, and a humble assent, but not divine faith.* He supposes that, in order for one to be bound to believe with divine assent, it is necessary that it be established *for him by divine revelation*, as if it did not suffice that it has been so defined by the Church. God grant that he himself not follow those who, for believing anything, prerequire the private testimony of the Holy Spirit. Yet I inquire: If someone should say that Luther was not a heretic, would he be guilty only of irreverence, and of a proud assent? So, it seems, it must be deduced; for he who is bound to nothing else ought to suffer only a punishment commensurate to the fault of which he can be accused. But the Councils prove otherwise, if we believe Socrates. (25) {marg. (25) Histor. Eccles. lib. 1. cap. 10.} For in the Nicene Synod Eusebius of Nicomedia and Theognis of Nicaea were held for heretics, because they refused to subscribe to the condemnation of Arius, on the ground *that they by no means believed that he who was accused was a man of the sort to teach those errors.* They willingly assented to the condemnation of the errors themselves; but nevertheless they were numbered among the heretics. That heresy is nothing other than an error in faith, even the unlearned know. Therefore it pertained to the faith that Arius was a heretic, because it was so defined by the Council. The Council of Constance also furnishes an example, where it was demanded of him who wished to profess the Catholic faith, that he believe Wyclif, John Hus, and Jerome of Prague to have been heretics, as Fr. Viva witnesses, *ubi supra.* Another example Ardeskin brings forward, *ubi sup.*, drawn from the Council of Chalcedon, *act. 8.* Theodoret had abjured the Nestorian heresy, but refused to say anathema to Nestorius, whom the Fathers of the Council had declared a heretic. But what did the Fathers do? With unanimous voice they cried out: *He is a heretic, he is a Nestorian; cast the heretic out.* He who shall have held the contrary concerning Luther, condemned by the Church — why can he not be called a heretic? This I leave to a better judgment to consider. |
| 86 Ejusdem farinæ est, quod ait *pag. 169. Ita in Jansenii negotio, dum Romana Cathedra, aut Ecclesia congregata explicet, si opus est, mea tamen sententia non est opus,* (en larva, qua contegi putat) *atque statuat, quem sensum, & quam doctrinam ipsa damnavit, damnatamque velit in quinque propositionibus Jansenio tributis: dogma fidei tunc in tuto erit, atque errori præcludentur viæ: neque ulla necessitas amplius erit credendi supernaturali assensu eamdem illam damnatam doctrinam in Jansenii verbis, atque intentione fuisse, cum id scire, aut credere, nescire, aut negare merum factum respiciat, à quo doctrina fidei nequaquam pendet.* Noto 1. illa verba: *Jansenio tributis*; quasi sic dubitet, an revera Jansenii fuissent, ut Jansenistæ negant. Noto 2. alia verba: *Dogma fidei tunc in tuto erit*; quasi in tuto non sit per sanctæ Sedis expressissimam damnationem. Noto 3. alia verba: *Quem sensum, & quam doctrinam ipsa damnavit*; veluti si in Pontificiis Diplomatibus id expressum non habeatur. aut non satis sit declarare errores, à quibus fideles fugere teneantur. Tandem iterum noto illa verba: *Merum tantummodo factum respiciat*; ac si liberum, licitumque sit aut credere, aut negare illam doctrinam in sensu, & verbis Jansenii contineri, cum tamen contrarium ab Alexandro VII. sit decretum. | | 86 Of the same flour is what he says *pag. 169.*: *So in the affair of Jansenius, provided the Roman Chair, or the Church assembled, explain — if there is need; yet in my opinion there is no need —* (behold the mask under which he thinks to be covered) *and determine what sense and what doctrine she herself has condemned, and wills to be condemned, in the five propositions attributed to Jansenius: the dogma of faith will then be in safety, and the ways will be barred to error; nor will there any longer be any necessity of believing with supernatural assent that that same condemned doctrine was in the words and intention of Jansenius, since to know or believe this, or not to know or to deny it, regards a mere fact, upon which the doctrine of faith in no wise depends.* I note, first, those words: *attributed to Jansenius*; as if he thus doubted whether they were really Jansenius's, as the Jansenists deny. I note, secondly, other words: *the dogma of faith will then be in safety*; as if it were not in safety through the most express condemnation of the holy See. I note, thirdly, other words: *what sense and what doctrine she herself has condemned*; as though this were not had expressly in the Pontifical Diplomas, or it were not enough to declare the errors from which the faithful are bound to flee. Finally, I note once more those words: *regards only a mere fact*; as if it were free and lawful either to believe or to deny that that doctrine is contained in the sense and words of Jansenius, whereas nevertheless the contrary has been decreed by Alexander VII. |
| 87 Ut fallibilitatem Ecclesiæ in damnandis privatorum librorum sensibus ostendat, alia congerit *pag. 171.* & in contrarium sentientes sic audacter scribit: *Ita argutentur quidem, splendida in speciem, sed fallaci tamen, & temeraria consecutione.* An modeste, veraciterque dictum, recogitent alii, dum ego, ut eodem ære rependam, Vigilii Pontificis verba in ejus indolis homines commemoro: *Cum.... consuetudo sit,* (ait ille in suo Constituto) *venena sua fucatis celare sermonibus, & verba præmittere, quæ quasi orthodoxis videantur professionibus consonare, & reservare indulgentia malignos aditus, per quos eorum pestilentia intromittatur erroris, sicut de eis Propheta testatur:* Mollierunt sermones suos super oleum, & ipsi sunt jacula; *& iterum:* Quoniam in multis erant mecum: *De quibus etiam & Doctor gentium dicit Apostolus.* Habentes formam pietatis, virtutem autem ejus abnegantes. | | 87 In order to show the fallibility of the Church in condemning the senses of the books of private men, he heaps up other things *pag. 171.*, and of those who hold the contrary he boldly writes thus: *So indeed they may argue, with a consequence splendid in appearance, but yet fallacious and rash.* Whether this be said modestly and truthfully, let others reconsider, while I, to repay in the same coin, recall the words of Pope Vigilius against men of that character: *Since.... it is their custom,* (says he in his Constitutum) *to hide their poisons under painted speeches, and to set forth words which may seem, as it were, to be consonant with orthodox professions, and by indulgence to reserve malignant entrances, through which the pestilence of their error may be let in, as the Prophet witnesses of them:* They have made their speeches smoother than oil, and the same are darts; *and again:* For among many they were with me: *Of whom also the Doctor of the Gentiles, the Apostle, says:* Having the form of godliness, but denying the power thereof. |
| 88 Postea prosequitur: *Fieri potest, ut ex aliis monumentis post judicium primum detectis, & ex diligentiori locutionum, verborumque collatione ille, qui antea prave sentire putabatur, in recta sententia fuisse postea deprehendatur, ejusque verba commodius, & accuratius explicata, ab impietate liberentur.* Recte dictum, si de facti quæstione tantum actum sit. Sed ipse expresse agit de factis connexis cum ipsa de fide quæstione, ut demonstrant sequentia verba: *Quando ejus naturæ judicium non est, ut necessario veritas cum ipso conjuncta incedat: quod in ecclesiasticis de doctrina, seu dogmate judiciis rite peractis semper fieri, est apud nos exploratum. Ex adverso etiam contingere potest, ut re melius explorata, atque discussa, qui Author, qui liber antea ab Ecclesia aut probatus, aut absolutus fuerit, male sensisse, & hæretica dogmata continere postea noscatur, & æquissime tandem damnetur.* Si hæc vera essent, nullum certum in hac re Ecclesiæ judicium esset, mutationibusque obnoxium profiteremur; quod quam periculosum sit, quam garrientium Novatorum dicteriis subjectum, cæcus solus non viderit. Quamobrem potius assentior sententiæ Ardekin *dict. q. 3.* qui veritati orthodoxæ conformior sic ait: *Ex dictis hactenus dilucide sequitur nurquam fieri, aut sperari posse, ut aliqua eadem propositio sive in materia juris, sive facti, semel ab Ecclesia, aut Pontifice damnata, postea definiatur ut vera, vel è contra, ut præclare deducit P. Bruno Neasser vir eximie doctus in suo Prodromo velitari p. 1. cap. 3.* | | 88 Afterwards he continues: *It can happen that, from other monuments discovered after the first judgment, and from a more diligent collation of expressions and words, he who before was thought to hold perversely may afterwards be found to have been of right opinion, and his words, more fitly and accurately explained, may be freed from impiety.* Rightly said, if it were a question only of fact that was treated. But he himself expressly treats of facts connected with the very question of faith, as the following words demonstrate: *When a judgment is not of such a nature that truth necessarily walks conjoined with it — which, in ecclesiastical judgments concerning doctrine, or dogma, duly carried out, we hold as ascertained always to happen. On the other side it can also come to pass that, the matter being better explored and discussed, an Author or a book which before had been either approved or absolved by the Church may afterwards be known to have held ill, and to contain heretical dogmas, and at last most justly be condemned.* If these things were true, there would be no certain judgment of the Church in this matter, and we should profess it liable to changes; and how dangerous this is, how subject to the gibes of the prattling Innovators, only a blind man would fail to see. Wherefore I rather assent to the opinion of Ardekin, *dict. q. 3.*, who, more conformable to orthodox truth, speaks thus: *From what has been said thus far it clearly follows that it can never happen, or be hoped, that any same proposition, whether in matter of right or of fact, once condemned by the Church, or by the Pontiff, should afterwards be defined as true, or contrariwise; as Fr. Bruno Neasser, a man eminently learned, excellently deduces in his Prodromus velitaris p. 1. cap. 3.* |
| 89 Neque Muratorio favet, quod in suæ sententiæ comprobationem ibidem adducit: *Ex hac regula* (ait) *Ibæ epistolam, atque Theodoreti capitula, quæ in Concilio Chalcedonensi censuram Patrum effugerant, eorum auctoribus catholicæ communioni redditis, deinde œcumenica Synodus V. execrata est, impiisque sententiis, atque erroribus scatere deprehensa justissimo anathemate perculit.* De Theodoreto jam diximus. De Iba nunc loquemur. Deceptus est Muratorius, quia non fontes adiit, sed ex quodam, nescio quo scriptore, excerpsit quod scripserit. Nam in primis, quod attinet ad V. Synodum, alias Constantinopolitanam II. eam œcumenicam fuisse non est omnino indubium; gravissimi namque scriptores id non sentiunt, tum quia Occidentales Patres nec adessent, nec vocati fuissent, tum quia Vigilius Romanus Pontifex, cum tunc Constantinopoli ageret, neque per se, neque per legatos suos ei interfuisset, neque ab eodem recepta, & approbata fuerit. Nam quæ legitur ejusdem Vigilii adprobatio non integræ fidei apud aliquos habetur; constat enim invicto roboris animo restitisse, & ob id à Justiniano Imperatore in exilium missum fuisse. Alii verò dicunt Vigilium carceri mancipatum, inedia, & squalore confectum, tandem tandem amissa spiritus constantia, ut liberaretur à vinculis, trium capitulorum damnationi in V. Synodo factæ consensisse, eo quia nihil in ea actum fuerit, quod Chalcedonensi Synodo detraheret. Sed hanc confirmationem, epistolamque, in qua ea continetur, supposititiam esse asserit P. Joannes Granerius in notis ad Breviarium Liberati. Immo ipse Vigilius in epistola ad Valentinianum Episcopum hujus imposturæ conqueritur. Eo vel magis, quod hujus epistolæ nullæ inveniantur literæ prototypæ, sed manuscripta exemplaria. | | 89 Nor does that favor Muratori which he adduces in the same place in proof of his opinion: *By this rule* (he says) *the epistle of Ibas, and the chapters of Theodoret, which in the Council of Chalcedon had escaped the censure of the Fathers, their authors being restored to catholic communion, the fifth ecumenical Synod afterwards execrated, and, having found them to teem with impious sentences and errors, struck them with a most just anathema.* Of Theodoret we have already spoken. Of Ibas we shall now speak. Muratori was deceived, because he did not go to the sources, but excerpted what he wrote from some writer, I know not whom. For in the first place, as regards the fifth Synod, otherwise the second of Constantinople, it is not altogether beyond doubt that it was ecumenical; for most weighty writers do not hold this: both because the Western Fathers were neither present, nor had been summoned; and because Vigilius the Roman Pontiff, though he was then dwelling at Constantinople, took part in it neither in person nor through his legates, nor was it received and approved by him. For the approbation of the same Vigilius which is read is held by some to be not of entire credit; for it is established that he resisted with an unconquered strength of soul, and on that account was sent into exile by the Emperor Justinian. Others, however, say that Vigilius, consigned to prison, worn out by hunger and squalor, at long last, the constancy of his spirit failing, in order that he might be freed from his chains, consented to the condemnation of the three chapters made in the fifth Synod, on the ground that nothing had been done in it which would detract from the Synod of Chalcedon. But Fr. Joannes Granerius, in his notes on the Breviarium of Liberatus, asserts that this confirmation, and the epistle in which it is contained, is supposititious. Nay, Vigilius himself, in his epistle to the Bishop Valentinian, complains of this imposture. And the more so, because of this epistle no original letters are found, but manuscript copies. |
| 90 Quidquid de hoc sit, illud certum est, Synodum illam non Pontificia authoritate, sed pessimis auspiciis inceptam fuisse, multisque postea dissentionibus dedisse causam, eo quod suasu Theodori Cæsariensis, ut Origeni, Acephalis, & Eutychianis faveret, ab Imperatore Justiniano in omnes, qui tria capitula damnare renuebant, sæviente, exilii pœna indicta fuerat, atque firmitatis robur accepisse posteaquam vel ab ipso Vigilio, vel à Pelagio II. etiam persecutionum metu adacto, vel à Gregorio I. approbata fuit, utpote in qua nihil contra fidem, aut contra œcumenica Concilia continebatur, ut ipse testatur *lib. 3. epist. 37.* ad Constantium Mediolanensem Episcopum; vel à Nicolao I. epist. ad Michaelem Imperatorem, vel tandem à VI. & VII.
