Synodo, ut refert citatus Cabassurius *pag. 440.* qui addit, quod *urgente ipsa Justiniani tyrannide nihil facere consultius poterant Romani Pontifices, quam ut cederent illius voluntati, quia sic occurrendum erat certissimo, & inevitabili totius Orientis adversus Romanam Ecclesiam schismati.* | | 90 Whatever may be the case with this, this much is certain: that that Synod was begun not by Pontifical authority, but under the worst auspices, and afterwards gave occasion to many dissensions, since, at the persuasion of Theodore of Caesarea, that he might favor Origen, the Acephali, and the Eutychians, the penalty of exile had been proclaimed by the Emperor Justinian, raging against all who refused to condemn the three chapters; and that it received the strength of firmness after it was approved either by Vigilius himself, or by Pelagius II, he too driven by fear of persecutions, or by Gregory I, inasmuch as nothing was contained in it against the faith, or against the ecumenical Councils, as he himself witnesses *lib. 3. epist. 37.* to Constantius, Bishop of Milan; or by Nicholas I, in his epistle to the Emperor Michael; or finally by the sixth and seventh Synods, as the cited Cabassutius reports, *pag. 440.*, who adds that *under the very pressure of Justinian's tyranny the Roman Pontiffs could do nothing more advisedly than to yield to his will, because thus there had to be met a most certain and inevitable schism of the whole East against the Roman Church.* |
| 91 Quibus sic obiter notatis, deceptus est Muratorius, ut dicebam; siquidem in Chalcedonensi Synodo nihil de fide, quod attinet ad Ibæ causam, actum est, sed tantum de mero facto, quod spectabat ad ipsius Ibæ accusationem, & nullitatem sententiæ contra eum latæ in Pseudo-Synodo antecedenti, ut testis est Gregorius I. in citata epistola in his verbis: *Sed post expressas Synodos, seu canones facta consentio, & extrema actio de personis ventilata est.* Id ex ipsis Synodi actis expresse liquet actione decima, in qua nihil aliud tractatum fuit, quam circa Ibæ accusationem, ejusque defensionem, pro eoque sententia lata fuit à Patribus, utriusque partis probationibus hinc inde discussis, eo quod nihil contra eum probatum sit concludenter de iis, quæ in libello accusantium continebantur, ipse verò suam probaret innocentiam tum ex sententia pro eo lata in Berytensi Synodo à Photio, & Eustathio judicibus ad id negotium constitutis; tum ex attestatione totius Cleri Edessenæ civitatis; tum denique, quia ipse ex superabundanti, ut omnem suspicionem in eum conceptam amoliretur, libenter semel, atque iterum anathematizavit Nestorium, & Eutychem, seque Cyrillo communicantem, postquam suorum capitulorum verum attigit sensum, demonstravit. Unde neque accusatores probarunt, quod objecerant, neque epistolam, quam Ibæ attribuebant, ab eo scriptam fuisse certum fecerunt; ideoque Patres Concilii unamini consensu eum innoxium, atque ad suam Sedem à qua injuste dejectus fuerat, restituendum esse declararunt: ac proinde tota quæstio facti erat independenter à quæstione fidei, quæ ibi nec incidenter mota fuerat. Quod totum ex ipsa *act. 10.* & ex duobus Vigilii Constitutis expresse constat. | | 91 These things being thus noted in passing, Muratori was deceived, as I was saying; since in the Synod of Chalcedon nothing concerning the faith, so far as regards the cause of Ibas, was treated, but only a mere fact, which regarded the accusation of Ibas himself, and the nullity of the sentence passed against him in the preceding Pseudo-Synod, as Gregory I is witness in the cited epistle, in these words: *But after the Synods, or canons, have been expressed, I consent to what was done, and the final action was ventilated concerning persons.* [?] This is expressly clear from the acts themselves of the Synod, in the tenth action, in which nothing else was treated than what concerned the accusation of Ibas, and his defense; and sentence was passed by the Fathers in his favor, the proofs of both parties having been discussed on either side: because nothing was conclusively proved against him of those things which were contained in the libellus of the accusers, while he himself proved his innocence, both from the sentence passed for him in the Synod of Berytus by Photius and Eustathius, judges appointed for that business; and from the attestation of the whole Clergy of the city of Edessa; and finally because he himself, out of superabundance, in order to remove all suspicion conceived against him, willingly once and again anathematized Nestorius and Eutyches, and showed himself in communion with Cyril, after he had touched the true sense of his own chapters. Whence neither did the accusers prove what they had objected, nor did they make it certain that the epistle which they attributed to Ibas had been written by him; and therefore the Fathers of the Council with unanimous consent declared him innocent, and that he was to be restored to his See, from which he had been unjustly cast down: and accordingly the whole question was one of fact, independently of the question of faith, which had not even incidentally been raised there. All of which is expressly established from *act. 10.* itself, and from the two Constituta of Vigilius. |
| 92 Identidem in V. Synodo nihil contrarium Chalcedonensi Concilio invenitur; neque in personas damnatio decreta fuit, ut supponere videtur Muratorius, sed tantum in scripta, eo quod illius Synodi Patribus visum fuit, in ipsis aliquid damnandum reperiri, tamquam ipsi Chalcedonensi, & Ephesinæ Synodo contrarium. Tantumque abest ut diversum quid statuerent aut in quæstione fidei, aut in quæstione facti, ut toti essent in Collatione VI. in ostendendo contenta in prædicta epistola adversantia esse Chalcedonensis Synodi definitionibus, collatis his cum præfatæ epistolæ propositionibus. Et postea in eadem collatione nihil contra Ibæ innocentiam de novo statuerunt, neque de facto, utrum ea epistola ab eodem Iba scripta esset, aliquid judicarunt, neque ei anathema dixerunt, sed tantum contra epistolam. Liceat Synodi referre verba: *Hæc omnes dixerunt: Epistola hæretica est: epistolam, quæ dicitur Ibæ, omnes condemnamus: istam omnes anathematizamus: epistola à Synodo aliena est: epistola contraria definitioni est: tota epistola hæretica est: tota epistola blasphema est.... Definitio Chalcedonensis Concilii epistolam, quæ dicitur Ibæ, ejecit. Unus Nestorius, unus Theodorus: Anathema Theodoro, & Nestorio, & epistolæ, quæ dicitur Ibæ.... Qui epistolam recipit, Chalcedonenses sanctos Patres rejicit &c.* Et postea collat. VIII. *Super hæc anathematizamus & impiam epistolam, quam dicitur Ibas ad Marim Persam scripsisse.... Et impiam epistolam, quæ dicitur Ibæ, & defensores eorum..... Et iterum siquis defendit epistolam, quam dicitur Ibas ad Marim Persam hæreticum scripsisse &c.* Ex quibus meridiana luce clarius ostenditur, Patres V. Synodi cum Theodorum, & Nestorium anathematizaverint, nullatenus anathema dixisse Ibæ, à Chalcedonensi Synodo innoxio judicato; neque sententiæ circa factum contravenisse, neque definiisse epistolam ab Iba fuisse conscriptam. Sicut etiam apertissime apparet, illius epistolæ censuram non effugisse Patrum Chalcedonensium censuram, quam V. Synodi Patres ostendunt factam, licet Chalcedonensis Synodus expresse de ea quæstione non ageret, eo quod inutilis erat ea disceptatio ad facti controversiam, de qua in dicta actione 10. agebatur. Proinde neque adducta à Muratorio probatio quidquam concludit, neque certa veritate consistit. | | 92 Likewise in the fifth Synod nothing contrary to the Council of Chalcedon is found; nor was a condemnation decreed against persons, as Muratori seems to suppose, but only against writings, because it seemed to the Fathers of that Synod that something was found in them to be condemned, as being contrary to the Synod of Chalcedon itself, and to that of Ephesus. And so far were they from determining anything different, either in the question of faith or in the question of fact, that in the sixth Collation they were wholly engaged in showing that the contents of the aforesaid epistle were adverse to the definitions of the Synod of Chalcedon, these being compared with the propositions of the aforesaid epistle. And afterwards in the same collation they determined nothing anew against the innocence of Ibas, nor did they judge anything concerning the fact, whether that epistle had been written by the same Ibas, nor did they say anathema to him, but only against the epistle. Let it be permitted to relate the words of the Synod: *All said these things: The epistle is heretical: the epistle which is said to be Ibas's we all condemn: this we all anathematize: the epistle is alien from the Synod: the epistle is contrary to the definition: the whole epistle is heretical: the whole epistle is blasphemous.... The definition of the Council of Chalcedon cast out the epistle which is said to be Ibas's. One Nestorius, one Theodore: Anathema to Theodore, and to Nestorius, and to the epistle which is said to be Ibas's.... He who receives the epistle rejects the holy Chalcedonian Fathers, etc.* And afterwards, collat. VIII: *Besides these things we anathematize also the impious epistle which Ibas is said to have written to Maris the Persian.... And the impious epistle which is said to be Ibas's, and the defenders of them..... And again, if anyone defends the epistle which Ibas is said to have written to Maris the Persian heretic, etc.* From which it is shown, more clearly than the noonday light, that the Fathers of the fifth Synod, though they anathematized Theodore and Nestorius, in no wise said anathema to Ibas, judged innocent by the Synod of Chalcedon; nor did they contravene the sentence concerning the fact, nor define that the epistle had been written by Ibas. As likewise it most plainly appears that the censure of that epistle did not escape the censure of the Chalcedonian Fathers [?], which the Fathers of the fifth Synod show to have been made, although the Synod of Chalcedon did not expressly treat of that question, because that disputation was useless to the controversy of fact, which was treated in the said action 10. Accordingly, neither does the proof adduced by Muratori conclude anything, nor does it stand upon certain truth. |
| 93 Omnia, quæ eleganter congerit *pag. 173.* ad stabiliendum obsequiosum assensum Ecclesiæ, & Romani Pontificis judiciis, securius suadent fidei assensum; negare enim non poterit, si credamus infallibili Romanæ Sedis etiam circa sensus verborum, & scriptorum mentes judicio, omnem aditum argumentis fallacibus, & ludentibus ingeniis penitus intercludi. Hoc ergo stabilius, ut ejuremus hæreticos, eorumque sensus inconcusse damnemus, nec amplius controversiam moveamus. Igitur non jure reprehendit recentiores Theologos, nec merito sibi assumpsit eos explodere, atque in contrariam abire sententiam. | | 93 All the things which he elegantly heaps together *pag. 173.* to establish an obsequious assent to the judgments of the Church and of the Roman Pontiff more securely persuade an assent of faith; for he will not be able to deny that, if we believe the infallible judgment of the Roman See even concerning the senses of words, and the minds of writers, every approach is utterly barred to fallacious arguments and to trifling wits. This, therefore, is the more stable course: that we abjure the heretics, and unshakenly condemn their senses, and no longer stir up controversy. Therefore he does not justly censure the more recent Theologians, nor has he deservedly taken it upon himself to hiss them off, and to go over to the contrary opinion. |
| 94 Dubitandi rationem proponit in hunc modum: *At inquiunt, nos doctrinam ab Apostolica Sede, & ab Ecclesia damnatam damnamus; non enim illic errori obnoxia est Ecclesia Christi sponsa. Hanc autem doctrinam verbis, & phrasibus illius libri contineri, eamque illius hominis menti revera insedisse, neque divinitus cognoscit Ecclesia, neque jure nos ad credendum cogere potest.* Quid ad hanc rationem rependit? *Silentium præ reverentia adhibebimus. Maiora præstare necessum non est.* Non astruo Jansenismum sequi, sed vereor odorari. Quid enim aliud Jansenii defensores præ reverentia in Ecclesiam demonstranda contendunt, quam silentium? Ecclesiam autem aliquid amplius à nobis exigere mihi certum est, cum Pontificiæ Constitutiones hunc à Jansenistis oblatum silentium non recipiunt, expetuntque assensum. Quî fieri potest, ut assensum omnino præbeamus, si *Ecclesia certos nos facere non potest de re illa, quam ipsa certo non cognoscit, & in qua judicanda errare potest?* Agnoscimus errare potuisse; certi non sumus rei, quam Ecclesia tenendam proponit, neque ipsa Ecclesia cum proponit certa est, quis ambigit quamplurimum assensum nostrum periclitari? Captivare intellectum in obsequium fidei Scriptura nos docet, nec difficile est verè Orthodoxis hoc obsequium; omnibus enim ratiociniis, & argumentis clauduntur portæ; captivare autem intellectum, & assensum in re dubia, in re incerta, in re, in qua error esse potest, vires naturæ lapsæ, ac humani judicii semper ad dubitandum proclivis transcendere videtur. Qui firmiter credit Ecclesiam non errasse, nec errare potuisse, *Ecclesiæ mandata non eludit, neque hoc nefas committit*; committit, eludit, qui *argutiis talibus* in verborum apparatu consistentibus, Ecclesiæ in his rebus certitudinem à nobis studet amoliri. | | 94 He proposes a reason for doubting in this manner: *But, say they, we condemn the doctrine condemned by the Apostolic See, and by the Church; for therein the Church, the spouse of Christ, is not liable to error. But that this doctrine is contained in the words and phrases of that book, and that it really sat in the mind of that man, the Church neither knows divinely, nor can she by right compel us to believe.* What does he answer to this reason? *We shall employ silence out of reverence. It is not necessary to perform more.* I do not affirm that Jansenism follows, but I fear that I scent it. For what else do the defenders of Jansenius contend for, in showing their reverence toward the Church, than silence? But the Church alito exact something more from us, I am certain, since the Pontifical Constitutions do not accept this silence offered by the Jansenists, and demand assent. How can it come about that we should give assent altogether, if *the Church cannot make us certain of that thing which she herself does not know with certainty, and in judging which she can err?* We acknowledge that she could have erred; we are not certain of the thing which the Church proposes to be held, nor is the Church herself certain when she proposes it—who doubts that our assent is thereby very greatly imperiled? Scripture teaches us to take the intellect captive in obedience to faith, nor is this obedience difficult for the truly Orthodox; for the gates are closed to all reasonings and arguments; but to take captive the intellect and assent in a doubtful matter, in an uncertain matter, in a matter in which there can be error, seems to transcend the powers of fallen nature and of human judgment, ever prone to doubting. He who firmly believes that the Church has not erred, nor could have erred, *does not elude the Church's commands, nor commit this crime*; he commits it, he eludes them, who by *such subtleties*, consisting in an apparatus of words, strives to remove from us the certitude of the Church in these matters. |
| 95 Has argutias Jansenistarum proprias esse, eosque in eo præcipue fundari, quod Pontifex possit errare in rebus facti, licet cum jure connexis, non meum judicium est, sed insignium Theologorum. Unum adhibeo testem P. Vivam in Synopsi de Jansenii propositionibus *n. 7. Verum quia Jansenistæ variis cavillationibus definitiones Pontificias eludere conabantur, perinde quasi posset Pontifex in definiendis quæstionibus facti, etiam connexis cum quæstionibus juris ad fidem pertinentibus, errare; & jam virus illarum propositionum varie evomebant, in Galliæ præsertim perniciem &c.* Et postea *n. 8. Ex quibus liquido constat, umbratile, ac ruinosum esse Jansenistarum fundamentum, quo nituntur ad tot cavillationes, & subterfugia ineunda, videlicet quia quæstio isthæc, utpote facti, (an in libro Jansenii propositiones in sensu à Jansenio intento falsæ, & hæreticales contineantur) est quæstio facti in qua Pontifex errare potest &c.* | | 95 That these subtleties are proper to the Jansenists, and that they are founded chiefly upon this, that the Pontiff can err in matters of fact, even though connected with right, is not my judgment, but that of eminent theologians. I bring forward one witness, Fr. Viva, in his Synopsis on the propositions of Jansenius *n. 7. But because the Jansenists strove to elude the Pontifical definitions by various cavils, just as if the Pontiff could err in defining questions of fact, even those connected with questions of right pertaining to the faith; and they were already vomiting forth in various ways the venom of those propositions, especially to the ruin of France, etc.* And afterwards *n. 8. From which it is clearly evident how shadowy and ruinous is the foundation of the Jansenists, upon which they rely for entering upon so many cavils and subterfuges, namely, that this question, as being one of fact (whether in the book of Jansenius there are contained propositions false and heretical in the sense intended by Jansenius), is a question of fact in which the Pontiff can err, etc.* |
| 96 Quid aliud continet Muratorii doctrina etsi tot splendidis locutionibus, tot erga Ecclesiam obsequiis, tot humilibus assensibus stipata? Suspicandi fundamentum præbet, quod hic liber examini Qualificatorum subreptus (quos ipse in hoc eodem libro abunde mordet) eorum effugeret censuram, & furtim spargeretur; neque vana suspicio est: *Omnis enim, qui male agit, odit lucem,* (26) *& non venit ad lucem, ut non arguantur opera ejus, qui autem facit veritatem, venit ad lucem, ut manifestentur opera ejus; quia in Deo sunt facta.* {marg. (26) Joan. cap. 1. 20.} | | 96 What else does the doctrine of Muratori contain, even though hedged about with so many splendid locutions, so many acts of homage toward the Church, so many humble assents? It affords ground for suspecting that this book, withdrawn by stealth from the examination of the Qualificators (whom he himself abundantly bites in this same book), escaped their censure and was furtively spread abroad; nor is the suspicion vain: *For every one that doeth evil hateth the light,* (26) *and cometh not to the light, that his works may not be reproved; but he that doeth truth cometh to the light, that his works may be made manifest; because they are done in God.* {marg. (26) Joan. cap. 1. 20.} |
| 97 Mira verborum, & rationum fucata candicantia se dealbare nititur, suismet tamen pigmentis proditur. Argutiis uti, dicit, qui *Fidei quidem leges se habere perspectas prodit, disciplinæ autem prorsus ignorat.* Quis dixerit, nisi insanus, argutari eum, qui fidei legibus instructus in arenam descendit? An disciplinæ potius, quam fidei assensum præstare tenemur? An disciplinæ regulis adversamur, cum fidei regulas amplectimur? Id ne disciplina præcipit, quod credamus ea, quæ Ecclesia tenenda proponit? Hoc Rigoristarum signaculum est, ut disciplinæ toti incumbant, fidei vero assensum, quam possint, detrectent. Quomodo fiet, ut disciplinæ præceptis *animum pie, fideliterque submittamus*, si dubio assensu, solaque reverentia silentium adhibendum sit? Ipse ait: *Corde nobis futura est præ omnibus catholica doctrina.... ergo damnanda scripta, & sensa, quibus ex Ecclesiæ sententia continetur male sana doctrina.* Credere corde est de fide tenere, dicente Apostolo: (27) *Fratres, corde creditur ad justitiam, ore autem confessio fit ad salutem.* {marg. (27) Ad Rom. 10.} Omnis catholica doctrina de fide tenenda est. Si ergo catholica doctrina est, quæ *circa scripta, & sensa, quibus ex Ecclesiæ sententia continetur male sana doctrina*, qua ratione defendi poterit de fide non esse eam doctrinam? Hunc cordis assensum exigit in suo Decreto Clemens XI. in illis verbis: *Ut hæreticum non ore solum, sed & corde rejici, ac damnari ab omnibus Christi fidelibus debere damnatum in quinque propositionibus Janseniani libri sensum*; omnemque aliam credulitatem excludit in sequenti periodo: *Nec alia mente, animo, aut credulitate formulæ Jansenianæ condemnationis subscribi licite posse.* Quis autem mente sanus ambigere potest, hic etiam reprobari credulitatem, quæ solum nititur humili assensui, & reverenti silentio, exclusa fide, qua *corde creditur ad justitiam*? | | 97 By a wondrous painted whiteness of words and reasons he strives to whiten himself, yet he is betrayed by his own pigments. He says that he uses subtleties who *shows that he has indeed the laws of Faith well examined, but is utterly ignorant of those of discipline.* Who would say, unless he were mad, that he quibbles who descends into the arena instructed in the laws of the faith? Are we bound to give assent to discipline rather than to faith? Do we oppose the rules of discipline when we embrace the rules of faith? Does discipline command this, that we believe those things which the Church proposes to be held? This is the seal of the Rigorists, that they apply themselves wholly to discipline, but withhold, so far as they can, the assent of faith. How shall it come about that we *submit our mind piously and faithfully* to the precepts of discipline, if silence is to be employed with a doubtful assent and reverence alone? He himself says: *The catholic doctrine is to be held by us in the heart before all things.... therefore those writings and opinions are to be condemned in which, by the judgment of the Church, unsound doctrine is contained.* To believe with the heart is to hold of faith, as the Apostle says: (27) *Brethren, with the heart we believe unto justice, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation.* {marg. (27) Ad Rom. 10.} All catholic doctrine is to be held of faith. If therefore that is catholic doctrine which concerns *the writings and opinions in which, by the judgment of the Church, unsound doctrine is contained*, by what reasoning could it be defended that that doctrine is not of faith? This assent of the heart Clement XI demands in his Decree, in these words: *That the sense of the Jansenian book condemned in the five propositions ought to be rejected and condemned as heretical, not with the mouth only but also with the heart, by all Christ's faithful*; and he excludes every other belief in the following sentence: *Nor can the formula of the Jansenian condemnation be licitly subscribed with any other mind, spirit, or belief.* But who of sound mind can doubt that here too is reprobated that belief which relies only upon a humble assent and a reverent silence, faith being excluded, by which *with the heart we believe unto justice*? |
| 98 Alia pulchro eloquio in Jansenium, & ejus asseclas apparenter exardescens effutit; si tamen oppugnat fidei assensum, quid aliud facit, quam venustis technis Sinonem induere, & Apostolicis definitionibus subtilissimo artificio contraire, ut qui lynceos oculos non habeant, facilius fallantur. Innocentius X. Pontifex Maximus quinque propositiones Jansenii hæreticas, & jam antea in Pelagio, Semipelagianis, Luthero, & Calvino damnatas declarat, & definit, etiam in sensu à Jansenio intento, ut explicat Alexander VII. Is, ut *catholicæ Religionis integritati, & propagationi* consulat, & *tortuosum colubrem in varios gyros, & cavillationum deflexus euntem* atterat, & extinguat, formam edidit juramenti ab omnibus præstandi de rejiciendis sincero animo, ac damnandi dictas quinque propositiones etiam in sensu à Jansenio intento, & prout ab Innocentio damnatæ inveniuntur. Innocentius damnavit ut hæreticas etiam in prædicto sensu: ergo neque juramento satisfacimus, neque definitioni Pontificiæ plene acquiescimus, si insimul non teneamus eum de Cathedra loquentem, & cum infallibili Spiritus Sancti assistentia rem de fide credendam definiisse. | | 98 Other things he pours forth with fair eloquence, apparently blazing out against Jansenius and his followers; yet if he assails the assent of faith, what else does he do than clothe Sinon in charming wiles, and by most subtle artifice go against the Apostolic definitions, so that those who have not lynx eyes may be the more easily deceived. Innocent X, Supreme Pontiff, declares and defines that the five propositions of Jansenius are heretical, and already condemned before in Pelagius, the Semipelagians, Luther, and Calvin, even in the sense intended by Jansenius, as Alexander VII explains. The latter, that he might provide for *the integrity and propagation of the catholic Religion*, and might crush and extinguish *the tortuous serpent going about in various coils and windings of cavils*, published a form of oath to be taken by all, concerning the rejecting with sincere mind and condemning of the said five propositions even in the sense intended by Jansenius, and as they are found condemned by Innocent. Innocent condemned them as heretical even in the aforesaid sense: therefore we neither satisfy the oath, nor fully acquiesce in the Pontifical definition, unless at the same time we hold that he, speaking from the Chair, and with the infallible assistance of the Holy Spirit, defined the matter as a thing to be believed of faith. |
| 99 Clemens insuper XI. Pontifex Maximus, quia ei constitit, *nonnullos iniquitatis filios asserere* prædictas propositiones in sensu Jansenii damnatas non fuisse, testatur authoritate Apostolica eamdem causam discussam fuisse, *ea profecto diligentia, qua maior desiderari non posset: & quamcumque dubitationem super præmissis in posterum auferre* intendens, *ut omnes Christi fideles, in ejusdem fidei unitate sese contineant ex debito Pastoralis officii, ac matura deliberatione præinsertam Innocentii prædecessoris Constitutionem, Declarationem, & Definitionem* confirmat, approbat, & innovat; declaratque & definit prædictas propositiones in sensu à Jansenio intento damnari, damnatasque fuisse. Posteaque, quippe *non defuere, nec adhuc desunt homines veritati non acquiescentes, & nunquam Ecclesiæ contradicendi finem facientes, qui variis distinctionibus, seu potius effugiis ad circumventionem erroris excogitatis Ecclesiam ipsam turbare, eamque interminatis quæstionibus, quantum in ipsis est, involvere, & implicare conantur.* Et quia, *præterea iidem irrequieti homines, sparsis undequaque scriptionibus, ac libellis exquisita ad fallendum arte compositis, non sine gravi Sedis Apostolicæ injuria, maximoque totius Ecclesiæ scandalo docere non sunt veriti; Ad obedientiam præfatis Apostolicis Constitutionibus debitam non requiri, ut quis prædicti Janseniani libri sensum in antedictis quinque propositionibus, sicut præmittitur, damnatum, interius ut hæreticum damnat; sed satis esse, si ea de re obsequiosum (ut ipsi vocant) silentium teneatur. Quæ quidem assertio quam absurda sit, & animabus fidelium perniciosa, satis apparet, dum fallacis hujus doctrinæ pallio non deponitur error, sed absconditur; vulnus tegitur, non curatur; Ecclesiæ illuditur, non paretur &c.* Si his Muratorii sententia, licet humili, & interno, mere tamen humano assensu palliata, non opponitur, quod omnes recentiores insignes Theologi fallantur, necesse fit, & quod ipse, hethorodoxique omnes orthodoxis prævaleant, consequens erit. Ego tamen his potius assentiri cupio. Si verò prædictæ constitutiones tam quoad doctrinam, quam quoad sensum certissimam, infallibilemque fidei definitionem non continent, quibus aliis fidei assensum præstabimus? Ad hæc dicit Muratorius, ut jam adnotavi, ea de fide tenenda esse, quæ ex Scripturis, & SS. Patribus constant, & quæ jam in Synodis œcumenicis definita inveniuntur. Quid superest, nisi ut Pontificibus Maximis omnem aliquid novum definiendi potestatem adimamus? Quid absurdius! Ille suo sensu, suisque phrasibus abundet: nos planius, sed securius non modo humanum, sed supernaturalem assensum definitionibus Pontificiis præbebimus; sic omnes ambages, omnes astus, omnia cavilla prudentissime vitabimus. Credo autem hunc nostrum fidelissimum assensum ab Apostolica Sede nunquam improbandum. Si Muratorii doctrina eâdem tabulâ salvetur, bonis avibus, & sine animarum periculo id contingat. Ego tamen ab ejus opinione manum potius amoverim, veritus *ignes suppositos cineri doloso.* | | 99 Clement XI, moreover, Supreme Pontiff, because it was established to him that *certain sons of iniquity assert* that the aforesaid propositions were not condemned in the sense of Jansenius, testifies by Apostolic authority that the same cause was discussed *with such diligence indeed that greater could not be desired: and* intending *to remove for the future every doubt concerning the aforesaid*, *that all Christ's faithful may contain themselves in the unity of the same faith, out of the debt of the Pastoral office, and with mature deliberation*, he confirms, approves, and renews *the above-inserted Constitution, Declaration, and Definition of his predecessor Innocent*; and he declares and defines that the aforesaid propositions are condemned, and were condemned, in the sense intended by Jansenius. And afterwards, since *there have not been lacking, nor are there yet lacking, men not acquiescing in the truth, and never making an end of contradicting the Church, who by various distinctions, or rather by evasions devised for the circumvention of error, endeavor to disturb the Church herself, and, so far as in them lies, to involve and entangle her in endless questions.* And because, *moreover, these same restless men, with writings and pamphlets scattered on every side, composed with exquisite art for deceiving, have not feared to teach, not without grave injury to the Apostolic See and the greatest scandal of the whole Church: That for the obedience due to the aforesaid Apostolic Constitutions it is not required that anyone should interiorly condemn as heretical the sense of the aforesaid Jansenian book, condemned, as is premised, in the aforesaid five propositions; but that it is enough if an obsequious (as they call it) silence be kept concerning that matter. Which assertion, how absurd it is, and how pernicious to the souls of the faithful, sufficiently appears, since by the cloak of this fallacious doctrine error is not laid aside, but hidden; the wound is covered, not cured; the Church is mocked, not obeyed, etc.* If the opinion of Muratori, although cloaked with a humble and internal, yet merely human, assent, is not opposed by these things, it becomes necessary that all the more recent eminent theologians be deceived, and the consequence will be that he and all the heterodox prevail over the orthodox. I, however, desire rather to assent to these. But if the aforesaid constitutions do not contain, both as to the doctrine and as to the sense, a most certain and infallible definition of faith, to what others shall we give the assent of faith? To these things Muratori says, as I have already noted, that those things are to be held of faith which are established from the Scriptures and the holy Fathers, and which are found already defined in ecumenical Synods. What remains, except that we take away from the Supreme Pontiffs all power of defining anything new? What more absurd! Let him abound in his own sense and in his own phrases: we, more plainly but more securely, shall give not only human but supernatural assent to the Pontifical definitions; thus we shall most prudently avoid all ambiguities, all wiles, all cavils. And I believe that this our most faithful assent will never be disapproved by the Apostolic See. If the doctrine of Muratori be saved by the same plank, may that befall with good omens and without danger to souls. I, however, would rather withdraw my hand from his opinion, fearing *fires laid beneath deceitful ash.* |
| 100 Exempla, quæ affert *pag. 175.* in suummet sinum convertuntur. Iisdem usus fui ad probandam contrariam Muratorio sententiam. Sic ait: *Quod autem Apostolica Sedes nunc exigit in Jansenii causa, non nudiustertius exigi cœptum est, sed ab ipsis Ecclesiæ incunabilis. Ast, ubi quempiam hæresis Ecclesia damnavit, tum continuo pro hæretico habuit anathematizare, quos Ecclesia hæreticos declarat, renuentem.* Ergo de fide tenebatur credere eum hæreticum esse, quem Ecclesia talem definierat: alias sic renuens contumax, inobediens, temerarius, non hæreticus diceretur. Id Theodoreti exemplum probat, id alia confirmant, quæ supra retuli. Quod de V. Synodo addit, & de Vigilio Papa, jam supra exposui, refricare opus non est. Tandem ad eumdem lapidem revertitur, ut scilicet doceat Ecclesiam falli posse, seu infallibile judicium non habere in factis à Deo non revelatis. Optarem scire à quo divino Numine accepit, hæc facta concernentia fidem à Deo Pontificibus è Cathedra definientibus, omni adhibito necessario examine, *minime fuisse revelata*? Fatetur doctrinæ catholicæ stabilitatem, & morum regimen ab hoc judicio circa facta quodammodo dependere. Quis autem nescit, infallibilem esse Pontificiam definitionem in his, quæ ad fidem, moresve spectant? | | 100 The examples which he adduces *pag. 175.* are turned back into his own bosom. I used the same ones to prove the opinion contrary to Muratori. Thus he says: *But what the Apostolic See now demands in the cause of Jansenius did not begin to be demanded the day before yesterday, but from the very cradle of the Church. But when the Church condemned anyone of heresy, then she straightway held for a heretic him who refused to anathematize those whom the Church declares heretics.* Therefore one was bound of faith to believe that he was a heretic whom the Church had defined to be such: otherwise the one so refusing would be called contumacious, disobedient, rash, not a heretic. The example of Theodoret proves this; other things which I related above confirm it. What he adds concerning the Fifth Synod, and concerning Pope Vigilius, I have already expounded above; there is no need to rub it up again. At length he returns to the same stone, namely, to teach that the Church can be deceived, or does not have infallible judgment in facts not revealed by God. I should wish to know from what divine Power he received it, that these facts concerning the faith *were by no means revealed* by God to the Pontiffs defining from the Chair, every necessary examination having been applied? He confesses that the stability of catholic doctrine and the government of morals in some manner depend upon this judgment concerning facts. But who does not know that the Pontifical definition is infallible in those things which regard faith or morals? |
| 101 Concludit facta à Deo minime revelata ea esse, nempe: *Qui sint legitimi successores Petri.... quæ sint œcumenica, aut probata Concilia, quæ decreta indubia Romanorum Pontificum, quæ germana Patrum scripta, & quid revera sentiat aliquis ex Patribus, & quis cœlitum albo sit adscriptus.* Quis non miretur, quanta fallacia quæstiones quæstionibus immisceat, aliasque cum aliis confundat! De Petri legitimis successoribus, de infallibilitate œcumenicarum Synodorum, de canonizatione Sanctorum jam supra dictum est. An Pontifex hoc, vel illud ediderit decretum, an vero ab aliquo impostore confictum sit, an in hoc, vel illo Concilio aliquid ab hæreticis vitiatum reperiatur, an hujus, vel alterius Patris scripta deturpata, vel adjecta sint, nemo, quem sciam, hucusque de fide credendum esse asseruit: cur ergo has quæstiones eâdem lance perpendit? Ut certa ab incertis non secernantur. Ipse secernere putat, dum sic claudit: *Uno verbo, hujusmodi factis, à Deo quidem non revelatis, sed tamen cum dogmate connexis, si evidentiam præferant, demens sit, & nonnunquam reputetur hæreticus, qui credere, & obtemperare nolit: sin ambigua, & dubia appareant, adhuc præstat Ecclesiæ sententiam ferenti &c.* Unum exprimit, & aliud supponit, utrumque non ferendum. Primum, plus deferendum esse morali evidentiæ, quam Pontificiæ definitioni, & fidei assensum à maiori, vel minori evidentia dependere. Secundum, quod post definitionem Ecclesiæ adhuc res dubia apparere possit, & ambigendi facultas supersit. En Doctor, qui Orthodoxorum immoderata ingenia reprehendit, & eos edocendi sibi assumit magisterium. | | 101 He concludes that the facts by no means revealed by God are these, namely: *Who are the legitimate successors of Peter.... which are the ecumenical or approved Councils, which the undoubted decrees of the Roman Pontiffs, which the genuine writings of the Fathers, and what some one of the Fathers really thinks, and who is enrolled in the register of the blessed.* Who would not marvel with how great fallacy he mingles questions with questions, and confounds some with others! Of the legitimate successors of Peter, of the infallibility of ecumenical Synods, of the canonization of Saints it has already been spoken above. Whether the Pontiff has issued this or that decree, or whether it has been forged by some impostor; whether in this or that Council something is found vitiated by heretics; whether the writings of this or that Father have been corrupted or interpolated—no one, so far as I know, has hitherto asserted that this is to be believed of faith: why then does he weigh these questions in the same scale? So that the certain may not be separated from the uncertain. He thinks he separates them, when he closes thus: *In a word, to facts of this kind, not indeed revealed by God, but yet connected with dogma, if they display evidence, he would be mad, and would sometimes be reputed a heretic, who should refuse to believe and obey: but if they appear ambiguous and doubtful, it is still better [to defer] to the Church pronouncing sentence, etc.* He expresses one thing and supposes another, neither of which is to be borne. First, that more deference is to be given to moral evidence than to the Pontifical definition, and that the assent of faith depends on greater or lesser evidence. Second, that after the definition of the Church the matter can still appear doubtful, and the faculty of doubting remain. Behold the Doctor who reprehends the immoderate wits of the Orthodox, and assumes to himself the magisterium of teaching them. |
| 102 Ad alia properemus. *Lib. 2. cap. 2.* varias regulas constituit circa propugnandam veritatem, quas si ipse ad unguem observasset, fortasse suum libellum non ederet circa Religionem Portugalliæ in administrando Pœnitentiæ sacramento, in quo non solum non loquitur veritatem, sed multa falsa congerit, & à certa opinione recedit. Sed de hoc postea. Illud aliquo sale aut confricandum, aut condiendum esse arbitror, quod ibi scribit: *Alii* (inquit) *sunt errores extra fidei doctrinam, sed tamen non extra religionis pomœria. Ejusmodi sunt.... in veneratione tum Sanctorum, sacrarumque imaginum, tum etiam reliquiarum, & in ipso externo Dei cultu, atque mysteriis.* Explicatione opus est. Si loquitur de actibus venerationis, aut cultus Dei, hi proculdubio ad religionem spectare dignoscuntur. Veneratio autem Sanctorum, imaginum, reliquiarum, juxta opinionem D. Thom. & communem, ad virtutem distinctam pertinet, nempe ad Duliam; licet aliàs iste cultus, quatenus Deus in suis Sanctis colitur, etiam ad religionem referatur. Si vero agit de obligatione venerationis, aut cultus exhibendi Deo, Sanctis, sacris imaginibus, Sanctorumque reliquiis, etiam indubium est, ea omnia ad fidei dogmata pertinere. Deum cultu non solum interno, sed etiam externo esse adorandum, & colendum, adeo lumen naturale demonstrat, ut etiam hæretici, & pagani non inficientur. Veritas hæc est infallibilis, & toties in Scripturis declarata, ut alia probatione opus non sit. Imagines autem, & reliquias Sanctorum venerandas esse, etiam est de fide definitum *in Concil. Nicæn. II. act. 3. Gangrensi can. ult. Carthagin. 5. can. 5. Bracharens. 3. can. 5. Moguntin. can. 51. Lateranens. sub Innocenti III. cap. 62. Trident. sess. 25. cap. 2.* certaque est inter Theologos sententia. Contrarium tenuerunt hæretici Iconoclaustæ, Lutherus, Calvinus, Madburgenses, & alii. Ideo qui Sanctos, aut eorum imagines spernunt, aut blasphemiis inhonorant, licet hoc delictum sit contra religionem, attamen ut suspecti de fide habentur, quia contra fidei dogma sentire præsumuntur. | | 102 Let us hasten to other things. *Lib. 2. cap. 2.* he establishes various rules concerning the defense of truth, which if he himself had observed to the letter, perhaps he would not have published his little book concerning the Religion of Portugal in administering the sacrament of Penance, in which he not only does not speak the truth, but heaps up many falsehoods, and recedes from certain opinion. But of this afterwards. That, I judge, is either to be rubbed or to be seasoned with some salt, which he writes there: *There are other errors* (he says) *outside the doctrine of faith, but yet not outside the precincts of religion. Of this kind are.... in the veneration both of the Saints and of sacred images, and also of relics, and in the external worship of God itself, and in the mysteries.* There is need of explanation. If he speaks of acts of veneration, or of the worship of God, these without doubt are recognized to pertain to religion. But the veneration of the Saints, of images, of relics, according to the opinion of St. Thomas and the common one, pertains to a distinct virtue, namely to Dulia; although otherwise this worship, insofar as God is worshiped in His Saints, is also referred to religion. But if he treats of the obligation of exhibiting veneration or worship to God, to the Saints, to sacred images, and to the relics of the Saints, it is likewise undoubted that all these pertain to the dogmas of faith. That God is to be adored and worshiped not only with internal but also with external worship, the natural light so demonstrates that even heretics and pagans do not deny it. This truth is infallible, and so often declared in the Scriptures that there is no need of other proof. But that the images and relics of the Saints are to be venerated is also defined of faith *in Concil. Nicæn. II. act. 3. Gangrensi can. ult. Carthagin. 5. can. 5. Bracharens. 3. can. 5. Moguntin. can. 51. Lateranens. sub Innocenti III. cap. 62. Trident. sess. 25. cap. 2.* and is a certain opinion among theologians. The contrary was held by the heretics the Iconoclasts, Luther, Calvin, the Magdeburgians, and others. Therefore those who despise the Saints or their images, or dishonor them with blasphemies, although this crime is against religion, are nevertheless held as suspect concerning the faith, because they are presumed to think against a dogma of faith. |
| 103 Postea in §. seq. sic ait: *Cum igitur in doctrina fidei quisquam incaute delirat, neque in vulgus sua commenta dispergit, sibi tantum canens, huic adhibere decet salutarem christianæ correctionis belleborum, ubi legibus ecclesiasticis aliter cautum non sit, hoc est clam, & amice ita monendus, atque docendus &c.* Non improbabilem sequitur doctrinam, præcipue, si quisquam non malo animo, sed per ignorantiam aliquam hæresim coram alio proferat, quo casu statim est monendus, ut errorem deponat. Sed contraria opinio est communior, & in praxi receptior. Immo alia nullatenus est sequenda post Decretum Alexandri VII. anno 1660. mense Augusto, in quo districte mandat, quod tam hæreticus, quam de hæresi quamvis levissime suspectus locorum Ordinariis, vel Inquisitoribus omnino, nulla præmissa correctione fraterna, denuntietur; adeo ut ad id teneatur, licet putet eum jam emendatum, ut cum Suario, Rojas, Simancas, & aliis tenet Delbene, (28) qui secutus antecedenter contrariam sententiam, postea eam retractat propter prædictum Decretum; quod quidem consonare videtur *Lateranens. Concil. sess. 9. §. Ad abolenda*, & Constitut. Pii I. *de qua in cap. Siquis per capillum 22. q. 1.* & Constitut. Julii III. quæ incipit *In multis*. {marg. (28) P. 1. dub. 18. n. 7.} Et tandem ea sententia caute est habenda, ne incidamus in propositionem 5. ab Alexandro VII. damnatam. Hæc obiter attigisse sat sit, ut quæ securius amplexanda fuerint ad Lydium lapidem probentur. | | 103 Afterwards, in the following §., he speaks thus: *When therefore anyone incautiously raves in the doctrine of faith, and does not spread his inventions among the common people, singing only to himself, it is fitting to apply to him the salutary hellebore of Christian correction, where it is not otherwise provided by ecclesiastical laws, that is, he is to be admonished and taught secretly and in a friendly manner, etc.* He follows a doctrine not improbable, especially if someone, not with evil mind but through ignorance, should utter some heresy in the presence of another, in which case he is at once to be admonished to lay aside the error. But the contrary opinion is more common, and more received in practice. Nay, no other is by any means to be followed after the Decree of Alexander VII, in the year 1660, in the month of August, in which he strictly commands that both the heretic and the one suspected of heresy, however lightly, be altogether denounced to the Ordinaries of the places, or to the Inquisitors, no fraternal correction being premised; so that one is bound to this even though he think him already amended, as Delbene holds with Suarez, Rojas, Simancas, and others, (28) who, having previously followed the contrary opinion, afterwards retracts it on account of the aforesaid Decree; which indeed seems to be consonant with *Lateranens. Concil. sess. 9. §. Ad abolenda*, and the Constitution of Pius I, *de qua in cap. Siquis per capillum 22. q. 1.* and the Constitution of Julius III which begins *In multis*. {marg. (28) P. 1. dub. 18. n. 7.} And finally that opinion is to be held with caution, lest we fall into the fifth proposition condemned by Alexander VII. Let it suffice to have touched upon these things in passing, so that the things which are the more securely to be embraced may be proved upon the Lydian stone. |
| 104 *Cap. 6. pag. 252.* eam sibi arrogat provinciam, ut ad ecclesiasticos Censores dirigendos aliquas regulas constituat, & Magistrorum Magister eruditionis sibi assumens supercilium, *magisterii pondus tanto levius æstimat, quanto vim magnitudinis illius ignorat*; (29) sed hoc illi benignissime condonandum esset, si in aliquas minus tutas doctrinas prolabi non videretur. {marg. (29) Gregor. in Prolog. pastor.} Statim enim *pag. 254.* hanc habet non multum prudentem propositionem: *Omnia ferme sanctorum Patrum, & antiquorum volumina aliquas habent falsæ pietatis accusationes. Immo acerrimæ apud illos leguntur orationes, quales ne imitari quidem prorsus nos vellemus, in corruptos Clericorum, & Monachorum mores, in superstitiosas, parumque pias consuetudines, in licentiam opinionum, in vitia Præsulum.* Si nostrum quipiam hæc proferre ausus esset, temeritatis nota inureretur, & forsan ipse fortissima objurgatione compellasset. In sanctis Patribus frequentissime, & in omnibus ferme falsæ pietatis accusationes! In Ecclesiæ Doctoribus orationes à nobis nullatenus imitandæ! Reprehensiones severæ in corruptos mores, in superstitiosas consuetudines, in opiniones noxias, in vitia nocentia, falsæ pietatis accusari possunt! Et hæc *doctissimi, atque prudentissimi inter catholicos* ad temeritatem accedere non arbitrabuntur? Hæc præludit, ut *piam pervicaciam* ausus sit dicere eam animi devotionem, qua defendimus, tuerique juramus immaculatam Deiparæ Conceptionem. Deliravit profecto. Sed de hoc ultimo loco agendum nobis est. | | 104 *Cap. 6. pag. 252.* he arrogates to himself this province, of establishing certain rules for directing the ecclesiastical Censors, and, assuming to himself, as Master of Masters, the supercilious brow of erudition, *he esteems the weight of the magisterium so much the lighter, the more he is ignorant of the force of its greatness*; (29) but this would be most kindly pardoned him, if he did not seem to slip into certain less safe doctrines. {marg. (29) Gregor. in Prolog. pastor.} For straightway, *pag. 254.*, he has this not very prudent proposition: *Almost all the volumes of the holy Fathers and of the ancients have some accusations of false piety. Nay, there are read among them most sharp discourses, such as we should by no means wish even to imitate, against the corrupt morals of Clerics and Monks, against superstitious and little pious customs, against license of opinions, against the vices of Prelates.* If any one of ours had dared to utter these things, he would be branded with the mark of temerity, and perhaps he himself would have assailed him with the strongest rebuke. In the holy Fathers most frequently, and in almost all of them, accusations of false piety! In the Doctors of the Church discourses by no means to be imitated by us! Severe reprehensions against corrupt morals, against superstitious customs, against noxious opinions, against harmful vices, can be accused of false piety! And will *the most learned and most prudent among catholics* not judge that these things approach temerity? These things he preludes, that he might dare to call a *pious obstinacy* that devotion of soul with which we defend, and swear to protect, the immaculate Conception of the Mother of God. He has raved indeed. But of this last point we must treat. |
| 105 *Pag. deinde 256.* Censores admonet, ne à prudentia discedant, quod fieri potest dum adeo caute agere studeant, ut in scrupulos, cavillationes, & asperitatem nimiam rapiantur. Et qui isti sunt, quos satagit edocere? Hispanos, Lusitanosque Censores, quos aliquantum pungit. Tales hi solent esse electi ex millibus, ut Muratorio admonitore non indigeant. Censoriam virgam semper rectam, nunquam inclinantem, ipsi ab optimis scriptoribus rite instructi manu tenent, ut æquali semper libra & noxia respuant, & salubria non contemnant. Harum censurarum quis Muratorium, nisi sui ipsius quam maxima confidentia, censorio munere constituit decoratum? Scrupulos appellat hæreses suspicari, errores metuere, & præcavere, ne Respublica, aut Religio aliquod nocumentum experiatur. Hoc tamen eorum munus est semper laudandum, semperque laudabile, nullatenusque reprehendendum. Tanta est librorum, sive libraliolorum copia ad nostras oras frequentissime appulsa, atque Censorum trutinæ subrepta, ut nulla sufficiat cautela, nullum superabundet examen, nullus scrupulus noxius esse possit. Furtive quandoque in vulgus sparguntur, ut & his Muratorii scriptionibus accidisse fertur. Ferme è regionibus hæresis veneno infectis ad nostram adventare, compertum est; & suspectos multoties habent Auctores. Hinc vigilantia summa opus est; occultissima namque toxica inter eruditionis flores sic recondunt, ut incauti lectores inmedicabili vulnere sauciati reperiantur, si eis Censorum opera anticipatione prudenti helleborum non præparetur. In his, similibusque libris, in quibus utilitas fortasse nulla, (exemplum habemus) forteque damnum inesse potest, expurgandis, quam maxime elaborandum est, neque centoculi exploratores ad detegendas eorum larvas superfluere videbuntur. Nimia in his rebus scrupulosa sedulitas nemini nocere potest: benigna condescendentia, aut parum intenta vigilantia facile est ut evadat exitialis. Postquam hæc librorum exterorum ingluvies furtive in nostram irruit Lusitaniam, pietatem, religionem, amorem in sanctam Sedem, in Pontificia Decreta reverentiam elanguisse experti sumus. | | 105 Then, *pag. 256.*, he admonishes the Censors not to depart from prudence, which can happen while they strive to act so cautiously that they are carried away into scruples, cavils, and excessive harshness. And who are these whom he busies himself to instruct? The Spanish and Portuguese Censors, whom he stings not a little. Such men are wont to be chosen out of thousands, so that they do not need Muratori for an admonitor. The censorial rod, always straight, never bending, they hold in hand, duly instructed by the best writers, so that with an ever-even balance they may both reject the noxious and not despise the wholesome. Who, except the very greatest confidence in himself, has constituted Muratori adorned with the censorial office over these censures? He calls it scruples to suspect heresies, to fear errors, and to take precaution lest the Commonwealth or Religion experience some harm. Yet this office of theirs is always to be praised, always praiseworthy, and in no wise to be reprehended. So great is the abundance of books, or booklets, most frequently landed upon our shores and withdrawn by stealth from the balance of the Censors, that no caution can suffice, no examination be superabundant, no scruple can be harmful. They are sometimes furtively spread among the people, as is reported to have happened also with these writings of Muratori. It is found that they come to ours almost entirely from regions infected with the poison of heresy; and they very often have suspect Authors. Hence there is need of the greatest vigilance; for they hide most secret poisons among the flowers of erudition in such a way that incautious readers are found wounded with an incurable wound, unless hellebore be prepared for them by the work of the Censors with prudent anticipation. In expurgating these and similar books, in which perhaps there is no utility (we have an example), and in which perchance harm may be present, the greatest labor must be expended, nor will hundred-eyed explorers seem superfluous for detecting their masks. Excessive scrupulous sedulity in these matters can harm no one: benign condescension, or too little intent vigilance, may easily turn out deadly. After this glut of foreign books furtively rushed into our Lusitania, we have found by experience that piety, religion, love toward the holy See, and reverence for the Pontifical Decrees have languished. |
| 106 Cavillationem vocat in deteriorem partem accipere sententias, sive voces, quæ commodissime in sanum sensum admitti valent. Si nihil aliud recondunt, neque fidei, aut honestati disconveniunt, nullus erit, qui condemnet, qui calumnietur. Si tamen, ut quandoque, non consonam sententiam sub vocibus duplicem sensum admittentibus abscondant, non cavillatio erit, sed prudentissima cautela, eam aut non admittere, aut explicare. Hæ voces fastosæ, hæ phrases æquivocæ, hæ enuntiationes elegantes, hæc verba vel impropria, vel per translationem significantia, ad res fidei, morum, disciplinæque expromendas non multum conducunt, & plus nocumenti, quam utilitatis afferre contingit; neque enim omnes his artibus sic instructi sunt, ut scribentis mentem assequantur. | | 106 He calls it cavillation to take in the worse part opinions or words which can most conveniently be admitted in a sound sense. If they conceal nothing else, and are not at variance with faith or honesty, there will be no one to condemn, no one to calumniate. If however, as sometimes happens, they hide a dissonant opinion under words admitting a double sense, it will not be cavillation, but most prudent caution, either not to admit it, or to explain it. These pompous words, these equivocal phrases, these elegant enunciations, these words either improper or signifying by metaphor, do not much conduce to setting forth matters of faith, of morals, and of discipline, and it happens that they bring more harm than utility; for not all are so instructed in these arts as to attain the mind of the writer. |
| 107 Asperitatem significat editioni librorum obices opponere, aut opprimere quæ facile emendari possunt. Hoc nihil aliud est, quam manu occulta Censorum lædere incorruptam integritatem. Quis nostrorum unquam obices non opponendos opposuit? Quis, quod facile corrigi potuisset, non benigne adnotavit, ut corrigeretur? Quis scriptores, aut libros, Ecclesiæ sanctæ necessarios, rigidissime excepit? Profecto non edocet Muratorius, sed calumniatur. Verum enim verò qui sunt hi libri Ecclesiæ utiles, & necessarii? An Novatorum inter elegantiæ flores noxia complurima intermiscentium? An suus *Lamindi* opinionibus piis sese opponens, hæreticorum libros ob eruditionem admittendos asserens, Censorum cavillationes, & asperitates accusans? Quæ Ecclesiæ necessitas, ut ejus infallibilitas aliquo modo proscindatur, ut Deiparæ sanctissimæ immaculata Conceptio non juretur, aliaque his similia? An sua opella *Portugalliæ fides* Tribunalis fidei authoritatem condulcans? Ad elegantiam styli quid dicemus? Non crepitantes periodi, non confragosæ voces, non inusitata verba nobis placent. Non vis facundiæ in hoc dicendi genere est collocanda. Si eloquia purissima ediscere cupiamus, habemus Cicerones, Quintilianos, Hieronymos, Leones, Ambrosios, Chrysostomos, aliosque similes, à quibus fontibus veras dicendi leges haurire possimus. | | 107 By harshness he means to oppose obstacles to the publication of books, or to suppress things which can easily be emended. This is nothing else than to wound with a hidden hand the incorrupt integrity of the Censors. Who of ours has ever opposed obstacles that ought not to be opposed? Who has not benignly noted what could easily have been corrected, so that it might be corrected? Who has treated with the utmost rigor writers or books necessary to the holy Church? Truly Muratori does not teach, but calumniates. But in truth, what are these books useful and necessary to the Church? Those of the Innovators, mingling very many noxious things among the flowers of elegance? Or his own *Lamindus*, opposing itself to pious opinions, asserting that the books of heretics are to be admitted on account of erudition, accusing the cavils and harshnesses of the Censors? What necessity of the Church is there, that her infallibility should be in some way cut down, that the immaculate Conception of the most holy Mother of God should not be sworn, and other things like these? Or his little work *Portugalliæ fides*, sweetening the authority of the Tribunal of the faith? As to elegance of style, what shall we say? Not crackling periods, not rugged words, not unusual terms please us. The force of eloquence is not to be placed in this kind of speaking. If we desire to learn the purest utterances, we have Ciceros, Quintilians, Jeromes, Leos, Ambroses, Chrysostoms, and others like them, from which fountains we may draw the true laws of speaking. |
| 108 Postea *pag. 257.* hanc regulam statuit: *Prudentis est ita depellere quæ nocere possunt, ut interim tamen remedium ipsum maiorem non adferat Ecclesiæ, atque reipublicæ perniciem, aut maiorem non impediat utilitatem.* Hanc optimam regulam quis non laudabit? Ast quam cito degenerat: *Venena non dantur, nisi melle circumlita: & vitia non decipiunt, nisi sub specie, umbraque virtutu n.* (30) {marg. (30) Ambros. lib. de Virgin.} Fatetur hæreticorum libros jure proscribi, è manibusque fidelium tolli: en quomodo depelluntur quæ nocere possunt. Statim subjicit: *Attamen siquis catholicum quemque, doctum licet, piumque virum ab hujusmodi lectione prohiberet, is in incommodum gravius Ecclesiam impelleret, læderetque prudentiæ leges apertas.* Lædit ergo Ecclesia prudentiæ leges, cum similes libros omnino prohibet? Et qui sunt hi viri docti, piique viri? Eruntne qui de suamet sapientia maxime sibi plaudunt, scioli inquam aliqui, qui librorum prohibitionem spernentes, eorum lectioni operam navare student, & eorum vel falsis, vel parum securis doctrinis instructi, jactantiose garriunt, quos equidem his male sanis doctrinis imbutos experimur? Nonne securius erit, quod horum virorum sapientiam, & pietatem Ecclesia prius perspectam habeat, ut eis facultatem hos libros legendi permittat, ut fieri assolet? Sed quæ prudentiæ leges isthæc sunt? Me non invenisse, fateri non erubesco. In quod tamen gravius incommodum impellent Ecclesiam qui hos libros prohiberet? Quia docti viri artes, sophismata, argumentaque hæreticorum noscere debent, ut melius orthodoxa dogmata tueantur? Et hæc est maior utilitas, ut libri noxii non prohibeantur? Hoc est maius nocumentum, quam ipsum remedium? Hæc est sufficiens ratio, ut ipse in hanc absolutam erumpat propositionem: *Facere nequeunt edicta Præsulum, ut perversi libri statim à confixione aboleantur*? Si sunt *perversi*, cur statim non sunt abolendi? Quo jure Præsules hac potestate, hoc sui officii munere exuit? *Quanto maiora timentur esse dispendia, tanto promptior, & perfectior debet esse cautela*, ait Chrysologus. Si statim noxii libri omnino non prohibeantur, multi indocti, quorum infinitus est numerus, & etiam docti aliqui, aut qui de se ipsis eam concipiunt existimationem, noxiis doctrinis decepti & corrumpentur, & alios corrumpent, & postea serò medicina parabitur: *Minus jacula feriunt, quæ prævidentur.* Securius est *ante tempus occurrere, quam post illatum vulnus remedium quærere.* Id dictant veræ prudentiæ leges, cæteræ omnes sunt fallaces. Nihil boni in horum librorum lectione inveniri poterit, quod posthabendum non sit in comparatione maximi, & irreparabilis damni, quod prudentissime timetur. Si ita non fiat, quot erunt, qui dum per amœnissima prata spatiari arbitrantur, latentis inter gramina viperæ morsu amarissimo remaneant vulnerati. Nec flocci pendendi sunt fructus illi, quos ex jucundis quidem, sed infectis pomæriis carpere poterit. Eruditio, elegantia, notitia ex aliis salubrioribus viridariis colligendæ supersunt. Ut errores, sophismata, fraudes, versutiæque hæreticorum explodantur, tam gravi dispendio opus non est: In promptu est necessaria, utilisque providentia. Ideo Ecclesia quos citra corruptionis periculum doctiores agnoscit, ad eos legendos, & refutandos libros facultate condecorat. Nullus tamen, etsi doctissimus, eam sibi assumere debet licentiam, quin prius prædicta facultate à sancta Sede præstita fulciatur. | | 108 Afterwards, *pag. 257.*, he lays down this rule: *It belongs to the prudent man so to drive away things that can harm, that meanwhile the remedy itself may not bring greater ruin to the Church and to the commonwealth, or may not impede a greater utility.* Who will not praise this excellent rule? But how quickly it degenerates: *Poisons are not given except smeared round with honey: and vices do not deceive except under the appearance and shadow of virtues.* (30) {marg. (30) Ambros. lib. de Virgin.} He confesses that the books of heretics are rightly proscribed, and taken from the hands of the faithful: behold how the things that can harm are driven away. Straightway he subjoins: *And yet if anyone should prohibit any catholic, though a learned and pious man, from reading of this kind, he would drive the Church into a graver inconvenience, and would wound the open laws of prudence.* Does the Church then wound the laws of prudence, when she altogether prohibits similar books? And who are these learned men, and pious men? Will they be those who most applaud themselves for their own wisdom—certain sciolists, I say, who, despising the prohibition of books, study to devote their effort to reading them, and, instructed with their either false or scarcely safe doctrines, boastfully chatter, whom indeed we find imbued with these unsound doctrines? Will it not be safer that the Church should first have the wisdom and piety of these men examined, so as to permit them the faculty of reading these books, as is wont to be done? But what laws of prudence are these? That I have not found them, I do not blush to confess. Yet into what graver inconvenience will they drive the Church that would prohibit these books? Because learned men ought to know the arts, sophisms, and arguments of the heretics, that they may better defend the orthodox dogmas? And is this the greater utility, that noxious books not be prohibited? Is this a greater harm than the remedy itself? Is this a sufficient reason for him to burst forth into this absolute proposition: *The edicts of Prelates cannot bring it about that perverse books be abolished immediately upon their fabrication*? If they are *perverse*, why are they not to be abolished at once? By what right does he strip the Prelates of this power, of this duty of their office? *The greater the losses that are feared, the more prompt and the more perfect ought the precaution to be*, says Chrysologus. If noxious books are not at once altogether prohibited, many unlearned men, whose number is infinite, and even some learned, or such as conceive that estimation of themselves, deceived by noxious doctrines, will both be corrupted and will corrupt others, and afterward the medicine will be prepared too late: *The darts that are foreseen strike with less force.* It is safer *to meet the danger before the time than to seek a remedy after the wound has been inflicted.* This the laws of true prudence dictate; all others are deceitful. Nothing good will be able to be found in the reading of these books which ought not to be set aside in comparison with the greatest and irreparable damage, which is most prudently feared. If it be not done thus, how many will there be who, while they think they are strolling through most pleasant meadows, remain wounded by the most bitter bite of a viper hiding among the grass. Nor are those fruits to be valued a straw which one will be able to pluck from orchards pleasant indeed, but infected. Erudition, elegance, knowledge remain to be gathered from other more wholesome gardens. That the errors, sophisms, frauds, and wiles of the heretics may be exploded, there is no need of so grave a loss: the necessary and useful provision is at hand. Therefore the Church adorns with the faculty of reading and refuting those books the more learned men whom she recognizes to be beyond the danger of corruption. Yet no one, however most learned, ought to assume that license to himself, without first being supported by the aforesaid faculty granted by the holy See. |
| 109 Verum quidem est, utinamque non foret, has exitiales contagiones non solum inter segregatas à gremio Ecclesiæ Provincias divagari, sed etiam orthodoxas invadere, ut corrumpant. Id circo tanta vigilantia, nullaque satis, ut omnino relegentur. Id circo tot cautelæ, tot fulminatæ censuræ, ut non legantur, ut non retineantur, ut in fidei Tribunalibus vel tradantur, vel denuntientur, qui eos constiterit retinere. Si *teterrima pestis est* hoc librorum genus, cur vagari, aut debacchari permittendum erit? Cur non prohibebuntur? Cur ejus lectio etiam doctis admittenda est? Cur non cremabuntur, ut omnino *pereat memoria eorum, & nomen eorum non memoretur ultra*? Id fieri consuevisse à tempore Apostolorum testatur sacra Pagina *Actor. 19. Multi ex eis, qui fuerant curiosa sectati, contulerunt libros, & combusserunt eos coram omnibus.* Id etiam apud gentiles observabatur, quoties liber aliquis bonis moribus, aut suæ falsæ religioni contrarius evulgabatur, testibus Cicerone *lib. 1. de Nat. Deor.* & Valerio Maximo *lib. 1. cap. 1.* Id etiam apud ipsos hæreticos moris est, semperque fuit. In doctissimo Carena me vidisse recordor, Italum quemdam doctum, quia in sua Bibliotheca inventus fuerit liber hæreticus, punitum, & abjurare coactum fuisse propter vehementem suspicionem ex tali asservatione resultantem. Credo, neque vana fides, Ecclesiam, Pontificesque Maximos prudentia præstare Muratorio. Cur ergo non probat ille, quod Ecclesia probat? Ecclesia præcipit, quod similes libri à nemine legantur, quod crementur: quomodo ergo prudentiæ legibus consonum Muratorius dicit esse, quod saltem viris doctis legendi permittantur? Ut refutentur? Securius sane est his disputationibus omnes aditus intercludere, silentiumque nostrum non *cessæ victoriæ*, sed catholico contemptui tribuendum erit. Sat in instructionem nostram in orthodoxis libris scriptum est. Ad hæreticorum conversionem? Dextræ Excelsi hæc conversio opus est. Ipsa juvabit, & dabit vera sapientibus in illa hora quid loquantur. | | 109 It is indeed true — and would that it were not — that these deadly contagions not only wander abroad among the Provinces separated from the bosom of the Church, but also invade orthodox ones, that they may corrupt them. Hence such great vigilance — and none is enough — that they may be altogether banished. Hence so many precautions, so many censures fulminated, that they be not read, that they be not retained, that they be either handed over in the Tribunals of the faith, or that those be denounced whom it shall be established to have retained them. If this kind of books *is a most foul pestilence*, why shall it be permitted to roam about, or to run riot? Why shall they not be prohibited? Why is the reading of it to be allowed even to the learned? Why shall they not be burned, that altogether *their memory may perish, and their name be remembered no more*? That this was customarily done from the time of the Apostles the sacred Page testifies, *Actor. 19. Many of them who had followed curious arts brought together their books, and burned them before all.* This was observed also among the gentiles, whenever some book contrary to good morals, or to their false religion, was published, as witness Cicero, *lib. 1. de Nat. Deor.*, and Valerius Maximus, *lib. 1. cap. 1.* This is also the custom among the heretics themselves, and always has been. I recall having seen in the most learned Carena that a certain learned Italian, because a heretical book had been found in his Library, was punished, and was compelled to abjure, on account of the vehement suspicion resulting from such keeping. I believe — nor is the belief vain — that the Church and the Supreme Pontiffs surpass Muratori in prudence. Why then does he not approve what the Church approves? The Church commands that such books be read by no one, that they be burned: how then does Muratori say it is consonant with the laws of prudence that they be permitted at least to learned men to read? That they may be refuted? It is surely safer to shut off all approaches to these disputations, and our silence will have to be attributed not to *a yielded victory*, but to Catholic contempt. Enough has been written in orthodox books for our instruction. For the conversion of heretics? This conversion is the work of the right hand of the Most High. It will itself assist, and will give to the truly wise in that hour what they are to speak. |
| 110 Alii libri, etiam hæreticorum, si à vera doctrina non abhorrent, & literariæ reipublicæ utilia continent, non reprobantur, neque à nostris Censoribus projiciuntur. Revidentur utique, & jure revidentur; immo eadem prudentia maiorem cautelam, accuratioremque indaginem præriquirit; nomen siquidem Authoris, aut ejus occultatio ingentem secum trahit suspicionem. Si ab omnibus maculis depurata conspicitur, publicæ luci, communi utilitati non denegatur. Rara tamen avis erit in terra hæretici cujuscumque liber, qui aliquo nævo non inficietur; qui enim fieri potest, ut corruptus homo nullum malum odorem redoleat, etiamsi mille Sabæis aromatibus oblinitum sese exhibeat. Si tamen in veritatis appensus statera inventus est minus habens, quæ noxia possunt esse, expunguntur, cæteris innocentibus nec in minimo derogatur. | | 110 Other books, even of heretics, if they do not depart from true doctrine, and contain things useful to the republic of letters, are not reprobated, nor cast away by our Censors. They are reviewed, to be sure, and rightly reviewed; nay, the same prudence requires beforehand a greater caution and a more accurate examination; since indeed the name of the Author, or its concealment, draws with it an immense suspicion. If it is seen to be purged from all stains, it is not denied to the public light, to the common utility. Yet a rare bird on the earth will be the book of any heretic whatsoever that is not infected with some blemish; for how can it come to pass that a corrupt man give off no evil odor, even though he present himself smeared with a thousand Sabaean perfumes. If, however, weighed in the balance of truth, he has been found wanting, those things that can be noxious are expunged, while the rest, being innocent, is not derogated from even in the least. |
| 111 Huic examini, & forte rigidiori calculo subjici debent qui christiano nomine, & à catholicis, ut dicuntur, scriptoribus, frequentissime eduntur libri, & per universum literarium orbem circumferuntur. Hi quidem prima facie nihil expresse hæreticum afferre visi sunt; quod tamen ex inquinatis fontibus ebiberant, inquinatum eructant. Tales sunt aliqui libri, præcipue ad Theologiam asceticam, aut disciplinam ecclesiasticam attinentes. Primi sub speciosissimo prætextu ducendi fideles animas per spirituales vias ad æternæ salutis portum, in Molinistarum, Illuminatorum, Quietistarum, Beguardorum, & Jansenistarum, aliarumque pestium perversa dogmata, nolentes, volentes insensibiliter dilabuntur; quarum perniciosissimas consequentias quotidie non sine mœrore magno, maximoque religionis detrimento experimur. Cur non invigilabunt Censores nostri, ut hæc nullatenus permittantur? Alii verò, sub reformandæ religionis velamine, antiquam disciplinam ecclesiasticam nostris moribus disconvenientem suscitare student, & ad hunc finem Concilia, sanctorumque Patrum libros pro suo sensu aut deturpant, aut pessime interpretantur: hincque plurima circa sacramentorum administrationem, quæ ipsi somniant antiquitus usitata, introducere iterum adnituntur; nec hodiernæ disciplinæ parcunt, quin potius convitiis lacessunt, ac dilacerant. Alii quoad moralem scientiam opiniones quidem speculative probabiles, sed in praxi nullatenus admittendas, à sanctaque Sede jam propscritas iterum docent, suscitareque intendunt. Hi Auctores, aliique ejusdem indolis multa utilissima, multa maximæ eruditionis interserunt, ideoque non sunt penitus obliterandi; sed necessarium est, ut Censorum cribo offerantur, ut bonum triticum à zizania separetur, illorum scilicet, qui butyro, melleque enutriti optime noverint reprobare malum, & eligere bonum: aliter fiet, (quod nos docet optima magistra experientia) ut qui labore alieno aliis eruditi videri ambiunt, nescientes certa ab incertis separare, in erroneas, falsasque sententias dilabantur. Unde prudentius ageret Muratorius, si mittenda manu in segetem alienam abstineret, Hispanosque Censores in suo munere explendo intactos relinqueret. | | 111 To this examination, and perhaps to a more rigid reckoning, ought to be subjected those books which are most frequently published under the Christian name, and by catholic writers, as they are called, and are carried about through the whole literary world. These indeed at first sight have seemed to bring forward nothing expressly heretical; yet what they had drunk in from polluted fountains, they belch forth polluted. Such are some books, especially those pertaining to ascetical Theology, or to ecclesiastical discipline. The first, under the most specious pretext of leading faithful souls through spiritual ways to the port of eternal salvation, slip insensibly, willing or unwilling, into the perverse dogmas of the Molinists, the Illuminati, the Quietists, the Beghards, and the Jansenists, and of other pests; whose most pernicious consequences we experience daily, not without great sorrow, and the greatest detriment to religion. Why shall our Censors not keep watch, that these be in no way permitted? Others, however, under the veil of reforming religion, strive to revive the ancient ecclesiastical discipline, unsuited to our manners, and to this end either disfigure the Councils and the books of the holy Fathers according to their own sense, or interpret them in the worst way: and hence they strive to introduce again very many things concerning the administration of the sacraments which they themselves dream were used in antiquity; nor do they spare the discipline of today, but rather assail it with reproaches, and tear it to pieces. Others, as regards moral science, teach again opinions speculatively probable indeed, but in practice by no means to be admitted, and already proscribed by the holy See, and intend to revive them. These Authors, and others of the same stamp, intersperse many most useful things, many things of the greatest erudition, and therefore are not to be utterly blotted out; but it is necessary that they be presented to the sieve of the Censors, that the good wheat may be separated from the cockle — of those, namely, who, nourished on butter and honey, know best how to refuse the evil and choose the good: otherwise it will happen (as experience, that best of teachers, instructs us) that those who by another's labor aspire to seem erudite to others, not knowing how to separate the certain from the uncertain, slip into erroneous and false opinions. Whence Muratori would act more prudently if he refrained from putting his hand into another's harvest, and left the Spanish Censors untouched in the fulfilling of their office. |
| 112 Mirum iane qua calidissima arte, & veluti mitissima manu, sed acutissimo calamo pungit, ac ferit, & usque ad penetralia famæ pertingit, dum eosdem insimulat, ac maximi facinoris accusat, eo quod non solum recentiorum libros, sed etiam sanctorum Patrum audeant emaculare. Imposturæ reus judicandus erit. Sanctorum Patrum scripta germana omnes nostri, Hispanique Censores quam maxime venerantur, quos Ecclesia recepit, ipsi reverenter recipiunt, amplectuntur. Sed Muratorium percontor. Aut in ipsis sanctorum Patrum voluminibus inveniuntur quædam fidei, aut religioni consona, vel non? Si non; quare de viris doctissimis, selectissimis eam temeritatem suspicatur, aut in publicum prodit, ut sugillet? Id de tantis viris credendum non est: superest, ut Muratorius non vello levato accuset, sed evidenter probet; aliàs calumniatoris pœnis mulctandus erit. Si verò noxiæ doctrinæ in eis reperiuntur, quæ ratio sufficiens assignari poterit, ut non deleantur? Sophistica penitus est Muratorii ratio. Utilitas non est paucorum, sed omnium: incommodum nullum inde paratur, nullum ipsis sanctis dedecus, nulla inde injuria Ecclesiæ instruitur. Non fidissimis editionibus fides adimitur, sed vitiatis. Non Patrum honos minuitur, sed augetur, atque ab illato dedecore vindicatur. Quis proferre audebit, aurum à fæcibus expurgatum de suo intrinseco valore deperdere, aut suam imminuere æstimationem? | | 112 It is wondrous indeed with what most cunning art, and as it were with a most gentle hand, but with a most sharp pen, he pricks and strikes, and reaches even to the inmost recesses of reputation, while he charges these same men, and accuses them of the greatest crime, because they dare to cleanse not only the books of the more recent authors, but even those of the holy Fathers. He will have to be judged guilty of imposture. The genuine writings of the holy Fathers all our Censors, and the Spanish, venerate in the highest degree; those whom the Church has received, they themselves reverently receive and embrace. But I question Muratori. Either there are found in the very volumes of the holy Fathers certain things not consonant [?] with faith, or with religion, or there are not? If not; why does he suspect such rashness of most learned, most select men, or come forth in public to taunt them? This is not to be believed of such great men: it remains that Muratori should not accuse with veil lifted [?], but should evidently prove it; otherwise he will have to be mulcted with the penalties of a calumniator. But if noxious doctrines are found in them, what sufficient reason will one be able to assign that they should not be deleted? Muratori's reasoning is thoroughly sophistical. The utility is not of the few, but of all: no inconvenience is thence prepared, no disgrace to the saints themselves, no injury to the Church is thence contrived. Credit is not taken away from the most faithful editions, but from the vitiated ones. The honor of the Fathers is not diminished, but increased, and is vindicated from the disgrace inflicted upon it.
