Who will dare to assert that gold purged of its dross loses anything of its intrinsic value, or diminishes its own estimation? |
| 113 Me hercle, si non latet anguis in herba, Muratorii mentem non attingo. Licitum fuerit hæreticis sanctorum Patrum libros mendis replere, suas sententias vitiare, ut eas cavillose in nos retorqueant; nobis verò in vitium dabitur, quod istos libros sic infectos emaculemus? Æquum erit, quod isti libri iterum, atque iterum imposturis maculatos typis mandentur; injustum verò quod à nobis hæ maculæ aspergantur? Dedecus Patrum judicabitur, quod ex eorum libris inserti à Novatoribus errores expungantur; & non vertetur in eorum ignominiam, quod cum prædictis erroribus publicam lucem videant? Quis nescit quanta hæretici machinentur, quanta falsa sanctorum Patrum dictis intermisceant, ut iis in perniciem religionis catholicæ abutantur? Quanta Augustino, quem suum semper faciunt, falsissime attribuunt? Ergo Sanctorum gloriam, & honorem quærunt, qui opera sua ab his dolosissimis impostoribus restituit vindicata. Non inde malam causam nostram suspicari possunt Catholici, immo plenius istorum hominum versutias agnoscent. Non istud est à sanctis Patribus metuere, ut effingit Muratorius, sed pro eorum doctrina acerrime dimicare; sic inimicorum arma fraudulenta franguntur, sic eis blasfema clauduntur ora, cum eorum astutiæ declamantur. Non hoc est, ut ille ait, malis artibus veritatis causam tueri, sed uno validissimo ictu falsitatem omnino prosternere. Aliis argumentationibus opus non est. Si nobis opponant: Sic Augustinus docet; uno verbo tota eorum machina ruet. Mentiris: non Augustini hic locus est, sed vestrorum, qui sententiam Augustini deturparunt. Hac in caput suum retorta sagitta repercussi cadent, & obmutescent. Si tamen adhuc garriant, quid pavendum est? Latrare poterunt, mordere non poterunt. Sic fecit Guillielmus Cave, cujus audacia vestigia premit Muratorius. | | 113 By Hercules, if a snake does not lie hidden in the grass, I do not grasp Muratori's meaning. Shall it have been lawful for heretics to fill the books of the holy Fathers with faults, to vitiate their sentences, that they may cavillously twist them back against us; but to us shall it be imputed as a fault that we cleanse those books so infected? Will it be equitable that those books be committed to the press again and again stained with impostures; but unjust that these stains be wiped away [?] by us? Will it be judged a disgrace to the Fathers that the errors inserted by the Innovators be expunged from their books; and will it not turn to their ignominy that they see the public light with the aforesaid errors? Who does not know how much the heretics contrive, how many falsities they intermingle with the sayings of the holy Fathers, that they may abuse them for the ruin of the catholic religion? How much do they most falsely attribute to Augustine, whom they always make their own? Therefore they seek the glory and honor of the Saints, who restore their works vindicated from these most crafty impostors. [?] Catholics cannot thence suspect our cause to be bad; nay rather, they will more fully recognize the wiles of those men. This is not to be afraid of the holy Fathers, as Muratori feigns, but to fight most keenly for their doctrine; thus the fraudulent arms of the enemies are broken, thus their blasphemous mouths are shut, when their cunning devices are denounced. This is not, as he says, to defend the cause of truth by evil arts, but with one most mighty blow to lay falsehood utterly low. There is no need of other argumentations. If they object to us: Thus Augustine teaches; with one word their whole machine will collapse. You lie: this passage is not Augustine's, but of your own men, who have disfigured Augustine's sentence. Struck back by this arrow turned upon their own head, they will fall, and be silent. Yet if they still chatter, what is there to dread? They will be able to bark, they will not be able to bite. So did William Cave, in whose audacious footsteps Muratori treads. |
| 114 Maxime laudat, quod aliæ Catholicorum Provinciæ *non imitentur hoc Hispanorum facinus*, (si moderatius loqueretur, melius ageret) *& prodire adhuc inter ipsos Catholicos sanctorum Patrum opera, nec ullo quidem apice dempto, in Gallia potissimum.* Forte si Hispanos, Lusitanosque imitarentur alii Catholici, suæ pietatis, suæ religionis, suæ maximæ in rebus fidei vigilantiæ laudabiliter æmuli, non adeo erroribus contaminati invenirentur? Quæ Catholicorum Provinciæ ita errorum immunes sunt, quæ in aliqua noxia non prolabantur? Prætermitto, quæ Lutheranorum, Calvinistarum, Protestantium, Reformatorum, & similium hæresibus omnino infectæ sunt. Quot tamen inter ipsas catholicas Provincias Hugonis, Baii, Arnaldi, Jansenii, Molinos conspurcati inveniuntur? Quot libri his pestibus corrupti, sed mira eruditione larvati in lucem prodeunt? Oh utinam mendacii reus efficerer! Oh utinam ad nostros fines non pervenirent! Profecto, si ita fieret, morum, ac religionis laxitatem nimiam non experiremur. | | 114 He most highly praises that other Provinces of Catholics *do not imitate this crime of the Spaniards* (if he spoke more moderately, he would do better), *and that the works of the holy Fathers still come forth among Catholics themselves, with not even a single tittle removed, in France above all.* Perchance, if other Catholics imitated the Spaniards and the Portuguese, becoming laudable emulators of their piety, of their religion, of their greatest vigilance in matters of faith, they would not be found so contaminated with errors? What Provinces of Catholics are so immune from errors that they do not slip into some harm? I pass over those which are altogether infected with the heresies of the Lutherans, Calvinists, Protestants, Reformed, and the like. Yet how many, among the catholic Provinces themselves, are found befouled with the filth of Hugo [?], Baius, Arnauld, Jansenius, Molinos? How many books corrupted by these pests, but masked with wondrous erudition, come forth into the light? Oh would that I might be convicted of falsehood! Oh would that they might not reach our borders! Assuredly, if it were so, we should not experience so excessive a laxity of morals and of religion. |
| 115 Quid tamen blatit Muratorius? Vel exteræ editiones genuina Patrum opera in lucem proferunt, vel à cavillosis Novatoribus deturpata? Si primum, laudandi plurimum ii sunt, & Censores nostri etiam laudandi, qui germanos Patrum fœtus nec in apice offendunt. Si secundum, injuriam maximam Patribus inferunt, dum quæ eis falso, & astute addita cognoscuntur, quasi sua, legenda proponunt, in quo non modo non laudandi, sed potius maxime vituperandi sunt qui hæc sic vitiata edere non reformidant. Quippe qui & Patrum existimationem audacter lædunt, dum perniciosas sententias eis attribuunt, & perniciem legentibus afferunt, qui erroneis doctrinis, putantes eas Patrum esse, confidenter acquiescunt, miserrimeque hallucinantur. In has supposititias, nocivasque additiones merito Censores nostri expungendo invehuntur. Neque satis est, quod hæ additiones in indicibus expurgatoriis adnotentur; non enim omnes eos habent, nec sic laborare tenentur, ut quoties alicujus sancti Patris sententia ad manum venerit, necesse habeant expurgatorios indices percurrere. Facienda est differentia inter legitima, spuriaque scripta. Priora intacta relinquuntur, & si forte aliqua indigent interpretatione, id in indicibus, vel in aliis libris explicantur: cætera expurgantur, ut legentes sciant, eam non esse illius Patris sententiam, sed ab alio subdole adjectam. Si id Muratorio non placet, suo sensu abundet, sed aliis absentibus non obloquatur. | | 115 Yet what does Muratori babble? Either the foreign editions bring forth into the light the genuine works of the Fathers, or works disfigured by cavilling Innovators? If the first, they are greatly to be praised, and our Censors also to be praised, who do not touch the genuine offspring of the Fathers even in a tittle. If the second, they inflict the greatest injury upon the Fathers, while they propose to be read, as though the Fathers' own, things known to have been falsely and craftily added to them; wherein they are not only not to be praised, but rather most greatly to be blamed, who do not shrink from publishing these things thus vitiated. For they both audaciously wound the reputation of the Fathers, while they attribute pernicious opinions to them, and bring ruin upon readers, who confidently acquiesce in erroneous doctrines, thinking them to be the Fathers', and are most miserably deceived. Against these supposititious and harmful additions our Censors deservedly proceed by expunging them. Nor is it enough that these additions be noted in the expurgatory indexes; for not all possess them, nor are men bound so to labor that, as often as some holy Father's sentence comes to hand, they must needs run through the expurgatory indexes. A distinction must be made between legitimate and spurious writings. The former are left untouched, and if perchance some need interpretation, this is explained in the indexes, or in other books: the rest are expurgated, that readers may know that that sentence is not that Father's, but was craftily added by another. If this does not please Muratori, let him abound in his own sense, but let him not speak ill of others in their absence. |
| 116 Ipse instar illius, qui alapam impingit, & abscondit manum, postea excusare intendit, & fatetur Hispanos Censores in germanum textum nihil tentasse, sed tantum in indices, infidasque interpretationes. Cur ergo hucusque accusavit? Cur de ipsis asserit eodem in loco eos *aperte, non clanculum, truncanda esse quædam Patrum loca edixisse*? Truncanda dixissent, quæ eis affinguntur; vera, germanaque delenda esse, neque eis in mentem venisse, certum est. Sed & falsissimum quoque in eos dicitur testimonium, cum asseritur, Cantica Canticorum legenda denegari, quasi nescirent ex divina lectione, ex qua homines, divina quadam impellente vi, ad amandum Deum incitantur, minus honesta animo concipi non posse. Id nunquam in nostrum mentem venire contingit; & debebat Muratorius, ut moderationem, quam alios docet, prius ipse observaret, his convitiis abstinere. Credidit tamen Hispanæ gentis invidis, contumeliosisque impostoribus: credidit levissime exterorum criticis, ac maledicis scriptionibus, in quibus maxime versatus ex suismet scriptis convincitur, & *quod levis mens concipit, levior protinus lingua prodit.* | | 116 He himself, like one who strikes a blow and hides his hand, afterwards endeavors to excuse himself, and confesses that the Spanish Censors attempted nothing against the genuine text, but only against the indexes and unfaithful interpretations. Why then has he accused them up to this point? Why does he assert of them, in the same place, that they *openly, not secretly, decreed that certain places of the Fathers were to be truncated*? What they would have said was to be truncated is what is falsely fathered upon them; that the true and genuine passages were to be deleted never came into their mind — that is certain. But a most false testimony also is spoken against them, when it is asserted that the Canticle of Canticles is denied to be read — as though they did not know that from divine reading, by which men, under the impulse of a certain divine force, are stirred to the love of God, things less honorable cannot be conceived in the mind. This never happens to enter our mind; and Muratori ought — so that he might first himself observe the moderation which he teaches others — to abstain from these reproaches. Yet he believed the envious and contumelious impostors against the Spanish nation: he believed most lightly the critical and slanderous writings of foreigners, in which he is convicted out of his very own writings of being deeply versed, and *what a light mind conceives, a lighter tongue straightway brings forth.* |
| 117 Huic vitio adscribendum est quod sequenti §. sic scribit: *In his procul dubio occurrunt aliqua.... aut etiam ab Ecclesiæ totius mente, ac traditione aliena. Expungenda autem ista non sunt è publicis editionibus.* Fortasse dici absque Patrum injuria poterit, eos scripsisse sententias *ab Ecclesiæ totius mente, & traditione alienas*? Audet hæc is criticus scriptor? Si tamen sunt alienæ, non suæ sunt, sed ab aliquo malevolo impostore insertæ; sancti Patres, nempe, quos Ecclesia colit *Magistros, & veritatis testes*, edocere nequaquam potuere quidquam menti Ecclesiæ non consonum, aut traditioni contrarium. Cur ergo *ex publicis editionibus* quidquid tale inveniatur scriptum, *expungendum non est*? Nulla alia ratio, nisi quia huic Magistrorum Magistro id non arridet. Ejus fundamentum est inane, nullaque soliditate stabilitum, in quo certe non Ecclesiæ, sed Novatorum jura tuetur. Non formidat Ecclesia à sanctis Patribus, non pavet ab hæreticorum insultibus, sed veritatem quærit, & adulterinos partus eisdem Patribus insidiose adoptatos, prudenter rejicit: lucem ambit, ac oppositas luminaribus suis subortas nebulas omnino dispellit. Quid nostra intersunt Novatorum querelæ? Oblatratus suos nec flocci pendamus: id in more habent; cur nobis cavendum est, ne conquerantur? Qua tamen ex causa has fundunt querimonias? Quia depuratis Patrum libris, eis omnino adimuntur arma, quibus nos ferire arbitrabantur. Eccur ergo hæc tella ex iisdem nostris armamentariis subministrabimus? Ipse fatetur mille injuriis affectos fuisse Monachos, eo quod M. Sc. Patrum Codices expurgassent, easque ignominias inique, & ingratiis jactata esse. Si Monachis id datum cum laude fuit, quare nostris Censoribus adimendum, ut quod aliorum vigilantia præteriit, ipsi suppleant, & Patrum scripta ab omni labe ostendant præservata? Solus Muratorius id argueret, id condemnaret. Sed quid, *solum*, dico! Ductores habuit optimos Danielem Francum, Bailletum, aut Morhofium: quam religionem hi profiterentur, suamet scripta patefaciunt. | | 117 To this vice must be ascribed what he writes in the following §. thus: *In these, beyond doubt, there occur some things.... or even foreign to the mind and tradition of the whole Church. Yet these are not to be expunged from the public editions.* Can it perhaps be said, without injury to the Fathers, that they wrote sentences *foreign to the mind and tradition of the whole Church*? Does this critical writer dare these things? If, however, they are foreign, they are not their own, but inserted by some malevolent impostor; for the holy Fathers, namely, whom the Church honors as *Masters, and witnesses of the truth*, could by no means have taught anything not consonant with the mind of the Church, or contrary to tradition. Why then is whatever such thing is found written *not to be expunged from the public editions*? No other reason, except that this does not please this Master of Masters. His foundation is empty, and established on no solidity, wherein assuredly he defends the rights not of the Church, but of the Innovators. The Church does not fear from the holy Fathers, is not terrified by the insults of the heretics, but seeks the truth, and prudently rejects the adulterine offspring insidiously fathered upon those same Fathers: she courts the light, and altogether dispels the clouds that arise in opposition to her luminaries. What do the complaints of the Innovators matter to us? Let us not value their barkings a straw: it is their custom; why must we take care that they not complain? Yet from what cause do they pour forth these complaints? Because, the books of the Fathers being purified, the arms with which they thought to strike us are altogether taken from them. Why then shall we supply these darts from our very own armories? He himself confesses that the Monks were visited with a thousand injuries because they had expurgated the manuscript Codices of the Fathers, and that those ignominies were hurled unjustly and thanklessly. If this was granted to the Monks with praise, why is it to be taken from our Censors, that they may supply what escaped the vigilance of others, and show the writings of the Fathers preserved from every stain? Muratori alone would censure this, would condemn it. But why do I say *alone*! He had excellent guides: Daniel Francus, Baillet, or Morhof: what religion these men professed, their very own writings make plain. |
| 118 Hæreticos contumaces, immo & relapsos plurimum excusare intendit *pag. 326. & seq.* Charitatem præ oculis habendam deprædicat, cui doctrinæ & nos libentissime adstipulamur. Hæc tamen debet esse non ficta, non falsa, non aliis virtutibus contraria. Non sic amplexanda est, ut justitia deseratur. Erga privatos homines aliàs delinquentes non ea charitas est exoptanda, quæ prævaleat charitati erga communitatem exercendæ. Similes homines rempublicam maxime lædunt aut doctrina, aut exemplo: membra putrida sunt, quæ si non abscindantur, reliquum est, ut & cætera contagione teterrima inficientur. Sed & discriminis ratio inter civiles, & ecclesiasticos Magistratus servanda est. Civiles Magistratus legum Ministri huic pietati vacare non possunt, quin in leges delinquant, aliàs sanctas, justas, atque ab ipsis sacris canonibus approbatas. Jubent illæ ultimo ignis supplicio punire delinquentes, probato plene crimine: nihil aliud possunt judices in conscientia, quam in cognitos reos omni legum severitate animadvertere. Si æternæ pœnæ inde subjiciantur, sibi imputent, qui edoctam veritatem à Theologis ad id vocatis amplecti nimia pervicacia respuerunt. Agitur in his legibus de communi bono tuendo, quod semper præponderat. Credo sapientissimos legislatores Muratorio prudentia excelluisse, qui tamen conveniens esse non duxerunt, aliis mitioribus pœnis uti, & tam atrox, tam perniciosum delictum inæquali punitione castigare. Si in exilia mittantur, in aliis regionibus venena sua liberius spargere poterunt, & ibi innocentes corrumpere; vel in eas libenter effugient, ut Judæi faciunt, in quibus sectæ suæ, sine pœnarum metu, & absque emendationis spe, gaudentes, tutissimique adhærescant. | | 118 He endeavors greatly to excuse contumacious, nay even relapsed heretics, *pag. 326. & seq.* He proclaims that charity must be kept before one's eyes, to which doctrine we too most gladly assent. Yet this charity must be not feigned, not false, not contrary to the other virtues. It is not so to be embraced that justice be forsaken. Toward private men who otherwise offend, that charity is not to be desired which should prevail over the charity to be exercised toward the community. Such men most greatly injure the commonwealth either by doctrine or by example: they are putrid members, which, if they be not cut off, it remains that the rest also will be infected with a most foul contagion. But the ground of distinction between civil and ecclesiastical Magistrates must also be observed. The civil Magistrates, Ministers of the laws, cannot give themselves to this tenderness without offending against the laws — laws otherwise holy, just, and approved by the sacred canons themselves. Those laws command that offenders be punished with the final penalty of fire, the crime being fully proved: judges in conscience can do nothing else than proceed against convicted culprits with all the severity of the laws. If eternal punishments thence be their lot, let them impute it to themselves, who with excessive obstinacy refused to embrace the truth taught them by the Theologians called for that purpose. In these laws it is a question of protecting the common good, which always preponderates. I believe that the most wise legislators excelled Muratori in prudence, who nevertheless did not judge it fitting to employ other milder penalties, and to chastise so atrocious, so pernicious a crime with an unequal punishment. If they be sent into exile, they will be able to scatter their poisons more freely in other regions, and there corrupt the innocent; or they will gladly flee into those regions, as the Jews do, in which, rejoicing and most secure, they may cling to their sect without fear of punishments, and without hope of amendment. |
| 119 Magistratus verò ecclesiastici, charitate semper præ oculis habita, nihil prætermittunt, quod ad conversionem hæretici proficere possit. Monent, arguunt, edocent, Doctores vocant, qui eos vero dogmate instruant, neque in eos animadvertunt, dum emendationis aliqua spes supersit. Eorum primus scopus est animas Deo lucrifacere; prudentiam, (ut Apostoli sententiam sequantur) modestiam, benignitatem, mansuetudinem, pietatem erga reos suos indifficienter exercent; dumque oleo curare possunt, non utuntur gladio, omniaque observant, quæ Muratorius expendit. Qui tamen, *ut acer pennis, & curvis unguibus ales*, his flexibus fertur, ut in sanctæ Fidei Lusitanum Tribunal, quasi in optatam prædam citissime descendat. Levitati maximæ tribuendum est, quod fugitivis Judæis falsissimas querimonias fundentibus assensum præbeat. Quæsitores Lusitani rigori pietatem semper præferunt, non falsis informationibus, non levibus suspicionibus ducuntur, non carceribus, non infamiæ addicunt, nisi quos sacræ leges præcipiunt; nihil agunt, nihil attentant, quod sanctionibus ecclesiasticis, Pontificiisque decretis statutum non sit: non ambitu, non æmulatione, non odio, amoreve moventur. In contumaces, in relapsos, in convictos, negativosque procedit, ut jura constituunt; cum nulla jam spes subest, Curiæ sæculari tradit, ut eos secundum leges puniat; nullam tamen perdit operam, ut ad Deum convertat. Si id sperat, non illico arripit securim, sed lentissime procedit, quosque vidit non voluntatis, non contumaciæ errore duci, sed intellectus, per multos annos retinet, ut eorum conversionem consequatur. | | 119 The ecclesiastical Magistrates, however, keeping charity always before their eyes, omit nothing that can profit toward the conversion of the heretic. They admonish, they reprove, they instruct, they summon Doctors to teach them the true dogma, nor do they proceed against them so long as some hope of amendment remains. Their first aim is to gain souls for God; prudence (that they may follow the sentence of the Apostle), modesty, kindness, mildness, tenderness toward their culprits they exercise unfailingly; and so long as they can heal with oil, they do not use the sword, and they observe all the things which Muratori weighs out. Who nevertheless, *like a bird fierce of wing, and with curved talons*, is borne through these windings, so as to swoop most swiftly upon the Portuguese Tribunal of the holy Faith, as upon a longed-for prey. It must be attributed to the greatest levity that he lends assent to fugitive Jews pouring forth most false complaints. The Portuguese Inquisitors always prefer tenderness to rigor; they are not led by false informations, nor by light suspicions; they consign no one to prisons, or to infamy, except those whom the sacred laws prescribe; they do nothing, they attempt nothing, that is not established by ecclesiastical sanctions and Pontifical decrees: they are moved by no ambition, no rivalry, no hatred, or love. Against the contumacious, the relapsed, the convicted, and the negative it proceeds as the laws establish; when no hope any longer remains, it delivers them to the secular Court, that it may punish them according to the laws; yet it spares no effort to convert them to God. If it hopes for this, it does not straightway snatch up the axe, but proceeds most slowly, and those whom it has seen to be led by an error not of will, not of contumacy, but of intellect, it keeps for many years, that it may obtain their conversion. |
| 120 Sibi contrarius (ut frequenter assolet) *pag. 511. & seq.* hæreticos damnandos puniendos esse, nec à Magistratibus aut civilibus, aut ecclesiasticis permittendos esse admonet, ibique in Phereponum vehementer, jureque optimo invehitur. Videat tamen ne in se ipsum sententiam dicat, quidquid enim circa hanc rem scribit Phereponus, ipse loco supradicto, ut Lusitanum Tribunal lacessiret, amplecti visus est. Sed quidquid ibi ipse contra Phereponum adducit, potiori jure in eum retorquemus. Discat scriptorum non oblivisci, ne præjactæ sagittæ in verticem sagittantis convertantur. Hæc circa Fidei Tribunal dicta volo, nam circa alia merito Phereponus confunditur, & impugnatur. | | 120 Contrary to himself (as he frequently is wont to be), at *pag. 511. & seq.* he admonishes that heretics are to be condemned and punished, and are not to be tolerated by Magistrates either civil or ecclesiastical, and there he inveighs vehemently, and with the best right, against Phereponus. Yet let him see to it that he not pronounce sentence against himself; for whatever Phereponus writes concerning this matter, he himself, in the place cited above, in order to assail the Portuguese Tribunal, seemed to embrace. But whatever he himself there adduces against Phereponus, we turn back upon him with better right. Let him learn not to forget his own writings, lest the arrows already cast be turned back upon the head of the archer. These things I wish said with regard to the Tribunal of the Faith; for as to other matters Phereponus is deservedly confounded and impugned. |
| 121 Nimia charitate sua documenta alia interserit; vult enim quod voluntatis inclinatio ad bonum, & verum hæresis culpam sic extenuet, ut quamvis in errore pertinaciter quis existat, non sit ultima pœna puniendus. Veluti si prona ad veritatem voluntas compati possit cum pertinacia in errore, non teneaturque iste sic obtenebratus veritati sibi propositæ assentiri, & cedere doctrinis ab Ecclesia semel, atque iterum definitis. Mysterium Trinitatis, Incarnationis, unionis hypostaticæ, Eucharistiæ quis capere intellectu poterit, si prævia fax non præcedat cæcæ fidei prona voluntas, humilisque assensus, quo intellectum ipsum fidei in obsequium captivemus? Pia affectio primus gradus est ad fidei assensum; hanc qui habeat, orthodoxis dogmatibus non difficulter annuet; qui non habeat, nullam ad bonum, & verum inclinationem habere dicendus erit, quæ insita quadam vi ad veræ fidei assensum provocat, & attrahit. Quisque id renuat, non sincera in bonum, & verum inclinatione præditus, sed ingenio elato, ac vinci nescio imbutus habendus erit. Hanc bonam voluntatem fictionibus assueti omnes fere hæretici fingunt, ut pœnas promeritas evadant. Sed edoctæ veritati acquiescere nullus scit. Si ex hac ratione extenuanda fuisset eorum culpa, omnes incolumes relinquendi essent, omnes expectandi ad resipiscentiam, quæ nunquam eveniret, & Ecclesiæ gladius illuderetur, nec alii timore saltem supplicii similibus erroribus abstinerent. En quo tendit Muratorii charitas, en sui documenti scopus, cujus intellectum hæc ratio non movet, sed in contrarium ducit alias futilis, nimiumque supertitiosa. Inquit, quod *teterrimus ille mortis terror facit, ut pro apertis hæreticis fictos non raro habeamus catholicos.* Congruentius diceret, quod mortis timor facit, ut pro veris Catholicis habeamus fictos catholicos, veros tamen & mente, & corde proculdubio hæreticos. | | 121 Out of his excessive charity he inserts other teachings; for he would have it that the will's inclination toward the good and the true so extenuates the guilt of heresy, that although one persist pertinaciously in error, he is not to be punished with the ultimate penalty. As if a will prone to the truth could be compatible with pertinacity in error, and as if such a man, thus darkened, were not bound to assent to the truth set before him, and to yield to doctrines once and again defined by the Church. Who will be able to grasp with his intellect the Mystery of the Trinity, of the Incarnation, of the hypostatic union, of the Eucharist, if there go not before, as a preceding torch, a will prone to blind faith, and a humble assent, whereby we take the intellect itself captive into the obedience of faith? Pious affection is the first step toward the assent of faith; he who has it will not with difficulty give his assent to orthodox dogmas; he who has it not must be said to have no inclination toward the good and the true, which by a certain inborn force provokes and draws one to the assent of true faith. Whoever refuses this must be held to be endowed not with a sincere inclination toward the good and the true, but imbued with a haughty temper, one that knows not how to be conquered. This good will almost all heretics, accustomed to fictions, feign, that they may escape the punishments they have deserved. But not one knows how to acquiesce in the truth that has been taught him. If on this ground their guilt were to be extenuated, all would have to be left unharmed, all would have to be waited upon for a repentance which would never come, and the sword of the Church would be mocked, nor would others abstain from similar errors at least through fear of punishment. Behold whither Muratori's charity tends; behold the aim of his teaching, whose intellect this reasoning does not move, but leads to the contrary — a reasoning otherwise futile, and exceedingly far-fetched. [?] He says that *that most dreadful terror of death brings it about that in place of open heretics we not rarely have feigned catholics.* He would say more congruently that the fear of death brings it about that in place of true Catholics we have feigned catholics, yet beyond doubt true heretics both in mind and in heart. |
| 122 Ejus ratio ea est; quia constantia, qua intrepide hi ad ultimum supplicium merito condemnati mortem subeunt, occasionem præbet, ut ipse error latius serpat, eo quod incautis hominibus videatur eam constantiam non animorum pertinaciæ, sed veritatis virtuti tribuendam esse. Quam ineptus sit is syllogizandi modus, quis non videat? Quis orthodoxus, quis veræ fidei principiis instructus in hanc fatuitatem declinabit? Dolebit equidem hominis cæcitatem, dolebit contumaciam, dolebit diabolicam deceptionem, dolebit miserum morientis statum; sed constantiam laudare, virtuti, aut veritati eam tribuere, ipsa moveri ad eumdem errorem admittendum, nullus, ut credo, ita demens erit, ut sic audeat, sic decipiatur. Si tamen aliquis fortasse fuerit, qui in simile prorumpat deliramentum, sua culpa id accidet, & saltem similis supplicii timore perterritus ab eadem pervicacia securius arcebitur. Ecclesia, ejusque Magistratus id intendunt, ut fides, & religio inconcussæ persistant, ut tam nocua delicta puniantur, & cæteros in vero dogmate pœnarum metu contineat. Quod aliquis, vel aliqui in suomet judicio, suamet culpa, aut ignorantia fallantur, id in consideratione Ecclesia non habet, quia multorum utilitas huic privatæ stultorum utilitati præferenda est. Idem Muratorii judicium in aliis delinquentibus æque procedit: fures, homicidæ, & alii ejusdem generis multoties maxima constantia, quæ animi robori tribuenda est, mortem subeunt. Quis adeo hebes erit, quis adeo incautus, ut eam constantiam veritatis virtuti adscribendam esse sentiat, & ita deliret, ut furta, homicidiaque licita esse secum judicet, & inde exemplum sumat, ut in eadem facinora prolabatur? Quis ex hac causa eorum punitionem differendam esse arbitrabitur? Rationem congruam differentiæ cuperem exaudire. Ipse Muratorius, statim post has ponderatas rationes, hos homines vivere diutius inter gentem orthodoxam non sinendos esse ait. Ad quid ergo tot in contrarium ponderationes, tot documenta? | | 122 His reason is this: because the constancy with which these men, deservedly condemned to the ultimate punishment, intrepidly undergo death, affords an occasion for the error itself to creep more widely, inasmuch as to incautious men it seems that that constancy is to be attributed not to the pertinacity of their spirits, but to the virtue of truth. How inept this mode of syllogizing is, who does not see? What orthodox man, what man instructed in the principles of the true faith, will sink into this fatuity? He will grieve, indeed, at the man's blindness, he will grieve at his contumacy, he will grieve at the diabolical deception, he will grieve at the wretched state of the dying man; but to praise his constancy, to attribute it to virtue or to truth, to be moved by it to admit the same error — no one, as I believe, will be so demented as thus to dare, thus to be deceived. If, nevertheless, there should perhaps be someone who bursts forth into a like delirium, it will happen by his own fault, and at least, terrified by fear of a like punishment, he will be more securely kept back from the same obstinacy. The Church and her Magistrates intend this: that faith and religion may persist unshaken, that crimes so harmful may be punished, and that she may hold the rest in true dogma by the fear of penalties. That someone, or some, are deceived in their own judgment, by their own fault or ignorance — this the Church does not take into consideration, because the utility of the many is to be preferred to this private utility of fools. The same judgment of Muratori proceeds equally in the case of other delinquents: thieves, murderers, and others of the same kind many times undergo death with the greatest constancy, which is to be attributed to strength of soul. Who will be so dull, who so incautious, as to think that that constancy is to be ascribed to the virtue of truth, and so rave as to judge within himself that thefts and homicides are licit, and thence take example, so as to slip into the same crimes? Who for this cause will think that their punishment is to be deferred? I should wish to hear a congruent reason for the difference. Muratori himself, immediately after these weighty reasonings, says that these men are not to be suffered to live longer among an orthodox people. To what end, then, so many ponderings to the contrary, so many admonitions? |
| 123 Transitum deinde facit ad alios non pertinaces, in vanumque desudat; hos enim Fidei Tribunalia Lusitana ultimo supplicio nunquam addicunt. Cum eis resipiscentibus, errorem suum abjurantibus, & vera dogmata profitentibus benignissime se habet, & juxta renitentiæ, aut confessionis modum, aut nullis, aut levioribus, aut gravioribus pœnis confessos, pœnitentesque subjiciunt. Id nullus est qui nesciat, qui non videat, qui quotidie non experiatur. In re notissima supervacua penitus fuit Muratorii aut admonitio, aut reprehensio. | | 123 He then makes a transition to others who are not pertinacious, and sweats in vain; for these the Portuguese Tribunals of the Faith never condemn to the last punishment. With them, when they repent, abjure their error, and profess true dogmas, it conducts itself most benignly, and according to the measure of their resistance or confession they subject the confessed and penitent either to no penalties, or to lighter, or to graver ones. There is no one who does not know this, who does not see it, who does not daily experience it. In a matter so well known, Muratori's admonition, or reprehension, was entirely superfluous. |
| 124 Id apud suos supervacaneum esse fatetur, siquidem apud eos *non sine prævia, eaque gravi cognitione rerum* videt procedi. Hæc eadem regula in nostris Tribunalibus observatur. Neque justa aliqua est causa, ut contrarium suspicetur. Superfluum igitur fuit ea referre. Si autem retulit, & suos excipere statim voluit, quid sentire possumus, nisi eum quoad nostra Tribunalia contrarium penitus sensisse? Cum tamen sine ullo fundamento eam opinionem concipiat, superest, ut arbitremur eum detractionis palliatæ stimulo fuisse commotum. | | 124 He confesses that this is superfluous among his own people, since among them he sees that proceedings are had *not without a previous, and that a grave, cognizance of the matters*. This same rule is observed in our Tribunals. Nor is there any just cause for suspecting the contrary. It was therefore superfluous to relate these things. But if he related them, and wished at once to except his own people, what can we think, except that as regards our Tribunals he thought entirely the contrary? Since, however, he conceives that opinion without any foundation, it remains for us to judge that he was moved by the goad of cloaked detraction. |
| 125 Ait errorem fortasse in iis rebus, & controversiis versari, quas nondum Ecclesia, aut Concilia credendas statuerint. Alibi sibi contrarius, ut jam notavimus, asserit, nihil de fide esse, quod ab Ecclesia, & Conciliis definitum non inveniatur. In rebus dubiis, opinionibusque adhuc non reprobatis nemo hæresis formalis accusatur, neque ob id punitur. Si tamen opinionibus erroneis, scandalosis, temerariis, male sonantibus insistat, cur non erit Tribunalis ad id instituti de eis judicium ferre, proferentes reprehendere, punireque, si contumaces in sua sententia persistant? Cur eis sic errantibus non acquiescere Tribunali Fidei, ejusque Qualificatoribus judicium suum non subjicere permissum erit? Hæretici forte non erunt, sed de hæresi ipsamet contumacia sua suspectos reddit. Id edocet omnis Theologorum cohors. Eluderent ipsi Tribunalium jurisdictionem, si eis liberum esset, suam sententiam nondum esse ab Ecclesia universali proscriptam, proclamare. Non sibi fidere, sed Quæsitoribus, & Theologis peritis mentem subjicire, & assensum præbere debent. Quis Theologis hanc qualificandarum propositionum facultatem negat? Non infallibile est judicium suum: hoc Sedis Apostolicæ privilegium est; sed adeo moraliter certum, & pro certo habendum, ut ei resistere, non assentiri, pervicaciter reluctari tenacitati tribuendum sit. Lirinensis, Cassianus, & alii, immo & Cyprianus ipse, hæretici non fuere, quia judicio Ecclesiæ, errore deposito, reverenter cesserunt. Si tamen semel, atque iterum admoniti suo judicio adhærerent, quis dubitat, ut hæreticos fuisse habendos? | | 125 He says that error is perhaps concerned with those matters and controversies which the Church or the Councils have not yet established as to be believed. Elsewhere, contrary to himself, as we have already noted, he asserts that nothing is of faith which is not found defined by the Church and the Councils. In doubtful matters, and in opinions not yet reproved, no one is accused of formal heresy, nor punished on that account. If, however, one insists upon erroneous, scandalous, temerarious, ill-sounding opinions, why shall it not belong to the Tribunal instituted for this purpose to pass judgment upon them, to reprehend those who utter them, and to punish them, if they persist contumacious in their opinion? Why shall it be permitted to those thus erring not to acquiesce in the Tribunal of the Faith, and not to submit their judgment to it and to its Qualifiers? Perhaps they will not be heretics, but their very contumacy renders them suspected of heresy. This the whole cohort of Theologians teaches. They would elude the jurisdiction of the Tribunals, if it were free to them to proclaim that their opinion has not yet been proscribed by the universal Church. They ought not to trust themselves, but to submit their mind to the Inquisitors and to expert Theologians, and to give their assent. Who denies to Theologians this faculty of qualifying propositions? Their judgment is not infallible: this is the privilege of the Apostolic See; but it is so morally certain, and so to be held for certain, that to resist it, not to assent to it, to struggle against it obstinately, must be attributed to tenacity. The Lerinian, Cassian, and others, nay even Cyprian himself, were not heretics, because, their error laid aside, they reverently yielded to the judgment of the Church. Yet if, admonished once and again, they had adhered to their own judgment, who doubts that they would have had to be held heretics? |
| 126 In magnam me vergere suspicionem cogit, quod ad suum assumptum probandum, nullæ aliæ ei in mentem venirent quæstiones, nisi quæ de gratia & libero arbitrio in scholis versantur: has scilicet, in quibus, ut Lutherum, Calvinum, Wiclefumque prætermittam, Arnaldus, Baius, Jansenius, Voetius, Maresius, aliique illorum asseclæ miserrime lapsi sunt. Dari in hac materia diversas scholas, qui Scientiæ Mediæ, & Physicæ prædeterminationis, cum aliis quæstionibus ex his emergentibus, aliqua notitia instructus fuerit, non dubitabit. Has scholas licet inter se dimicantes tuto defendi, & teneri posse post diuturnas concertationes decrevit Paulus V. nulla siquidem à vero fidei sensu deviare visa est. Has sententias qui sequatur, vel alias ex his provenientes hucusque admissas, & ab Oraculo Vaticano minime reprehensas, nec Fidei Tribunali subjicitur, neque à sancti Officii Censoribus inuritur; sciunt enim optime Qualificatores, quæ sententiæ in scholis versentur, & quæ sine crimine defendi possint. Cautela tamen nimia adhibenda est, ne harum opinionum prætextu in propositiones dubias, & male sonantes, aut hæreticis consonas pro suo arbitrio quisque prorumpat. Hunc verum sensum examinare, & depromere ad Fidei Tribunal attinere certum est, auditis prius eximiis, doctissimisque Theologis, quibus profecto temeritatis maximæ erit non assentiri. Solent enim qui male sentiunt, has excusationes prætendere, & suos errores sic technis, & pigmentis ornatos exhibere, ut omnem Theologorum censuram effugere conentur. In hac materia sic caute procedere solet Tribunal, sic Theologi, ut si propositio apta sit, ut in bono, vel malo sensu accipi possit, consideret qualitatem, moresque proferentis, & an contra eum aliqua resultet sinistra suspicio; si nulla in proferentem suspicandi ratio detur, semper propositio in vero sensu accipiatur. Sin autem proferens suspectus sit, de sensu suo examinetur. Hæc est communis sententia in praxi observata, de qua Simancas, Rojas, Navarrus, & alii apud Delbene. (31) {marg. (31) P. 2. dub. 195. sect. 53. & dub. 198. sect. 11.} | | 126 It forces me to incline into great suspicion, that for proving his assumption no other questions came into his mind except those concerning grace and free will which are agitated in the schools: those, namely, in which — to pass over Luther, Calvin, and Wyclif — Arnauld, Baius, Jansenius, Voetius, Maresius, and other followers of theirs have most miserably fallen. That there are diverse schools in this matter, whoever has been furnished with some knowledge of Middle Knowledge and of Physical Predetermination, together with the other questions emerging from these, will not doubt. That these schools, although contending among themselves, can safely be defended and held, Paul V decreed after long contests, since none of them appeared to deviate from the true sense of the faith. Whoever follows these opinions, or others proceeding from them hitherto admitted, and in no wise reprehended by the Vatican Oracle, is neither subjected to the Tribunal of the Faith, nor branded by the Censors of the Holy Office; for the Qualifiers know very well which opinions are agitated in the schools, and which can be defended without crime. Yet very great caution must be applied, lest under the pretext of these opinions anyone burst forth at his own pleasure into propositions doubtful and ill-sounding, or consonant with heretics. That it pertains to the Tribunal of the Faith to examine and draw out this true sense is certain, after first hearing eminent and most learned Theologians, to whom assuredly it will be a matter of the greatest temerity not to assent. For those who think ill are wont to pretend these excuses, and so to exhibit their errors adorned with artifices and paints, that they endeavor to escape every censure of the Theologians. In this matter the Tribunal is wont to proceed so cautiously, and so the Theologians, that if a proposition be apt to be taken in a good or in a bad sense, it considers the quality and morals of the one uttering it, and whether any sinister suspicion results against him; if no reason for suspecting the utterer be given, the proposition is always to be taken in the true sense. But if the utterer be suspect, let him be examined concerning his sense. This is the common opinion observed in practice, on which see Simancas, Rojas, Navarrus, and others in Delbene. (31) {marg. (31) P. 2. dub. 195. sect. 53. & dub. 198. sect. 11.} |
| 127 Alia addit superflua sane, aut à communiori sententia discrepantia. Qui sola ignorantia errant, falsa dogmata non amplexuri, si ea talia agnoscerent, si eorum sincerus animus deprehenditur, benignissime excipiuntur; nec rigide, ut supponit Muratorius, hæreticorum pœnis mulctantur. Suaviter commonentur, & si acquiescunt, in pace remittuntur, vel saltem, cùm juris regulæ aliud exposcunt, levissimis pœnis paternaliter castigantur. Multoties accitum monent, & sine reluctamine acquiescentem leniter dimittunt. Nihil, quod pietatis, ac charitatis sit, nostri Quæsitores prætermittunt; Christianos vult facere Tribunal benignitate, non rigore. Hoc solum utitur, cum nullum aliud superest remedium. | | 127 He adds other things, superfluous indeed, or discrepant from the more common opinion. Those who err by ignorance alone, who would not embrace false dogmas if they recognized them as such, if their sincere mind is discovered, are received most benignly; nor are they rigidly, as Muratori supposes, punished with the penalties of heretics. They are gently admonished, and if they acquiesce, they are sent away in peace, or at least, when the rules of law demand otherwise, they are paternally chastised with the lightest penalties. Many times they admonish one who has been summoned, and gently dismiss him when he acquiesces without struggle. Our Inquisitors omit nothing that belongs to piety and charity; the Tribunal wishes to make Christians by benignity, not by rigor. This last it employs only when no other remedy remains. |
| 128 À communi autem sententia recedere manifestum est, cum asserit, contra jus pugnare eam agendi rationem, si errantem in opinionibus Theologicis, nondum ab Ecclesia, aut à Romano Pontifice definitis, cogat Fidei Tribunal ad recipiendam opinionem contrariam. Et ut suam probet sententiam, sic discurrit: *Numquid enim tua opinio ab illorum opinione diversa, ideo verior, & sanior est, quia tu potentior quam illi? Ne tu quidem, ut puto, id arbitrere.* Optima ratio, si contentio esset inter duos privatos Theologos. Sed quando res agitur cum Tribunali, aut cum Episcopo judicante, & docente, futilissima est argumentatio, futilissimum quidquid ad idem intentum ratiocinatur. Tenetur ipse Tribunali, aut Episcopo, virisque doctis ad id vocatis credere, & assentiri, aliàs ut suspectus de hæresi, & aliquando ut contumax puniendus erit. Videatur Delbene, alios referens, qui optimas rationes ad id expendit. (32) {marg. (32) P. 1. dubit. 4. petit. 2.} Debuerat ergo Muratorius non condemnare quod optimi DD. docent, suoque non fidere judicio. | | 128 But that he departs from the common opinion is manifest, when he asserts that that manner of acting fights against right, if the Tribunal of the Faith compels one erring in Theological opinions, not yet defined by the Church or by the Roman Pontiff, to receive the contrary opinion. And to prove his opinion he discourses thus: *For is your opinion, differing from their opinion, therefore truer and sounder, because you are more powerful than they? Not even you, as I suppose, would think so.* An excellent reason, if the contention were between two private Theologians. But when the matter is transacted with a Tribunal, or with a Bishop judging and teaching, the argumentation is most futile, most futile whatever is reasoned to the same purpose. He is himself bound to believe and to assent to the Tribunal, or to the Bishop and the learned men called for this purpose; otherwise he will have to be punished as suspected of heresy, and sometimes as contumacious. Let Delbene be seen, citing others, who weighs excellent reasons for this. (32) {marg. (32) P. 1. dubit. 4. petit. 2.} Muratori ought, therefore, not to condemn what the best Doctors teach, and not to trust his own judgment. |
| 129 Multa alia adnotare possem, quæ per totum suum opus de *ingeniorum moderatione in fidei negotio*, & de scriptoribus, quos quandoque plurimum extollit, profecto magna ex parte heterodoxos inutiliter congessit. Sed res ista latiorem exposcit indaginem. Non silebo tamen Drusium, & Grotium certe hæreticos insignes. De Drusio lectionem non habeo. Grotius verò, quidquid ipse dicat, atheus semper vixit, & tandem Protestantibus addictus obiit. Certe in jure civili optimus fuit, præcipue circa jus publicum. In epistolis malus non fuit, sed inter primates locum non obtinuit; nec stylus fuit semper idem. Multa ex Bodino, (quem laudat in *epist. ad Gallos* 146. & 170.) atheo usurpavit, similiter ex Soccino ejusdem sectæ complurima mutuatus est, præcipue in commentariis criticis in universam Bibliam, in quibus per quatuor tomos dispersa complurima inveniuntur erronea, complurima Soccianismum redolentia, ubi in explicandis Rabbinorum locis multoties fallitur, multos ejus errores adnotavit Esdras Edzardi. Si verum fateor, risu dignum mihi videtur, (parcat Petavius) quod ipse eo animo esset, ut è Suecia reversus catholicam ingrederetur Ecclesiam. Si ea sua mens erat, quis eum prohibuit, ne ingrederetur? Cur saltem in morte christianam religionem non fuit professus? Potuit ex Atheismo ad Protestantes declinare, non potuit ad Orthodoxos? Neque plurimum movet me, quod Scripturæ, & sanctorum Patrum sententias in recto sensu multoties explicaret. Id etiam in aliis hæreticis experimur, cum inter se digladiantur. Nescit enim nemo quam multæ, quam diversæ sint eorum sectæ. Unusquisque ut suam defendat, in alios optimis & perspicuis utitur Scripturæ locis. Sectarii isti non in omnibus à fide catholica, ejusque dogmatibus dissentiunt, sed in aliquibus articulis: quoad hos Scripturæ sensum deturpant, quoad alios verum sensum retinent, veram doctrinam amplectuntur. Placebant ei plurimum Tridentini regulæ, quia rationi conformes: sed ideo infelicior, ideo damnabilior, quia eas non recepit. Traditionibus assentiendum ei visum fuerat, quia non sequebatur sectarios asserentes soli Scripturæ, non verò traditionibus standum esse; ideo contra Rivetum sic scripsit; & per consequens necesse fuit, ut ad ejus interpretationem confugeret ad veteres scriptores, & universalia Concilia. Id etiam amplectuntur alii sectarii, & tamen sectarii sunt. Suus liber *de Veritate Relig. Christ.* teste Sponio, plagii accusandus est, ut traductus à quodam libro arabice scripto adversus Mahumedanos, & Judæos; & multa tamen continat nullatenus approbanda. Alius liber *de Antichristo* multa continet catholicæ doctrinæ consona, sed multa dissona, quod facile fuisset demonstrare, si ad alia non provocarer. Videatur doctissimus Calmet in suis Dissertationibus Biblicis, ubi passim Grotium respuit, ac explodit; atque doctissimus etiam Bosius in eumdem hæreticum acriter dimicans, ac in numeros ejus errores ostendens. Et Pouget *in Inst. Cathol. tom. 1. sect. 4. cap. 2. §. 18.* Illud certum, eum semper hæreticum vixisse, hæreticum obiisse. Placeat Grotius Muratorio, aliis placeat garrulis, mihi non placet, quia non placuit Deo, testante Apostolo, quod *sine fide impossibile est placere Deo*. Nullus hæreticus, etsi doctissimus, etsi eruditione plenus, mihi arridet; suorum librorum lectioni incumbere non juvat, quamvis suarum eruditionum expers remaneam: *Melior est fidelis ignorantia, quam temeraria scientia.* (33) {marg. (33) August.} *Ubi non est scientia animæ, non est bonum.* (34) {marg. (34) Proverb. 19.} *Non plus sapere, quam oportet sapere, sed sapere ad sobrietatem.* (35) {marg. (35) D. Paul.} Sine hæreticorum lectione salvabimur, sine eorum eruditione sapiemus, & ab eorum insidiis fugientes, pro fide, pro vera religione imperterriti semper adstabimus. | | 129 I could note many other things, which throughout his whole work *on the moderation of minds in the business of faith*, and concerning writers whom he at times greatly extols — assuredly in great part heterodox — he has uselessly heaped together. But that matter demands a wider investigation. I shall not, however, be silent concerning Drusius and Grotius, certainly notable heretics. Of Drusius I have no reading. But Grotius, whatever he himself may say, always lived an atheist, and at last died attached to the Protestants. Certainly in civil law he was excellent, especially concerning public law. In his epistles he was not bad, but he did not obtain a place among the first rank; nor was his style always the same. He usurped many things from Bodin (whom he praises in *epist. ad Gallos* 146 and 170), an atheist; likewise from Socinus, of the same sect, he borrowed very many things, especially in his critical commentaries on the whole Bible, in which, scattered through four tomes, are found very many erroneous things, very many things redolent of Socinianism, where in explaining the passages of the Rabbis he is many times deceived; many of his errors Esdras Edzardi has noted. If I confess the truth, it seems to me worthy of laughter (may Petavius pardon me) that he was of such a mind that, having returned from Sweden, he would enter the Catholic Church. If that was his mind, who prohibited him from entering? Why at least in death did he not profess the Christian religion? Could he decline from Atheism to the Protestants, and could he not to the Orthodox? Nor does it move me much that he many times explained the sentences of Scripture and of the holy Fathers in the right sense. This too we experience in other heretics, when they fight it out among themselves. For no one is ignorant how many and how diverse are their sects. Each one, to defend his own, uses excellent and perspicuous places of Scripture against the others. Those sectaries do not dissent from the Catholic faith and its dogmas in all things, but in certain articles: as regards these they disfigure the sense of Scripture, as regards the others they retain the true sense, they embrace true doctrine. The rules of Trent pleased him greatly, because conformable to reason: but he is for that the more unhappy, for that the more damnable, because he did not receive them. It had seemed to him that assent must be given to traditions, because he did not follow the sectaries who assert that one must stand by Scripture alone, and not by traditions; therefore he wrote thus against Rivetus; and in consequence it was necessary that for its interpretation he should have recourse to the ancient writers and the universal Councils. This too other sectaries embrace, and yet they are sectaries. His book *de Veritate Relig. Christ.*, on the witness of Spon, is to be accused of plagiarism, as translated from a certain book written in Arabic against the Mahometans and Jews; and yet it contains many things in no wise to be approved. Another book, *de Antichristo*, contains many things consonant with Catholic doctrine, but many dissonant, which it would have been easy to demonstrate, were I not called away to other things. Let the most learned Calmet be seen in his Biblical Dissertations, where throughout he rejects and explodes Grotius; and also the most learned Bosius, sharply contending against the same heretic, and showing his innumerable errors [?]. And Pouget *in Inst. Cathol. tom. 1. sect. 4. cap. 2. §. 18.* This is certain: that he always lived a heretic, and died a heretic. Let Grotius please Muratori, let him please other praters; me he does not please, because he did not please God, the Apostle witnessing that *without faith it is impossible to please God*. No heretic, however most learned, however full of erudition, smiles upon me; it profits not to devote oneself to the reading of his books, though I remain devoid of his eruditions: *Better is faithful ignorance than temerarious knowledge.* (33) {marg. (33) August.} *Where there is no knowledge of the soul, there is no good.* (34) {marg. (34) Proverb. 19.} *Not to be more wise than it behoveth to be wise, but to be wise unto sobriety.* (35) {marg. (35) D. Paul.} Without the reading of heretics we shall be saved, without their erudition we shall be wise, and fleeing from their snares, we shall ever stand undaunted for the faith, for the true religion. |
| 130 Ex consulto ad hunc locum reservavi, quod Muratorius sub nomine Lamindi *lib. 2. cap. 6. pag. 254.* impiissime, nimisque audacter scripsit circa pietatem, (quam pervicaciam pervicax ipse vocat) qua immaculatam Deiparæ sanctissimæ Conceptionem Hispani, Lusitanique præcipue venerantur, jurantque tueri. Temerarius ipse in ea se proripit deliramenta, ut reprobare intendat, quod amplissima Orthodoxa Regna tot sapientissimis Theologis referta, quod eximiæ nulli inferiores Universitates edocent, ac defendunt. Infelix ego, qui toties eamdem Deiparam immaculate conceptam pro viribus defendere juravi! Infelices tot egregii Conimbricenses Magistri, quotannis idem jurantes! Infelix Regnum, infelices Hispaniæ hac perjurii labe infectæ! Supererat unus Muratorius, qui audenter nimis in tot sapientissimos Præceptores, in Theologos doctissimos, in viros virtutibus, & sapientia pollentes, in universas Religiones, in omnes nostri Regni, & Hispaniæ Ordines caput extolleret, asperisque vocibus clamaret. Errant omnes; solus Muratorius veritatis viam feliciter adinvenit. Oh quantum superbiæ, elatæque mentis ausu tanto apertissime demonstrat! Melius illi foret, si taceret. Nullum bonum eventum inaugurari possumus in immunitatem sanctissimæ Virginis MARIÆ, sic garrienti, ac in tanti privilegii devotissimos defensores incompte debachanti. | | 130 Of set purpose I have reserved to this place what Muratori, under the name of Lamindus, *lib. 2. cap. 6. pag. 254.*, has written most impiously and too audaciously concerning the piety (which he, obstinate himself, calls obstinacy) with which the Spaniards, and especially the Portuguese, venerate the immaculate Conception of the most holy Mother of God, and swear to defend it. Temerarious himself, he rushes into such deliriums that he intends to reprove what most ample Orthodox Kingdoms, filled with so many most wise Theologians, what eminent Universities inferior to none, teach and defend. Unhappy I, who have so many times sworn to defend with all my powers the same Mother of God immaculately conceived! Unhappy so many distinguished Masters of Coimbra, swearing the same every year! Unhappy the Kingdom, unhappy the Spains, infected with this stain of perjury! There remained one Muratori, who too boldly should lift up his head against so many most wise Preceptors, against most learned Theologians, against men mighty in virtues and wisdom, against all the Religious Orders, against all the Orders of our Kingdom and of Spain, and cry out with harsh words. All err; Muratori alone has happily found the way of truth. Oh how much pride, how much of an elated mind, he most openly demonstrates by so great a daring! It were better for him if he kept silence. We can augur no good outcome, as regards the immunity of the most holy Virgin MARY, for one so prattling, and so uncouthly raving against the most devoted defenders of so great a privilege. |
| 131 Excusari fortasse adnitatur, quia non in opinionem immaculatæ Conceptionis, sed in juramentum de ea defendenda jacula immittat; in promptuque habebit ea verba *dicti cap. 6. Satis quibusdam non fuit, calamo, & rationibus immaculatam Deiparæ Conceptionem tueri; quod proculdubio cum vera pietate consonat.* Hæc est horum hominum versutia, ut sic sermones suos componere studeant, ut quod in corde retinent, recondere valeant; semperque verba habeant ad sui defensionem parata. Si immaculatæ Conceptionis opinio *proculdubio cum vera pietate consonat*, quid, quæso, significant illa verba: *Quæ falsa esse potest, & certe necessario defendenda non est?* Quomodo pium, & vere pium esse potest, quod falsum esse valet? Quomodo necessario defendendum non est, quod proculdubio est vere pium? Quid aliud sonant alia verba: *At mihi certissima est hujusmodi opinio, aliquis dicet. Cui? Homini nempe creaturæ fallacissimæ. Num, rogo, certum est aliquid, quia id sibi certum videtur? Revelavit Deus quæ sine erroris suspicione credenda sunt vera.* Inquiro tamen: Cui homini? An huic, vel illi Doctori? Certe universo fere populo Christiano hæc sententia ut verissima arrisit miraculis innumeris comprobata. Miraculis non credit Muratorius; credat tamen vel invitus S. P. Alexandro VII. sic asserenti: *Sane vetus est Christi fidelium erga ejus Beatissimam Matrem Virginem Mariam pietas, sentientium ejus animam in primo instanti creationis &c.* Et ibi: *Accedentibus quoque plerisque celebrioribus Academiis ad hanc sententiam, jam omnes fere Catholici eam complectantur &c.* Num quod universi fere Catholici, atque Doctores eximii celebriorum Academiarum in unum sentientes sine formidine aliqua, num quod Ecclesia non respuit, immo suo consensu approbat, non heri, & nudiustertius, sed à sæculis libens, lubensque permittit, & privilegiis, gratiis, indulgentiisque fovet, dicendum est, limites incertæ opinionis falsitati obnoxiæ non transcendere, solumque ita videri *homini creaturæ fallacissimæ*? Num solum certa est ea sententia huic, vel illi, quæ certissima habetur apud plerosque pios, plerosque doctos, & Christianos omnes? Certe doctissimus Duvallius in suo tractatu de Conceptione non solum certam, sed certissimam appellat, quem sequuntur Nieremberg, & alii ejusdem auctoritatis. Hic universalis omnium consensus nihil moralis certitudinis affert tam piæ, receptæque sententiæ? Num solum certa sunt, quæ constat fuisse revelata? Multa adhuc ex certa revelatione non constant, & tamen aut vera sunt, aut ut verissima habentur. Non erit de fide credendum quod adhuc revelatum non est, aut ab Ecclesia, ut de fide credendum, definitum. Sed multa sunt, quæ ut certa haberi possunt, non fide divina, sed humana certitudine, qua maior, extra res fidei, esse non possit. Complurimæ sunt conclusiones Theologicæ, quæ adhuc revelatæ non sunt, aut ab Ecclesia definitæ; & tamen effrons, temerariusque erit, qui his conclusionibus omnimodam denegent humanam, immo & theologicam certitudinem. Nunc Mutinensem scriptorem reconvenio. Cui incerta, & falsitati obnoxia videtur ea de Conceptione Deiparæ sententia? Cui? Muratorio, *homini* scilicet, *creaturæ fallacissimæ*. Falli igitur potest. Teneat ergo suam opinionem, sed modeste tueatur, ostendat ingenii moderantiam, quam deprædicat, atque superbe non irrumpat in contrariæ piæ sententiæ assertores. | | 131 He will perhaps strive to be excused, on the ground that he hurls his darts not against the opinion of the immaculate Conception, but against the oath to defend it; and he will have ready at hand those words of the said cap. 6: *It was not enough for certain men to defend the immaculate Conception of the Mother of God with pen and reasons; which beyond doubt is consonant with true piety.* This is the cunning of these men: they study so to compose their discourses that they may be able to hide what they retain in their heart, and may always have words prepared for their own defense. If the opinion of the immaculate Conception *beyond doubt is consonant with true piety*, what, I ask, do those words signify: *Which can be false, and certainly is not necessarily to be defended?* How can that be pious, and truly pious, which can be false? How is that not necessarily to be defended, which beyond doubt is truly pious? What else do the other words sound: *But to me, someone will say, an opinion of this kind is most certain. To whom? To a man, namely, a most fallacious creature. Is anything, I ask, certain because it seems certain to him? God has revealed the things which are to be believed true without suspicion of error.* Yet I inquire: To what man? To this or that Doctor? Certainly to almost the whole Christian people this opinion has smiled as most true, confirmed by innumerable miracles. Muratori does not believe in miracles; yet let him believe, even unwilling, the Holy Father Alexander VII, thus asserting: *Ancient indeed is the piety of Christ's faithful toward his most Blessed Mother the Virgin Mary, holding that her soul, in the first instant of its creation, etc.* And in the same place: *Very many of the more celebrated Academies also acceding to this opinion, now almost all Catholics embrace it, etc.* Shall it be said that what almost all Catholics, and the eminent Doctors of the more celebrated Academies, thinking as one without any fear, hold — what the Church does not reject, nay approves by her consent, not yesterday and the day before, but for centuries willingly and gladly permits, and fosters with privileges, graces, and indulgences — does not pass beyond the limits of an uncertain opinion liable to falsity, and only so seems *to a man, a most fallacious creature*? Is that opinion certain only to this man or that, which is held most certain among very many pious men, very many learned men, and all Christians? Certainly the most learned Duvallius, in his treatise on the Conception, calls it not only certain, but most certain; whom Nieremberg and others of the same authority follow. Does this universal consensus of all bring no moral certitude to so pious and so received an opinion? Are those things alone certain which are established to have been revealed? Many things are not yet established from certain revelation, and yet they are either true, or are held as most true. That is not to be believed as of faith which has not yet been revealed, or defined by the Church as to be believed of faith. But there are many things which can be held as certain, not by divine faith, but with a human certitude, than which, outside matters of faith, none can be greater. There are very many Theological conclusions which have not yet been revealed, or defined by the Church; and yet he will be shameless and temerarious who would deny to these conclusions all human, nay even theological, certitude. Now I call the Modenese writer to account. To whom does that opinion concerning the Conception of the Mother of God seem uncertain and liable to falsity? To whom? To Muratori — *to a man*, namely, *a most fallacious creature*. He can therefore be deceived. Let him hold, then, his own opinion, but let him defend it modestly; let him show the moderation of mind which he preaches, and let him not proudly burst in upon the assertors of the contrary pious opinion. |
| 132 Ipse non audet expresse hanc piam, veramque sententiam oppugnare, nec contrariam defendere, ne Pontificiis Constitutionibus contraire videatur. Quod tamen directe non potest, id indirecte contendit. Audiamus ejus verba: *Nihil aliud tamen est, quam opinio humana errori obnoxia.... tamquam certissimam eis opinionem, quam Ecclesia ipsa tot licet piorum quorumdam precibus fatigata certam decernere nondum sustinuit, & prope est ut dicam decerni non posse silentio suo quodammodo significavit.* Quis adeo obtusus erit, qui non deprehendat, eam de immaculata Conceptione contrariam sententiam, etsi palam non proferat, mente, & animo retinere? Quantum autem Pontificiis Constitutionibus adversetur, ex ipsis postea demonstrabimus. Si enim errori obnoxia est ea sententia; si ut vera ab Ecclesia decerni non potest: ergo vera non est: ergo contraria vera est; siquidem duæ contrariæ opiniones non possunt simul esse veræ, & si una vera non est, necessario altera vera esse debet. Id autem asserere expresse prohibent præfatæ Constitutiones. | | 132 He himself does not dare expressly to oppugn this pious and true opinion, nor to defend the contrary, lest he seem to contravene the Pontifical Constitutions. Yet what he cannot do directly, he attempts indirectly. Let us hear his words: *Nevertheless it is nothing else than a human opinion liable to error.... as an opinion most certain to them, which the Church herself, though wearied by the prayers of so many pious men, has not yet brought herself to decree certain, and — I am near to saying — has in a manner signified by her silence that it cannot be decreed.* Who will be so obtuse as not to detect that he retains in mind and soul the contrary opinion concerning the immaculate Conception, even if he does not utter it openly? But how much he is adverse to the Pontifical Constitutions, we shall afterwards demonstrate from them. For if that opinion is liable to error; if it cannot be decreed true by the Church: therefore it is not true: therefore the contrary is true; since two contrary opinions cannot be true at the same time, and if the one is not true, the other must necessarily be true. But to assert this the aforesaid Constitutions expressly prohibit. |
| 133 Se tueri iterum conabitur asserendo, nullatenus improbare pietatem, & probabilitatem opinionis docentis Beatam Virginem fuisse in suo primo instanti à macula originali præservatam; solummodoque invehi in temeritatem, qua ejusdem opinionis sectatores se juramento adstrigunt ad defendendam immaculatam Conceptionem etiam cum proprio vitæ dispendio: quod ut reprehendat, sic exclamat: *Novitium certe Martyrum genus, quod nusquam maiores nostri somniarunt, nunquam posteri nostri, si quidquam sapient, probent. Neque Euangelium, nec ratio recta nos docent adeo levibus de causis animam prodigere.* Terminos quæstionis prætergreditur. Aliud quidem est, & sane diversissimum asserere licitum esse prædictum juramentum; aliud vero defendere verum Martyrem esse, qui pro Conceptione immaculata vitam profuderit. Illud primum nunquam Ecclesia prohibuit; hoc secundum fortasse in Theophilo Raynaudo reprehenderit. Siquidem ut habetur in Portel verbo *Conceptio*, & in P. Casnedi *tom. 5. disp. 12. sect. 5. §. 3. n. 188.* S. P. Paulus V. hanc damnavit propositionem: *Verum martyrium subiret, qui pro defensione Conceptionis subiret martyrium.* Quam tamen damnationem invenire non potui, sed quamdam prohibitionem Lusitanæ Inquisitionis, cui Paulus V. annuit, ut ait Delbene. Non disputo, an qui ex errore, aut ex sententia firmiter retenta judicaret de fide esse, & in Scripturis revelatam contineri præservationem B. Virginis in primo suæ Conceptionis instanti, vitam, jugulumque offerret carnifici, verus Martyr dicendus esset? Credo non defuturos esse Theologos, qui assentiantur, neque ea doctrina à Paulo V. damnata dicenda erit. Non disputo, inquam, sed solum assero, diversissima esse prædicta asserta, quæ Muratorius cavillose confundit, ut vehementius reprehendat. Licitum est pro tuenda patria, pro incolumi servando rege, pro defendendo proximo injuste aggresso vitam imminenti periculo exponere; si tamen occumbat, quis Martyrem dicat? Et tamen licite facit, ac ad hoc faciendum licite juramento adstringi potest. Sic defendentem, aut defendere jurantem immaculatam B. Virginis Conceptionem licite operari, pro viribus defendimus. Inter Catholicos nullum in hoc juramento præstando inest vitæ periculum, nulla temeritas. Si tamen tyrannus quisque, vel temerarius, aut protervus homo quemque pie sentientem, ei certam minitans mortem, cogeret ad abjurandam piam sententiam, ad frangendum juramentum præstitum, ad affirmandum piam sententiam veram non esse, incertam esse opinionem, sanctissimam Virginem non fuisse ab originali præservatam, num posset id licite facere, tenereturque tyranno assentiri, ne imminenti mortis periculo se exponeret? Respondeat magnus Theologus Muratorius. Profecto ii juramenti sui termini esse possunt; siquidem inter Christianos neutiquam negotium ad arma, & sanguinis effusionem devenire poterit, præcipue postquam Ecclesia has contentiones omnino prohibuit. | | 133 He will again attempt to defend himself by asserting that he in no wise disapproves the piety and probability of the opinion teaching that the Blessed Virgin was in her first instant preserved from original stain; and that he only inveighs against the temerity by which the followers of the same opinion bind themselves by oath to defend the immaculate Conception even with the loss of their own life: which, to reprehend it, he thus exclaims: *A new kind of Martyrs indeed, which our ancestors never dreamed of, which our posterity, if they have any wisdom, will never approve. Neither the Gospel nor right reason teaches us to squander the soul for causes so light.* He passes beyond the terms of the question. It is one thing, and indeed most different, to assert that the aforesaid oath is licit; another to defend that he is a true Martyr who has poured out his life for the immaculate Conception. The first the Church has never prohibited; the second she may perhaps have reprehended in Theophilus Raynaudus. For, as is found in Portel under the word *Conceptio*, and in Fr. Casnedi *tom. 5. disp. 12. sect. 5. §. 3. n. 188.*, the Holy Father Paul V condemned this proposition: *He would undergo a true martyrdom who for the defense of the Conception should undergo martyrdom.* Which condemnation, however, I could not find, but rather a certain prohibition of the Portuguese Inquisition, to which Paul V assented, as Delbene says. I do not dispute whether one who, from error, or from an opinion firmly retained, should judge that the preservation of the Blessed Virgin in the first instant of her Conception is of faith, and is contained as revealed in the Scriptures, and should offer his life and his throat to the executioner, would have to be called a true Martyr. I believe there will not be lacking Theologians who assent, nor will that doctrine have to be said to be condemned by Paul V. I do not dispute, I say, but I only assert that the aforesaid assertions are most different, which Muratori cavillously confounds, that he may reprehend the more vehemently. It is licit to expose one's life to imminent danger for defending one's fatherland, for keeping the king safe, for defending a neighbor unjustly attacked; yet if one should fall, who would call him a Martyr? And yet he acts licitly, and to do this he can licitly be bound by oath. So we defend with all our powers that one who defends, or swears to defend, the immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin acts licitly. Among Catholics there is in taking this oath no danger of life, no temerity. If, however, some tyrant, or some temerarious or insolent man, threatening certain death, should compel someone piously minded to abjure the pious opinion, to break the oath he has taken, to affirm that the pious opinion is not true, that it is an uncertain opinion, that the most holy Virgin was not preserved from original sin — could he licitly do this, and would he be bound to assent to the tyrant, lest he expose himself to imminent danger of death? Let the great Theologian Muratori answer. Assuredly these can be the terms of his oath; since among Christians the business can in no wise come to arms and to the shedding of blood, especially after the Church has altogether prohibited these contentions. |
| 134 Muratorius ergo, qui veritus immunitati Marianæ sese opponere, in præstitum juramentum se convertit: *Adeo levem esse causam* ad hoc juramentum præstandum proferre non exhorruit. Immunitas sacratissimæ Dei Genitricis, ejus privilegium, quod alteri non dabitur, singularis prærogativa Matri concessa levis, & adeo levis causa dicenda est? Temeraria propositio! Non levem causam judicant homines sua privilegia tueri, conservare, levis vero causa erit Marianum privilegium pro viribus defendere? Inane Muratorii studium! Qui tamen ejus finis, quæ ratio? Quia *Ecclesiæ* (ait) *causa male agitur*, cum hac *interna superstitione perturbatur*. Proh facinus execrabile! Proh deliramentum nullatenus condonandum! Imperitos ipse, sibi solum sapiens, vocat omnes, quos immaculatæ Conceptionis firma opinio impellit; & judicat Ecclesiæ multum interesse *hoc superstitionis vitium populo universo innotescere*. Piarum aurium offensiva hæc propositio judicanda est. Sequutus fuit Muratorius (si forte ipse ejus Auctor non fuit) libellum anonymum, cui titulus: *Monita quædam salutaria B. Virginis ad cultores suos indiscretos*, in quo etiam fidelium devotio, & opinio circa immaculatam Conceptionem tamquam indiscreta reprehenditur. Qui quidem libellus Apostolicæ Sedis decreto reprobatus, & prohibitus fuit ob varias causas, & præcipue ob fidelium scandalum; cui etiam nunc causam dedit inconsideratus Muratorius. Vel etiam didicit à quodam Doctore, qui per decem & novem propositiones in Conceptionem, & juramentum de ea tuenda debachatur, & quem plenissime refellit Portel in *respons. casuum moral: p. 2. cas. 32. per tot.* Non immerito Lusitanum Fidei Tribunal aversatur, cui certe obnoxius esset, si inter nostros hæc audacissima proferret. Supponit, hanc piissimam, & fidei proximam sententiam à Summis Pontificibus, Episcopis, & Conciliis superstitiosam judicari; quod falsissimum esse nullus sanæ mentis dubitabit. Si ea sententia superstitiosa non est, immo est licita, pia, & contraria probabilior, utique superstitiosum non erit juramentum de ea defendenda. Multa sæcula habet hæc sententia, multas ætates in facie totius orbis computat hoc juramentum; & tamen neutiquam illo Ecclesia perturbata fuit; immo quietem ei attulit, quia omnes circa eam sententiam contentiones sedatæ inveniuntur. Si superstitiosum esset illud juramentum, proculdubio prohiberet Ecclesia; non enim sineret universum populum Christianum hoc peccato fœdari. Nota Romanæ Sedi hæc nostra sunt juramenta, nota Episcopis, nota Fidei Tribunalibus, sed ad hæc usque tempora minime reprobata. Ipsum sanctæ Matris Ecclesiæ in re tam nota, tamque omnibus Academiis communi silentium pro tacita approbatione satis est. Quod Ecclesia hucusque superstitiosum, & peccaminosum non judicavit, pronuntiat Muratorius. Mira ingenii libertas, mira confidentia! Nullum fidei, nullum religioni periculum, nullum animabus nocumentum hæc tutissima sententia secum affert. Innumeras proferre possem Hispanas traditiones, quas critici moderni, licet eruditissimi, non juste respuunt, fortasse ob invidiam in Hispanos creatam: proferre possem antiquorum Patrum auctoritates, quas adversarii pro suo modulo interpretantur: possem in medium adducere Monologium Græcum: innumera facile esset recensere miracula, quibus hæc in Conceptionem devotio mirifice, evidenterque comprobatur. His tamen omnibus supersedeo, quæ piissimo Muratorio putida figmenta videbuntur, breviterque non spissa, crepitanti, confragosaque phrasium congerie, sed scholastica methodo ostendam, prædictum juramentum licitum, & religiosum esse. | | 134 Muratori, therefore, who feared to oppose himself to the Marian immunity, turns himself against the oath that has been taken: he did not shrink from declaring the cause for taking this oath *to be so light a cause*. Shall the immunity of the most sacred Mother of God, her privilege, which shall not be given to another, the singular prerogative granted to the Mother, be called a light — and so light — a cause? A temerarious proposition! Men judge it no light cause to defend and conserve their own privileges; and shall it be a light cause to defend with all one's powers the Marian privilege? Vain zeal of Muratori! Yet what is his end, what his reason? Because (he says) *the cause of the Church is ill served*, when it is *disturbed by this internal superstition*. Oh execrable crime! Oh delirium in no wise to be pardoned! He, wise only to himself, calls unskilled all those whom the firm opinion of the immaculate Conception impels; and he judges that it greatly concerns the Church *that this vice of superstition become known to the whole people*. This proposition must be judged offensive to pious ears. Muratori followed (if perchance he was not himself its Author) an anonymous booklet, whose title is: *Monita quædam salutaria B. Virginis ad cultores suos indiscretos*, in which likewise the devotion and opinion of the faithful concerning the immaculate Conception is reprehended as indiscreet. Which booklet indeed was reproved and prohibited by decree of the Apostolic See for various causes, and especially on account of the scandal of the faithful; to which even now the inconsiderate Muratori has given cause.
