Concilium illud ut acephalum, & schismaticum, rejecit decreta, quæ ab eodem Concilio adversus eum, & adversus Concilium Ferrariæ primum, & postea Florentiæ translatum emanaverant; rejecit quæ circa potestatem Concilii supra Papam promulgata fuerant, quod postea etiam rejecit Concilium Lateranense V. sub Leone X. *sess. 11.* rejeceruntque Theologi omnes Orthodoxi. Multa in prædicto Concilio acta sunt rectæ fidei conformia, ut circa Bohemorum propositiones, Græcorum errores, circa disciplinam ecclesiasticam, circa punitionem peccatorum publicorum, circa communionem sub utraque specie, innumeraque alia, quæ neque damnata sunt, nec damnari potuerant, à quibus nullatenus recesserunt Theologi, immo retinuerunt; non quidem quia decreta fuerant in Concilio, cui nulla definiendi authoritas, sed quia alias ex Pontificiis Constitutionibus, & aliorum Conciliorum decretis retinenda erant. Sicut similiter Nicolaus V. multa ipsius Concilii acta ob bonum pacis postea confirmavit. Identidem falsum est, quod quidam Anonymus asseruit, nempe præfatum de Conceptione Decretum à Sede Apostolica rejectum fuisse; nullibi siquidem demonstrabitur sequentium Romanorum Pontificum Constitutio aliqua, in qua illud Decretum inveniatur aut reprobatum, aut correctum. Et si juxta Natalem Alexandrum prædictum Concilium eam sententiam tantummodo pronuntiavit *tamquam piam*, quomodo reprobaret Ecclesia, quod postea Xistus, Clemens, Pius, Paulus, & Gregorius expressissime decreverunt? | | 150 It is urged, sixthly, from the Fathers of the Council of Basel, *sess. 36.*, who by common vote decreed that the Blessed Virgin was immune from original sin, and established that her feast should be celebrated in the whole Church. Thus it stands in *sess. 36.*: *But we, having diligently inspected the authorities and reasons which now for many years have been alleged in public reports on behalf of each doctrine before this most holy Synod, and having seen also very many other things upon this matter, and weighed them with mature consideration, define and declare that that doctrine which maintains that the glorious Virgin MARY, Mother of God, by the singular prevenient and operating grace of the divine Godhead, never actually lay under original sin, but was ever immune from all original and actual guilt, and holy, and immaculate, is, as pious, and consonant with ecclesiastical worship, with the Catholic faith, with right reason, and with sacred Scripture, to be approved by all Catholics, to be held, and to be embraced; and that henceforth it is lawful for no one to preach or teach to the contrary, etc.* And these judgments of those Fathers are related in the history of the Council of Florence, *tom. 9.* of the Councils, in these words: *The matters therefore having been diligently examined, they celebrate the thirty-sixth public session on the fifteenth day before the Kalends of October of the same year, in which after the solemn manner they declare that the glorious Virgin Mary, Mother of God, by the singular prevenient and operating grace of the divine Godhead, never actually lay under original sin, but was ever immune from all original and actual guilt, holy, and immaculate: and they judge that this doctrine is to be approved and held by all, decreeing that thereafter it is lawful for no one at all to preach or assert anything to the contrary. And they approved that her festivity should be celebrated on the sixth day before the Ides of December after the manner of the Roman Church.* Hence I marvel that the most learned Natalis Alexander asserted that the Fathers of the Council decreed nothing else than that this *sententia* is pious; whereas from both texts it plainly appears that they approved it as consonant with the Catholic faith and with sacred Scripture, and commanded it to be held by all. We do not intend hereby to attribute to the Synod of Basel any definitive authority of a true Council; for we know full well that that Synod, although legitimate at its beginning, as having been convoked by the Holy Father Martin V, afterwards became illegitimate, and, as celebrated in time of schism, was destitute of the weight of definition. Yet we demonstrate what was the mind of all those Fathers concerning the immunity of the Blessed Virgin. We wish it noted, however, that it is falsely asserted by some that all the decrees of the Council of Basel were reprobated, and that all Theologians devoted to the Holy See withdrew from them. Eugene IV rejected that Council as acephalous and schismatic; he rejected the decrees which had issued from the same Council against him, and against the Council first of Ferrara, and afterwards translated to Florence; he rejected what had been promulgated concerning the power of a Council above the Pope — which afterwards the Fifth Lateran Council under Leo X, *sess. 11.*, also rejected, and all orthodox Theologians rejected. Many things were done in the aforesaid Council conformable to right faith, as concerning the propositions of the Bohemians, the errors of the Greeks, concerning ecclesiastical discipline, concerning the punishment of public sins, concerning communion under both species, and innumerable other things, which neither were condemned, nor could be condemned, from which the Theologians in no wise withdrew, nay rather retained them; not indeed because they had been decreed in a Council which had no authority of defining, but because on other grounds they were to be retained from the Pontifical Constitutions and the decrees of other Councils. As likewise Nicholas V afterwards, for the good of peace, confirmed many acts of that same Council. Likewise it is false, what a certain Anonymous writer asserted, namely that the aforesaid Decree on the Conception was rejected by the Apostolic See; for nowhere will there be shown any Constitution of the succeeding Roman Pontiffs in which that Decree is found either reprobated or corrected. And if, according to Natalis Alexander, the aforesaid Council pronounced that *sententia* only *as pious*, how should the Church reprobate what afterwards Sixtus, Clement, Pius, Paul, and Gregory most expressly decreed? |
| 151 Suadetur 7. ex Concilio Avenionensi celebrato anno 1457. quod habetur *tom. 9. Concil. ex collect. regia* opera P. Philippi Labboi, & P. Gabrielis Cossarcii *pag. 1388.* ubi ex P. Hippolyto Maraccio sic habetur: *Petrus de Fuxo Senior, Gallus, Ordinis Minorum à Martino V. Pontifice Maximo Cardinalitio honore insignitus, vir suo sæculo magnus, qui in Concilio Constantiensi cum Cardinalibus, & in Hispania Legatus, schisma delevit, duos Hispaniæ Reges confœderavit: & diversas Provincias, ut pater patriæ, annis triginta duobus summa cum laude administravit. Inter alia pietatis obsequia, quibus Deiparæ Virginis amorem in corde suo ardere manifeste declaravit, anno M.CCCC.LVII. Sedis Apostolicæ à latere Legatus celebravit Avenione Concilium Provinciale, in quo inter alias ordinationes statutum edidit tenoris sequentis:* Decretum in Concilio Basiliensi factum de Conceptione Beatissimæ Virginis MARIÆ statuimus inviolabiliter observari: districte omnibus inhibendo sub excommunicationis pœna, ne quisquam aliquid in contrarium prædicare, vel publice disputare præsumat: quod si secus aliquis fecerit, dictam sententiam eum incurrere volumus ipso facto: & in prima Synodo per Diœceses per quemlibet celebranda, prædicta statuimus publicari, & curatis Ecclesiarum injungi, ut hæc populo manifestent. Datum in Ecclesia Cathedrali Avenionensi die vii. mensis Septembris anno Incarnationis Dominicæ MCCCC.LVII. Pontificatus Domini nostri Calisti Papæ III. anno tertio: *Subscripserunt huic statuto non solum ipse Petrus, qui toti præerat Concilio, sed Alanus Coëtivus S. R. E. Cardinalis, de quo supra: Robertus Aquensis Archiepiscopus, Petrus Episcopus Aptensis, Georgius Senecensis, Gaucherius Vapincensis, Nicolaus Massiliensis, Petrus Dignensis, Petrus Glandavensis, Palamedes Cavallicensis, Pontius Vasionensis, Joannes Rhegionensis, Stephanus Tricassinensis, Michael Carpentoractensis, & Joannes Arausiensis. Cæterum animam cœlo reddidit Petrus hic noster anno post redemptum genus humanum M.CCCC.LXIV. die iii. Decembris.* Placuit ad literam referre, ut ex eo ostenderem supposititium dici non posse hoc Concilium, neque à Maraccio confictum, qui adeo individuam mentionem ejus fecit. Neque P. Labbe eum supposititium vocat, quod faceret, ut in aliis solet; immo ei fidem adhibuit, solumque asseruit se integra acta non vidisse. Referam ejus verba: *Avenionense Concilium pro restitutione disciplinæ ecclesiasticæ celebratum Præside Petro de Fuxo Albanensi Episcopo S. R. E. Cardinale, Archiepiscopo Arelatensi, & Legato Avenionensi, dicitur extitisse in bibliotheca Illustrissimi Episcopi Vasionensis, ejusque fit mentio in Purpura Mariana R. P. Hippolyti Maraccii Clerici Regularis pag. 369. quare hic §. XVI. exhibebimus, donec integra illa acta nancisci licuerit.* Non ideo tamen huic Concilio vim definitivam tribuimus, sed auctoritativam, ut demonstremus antiquissimum esse non modo in Hispania, sed etiam in Gallia hunc immaculatæ Conceptionis cultum, atque præceptum: quod Marianæ immunitati maximam conciliat certitudinem, non talem, qualem veritates definitæ habere debent; sed moralem, talemque, ut eam negare temeritati, & impietati tribuendum sit. Quod autem assentatores aliqui dicunt, nempe prædictum Concilium rejectum fuisse, libere dictum judico; non enim aliquod producitur decretum, ex quo id probari possit, præcipue cum id ipsum, quod Concilium illud constituit, à sequentibus Summis Pontificibus constitutum inveniatur. Sicut etiam sine auctore dictum sentio libros Maraccii pro immaculata Conceptione proscriptos fuisse. Neque enim in Indice Expurgatorio nomen Hippolyti Maraccii me legisse memini, neque in Indice librorum prohibitorum Purpuram Marianam invenisse recordor. Fortasse aliqua in ejus operibus expungerentur: id tamen non est proscribere libros, sed aliqua inconcinna delere. | | 151 It is urged, seventhly, from the Council of Avignon celebrated in the year 1457, which is found in *tom. 9. Concil. ex collect. regia*, by the labor of Fr. Philippus Labbe and Fr. Gabriel Cossart, *pag. 1388.*, where from Fr. Hippolytus Maraccius it stands thus: *Petrus de Fuxo the Elder, a Frenchman, of the Order of Minors, adorned with the honor of the Cardinalate by Martin V, Supreme Pontiff, a man great in his own age, who in the Council of Constance with the Cardinals, and as Legate in Spain, blotted out the schism, confederated two Kings of Spain: and administered divers Provinces, as a father of his country, for thirty-two years with the highest praise. Among the other offices of piety by which he manifestly declared that the love of the Virgin Mother of God burned in his heart, in the year M.CCCC.LVII., as Legate a latere of the Apostolic See, he celebrated at Avignon a Provincial Council, in which among other ordinances he issued a statute of the following tenor:* We decree that the Decree made in the Council of Basel concerning the Conception of the Most Blessed Virgin MARY be inviolably observed: strictly forbidding to all, under pain of excommunication, that anyone presume to preach, or publicly to dispute, anything to the contrary: and if anyone shall have done otherwise, we will that he incur the said sentence by that very fact: and we decree that the foregoing be published in the first Synod to be celebrated by each one throughout the Dioceses, and that it be enjoined upon the curates of the Churches to make these things known to the people. Given in the Cathedral Church of Avignon, on the vii. day of the month of September, in the year of the Lord's Incarnation MCCCC.LVII., in the third year of the Pontificate of our Lord Pope Callistus III: *There subscribed to this statute not only Petrus himself, who presided over the whole Council, but also Alanus Coëtivus, Cardinal of the Holy Roman Church, of whom above: Robertus, Archbishop of Aix; Petrus, Bishop of Apt; Georgius of Senez; Gaucherius of Gap; Nicolaus of Marseilles; Petrus of Digne; Petrus of Glandèves; Palamedes of Cavaillon; Pontius of Vaison; Joannes of Riez; Stephanus of Trois-Châteaux; Michael of Carpentras; and Joannes of Orange. For the rest, this our Petrus rendered his soul to heaven in the year M.CCCC.LXIV. after the redemption of the human race, on the iii. day of December.* It has pleased me to relate this to the letter, that from it I might show that this Council cannot be called supposititious, nor feigned by Maraccius, who made so particular a mention of it. Neither does Fr. Labbe call it supposititious, which he would do, as is his wont in other cases; nay rather he gave it credence, and asserted only that he had not seen the complete acts. I shall relate his words: *The Council of Avignon, celebrated for the restoration of ecclesiastical discipline under the presidency of Petrus de Fuxo, Bishop of Albano, Cardinal of the Holy Roman Church, Archbishop of Arles, and Legate of Avignon, is said to have existed in the library of the Most Illustrious Bishop of Vaison, and mention is made of it in the Purpura Mariana of the Rev. Fr. Hippolytus Maraccius, Clerk Regular, pag. 369.; wherefore we shall exhibit it here, §. XVI., until it shall have been possible to come upon those complete acts.* Not for that reason, however, do we attribute to this Council a definitive force, but an authoritative one, that we may demonstrate that this cult, and precept, of the Immaculate Conception is most ancient not only in Spain, but also in France: which wins for the Marian immunity the greatest certitude — not such as defined truths ought to have, but a moral certitude, and such that to deny it must be ascribed to temerity and impiety. And as for what certain flatterers say, namely that the aforesaid Council was rejected, I judge it said gratuitously; for no decree is produced from which this could be proved, especially since that very thing which that Council established was by the succeeding is found to have been established by the Supreme Pontiffs. Just as I also perceive that it was said without an author that the books of Maraccio on behalf of the immaculate Conception had been proscribed. For neither do I recall having read the name of Hippolytus Maraccio in the Expurgatory Index, nor do I remember having found the Purpura Mariana in the Index of prohibited books. Perhaps some things in his works might be expunged: yet that is not to proscribe books, but to delete certain unfitting things. |
| 152 Suadetur 8. ex communi consensu Episcoporum, & totius populi Christiani, apud quem non sine gravi scandalo negaretur immunitas B. Virginis. Audiatur doctrina, & sanctitate conspicuus P. Jacobus Granado *tract. de Concept. disp. 3. q. 29. ibi: Singularis devotio, plausus populi Christiani, celebritas Conceptionis jam pridem instituta clamare videntur, & suo quasi jure definitionem postulare. Et certe vix aliud est dogma definitum ab Ecclesia, quod ante definitionem tanta acclamatione receptum fuerit.* Audiatur P. Vasques *3. part. q. 27. art. 2. disp. 117. cap. 2. ibi: Omnes, qui Christiani nominis fidem profitentur, in hanc sententiam uno animo, & affectu ita conspirant, ut sine magno populi scandalo jam nemo possit oppositum in concionibus exponere.* Audiatur insignis Cancellarius Parisiensis Gerson *in sermon. de Concept. Beatam MARIAM non fuisse conceptam in peccato originali, de his veritatibus est, quæ noviter sunt revelatæ, vel declaratæ tam per miracula, quæ leguntur, quam per maiorem partem Ecclesiæ, quæ hæc modo tenet.* Audiatur eruditissimus Calmet, qui Muratorio suspectus esse non potest, *in dissert. Bibl. JESUS Christus Matrem habuit Virginem frugi, æque modestam, ac puram; cujus phantasiæ serenum nulla à peccato originali inducta nubes offuscare potuerat.* Si ita indubitabiliter tenet maior pars Ecclesiæ, quid culpamur, quid arguimur, quod intrepide juremus tenere, & defendere quod omnes fere Ecclesiæ tenent, & tuentur? Audiatur Surius in Append. ad historiam Naucleri contra impugnatores immunitatis B. Virginis: *Videant, (ait) ne dum Matri honori, & privilegio cum multorum gravi scandalo, & offensione derogant, Filii ejus in se severum judicium provocent.* Hoc non veretur Muratorius, cum in conspectu totius orbis, in hujus veritatis juramentum, nænias (ne aliud dicam) audacter eructat: *Qui elucidant me, vitam æternam habebunt,* ait Dei Patris sapientia: (36) quod quidem de B. Virgine ipsius sapientiæ Matre purissima canit Ecclesia. {marg. (36) Eccles. 24.} Quidquid de sublimitate, & immunitate B. Virginis dicitur, ad honorem Filii sui pertinere indubium est. Si autem qui in Matre Filium elucidant, æternitatis gloriam possidebunt, quid sperare poterit Muratorius, qui non elucidat, sed denigrat MARIÆ immunitatem cum eam dubiam, incertam, opinativam, & indefinibilem deprædicat? | | 152 It is urged, eighthly, from the common consensus of the Bishops and of the whole Christian people, among whom the immunity of the Blessed Virgin could not be denied without grave scandal. Let Father James Granado, conspicuous for learning and sanctity, be heard, *tract. de Concept. disp. 3. q. 29., there: The singular devotion, the applause of the Christian people, the celebration of the Conception long since instituted, seem to cry out, and, as if by their own right, to demand a definition. And certainly there is scarcely any other dogma defined by the Church which before its definition was received with such acclamation.* Let Father Vasquez be heard, *3. part. q. 27. art. 2. disp. 117. cap. 2., there: All who profess the faith of the Christian name conspire in this judgment with one mind and affection in such wise that without great scandal of the people no one can now expound the opposite in sermons.* Let the illustrious Chancellor of Paris, Gerson, be heard, *in sermon. de Concept.: That Blessed MARY was not conceived in original sin is among those truths which have newly been revealed or declared, both through the miracles which are read, and through the greater part of the Church, which now holds this.* Let the most erudite Calmet be heard, who cannot be suspect to Muratori, *in dissert. Bibl.: JESUS Christ had for Mother a Virgin sober, alike modest and pure, the serenity of whose fancy no cloud induced by original sin could have darkened.* If the greater part of the Church holds it thus indubitably, why are we blamed, why are we accused, because we intrepidly swear to hold and to defend what almost all the Churches hold and maintain? Let Surius be heard, in the Appendix to the history of Nauclerus, against the impugners of the immunity of the Blessed Virgin: *Let them see to it, (he says,) lest, while with the grave scandal and offense of many they derogate from the honor and privilege of the Mother, they provoke against themselves the severe judgment of her Son.* This Muratorius does not fear, when in the sight of the whole world, against the oath of this truth, he audaciously belches forth trifles (to say nothing worse): *They that elucidate me shall have life everlasting,* saith the wisdom of God the Father: (36) which indeed the Church sings of the Blessed Virgin, the most pure Mother of Wisdom itself. {marg. (36) Eccles. 24.} Whatever is said of the sublimity and immunity of the Blessed Virgin, it is beyond doubt that it pertains to the honor of her Son. But if they who elucidate the Son in the Mother shall possess the glory of eternity, what will Muratorius be able to hope for, who does not elucidate, but blackens the immunity of MARY, when he proclaims it doubtful, uncertain, a matter of opinion, and indefinable? |
| 153 Interminatam lineam protenderem, si omnes insignes Theologos sic sentientes commemorarem, ipsis tamen maior his omnibus Theologus non assentietur: sed audiat Gallicum modernum Scriptorem, ac S. R. E. Cardinalem de Noailles, qui certe, ac si audiret Muratorium, suam temeritatem reprehendit. Dabo ejus verba gallice scripta. Sic habet titulus: *Heures du C. de Noailles sur la Fete de la Conception de la Sainte Viergi. Typis datus libellus in 12. Parisii anno 1707. ubi pag. 267.* sic habet: *Le jour est un jour de joye: il annonee le salut qui doit nous être apportè por JESUS Christ, dont la Mere est aujord' hui conçûe. En qualque moment que nous considerions la Sainte Mere de Dieu, èloignous d' elle pour l' honneur de son File, toute idée, tout soubçon de pechê. Celle que l' Eglise tien avoir reçû de Dieu une grace particulieri qui l' a preservèe de toute faute, même veniele, pendant tout le tems de sa vie, a bien pû être préservée de la tache originele par un privilege singulier. C' est le sentiment commun, & le plus autorisé dans l' Eglise, quoique l' Eglise n' en ait pas fait une decision, & que cenesoit pas un article de foy; e les Papes en defendu de precher & d' enseigner publiquement l' opinion contrarie. On ne peut trop blâmer la temeritè de ceux qui sans avoir examiné, & même sans être capables d' examiner les raisons de part, & de autre, prennent d' abord le parti qui leur paroît le moins contribuer à la gloire de la Sainte Viergi. Les simples fideles doivent respecter & recevoir avec joye le pieux sentiment qui semble honorer davantage la Mere & le Fils; ou du moins arrêter leur curiosité, s' en tenir à u que la foy & la pietè lèur enseignent, & ne point douter que Dieu n' ait rendu Marie toute pure, & toute sainte, au moment & de la maniere qui ont etè les plus convenables à l' execution ces desseins éternels, à la misericorde qu' il vouloit exercer envers elle, & aux choix qu' il en avoit fait pour être la Mere de JESUS Christ.* En quam opinionem pie pervicacem temerarius explodit Muratorius, ac veluti nimium incertum audax, inconsultusque deludit. Neque se defendere arbitretur asserens eum non impugnare piam opinionem, sed impium juramentum de ea defendenda. Agnoscimus larvam. Si ipse pie crederet, non impie impugnaret. Si ea sibi opinio, non opinio, sed moraliter certa sententia haberetur, in juramentum de ea defendenda non exagitaretur. Si tamen ea sibi non placet sententia, quod arriserit, teneat; sileat tamen, nec alios exprobet, id enim Pontificum jubent decreta, ac ipsis nostrum juramentum relinquat condemnandum. | | 153 I should stretch out an endless line if I were to commemorate all the illustrious Theologians who think thus; yet that Theologian, greater than all of these, will not assent to them: but let him hear a modern French Writer, and Cardinal of the Holy Roman Church, de Noailles, who assuredly, as if he were hearing Muratorius, reprehends his temerity. I shall give his words as written in French. The title runs thus: *Heures du C. de Noailles sur la Fete de la Conception de la Sainte Viergi. The little book was printed in 12mo at Paris in the year 1707, where at pag. 267* it has thus: *This day is a day of joy: it announces the salvation which is to be brought to us by JESUS Christ, whose Mother is today conceived. At whatever moment we consider the holy Mother of God, let us remove from her, for the honor of her Son, every idea, every suspicion of sin. She whom the Church holds to have received from God a particular grace which preserved her from every fault, even venial, during the whole time of her life, could well have been preserved from the original stain by a singular privilege. This is the common sentiment, and the most authorized in the Church, although the Church has not made a decision upon it, and it is not an article of faith; and the Popes have forbidden the preaching and the public teaching of the contrary opinion. One cannot too much blame the temerity of those who, without having examined, and even without being capable of examining, the reasons on either side, straightway take the part which seems to them to contribute least to the glory of the holy Virgin. The simple faithful ought to respect and to receive with joy the pious sentiment which seems the more to honor the Mother and the Son; or at least to check their curiosity, to hold to what faith and piety teach them, and in no wise to doubt that God rendered Mary all pure and all holy, at the moment and in the manner which were the most fitting to the execution of his eternal designs, to the mercy which he willed to exercise toward her, and to the choice which he had made of her to be the Mother of JESUS Christ.* Lo, this is the opinion which the rash Muratorius hoots down as piously obstinate, and which, audacious and ill-advised, he derides as all too uncertain. Nor let him think to defend himself by asserting that he does not impugn the pious opinion, but the impious oath of defending it. We recognize the mask. If he himself believed piously, he would not impugn impiously. If that opinion were held by him not as an opinion, but as a morally certain judgment, he would not be so agitated against the oath of defending it. If, however, that judgment does not please him, let him hold what has smiled upon him; yet let him be silent, nor upbraid others, for this the decrees of the Pontiffs command; and let him leave our oath to them for condemning. |
| 154 Suadetur 9. ex infinita propemodum omnium Ordinum Doctorum, item Academiarum multitudine B. Virginis immunitatem adstruente. Legatur eruditissimus Augustinus Barbosa ad Concilium Tridentinum *dict. sess. 5.* de Peccato Originali, ubi postquam quadraginta Doctores magni nominis (inter quos Abulensis, Albertus Magnus, & Magister Sententiarum recensentur) allegaverat, sic ait: „Quorum opinionem, & sententiam tamquam receptiorem, probabiliorem, & pietati ac devotioni fidelium congruentiorem, hodie jam ab omnibus fere Ecclesiasticis, & sæcularibus, Prælatis, & subditis, ac cæteris omnibus cujuscumque gradus, dignitatis, & sexus existentibus, adeo receptam invenimus, ut eam veritatis scopum attingere dicendum videretur: præsertim cum omnes insignes Academiæ non solum illam receperint, sed & publico, & solemni juramento ejus definitionem hilari fronte susceperint, (*solum Muratorius, & alii ejusdem ingenii, indignati suscipiunt*) ut de Coloniensi, & Moguntina testatur Laurentius Surius de rebus memorabilibus anno 1501. & de antiquissima, & famigerata Parisiensium Academia scriptum reliquerunt Canisius lib. 1. Deiparæ cap. 7. Jacobus Gordonus tom. 2. Chronolog. in annum 11. Papæ Urbani VI. Cævall. d. q. 346. n. 18. & Balthasar Porreño quo loco nuper citavimus in cap. 28. insuper affirmans tam in Universitate Salmantina, Toletana, & Complutensi, quam in nostra Cæsaraugustanorum Academia, & in Ilerdensi, Tarraconensi, Barchinonensi, Hispalensi, Valentina, & cæteris omnibus Hispaniæ speciali decreto stabilitum fuisse, ut nulli ad Doctoratus gradum aditus pateat, quin prius sub juramento non promiserit nunquam se veritatem hanc oppugnaturum, & de Parisiensi, Complutensi, & Valentina id etiam affirmat P. Joan. Baptist. Lozana in citat. cap. 22. fol. 98. qui non solum variis Pontificum, & gravissimorum Doctorum auctoritatibus ab eo congestis cap. 13. & seqq. verum etiam urgentissimis rationibus cap. 35. in medium propositis hanc piam, & verissimam assertionem ingeniose confirmat: quam & nos triginta abhinc annis adversus Gloss. verbo Nativitas in cap. 1. de Consecrat. dist. 3. & verbo Miraculo in cap. 2. de Consecrat. dist. 4. dum hæc jura publice in nostra Academia doceremus, libenti animo secuti fuimus variis rationibus, & fundamentis illam comprobantes. Et postmodum anno 1618. cum hujus nobilissimæ Civitatis Cæsaraugustanæ supremum Consulatum exerceremus, una cum aliis ejusdem Civitatis moderatoribus, copiosaque Nobilium, ac Civium, & Equitum multitudine in Angelico Sacello Serenissimæ Deiparæ, & immaculatæ Virginis del Pilar communiter à nostratibus nuncupato, ejusdem piæ assertionis defensionem solemni juramento firmavimus. His tamen omnibus contraire Muratorius, veluti si plus omnibus saperet, non addubitat, nullis rationibus, nulli auctoritatum ponderi, sed suis solum phrasibus, & declamationibus innixus. Si enim quod scribit perpendatur, totus consistit in deneganda certitudine sententiæ, quæ B. Virginis immunitati adstipulatur. Si enim eam fateatur, necesse erit, quod omnis suorum argumentorum machina dissolvatur. | | 154 It is urged, ninthly, from the well-nigh infinite multitude of Doctors of all Orders, and likewise of Academies, maintaining the immunity of the Blessed Virgin. Let the most erudite Augustinus Barbosa be read, on the Council of Trent, *dict. sess. 5.*, on Original Sin, where, after he had alleged forty Doctors of great name (among whom are counted the Abulensis, Albert the Great, and the Master of the Sentences), he says thus: „Whose opinion and judgment, as the more received, the more probable, and the more congruent with the piety and devotion of the faithful, we find today so received by almost all, Ecclesiastics and seculars, Prelates and subjects, and all the rest of whatever grade, dignity, and sex, that it would seem it must be said to attain the mark of truth: especially since all the illustrious Academies have not only received it, but have also with a public and solemn oath embraced its definition with cheerful brow, (*only Muratorius, and others of the same temper, receive it with indignation*) as of Cologne and Mainz Laurentius Surius testifies, de rebus memorabilibus anno 1501, and of the most ancient and famed Academy of the Parisians there wrote Canisius lib. 1. Deiparæ cap. 7., Jacobus Gordonus tom. 2. Chronolog. in annum 11. of Pope Urban VI, Cævall. d. q. 346. n. 18., and Balthasar Porreño in the place we lately cited, in cap. 28., affirming moreover that both in the University of Salamanca, of Toledo, and of Alcalá, and in our Academy of Saragossa, and in those of Lérida, Tarragona, Barcelona, Seville, Valencia, and all the rest of Spain, it was established by special decree that access to the grade of the Doctorate should lie open to none unless he had first promised under oath that he would never oppugn this truth; and of Paris, Alcalá, and Valencia Father Joan. Baptist. Lozana affirms the same, in the cited cap. 22. fol. 98., who not only by various authorities of Pontiffs and of most grave Doctors heaped up by him in cap. 13. & seqq., but also by most urgent reasons brought forward in cap. 35., ingeniously confirms this pious and most true assertion: which we also, thirty years ago, against the Gloss, at the word Nativitas in cap. 1. de Consecrat. dist. 3., and at the word Miraculo in cap. 2. de Consecrat. dist. 4., while we were publicly teaching these laws in our Academy, with willing mind followed, proving it by various reasons and foundations. And afterwards, in the year 1618, when we were exercising the supreme Consulship of this most noble City of Saragossa, together with the other governors of the same City, and a copious multitude of Nobles, Citizens, and Knights, in the Angelic Chapel of the most Serene Mother of God and immaculate Virgin, commonly called by our countrymen del Pilar, we confirmed by solemn oath the defense of the same pious assertion. Yet Muratorius does not hesitate to gainsay all these, as though he were wiser than all, leaning upon no reasons, upon no weight of authorities, but only upon his own phrases and declamations. For if what he writes be weighed, the whole of it consists in denying the certitude of the judgment which supports the immunity of the Blessed Virgin. For if he should confess it, it would needs be that the whole machine of his arguments be dissolved. |
| 155 Suadetur 10. ex veritate immunitatis cœlitus edocta, ac revelata S. Birgittæ, ut ex *lib. 5. cap. 4.* luculenter constat. Quantum autem hujusmodi revelationibus deferendum sit, ex eo liquet, quod Gregorii XI. & Urbani V. jussu recognitæ, demumque à Bonifacio XI. & Martino V. examini, & libræ commissæ, laudatæ, & confirmatæ fuerint. Eadem immunitas revelata est S. Elisabethæ Hungariæ Reginæ, ut ait Pelbart *lib. 4. Stellarii p. 1. art. 3.* Eamdem ipsamet Virgo sanctissima edocuit Venerabilem Matrem Mariam de Agreda, ut constat ex suo mirabili libro *Mistica Ciudad de Dios*, jam olim à S. Sede post accuratum examen permisso, & nunc ut speramus approbando. Juvant apparitiones Angelici Doctoris, & Melliflui Bernardi ad quosdam suos devotos, ut ex P. Bernardes supra tetigi. Favent innumera miracula, quæ apud pios scriptores passim habentur, quibus omnem negare fidem, pervicacis incredulitatis, & tenacissimæ obcæcationis crimen erit. | | 155 It is urged, tenthly, from the truth of the immunity taught from heaven, and revealed to St. Bridget, as is lucidly evident from *lib. 5. cap. 4.* How much deference is to be given to revelations of this kind is clear from this, that by the command of Gregory XI and Urban V they were reviewed, and at length by Boniface XI and Martin V committed to examination and to the balance, praised, and confirmed. The same immunity was revealed to St. Elisabeth, Queen of Hungary, as Pelbart says, *lib. 4. Stellarii p. 1. art. 3.* The same the most holy Virgin herself taught the Venerable Mother Maria de Agreda, as is evident from her marvelous book *Mistica Ciudad de Dios*, long since permitted by the Holy See after accurate examination, and now, as we hope, to be approved. The apparitions of the Angelic Doctor and of the Mellifluous Bernard to certain of their devotees lend aid, as I touched upon above from Father Bernardes. Innumerable miracles favor it, which are found everywhere among pious writers; to deny all credence to which will be the crime of obstinate incredulity and of most tenacious blindness. |
| 156 Suadetur 11. quia licitum est juramentum de tuenda pro viribus aliqua veritate, quæ morali evidentia habeatur tamquam de fide certa, & implicite in sacris Scripturis revelata. Hujus generis sunt complurimæ conclusiones Theologicæ, quæ licet non ultimo Ecclesiæ judicio expresse definitæ, apud Theologos habentur tamquam certæ. Atqui licite credi potest fide divina B. Virginem fuisse immaculate conceptam, liberamque ab omni originali nævo, quoties alicui moraliter constet eam fuisse in Scripturis revelatam. Igitur &c. Minor, in qua difficultas esse potest, non mea est, sed P. Viva *ad proposit. 21. Innocent. XI. n. 19.* ubi inquirit: *An possit fide supernaturali credi Deiparam fuisse sine labe conceptam?* Et sic resolvit: *Equidem censeo posse immaculatam Virginis Conceptionem fide divina credi ab eo, cui moraliter constat eam esse implicite in Scripturis revelatam: etenim juxta doctrinam Suarii tom. 2. in 3. part. disp. 3. sect. 6. prohibitio Pontificia ne alicui propositioni censura aliqua inuratur, bene cohæret cum summa probabilitate, necnon cum morali certitudine sententiæ oppositæ: unde non impedit, quominus sententia, quæ censurari non potest, dici possit omnino improbabilis, ut habet Lugo disp. 20. de Fide n. 104. ex Hurtado..... Quare sicut fide supernaturali credere potest, v. g. Incarnationem propositam à Parocho, ille rusticus, cui moraliter certum est Parochum nec fallere, nec falli; secus verò si hoc tantum sit probabile, & incertum: ita potest fide supernaturali credere Deiparam sine macula conceptam fuisse is, cui moraliter constat id in Scripturis implicite revelari; secus vero si hoc tantum putet esse tantum probabile, & incertum.* Unde id non poterit credere Muratorius, cui ea sententia est tantummodo probabilis, & incerta, atque errori obnoxia. Cum tamen tot sint sacræ Scripturæ auctoritates, ex quibus Mariani Theologi immaculatam Deiparæ Conceptionem deducunt, & in eis revelatam sine formidine credunt, ad id juvantibus innumeris sanctorum Patrum auctoritatibus; nihil mirum, quod inde resultet quædam moralis certitudo, quæ omnino licitum faciat prædictum de tuenda immaculata Deiparæ Conceptione juramentum. Reponet fortasse Muratorius, ut alii nugatores faciunt, prædictas auctoritates à nostratibus deturpari, & detorqueri. Sed quis judex erit similis imposturæ? An Muratorius? An superiore lumine instructus est, ut non fallatur ipse, nos verò fallamur? Non inficior multas obscuras esse, aliumque sensum habere posse; aliæ verò non adeo obscuræ sunt, ut ad B. MARIÆ immunitatem adduci non possint; immo videmus aliquas ab ipsa Ecclesia Conceptioni applicari, alias vero applicari permitti. Non igitur quæstionis hujus aliquo jure Muratorius arbiter constituitur. | | 156 It is urged, eleventhly, because an oath is licit for defending with all one's strength some truth which by moral evidence is held as certain *de fide* and implicitly revealed in the sacred Scriptures. Of this kind are very many Theological conclusions which, though not expressly defined by the final judgment of the Church, are held among Theologians as certain. But it can licitly be believed with divine faith that the Blessed Virgin was immaculately conceived, and free from every original blemish, whenever it is morally evident to someone that this has been revealed in the Scriptures. Therefore, etc. The minor, in which the difficulty can lie, is not mine, but Father Viva's, *ad proposit. 21. Innocent. XI. n. 19.*, where he inquires: *Whether it can be believed with supernatural faith that the Mother of God was conceived without stain?* And he resolves thus: *For my part I judge that the immaculate Conception of the Virgin can be believed with divine faith by him to whom it is morally evident that it is implicitly revealed in the Scriptures: for according to the doctrine of Suarez, tom. 2. in 3. part. disp. 3. sect. 6., a Pontifical prohibition that some censure not be branded upon a proposition coheres well with the highest probability, and even with the moral certitude, of the opposite judgment: whence it does not prevent the judgment which cannot be censured from being able to be called altogether improbable, as Lugo has it, disp. 20. de Fide n. 104., from Hurtado..... Wherefore, just as that rustic can believe with supernatural faith, for example, the Incarnation as proposed by his Parish priest, to whom it is morally certain that the Parish priest neither deceives nor is deceived; but otherwise, if this be only probable and uncertain: so he can believe with supernatural faith that the Mother of God was conceived without stain, to whom it is morally evident that this is implicitly revealed in the Scriptures; but otherwise, if he think this to be only probable and uncertain.* Whence Muratorius will not be able to believe this, to whom that judgment is only probable and uncertain, and liable to error. Since, however, there are so many authorities of sacred Scripture from which the Marian Theologians deduce the immaculate Conception of the Mother of God, and believe without misgiving that it is revealed in them, innumerable authorities of the holy Fathers aiding thereto; it is no wonder that thence results a certain moral certitude which makes altogether licit the aforesaid oath of defending the immaculate Conception of the Mother of God. Muratorius will perhaps reply, as other triflers do, that the aforesaid authorities are disfigured and distorted by our party. But who will be the judge of such an imposture? Muratorius? Has he been instructed with a higher light, so that he himself is not deceived, but we are deceived? I do not deny that many are obscure, and can bear another sense; but others are not so obscure that they cannot be adduced for the immunity of Blessed MARY; nay, we see that some are applied by the Church herself to the Conception, and that others are permitted to be applied. By no right, therefore, is Muratorius constituted arbiter of this question. |
| 157 Suadetur 12. quia attento controversiæ præsenti statu, ita vera est immunitas B. Virginis, ut à parte rei illi falsitas subesse non possit, prout late probat Salazar *cit. cap. 43. §. 4.* ita proxime, & ultimo disposita ad definitionem, ut non expectato Concilio, tuto possit Pontifex ad definitivam sententiam procedere, ut cum multis tenet idem *Salaz. cit. §. 6.* Ex quibus concluditur B. Virginem in possessione suæ immunitatis constitutam nonnisi per summam injuriam deturbandam: hanc verò injuriam à Regina Cœli, ac terræ propulsare, effuso millies servorum sanguine licitum, ac religiosissimum est. Licet utique, immo maioris dilectionis præconio à divino Oraculo dilaudatur, qui *animam suam ponit pro amicis suis*, (37) & animam ponere pro tuenda immunitate Virgini ipsi dilectissimæ longe vita chariori superstitiosum, pervicax, & illicitum appellabitur? {marg. (37) Joan, 15. 15.} Oh temeritatem inauditam! Licuit Eleazaro confossa bellua triumpho suo obrui: *Melius est nobis mori in bello, quam videre mala gentis nostræ, & sanctorum*, (38) & sanctissimæ Virginis immunitatem proprio sanguine vindicare nefas erit illicitum, & superstitiosum? {marg. (38) Machab. 3.} Oh audaciam inauditam! Licet pro fratribus animas ponere: *Debemus pro fratribus animas ponere*, (39) & pro asserendo Dei Genitricis, & nostræ amabilissimæ Matris singularissimo, Virginique honorificentissimo privilegio Ecclesiæ auctoritate, Patrum testimonio, & totius populi Christiani consensu firmato vitam profundere, superstitiosum erit & illicitum? {marg. (39) Joan. 1. 3.} Oh vocem, quæ à Christiano ore proferatur, indignam! Licuit Samsoni, ut fidelis populi hostes debilitaret, inclamare: *Moriatur anima mea cum Philisthiim*; (40) mortem verò pro avertendis adversus Marianam immunitatem injuriis subire, illicitum, & superstitiosum proferetur? {marg. (40) Judic. 16. 30.} Oh verbum impietate fætum, Dei Matri injurium, Deo invisum, Angelis, & hominibus scandalosum! | | 157 It is urged, twelfthly, because, the present state of the controversy being considered, the immunity of the Blessed Virgin is so true that in the reality of the thing falsity cannot underlie it, as Salazar proves at large, *cit. cap. 43. §. 4.*; so proximately and ultimately disposed for definition that, a Council not being awaited, the Pontiff can safely proceed to a definitive sentence, as the same *Salaz. cit. §. 6.* holds with many. From which it is concluded that the Blessed Virgin, established in the possession of her immunity, is not to be dislodged save by supreme injury: but to repel this injury from the Queen of Heaven and earth, even with the blood of her servants shed a thousand times, is licit and most religious. It is licit indeed—nay, he is extolled by the divine Oracle with the proclamation of greater love who *lays down his life for his friends* (37)—and shall it be called superstitious, obstinate, and illicit to lay down one's life for defending the immunity of the Virgin, most beloved, far dearer than life? {marg. (37) Joan, 15. 15.} O unheard-of temerity! It was licit for Eleazar, the beast run through, to be crushed beneath his own triumph: *It is better for us to die in battle than to see the evils of our nation and of the holy places* (38)—and shall it be a crime, illicit and superstitious, to vindicate with one's own blood the immunity of the most holy Virgin? {marg. (38) Machab. 3.} O unheard-of audacity! It is licit to lay down our lives for the brethren: *We ought to lay down our lives for the brethren* (39)—and shall it be superstitious and illicit to pour out one's life for asserting the most singular privilege, most honorable to the Virgin, of the Mother of God and our most amiable Mother, confirmed by the authority of the Church, the testimony of the Fathers, and the consensus of the whole Christian people? {marg. (39) Joan. 1. 3.} O word unworthy to be uttered by a Christian mouth! It was licit for Samson, that he might weaken the enemies of the faithful people, to cry out: *Let my soul die with the Philistines* (40)—and shall it be pronounced illicit and superstitious to undergo death for averting injuries against the Marian immunity? {marg. (40) Judic. 16. 30.} O word teeming with impiety, injurious to the Mother of God, hateful to God, scandalous to Angels and to men! |
| 158 Tandem suadetur nostræ propositionis veritas ratione extrinseca jam supra tacta. Pium, licitum, & religiosum est juramentum, quod à viris doctissimis, piis, timoratis, ac religione conspicuis non probatur modo, sed usurpatur, & præcipitur: tale est juramentum de tuenda immunitate B. Virginis ab originali: Ergo &c. Pro minoris probatione prima immaculatæ Conceptionis vexillum protulit anno 1383. facultas Theologica Parisiensis, edito decreto, ne quis gradum, vel Doctoris lauream arriperet, qui immaculatam Virginis Conceptionem à se defendendam jurejurando non promitteret. Et licet Natalis Alexander asserat non aliam esse *sacræ facultatis Parisiensis* mentem, quam quæ à Concilio Tridentino, & ab Ecclesia probatur, id certe ab omnibus aliis orthodoxis Academiis habetur. Nullus tam delirus erit, ut contra Ecclesiæ sensum aut votum, aut juramentum emittat. Ea tamen Natalis sententia non contrariatur Decreto supra memorato, neque juramento ab omnibus gradum suscipientibus emisso de defendenda scilicet ea sententia, eoque cultu, quem Ecclesia probavit, & tot privilegiis munivit. Quo posito juramento de re proculdubio licita, & laudabili, non video, cur non licite possit mortem pati qui ad infringendum licitum juramentum, & ad asserendum Virginem Matrem in peccato fuisse conceptam violenter adigatur. Hinc miror quanta confidentia ab aliquibus asseratur post Decretum Gregorii XV. & initio sæculi xvii. incepisse in Hispaniis hoc juramentum introduci, cum constet jam Parisiis observatum fuisse sæculo xiv. | | 158 Finally, the truth of our proposition is urged by the extrinsic reason already touched upon above. That oath is pious, licit, and religious which by most learned, pious, God-fearing men, conspicuous for religion, is not only approved, but employed and prescribed: such is the oath of defending the immunity of the Blessed Virgin from original sin: Therefore, etc. For the proof of the minor: the Theological faculty of Paris first bore forth the standard of the immaculate Conception in the year 1383, a decree being published that no one should seize a degree or the Doctor's laurel who did not promise by oath that the immaculate Conception of the Virgin was to be defended by him. And although Natalis Alexander asserts that the mind of the *sacred faculty of Paris* is no other than that which is approved by the Council of Trent and by the Church, that assuredly holds for all the other orthodox Academies. No one will be so crazed as to emit either a vow or an oath against the sense of the Church. Yet that judgment of Natalis is not contrary to the Decree above mentioned, nor to the oath emitted by all who take a degree, of defending, namely, that judgment, and with that cult, which the Church has approved and fortified with so many privileges. Which oath being posited, concerning a thing beyond doubt licit and laudable, I do not see why he may not licitly suffer death who is violently constrained to break a licit oath and to assert that the Virgin Mother was conceived in sin. Hence I marvel with what confidence it is asserted by some that after the Decree of Gregory XV, and at the beginning of the seventeenth century, this oath began to be introduced in the Spains, since it is evident that it was already observed at Paris in the fourteenth century. |
| 159 Parisiensem imitata est anno 1451. Coloniensis Academia, Coloniensem Moguntina anno 1601. Moguntinam Valentina, & Ursaonensis Academiæ in Hispania, & cæteræ, quas supra recensuimus, Conimbricensis, & Eborensis in Lusitania; quarum demum vestigia prementes, pleræque Christiani orbis Academiæ in Germania, Gallia, Italia, Hispaniis, & Indiis in eamdem sententiam conspirarunt. Adstipulantur Ordines sanctissimi, & in primis Seraphicus, duce Doctore Subtilissimo, immaculatæ Conceptionis vindex acerrimus, qui præstito juramento ad ultimum vitæ spiritum Marianam immunitatem proclamat. Societas JESU, ubi nullus cathedram conscendit, laureave insignitur, quin pari religione obstringatur. Censeantur singuli, & vix aliquis offendetur, qui pari obsequio se B. Virgini non emancipet. Carmelitarum antiquissimus Ordo idem sequitur erga Deiparam obsequium, qui certe pro primatu in tuenda B. Virginis immunitate pugnat, & non contemptibilibus fundamentis innititur, de quo videndi Salmanticenses, & ibi citati *tract. 23. cap. 1. punct. 5. §. 4. n. 78.* Nec desunt Militares Ordines S. Jacobi, (cui, ut supra tetigi, hujus debiti cultus in Hispaniis primatus debetur) Calatravæ, & Alcantaræ in Hispania, Christi, S. Jacobi, & S. Benedicti in Lusitania, qui eodem juramento sunt adstricti. Quot igitur in his viri sanctissimi, ac doctissimi superstitionis, & peccati labe inuruntur. Et ne superstitiosi, peccatique rei dicentur S. Ignatius, S. Franciscus Xaverius, primique Societatis Patres, qui omnes Parisiis pro suscipienda laurea hoc juramentum emisere? Peccavit juvenis sanctitate clarissimus Joannes Bukmans è Societate, renovato quotidie voto de tuenda Conceptione? Peccarunt tot Ecclesiæ lumina, Pontifices, Cardinales, Patriarchæ, Archiepiscopi, Episcopi, Doctoresque innumeri, qui in Academiis, ubi præceptum viget, pro suscipienda laurea Marianam immunitatem defendendam jurarunt? Peccarunt hoc sacramento dicto tot sapientissimæ, & religiosissimæ Academiæ, & in his Regia Lusitana, duce piissimo Joanne V. ac Principibus serenissimis? Comitia pro inauguratione Joannis IV. in Lusitania habita; item habita Matriti sub Philippo IV. anno 1621. præstito per Procuratores à Populis juramento colendi, & profitendi Conceptionis immunitatem? Unus solus autem innocens, impollutus, & segregatus à peccatoribus Muratorius, qui in Christiani populi faciem absurdam ad minus, & temerariam propositionem evomat. Juramentum de tuenda B. Virginis immunitate ab originali peccato est superstitiosa, & pia pervicacia! Dicamus potius suam sententiam esse audacem, irreligiosam, & pervicacissimam impietatem. | | 159 The Academy of Cologne imitated that of Paris in the year 1451; Mainz followed Cologne in the year 1601; Valencia and the Academy of Osuna in Spain followed Mainz, and the rest which we recounted above, Coimbra and Évora in Portugal; whose footsteps at length pressing, most of the Academies of the Christian world in Germany, France, Italy, the Spains, and the Indies conspired into the same judgment. The most holy Orders add their assent, and first of all the Seraphic, under the leadership of the Most Subtle Doctor, that keenest champion of the immaculate Conception, which, the oath having been taken, proclaims the Marian immunity to the last breath of life. The Society of JESUS, where no one ascends the chair, or is adorned with the laurel, without being bound by a like religious obligation. Let them be counted man by man, and scarcely anyone will be found who does not surrender himself with like homage to the Blessed Virgin. The most ancient Order of the Carmelites follows the same homage toward the Mother of God, which indeed contends for the primacy in defending the immunity of the Blessed Virgin, and rests upon foundations not contemptible, on which see the Salmanticenses, and those cited there, *tract. 23. cap. 1. punct. 5. §. 4. n. 78.* Nor are there lacking the Military Orders of St. James (to which, as I touched upon above, the primacy of this due cult in the Spains is owed), of Calatrava, and of Alcántara in Spain, of Christ, of St. James, and of St. Benedict in Portugal, which are bound by the same oath. How many most holy and most learned men among these, then, are branded with the stain of superstition and of sin! And shall not St. Ignatius, St. Francis Xavier, and the first Fathers of the Society be called superstitious and guilty of sin, who all at Paris, for the taking of the laurel, emitted this oath? Did the youth most illustrious for sanctity, John Bukmans of the Society, sin, the vow of defending the Conception being daily renewed? Did so many lights of the Church sin—Pontiffs, Cardinals, Patriarchs, Archbishops, Bishops, and Doctors innumerable—who in the Academies where the precept is in force swore, for the taking of the laurel, that the Marian immunity was to be defended? Did so many most wise and most religious Academies sin, this solemn oath having been pronounced, and among these the Royal Portuguese, under the leadership of the most pious John V and the most serene Princes? The Cortes held in Portugal for the inauguration of John IV; likewise those held at Madrid under Philip IV in the year 1621, the oath of venerating and professing the immunity of the Conception having been taken by the Procurators on behalf of the Peoples? But Muratorius alone is innocent, unpolluted, and set apart from sinners, who into the face of the Christian people vomits forth a proposition at the least absurd, and rash. The oath of defending the immunity of the Blessed Virgin from original sin is a superstitious and pious obstinacy! Let us rather say that his judgment is an audacious, irreligious, and most obstinate impiety. |
| 160 Hujus generis sunt propositiones, (ne dicam blasphemias) in quas prorumpit, nempe: *Maiores nostros nunquam id somniasse*, cum tamen adeo antiqua sit maiorum nostrorum in Conceptionem pietas, & devotio: *Posteros nostros nunquam id, si quidquam sapiant, probaturos*, quasi putet suamet inflata sapientia deceptus, devotionem, & sententiam istam à patribus in filios traductam suis compositis eloquiis penitus esse delendam: *Levem esse causam istam ad vitam prodigendam*; quasi levis causa dicenda sit Virginis honor eximius, & pacifica immunitatis possessio: *Hanc opinionem falsam esse posse, & necessario non defendendam*; ac si honori Virginis, amori, & omnipotentiæ Dei non congruat defendere ejus Matris purissimæ, & amabilissimæ immunitatem, ne admissa opinione contraria, diceremus Deum Patrem omnipotentem Filiam suam primogenitam ante omnem creaturam, Filium suum sapientem Matrem suam ab æternitate electam, Spiritum Sanctum amantem Sponsam suam speciosissimam, cum possent hoc insigni privilegio decorare, id noluisse. Quam absurdum! *Istam sententiam certam videri homini creaturæ fallacissimæ*, quasi ipse etiam non sit fallacissimus homo: *Errori obnoxiam esse*, sententiam scilicet, quam Ecclesia probat, & piam agnoscit, & tot privilegiis condecorat: *Non posse dici certam opinionem, quam Ecclesia ipsa, tot licet piorum quorumdam precibus fatigata, certam decernere nondum sustinuit, immo decerni non posse silentio suo significavit.* Veluti si Ecclesiæ dilatio in definienda Conceptionis immunitate argumento esse possit ejus indefinibilitati, aut aliquid detrahat intrinsecæ veritati, siqua insit; cum tamen sciamus multa in Scripturis revelata diu Patribus latuisse. Noluit hucusque Deus id Ecclesiæ indeficienter revelare; fortasse, ut in maiorem sui, & Matris gloriam debeatur devotioni, quod fidei præcepto deberetur. Speramus tamen, & certo confidimus id Ecclesiam tandem tandem esse definituram, ut garrientium ora omnino intercludantur: *Hunc zelum sodem esse, & zelum sine scientia, zelum inconsultum, & prudentiæ monitis injurium.* Veluti si zelus suus imprudentior non sit habendus, quo hanc sopitam, vel silentio obvolutam quæstionem ipse rixarum, & contentionum æmulus iterum movet, turbinesque jam dudum sedatos iterum excitat: *Inde sperandum non esse aut præmium, aut laudem. Irascendam potius esse Christi Matrem, (si in eam cadere posset iræ sensus) eo quod ejus immunitatem eodem ardore, ac fidei revelationes tueamur.* Num ipsi revelatum est Deo, ejusque Matri non placere hoc nostrum obsequium? Num commemorabit aliquando Deum, aut Virginem Matrem suæ displicentiæ signa demonstrasse? E' contra sunt favores, & beneficia multa erga Marianæ immunitatis defensores à sanctissima Domina, & Matre amabilissima, quæ ex historiis constant. Ego quidem spero firmiter B. Virginis patrocinium, eo quod, licet tantillus, pro meo posse eam defenderim. Sic imploro cum D. Augustino, & cum Ecclesia sic rogantibus: (41) *Sentiant omnes tuum juvamen quicumque celebrant tuam sanctam Conceptionem.* {marg. (41) D. August. serm. 18. de Sanctis. quod Ecclesia recitat 2. die infra octavam in 2 nocturno lect. 3.} *Si* sancta est, quomodo peccato originali maculata? *Incertum esse quod aliter Deus voluisse potest, & incertum adhuc est, an aliter esse voluerit.* Quomodo incertum est? Creavit Deus Angelos in sua creatione purissimos, & impuram crearet Reginam Angelorum? In justitia originali creavit Hevam, quæ mortem intulit mundo, & impuram crearet MARIAM, quæ vitam mundo attulit? Crearetur contagione originali quæ ab æterno ordinata fuerat, & antequam terra fieret, jam erat prædeterminata, & concepta in mente divina, ut ipsius vitæ Mater esset? Præparabatur, ut esset templum Dei vivi, & habitaculum Spiritus Sancti, & non descenderet de cœlo tamquam sponsa ornata viro suo? Potuit Deus omnipotens ei tantum conferre privilegium, & credendum est noluisse, aut incertum erit an ita voluerit? Id sentiat Muratorius. Ego id nunquam mihi persuadere fas esse docebo, & cum P. meo Augustino in Manichæum sic similiter obloquentem dicam: *Hanc, quam despicis, Manichæe, mater mea est, sed manu fabricata est mea.* (Quod opus manu Altissimi fabricatum quandoque impurum factum est?) *Si potui inquinari cum eam facerem, potui inquinari cum ex ea nascerer. Vidit Deus cuncta, quæ fecerat, & erant valde bona.* Faceret Deus manu propria Matrem suam, & non faceret eam valde bonam, sed macula originali deformatam? Certe inquinari diceretur ab hæreticis Deus, si inquinatam Filiam, Matrem, & Sponsam fabricaret. Id sentiat Muratorius; nobis enim quod arrideat, nullatenus consequetur. Hæc, & alia similia eructare visus est, quæ proculdubio à sancti Officii Tribunali permittenda non sunt. In memoriam revocet rectissimum Tribunal Alexandri VII. Decretum constituens similes propositiones, earumque assertores proscribendos esse. Quod munus sanctæ Fidei Inquisitioni, ei ad id data amplissima facultate, expressissime commendat. Hæc, quin multos libros evolverem, dixisse sufficiat ad hominis istius in religionis negotio moderationem agnoscendam, & ut omnibus ejus suspecta indoles, tot in sanctam Sedem obsequiis larvata, innotescat; atque clarissime appareat, quem causæ suæ defensorem Antisigillistæ conducere non dubitarunt. Patronum scilicet, eruditum quidem, & eloquii fastu æstuantem, sed meo, multorumque judicio non vera sapientem. Nunc ad alia. | | 160 Of this kind are the propositions (not to say blasphemies) into which he bursts forth, namely: *That our forefathers never dreamed of this*, although the piety and devotion of our forefathers toward the Conception is so ancient: *That our posterity, if they have any wisdom, will never approve it*, as if he thought, deceived by his own inflated wisdom, that this devotion and judgment, handed down from fathers to sons, is to be utterly blotted out by his well-composed eloquence: *That this is a light cause for which to squander one's life*; as if the eximious honor of the Virgin, and the peaceful possession of her immunity, are to be called a light cause: *That this opinion can be false, and is not necessarily to be defended*; as if it were not congruent with the honor of the Virgin, and with the love and omnipotence of God, to defend the immunity of His most pure and most amiable Mother, lest, the contrary opinion being admitted, we should say that God the Father omnipotent, though they could adorn her with this signal privilege—His Daughter first-begotten before every creature; the wise Son, His Mother elect from eternity; the loving Holy Spirit, His most beautiful Spouse—willed not to do so. How absurd! *That this judgment seems certain to man, a most fallacious creature*, as if he himself also were not a most fallacious man: *That it is liable to error*—the judgment, namely, which the Church approves, and acknowledges as pious, and adorns with so many privileges: *That that opinion cannot be called certain which the Church herself, though wearied by the prayers of so many pious persons, has not yet brought herself to decree certain—nay, by her silence has signified that it cannot be decreed.* As if the Church's delay in defining the immunity of the Conception could be an argument for its indefinability, or could detract anything from its intrinsic truth, if any be in it; since we know that many things revealed in the Scriptures long lay hidden from the Fathers. God has not hitherto willed to reveal this unfailingly to the Church; perhaps so that, unto the greater glory of Himself and of His Mother, that might be owed to devotion which would be owed to the precept of faith. Yet we hope, and confidently trust, that the Church will at last, at last, define it, that the mouths of the praters may be entirely stopped: *That this zeal is a base zeal* [?], *and a zeal without knowledge, an ill-advised zeal, and injurious to the counsels of prudence.* As if his own zeal should not be held the more imprudent, whereby, a rival of quarrels and contentions, he himself stirs up again this question, lulled asleep or wrapped in silence, and again arouses whirlwinds long since stilled: *That thence neither reward nor praise is to be hoped for. That the Mother of Christ should rather be angered (if the sense of anger could fall upon her), because we defend her immunity with the same ardor as the revelations of faith.* Has it been revealed to him that this our homage does not please God and His Mother? Will he ever recount that God, or the Virgin Mother, has shown signs of her displeasure? On the contrary, there are many favors and benefits toward the defenders of the Marian immunity from the most holy Lady and most amiable Mother, which are evident from the histories. I for my part firmly hope for the patronage of the Blessed Virgin, because, though so slight, I have defended her according to my power. Thus I implore, with St. Augustine, and with the Church praying thus: (41) *Let all perceive thy aid, whosoever celebrate thy holy Conception.* {marg. (41) D. August. serm. 18. de Sanctis. quod Ecclesia recitat 2. die infra octavam in 2 nocturno lect. 3.} *If* she is holy, how was she stained with original sin? *That it is uncertain, because God could have willed otherwise, and it is still uncertain whether He willed it to be otherwise.* How is it uncertain? God created the Angels most pure in their creation, and would He create the Queen of Angels impure? In original justice He created Eve, who brought death upon the world, and would He create MARY impure, who brought life to the world? Should she be created with the original contagion who had been ordained from eternity, and, before the earth was made, was already predetermined and conceived in the divine mind, that she might be the Mother of Life itself? She was being prepared to be the temple of the living God and the dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit, and would she not come down from heaven as a bride adorned for her husband? Could the omnipotent God confer upon her so great a privilege, and is it to be believed that He willed not, or shall it be uncertain whether He so willed? Let Muratorius think that. I shall teach that it is never lawful for me to persuade myself of it, and with my Father Augustine, against the Manichean speaking in like manner, I shall say: *She whom thou despisest, O Manichean, is my mother, but she was fashioned by my hand.* (Was a work fashioned by the hand of the Most High ever made impure?) *If I could be defiled when I made her, I could be defiled when I was born of her. God saw all the things that He had made, and they were very good.* Would God make His own Mother with His own hand, and not make her very good, but deformed by the original stain? Surely God would be said by the heretics to be defiled, if He fashioned a defiled Daughter, Mother, and Spouse. Let Muratorius think that; for from us he will in no wise obtain what may smile upon him. These and other like things he has seemed to belch forth, which beyond doubt are not to be permitted by the Tribunal of the holy Office. Let that most upright Tribunal recall to memory the Decree of Alexander VII, establishing that like propositions, and their assertors, are to be proscribed. Which office it most expressly commends to the Inquisition of the holy Faith, most ample faculty for that purpose being given to it. Let it suffice to have said these things, without turning over many books, for recognizing the moderation of this man in the business of religion, and that his suspect character, masked by so many acts of homage toward the holy See, may become known to all; and that it may most clearly appear what manner of defender of their cause the Antisigillists did not hesitate to hire. A patron, namely, learned indeed, and seething with the pomp of eloquence, but, in my judgment and that of many, not wise unto the truth. Now to other matters. |
